# FAANG L4 PM Interview Prep: Question Bank

**Version 2 · October 2026** · Interview preparation · PM + AI Master Library

> All 170 practice questions in one layout: what is being tested, what to clarify, an outline to rehearse from, a spoken model answer, and how it is scored.

---

## Start here

### What this book is

This is the practice half of the FAANG L4 PM Interview Prep. The Playbook teaches each round: what it tests, the framework, and worked interviews. This book holds the 170 questions to practice on, nine categories in the order of the Playbook's parts, each question in the same layout.

Questions keep the numbers they have always had, Q1 to Q170, so the eight-week plan in the Playbook and any notes that cite a question still point to the right place.

### How every question is laid out

| Section | What it gives you |
|---|---|
| The question and its tags | Category, Standard or Stretch, and Tailored when it draws on my own background |
| What they are testing | The skill behind the prompt, in a sentence or two |
| Clarify first | The two or three questions to ask before answering, with the assumption to proceed on |
| Outline | Five to seven bullets: the skeleton to rehearse from |
| Model answer | The answer as it would be spoken |
| What a strong answer shows | Three signals an interviewer is looking for |
| Where answers fail | The two most common ways to lose the question |
| If they push | Likely follow-ups with short replies |

Numbers about real products inside a prompt or an answer are either part of the hypothetical or a stated assumption. They are there to make the reasoning concrete, and none of them is a company's reported figure unless the answer says where it comes from.

### Three ways to use it

**One category tonight.** Open the chapter for tomorrow's round. Read three outlines, then answer three other questions out loud against a timer before reading their model answers.

**Mock yourself.** Pick a question from the finder without reading ahead. Say your clarifying questions, then your answer, in the time the round allows. Compare with "What a strong answer shows" and "Where answers fail", and write down one thing to change.

**The full pass.** Follow the eight-week plan in the Playbook, which assigns questions from this book by number.

For a tailored question, check the stories it names against the Story Bank before using the answer, and settle anything listed under "Facts to reconcile" and "Your facts needed" at the back of this book.

### What's new in Version 2

- Every question has the same eight-part layout, so the outline, the answer and the scoring signals are always in the same place.
- Model answers are written to be spoken and are about half their earlier length, with the reasoning kept and the padding removed.
- A question finder lists all 170 questions with their level and the stories they use.
- Every arithmetic chain was re-run, and technical explanations were checked against primary sources as of October 2026.
- Facts about my own work are unchanged from my source documents; conflicts between those documents are listed, not resolved.

# Part I — Product design

## 1. Product design questions

A product design question asks you to invent something for a named user. The interviewer scores four things: whether you pick a specific user and say why; whether you find the real problem before proposing anything; whether your solution follows from that problem and from what the company uniquely has; and whether you can say how you would know it worked.

To rehearse, read a question and answer it aloud for ten minutes before looking at anything else. Compare yourself against the outline first and the model answer second. The model answer is one good path, and yours can differ. Numbers inside answers are stated assumptions unless a source is named. Products change, so check what the company has shipped before your interview.

### Q1. Design a product that helps elderly users in the US manage their daily medications.

*Product design · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you design for one person's real constraints and explain why existing reminders fail her.

**Clarify first.**

- *Is the user the senior or a caregiver?* I'll assume the senior, living alone; family is secondary.
- *App, hardware, or both?* I'll assume a simple device plus software.

**Outline.**

- User: over 70, at home, three to seven chronic medications
- Journey: fill, organize, remember, understand, report
- Biggest gaps: remembering, and the doctor not knowing
- Why apps, dispensers and nagging fail
- Solution: voice reminders, passive verification, caregiver digest
- Metrics, then privacy and regulatory risk

**Model answer.** I'll design for a person over 70 who lives alone and manages three to seven chronic medications.

Her journey has five steps: filling prescriptions, organizing them, remembering each dose, understanding each drug, and getting adherence data to her doctor. Remembering fails most often. The doctor's blind spot is the most dangerous, because prescriptions get adjusted on guesswork.

Existing tools miss her. Reminder apps assume smartphone fluency. Smart dispensers are expensive and break down when a prescription changes. Family nagging adds anxiety. I want something ambient, low effort and trusted.

I'd build a small countertop device for the kitchen, with three parts. First, voice reminders in a warm tone: "Ruth, it's 8 AM, time for your metformin and lisinopril. Want me to read what each one does?" Second, passive verification. A camera confirms the bottle was opened and a pill taken, processes the image on the device, and stores only "taken" or "not taken." That removes self-reporting, where most adherence tools break down. Third, a weekly digest for the family instead of daily alerts, a nudge after two missed days, and a monthly one-page report for the physician.

I chose voice over mobile, and passive verification over self-report, because trust and accuracy matter more than features. Pharmacy auto-refill waits for version two; those partnerships take 6 to 12 months.

| Metric | Role | Target |
|---|---|---|
| 7-day adherence rate | Primary | From an assumed 50% to 75% in 90 days |
| Caregiver anxiety survey; daily device use | Secondary | Improving |
| False missed-dose alerts | Guardrail | Under 5% |

Risks: the camera, handled with on-device processing and a physical shutter. HIPAA once we share with providers, handled with data minimization. And tremor or weak grip, where blister packs give the camera a standard format to read.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- A full journey map, and why existing solutions fail this user
- Technical choices tied to needs: voice, passive verification
- Layered metrics with a guardrail; privacy and regulation raised unprompted

**Where answers fail.**

- A reminder app, without asking why apps already failed this group
- Generic metrics such as daily active users instead of adherence

**If they push.**

- *The vision model misses 15% of real doses. Do you ship?* Not as the only signal. Each miss alarms the family, so I'd add a spoken "Did you take it?" on low-confidence events first.
- *A pharmacy chain wants to co-brand the device.* I'd weigh distribution and refill data against lock-in and patient trust, and ask for non-exclusive terms.

### Q2. Design a feature for Google Maps to help users with mobility impairments navigate cities more confidently.

*Product design · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you narrow to one segment, find the failure that breaks a trip, and reason about where the data comes from.

**Clarify first.**

- *Which impairment first?* I'll assume wheelchair users; visual impairment needs a different product.
- *A feature or a separate mode?* I'll assume a mode inside Maps, set once in the profile.

**Outline.**

- Segment: wheelchair users
- Journey: before, in transit, at the destination
- Friction: elevator outages, sidewalks, entrances, stale data
- Solution and priority: elevator status, then sidewalks, then entrances
- Metric: trips completed without repeated re-routing
- Risk: false confidence

**Model answer.** I'll focus on wheelchair users. For them one broken elevator or missing curb cut can turn a 20-minute trip into 90 minutes, or end it. Maps already has a wheelchair-accessible routing option, so the gap is depth and freshness.

Before the trip she checks that the destination is step-free. In transit she needs to know the elevator is working, not just present, and that each crossing has a curb cut. At the destination she has to find the accessible entrance, often not the main one.

Four frictions stand out: elevator status, sidewalk detail (a two-inch step can stop a power chair), entrance wayfinding, and trust, because accessibility data is often stale.

I'd build an accessibility mode with three parts.

| Priority | Component | How it works |
|---|---|---|
| P0 | Live elevator status | Agency feeds where they exist; elsewhere a one-tap "Is the elevator working?" to passing users, weighted by recency and reporter reliability |
| P1 | Sidewalk passability | A vision model on Street View scores curb cuts, surface and slope; routes avoid low scores and flag unverified stretches |
| P2 | Entrance guidance | A Live View arrow to the step-free entrance, from Places data enriched by owners and contributors |

Elevators come first: highest impact, some data already exists, and one outage invalidates a whole route. Sidewalks need model training. Entrances need data on millions of venues.

I rejected a standalone app. It fragments the experience and shrinks the crowdsourcing pool.

My primary metric is route completion in wheelchair mode: arriving with at most one re-route. Secondary is elevator-status accuracy, confirmed by the next user through the station. The guardrail: accessible routes shouldn't run more than 10% longer than standard ones.

The main risk is false confidence, so the interface shows data age: "Elevator verified 2 hours ago" or "No recent reports."

**What a strong answer shows.**

- One segment, a full journey, and elevator status named as the biggest gap
- A data-sourcing plan: agency feeds, crowdsourcing, Street View
- Outcome metrics (trips completed) instead of engagement

**Where answers fail.**

- Proposing a "wheelchair accessible" toggle that already exists
- A standalone app, with no argument for building into Maps

**If they push.**

- *A civil liberties group says tracking wheelchair users' paths violates privacy.* The mode is opt-in and reports are aggregated and detached from identity. I wouldn't keep per-person path histories.
- *Twelve transit agencies refuse to share elevator data.* I'd run those cities on crowdsourced confirmations and show lower confidence in the interface.

### Q3. Design a merchant-facing product for a payments platform that helps small street vendors in India accept digital payments for the first time.

*Product design · Standard · Tailored*

**What they are testing.** Whether you can explain why a cash vendor distrusts digital money, and what removes that fear.

**Clarify first.**

- *New product or an extension?* I'll assume an extension of an existing platform and its payment aggregator license.
- *Smartphones only?* I'll assume a low-end Android on patchy data, with a feature-phone fallback.

**Outline.**

- Portrait: Ramesh, vegetable cart, about ₹800 a day
- Frictions: onboarding, confirmation, cash-out
- Solution: five-minute onboarding, spoken confirmation, Daily Cash Mode
- Monetization: lending, not MDR
- Metric: weekly-active merchants at day 30

**Model answer.** Merchant onboarding and activation sat in my scope at Paytm (S2), so I'll be concrete.

Picture Ramesh, who runs a vegetable cart in Pune. He has an Android phone on patchy data and has never set up UPI. He earns about ₹800 a day, so losing ₹50 to a scam matters. He fears the money won't arrive, a customer will cheat him, or every sale will be taxed.

Three frictions follow. Onboarding needs literacy, patience and a phone that doesn't freeze. Confirmation: he can't easily read the bank SMS, and customers show fake payment screenshots. Cash-out: he needs cash tonight to buy stock.

My product is a QR code with a voice-first app.

Onboarding takes under five minutes: mobile number, Aadhaar OTP for KYC, a bank account verified by a ₹1 penny drop, then a test payment so he feels a real transaction. Voice prompts run in regional languages, and feature phones get a USSD flow.

When a payment lands, the app says in his language, "Ramesh ji, ₹120 received." He trusts his own phone's voice, not the customer's screen, and that ends the fake-screenshot problem. Offline, a local ledger shows "pending" and reconciles later.

Daily Cash Mode lets him withdraw the day's digital earnings at a partner micro-ATM. His transaction history later qualifies him for working-capital loans. Lending is the revenue model; acceptance stays free. In my retention work (S6), a significant share of churn came from merchants whose fixed monthly rental stopped making sense in slow months, so this segment pays no fixed fee.

| Metric | Role |
|---|---|
| Merchants taking a digital payment weekly at day 30, target 60% | Primary |
| Fake-payment reports near zero | Secondary |
| Share moving from Daily Cash Mode to keeping funds digital by day 90 | Trust signal |
| Settlement failures under 0.1% | Guardrail |

**What a strong answer shows.**

- A vivid portrait, with the fake screenshot named as the trust blocker
- Spoken confirmation, plus offline and feature-phone fallbacks
- Monetization through lending, which removes the fee objection

**Where answers fail.**

- "Add a UPI QR code," without asking why vendors haven't adopted existing ones
- A dashboard built for literate, always-connected users

**If they push.**

- *A competitor offers ₹500 cashback to switch.* I wouldn't match cash. Ramesh stays for same-day cash-out, the voice he trusts and a loan limit that grows with his history.
- *India has introduced MDR on larger UPI payments. Does that change the model?* Not for Ramesh: under the rules announced in September 2026, small merchants and payments up to ₹2,000 stay exempt. If he outgrows that, I'd show every charge plainly. I've treated small-merchant pricing as a trust problem before (S10).

### Q4. Design a product for Gen Z users that helps them build a consistent savings habit.

*Product design · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you find behavioral root causes and design a habit loop instead of a feature list.

**Clarify first.**

- *Standalone app or a bank feature?* I'll assume standalone, with a partner bank holding funds.
- *Which market, and who?* I'll assume the US, working 22 to 27 year olds, often on irregular income.

**Outline.**

- Root causes: irregular income, constant temptation, abstract goals
- Principles: rewarding now, social, automatic, visible
- Core: Goal Jars filled by automatic rules
- Social jars and payday nudges
- Habit loop: trigger, routine, reward
- Monetization without user fees; metrics; risks

**Model answer.** Gen Z worries about money and still saves little. I see three causes. Income is irregular: gig work, tips and hourly shifts make a fixed monthly transfer impractical. Temptation is constant: pay-later offers and social commerce spend the paycheck before it lands. And "save for a rainy day" is abstract when rent is due next week.

So saving must feel rewarding now, be part of identity, happen without a decision, and be visible and shareable.

The product is a goal-first, automatic micro-savings app.

The core is Goal Jars. Each jar has a picture, a target and a date: "Japan by March," "three months' rent." Earmarking research by Soman and Cheema suggests money labeled for a concrete purpose is more likely to stay saved.

Money moves by rules. Round-ups: a $4.73 coffee sends $0.27 to the top jar. And a percentage rule: "save 5% of every deposit over $200." A percentage copes with uneven income where a fixed amount can't.

Social jars let up to five friends save toward a shared goal, with contributions visible. That adds accountability and brings in new users.

Nudges arrive on payday and Sunday evening, in a casual voice: "You made $847 this week. Put $85 toward Tokyo?" A filled jar produces a shareable card.

The habit loop: the paycheck notification is the trigger, the automatic transfer is the routine, the jar animation is the reward. No decision is needed in the middle.

We earn interest margin on balances and affiliate revenue when users spend savings with partner brands. No user fees; this audience won't accept them.

| Metric | Role |
|---|---|
| Still saving after 90 days | Primary |
| Jar completion rate; average monthly savings per active user | Secondary |
| Round-up dispute rate under 1% | Guardrail |

Risks: shared-jar disputes, handled with transparent contributions and penalty-free exit. And holding funds requires a partner bank with pass-through deposit insurance.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Behavioral root causes, including irregular income
- A complete habit loop, with social jars as the differentiator
- Monetization without fees on a fee-averse audience

**Where answers fail.**

- A standard savings account with badges
- A budgeting or expense tracker, which is a different product

**If they push.**

- *70% of users never reach their first goal.* First jars are probably too big. I'd default the first goal to something reachable in a month and split large jars into milestones.
- *How would this change for India?* Recurring UPI mandates replace card round-ups, and goals shift toward festivals, gold and family.

### Q5. Design a voice-first interface for Amazon's shopping experience targeting users who are visually impaired.

*Product design · Stretch*

**What they are testing.** Whether you rethink the interaction model for voice, and design for error recovery and trust.

**Clarify first.**

- *The app with a voice layer, or Alexa on a speaker?* I'll assume both, designed as if there is no screen.
- *What is success?* I'll assume task completion and satisfaction, not compliance.

**Outline.**

- Problem: pages built to be scanned, read one element at a time
- Principles: one decision per turn, recovery, progressive disclosure, two-item memory limit
- Four use cases by frequency, a pattern for each
- Purchase safeguards
- Metrics
- Trade-off: comparison is slower by voice

**Model answer.** A product page has hundreds of elements, and a screen reader walks through them one at a time. I want to move from "scan and click" to "converse and decide."

Four principles. Each turn carries one decision. The user can always say "repeat," "go back" or "start over" without losing progress. Lead with the most decision-relevant fact and go deeper on request. Never ask the user to hold more than two items in memory.

Then four use cases, most frequent first.

| Use case | Pattern |
|---|---|
| Repurchase | "You last ordered dish soap, 25 ounces, $6.49. Reorder?" One confirmation. Flag discontinued items or a price rise over 20%. |
| Discovery with constraints | "I found 34 speakers under $50. The top rated is $49.95, 4.7 stars, waterproof. Next option, or more on this one?" Voice filters at any point. |
| Comparison | Preselect the top three by rating and review count, then compare one dimension at a time: "On battery life: 20, 30 and 15 hours." |
| Gifting | Targeted questions ("How old is she? What does she enjoy?"), like a sales associate. |

For recovery, context persists through the session. Before any order the system reads back item, price, delivery date and address, and waits for an explicit "yes, order this." Above $100 there is a 60-second window to say "cancel," which matters most on speakers.

The primary metric is task completion on discovery tasks, with parity against sighted users on the visual app as the target. Secondary: repurchase in under 30 seconds, and a spoken satisfaction rating. The guardrail is accidental purchases under 0.05%.

One honest trade-off: comparison stays slower by voice, perhaps 90 seconds against 10 by eye. So I'd offer a recommended pick with the reason ("based on your past purchases, option B") and let the user override.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- A conversational architecture with progressive disclosure, not a page read aloud
- Distinct patterns for all four use cases
- Accidental-purchase safeguards and accessibility-appropriate metrics

**Where answers fail.**

- Voice commands added to the existing app, with the same interaction model
- Treating accessibility as compliance, with no error recovery

**If they push.**

- *Word error rate is 20% for users with speech disabilities.* I'd offer opt-in personalized speech models, confirm low-confidence turns, and make switch or keyboard input an equal path.
- *Legal says voice recordings of medical purchases could be subpoenaed.* Keep transcripts only as long as the order needs, and keep sensitive categories out of voice history by default.

### Q6. Design a feature for Spotify that helps users discover live music events near them.

*Product design · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you build on the company's unique asset (taste data) and tie a consumer feature to a business model.

**Clarify first.**

- *Improve the existing Live Events listing, or start fresh?* I'll assume a much more personal and social version of it, inside the app.
- *Revenue or retention?* I'll assume retention first, with affiliate revenue.

**Outline.**

- Asset: Spotify knows taste; ticketing sites know events
- Segments: fanatic, social concertgoer, lapsed fan
- Design: ranked Live feed, Friday notification, taste-matched discovery, plan with friends
- Ticketing: affiliate links now, native later
- Metrics, then risks

**Model answer.** Ticketing platforms know events. Spotify knows taste, and how deep it runs. Someone who plays 90% of a band's catalog is a different prospect from someone with one saved song. The value is closing the gap between "I love this band" and "they play 30 minutes away next Friday."

I see three segments. The fanatic streams releases on day one and would travel two hours. The social concertgoer goes two to four times a year with friends. The lapsed fan needs a low-effort nudge. I'll design for the fanatic, who has the highest intent, and extend to the social group.

The feature is a personalized Live feed: upcoming shows within an adjustable radius, 50 miles by default. Ranking blends four signals: how much the user streams the artist (the heaviest weight), genre affinity, date (weekends rank higher), and whether friends are interested. The model learns from which events users actually attended.

Three things sit around the feed. A Friday-morning notification, opt-in, with the single best match: "Your top artist plays tonight, and three friends listen to them." A row of artists you don't know yet but would probably enjoy live, so the feed has value when favorites aren't touring. And "Plan with friends": tap Going, see who else is interested, coordinate.

For tickets I'd deep-link to partners and take an affiliate commission; assume 3 to 5%. Native ticketing needs venue contracts, fraud controls and compliance, which would add a year or more.

| Metric | Role |
|---|---|
| Click-through from the Live feed to a ticket page | Primary |
| Ticket conversion after the click | Secondary |
| 30-day retention lift against a control | Business case |
| Friday notification opt-outs above 5% | Guardrail |

Risks: small markets look empty, so I'd fill with livestreams. And partner dependency, so I'd integrate at least two ticket sources.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Taste data as the differentiator, with concrete ranking signals
- Both the fanatic and the social use case served
- Monetization staged across versions; metrics beyond clicks

**Where answers fail.**

- A generic nearby-concerts tab with no personalization
- A standalone events app, or no word on monetization

**If they push.**

- *A ticketing giant offers 10% commission for exclusivity.* I'd decline. Coverage is the product; dropping other sellers empties the feed in some cities.
- *Nashville's feed is full but click-through is 30% lower than New York's.* I'd suspect ranking, not supply. A dense local scene has many small acts with little streaming history, so I'd add venue and local-scene signals.

### Q7. Design a Google product that helps first-generation college students in the US navigate the financial aid process.

*Product design · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you find where the funnel really breaks (belief about eligibility, before the form) and use the company's existing surfaces.

**Clarify first.**

- *New product or inside existing ones?* I'll assume inside Search, Gemini and Calendar.
- *FAFSA only, or all aid?* I'll assume FAFSA first; it gates most other aid.

**Outline.**

- Problem: complexity and no trusted guide
- Pain points: eligibility myth, documents, the form, scholarships, award letter
- Five steps: estimator, checklist, walk-through, deadlines, award decoder
- Privacy: guide, never hold financial data
- Metrics: completion, then aid received

**Model answer.** Many eligible students never complete the FAFSA, and by one nonprofit estimate about $4 billion in Pell Grants goes unclaimed each year. The barrier is complexity and the lack of a trusted guide. And when these students don't know what to do, they search.

Picture Maria, 17, in San Antonio. Her parents didn't attend college. She has a 3.8 GPA and wants to study nursing. She believes her family earns too much to qualify. It doesn't, and that misconception stops many students before they start.

There are five pain points: the eligibility myth; gathering documents; the form itself, with its federal jargon; scholarship search, with its scams; and an award letter that mixes grants, loans and work-study.

I'd build an aid navigator across Google's surfaces, in five steps.

1. An eligibility estimator in Search. A query like "do I qualify for financial aid" shows a card with four questions: citizenship status, household size, family income, and at least half-time attendance. It returns an estimate and a next step.
2. A personalized document checklist, saved to her Google Account, with a plain explanation of where to find each item.
3. A walk-through in Gemini, in English and Spanish. It explains each question, and she enters answers on StudentAid.gov. We link to the form and never copy it.
4. Deadlines for her state and schools pushed to Calendar, with reminders at two weeks, one week and one day.
5. An award-letter decoder: "$5,000 Pell Grant, free money. A $3,500 subsidized loan, repaid after graduation. And $2,000 of work-study, earned by working on campus."

On privacy, Google guides and never collects financial data. Tax information moves through the form's own exchange with the IRS.

| Metric | Role |
|---|---|
| FAFSA completion among users, from an assumed 60% baseline to 75% | Primary |
| Pell award rate against a control | Secondary |
| Unclaimed Pell dollars recovered over three years | Societal |

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Framing around unclaimed aid, with the eligibility myth at the top of the funnel
- A multi-step journey built on Search, Calendar and Gemini
- Financial data kept out of Google's hands; a societal metric

**Where answers fail.**

- A scholarship search that skips the FAFSA completion bottleneck
- A FAFSA clone, with privacy and compliance ignored

**If they push.**

- *Starts rise 25% but completions only 5%.* The top of the funnel works and the middle doesn't. I'd find the abandonment step, probably parent information, and put the checklist and reminders there.

### Q8. Design a product that helps remote-first software engineering teams maintain strong culture and connection.

*Product design · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you can make "culture" concrete: name the gaps remote work creates, then give each a mechanism and a metric.

**Clarify first.**

- *Standalone tool or inside Slack or Teams?* I'll assume a product that plugs into the tools the team already uses.
- *What does culture mean here?* I'll assume social connection and trust, async first.

**Outline.**

- Productivity is solved; social infrastructure isn't
- Why chat, pairing bots and video calls fall short
- Five gaps, one mechanism each
- Metric: weak-tie index
- Risks: fatigue, privacy, adoption

**Model answer.** Remote teams have solved shipping code across time zones. They haven't solved what hallways did for free: psychological safety, shared identity and trust.

Existing tools fall short. Chat is organized around topics, not relationships. Coffee-pairing bots such as Donut match people at random, with no context. Video calls punish distant time zones.

Four personas. The new hire doesn't know who the informal influencers are. The mid-tenure engineer talks to the same five people every day. The manager can't sense morale. The executive wants culture to scale without attending every ritual.

Remote work creates five gaps, and I'd build one mechanism for each.

| Gap | Mechanism |
|---|---|
| Weak ties | A weekly "meet someone new" suggestion from a relationship graph, with a two-sentence intro and an icebreaker; accepting creates a 20-minute invite |
| Serendipity | An async water-cooler feed, emoji reactions only, ranked toward people you interact with least |
| Shared experience | Fixed-format rituals: weekly "Wins and Wonders," a monthly five-minute async show-and-tell |
| Recognition | Signals from GitHub, Jira and chat suggest shout-outs; the manager approves before posting |
| Morale signal | A weekly three-question anonymous pulse from a validated library; aggregate trends only |

The pairing is a recommendation, not a random draw: it uses interaction frequency, shared projects and stated interests. And every ritual has a set format, so nobody has to invent how to take part.

My primary metric is a weak-tie index: the number of distinct colleagues each person has a meaningful interaction with per month, with a target of eight or more. Secondary: pulse participation of 70% or more. The guardrail is the skip rate on introductions. Above 20%, the suggestions are miscalibrated.

Risks: fatigue, so everything is async by default and calls are opt-in. Privacy, since pulse data could surface in employment disputes, so I'd minimize and expire it. And adoption: culture tools die when leaders don't take part, so leadership participation is made visible.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Weak ties named as the core gap, with a metric to match
- A mechanism per gap, including a morale signal for managers
- Async-first design; executive participation addressed

**Where answers fail.**

- "Chat with more emoji": features with no structural diagnosis
- Synchronous or VR events that ignore time zones

**If they push.**

- *20% of users post 80% of the feed.* That is normal for feeds. I'd measure reactions and connections made instead of posts, and prompt quiet people.
- *A manager uses pulse data to penalize a team.* Minimum group sizes, no individual responses, no raw exports, and monitoring for misuse.

### Q9. Design a BNPL (Buy Now Pay Later) product for healthcare expenses targeting middle-income Americans.

*Product design · Stretch · Tailored*

**What they are testing.** Whether you can design credit responsibly: sound economics on one side, guardrails against debt harm on the other.

**Clarify first.**

- *What bill sizes?* I'll assume $500 to $10,000: dental, vision, dermatology, physical therapy.
- *Is there interest?* I'll assume none on short plans and a fixed, disclosed APR on long ones.

**Outline.**

- Gap: insured, high deductible, no spare cash
- Distribution: through providers, at point of care
- Underwriting in two tiers
- No deferred interest, ever
- Guardrails against debt harm
- Metrics and regulation

**Model answer.** I worked on a pricing engine that handled BNPL as one of its instruments (S3), so I'll start with who pays and who carries the risk.

The user is insured, has a deductible in the thousands, and can't pay a $2,000 bill at once. Jamie earns $68,000 a year. Her crown costs $1,400 after insurance. She can use a credit card at, say, 22% APR, take a medical card with deferred interest, or delay care.

The product sits at the provider's payment desk: "Pay today or split into four payments." The provider is paid in full immediately, less a 3 to 5% fee, and we carry the installment risk. I'd sell to dental and therapy chains, where one sale covers 50 to 200 locations.

Underwriting has two tiers. Under $1,000: four payments over six weeks, a soft credit pull plus income verification, approval in 30 seconds. From $1,000 to $10,000, over 3 to 24 months: a hard pull, a payment-to-income check, and limits that grow with on-time repayment. Cosmetic surgery is excluded.

| Plan | Cost to the patient |
|---|---|
| Pay in four | No interest, no fees |
| 12 to 24 months, credit score above 720 | 0% APR |
| Score 620 to 719 | 8.99% fixed |
| Score 580 to 619 | 14.99% fixed |

There is no deferred interest, ever. Charging back-interest on the full amount after a missed deadline is where patients get hurt. Refusing it is the ethical differentiator.

Four guardrails. Total balance is capped at 15% of verified monthly income times six, about $5,100 for Jamie. Procedures over $3,000 get a 24-hour confirmation window. A verified job loss pauses plans for 60 days with no late fees. And the confirmation screen lists every payment and date: four of $350, total $1,400, nothing else.

Metrics: default rate, under 3% on pay-in-four and under 6% on 12-month plans; and the share of financed procedures actually completed, which is the health outcome.

On regulation, federal BNPL rules are unsettled, so I'd build to the stricter standard: plain disclosure and reporting on-time payments to credit bureaus. Interest-bearing plans run through a sponsor bank. We store the procedure category, never the diagnosis.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- The middle-income gap, and distribution through providers at point of care
- Deferred interest rejected on ethical grounds, with hardship guardrails
- A health outcome metric, and a position on regulation

**Where answers fail.**

- A generic BNPL app with no medical context or regulatory awareness
- High-interest lending to stretched patients, with no mention of harm

**If they push.**

- *Suppose a regulator caps APR on medical BNPL at 10%.* The 14.99% tier stops working. I'd offer those patients pay-in-four on smaller amounts. I wouldn't hide the cost in fees; pricing is a trust issue (S10).

### Q10. Design a product that helps independent musicians in the US generate sustainable income from their music.

*Product design · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you diagnose the economics first, and whether your product changes who owns the fan relationship.

**Clarify first.**

- *Which tier of musician?* I'll assume mid-tier independents: 1,000 to 100,000 monthly listeners, no label.
- *Which revenue: recorded, live, merchandise?* I'll assume all three, joined by a direct fan relationship.

**Outline.**

- Economics: per-stream income can't support most artists
- Root causes: no fan relationship, one revenue source, admin load
- Core: paid fan membership fed by streaming data
- Around it: income forecast, booking assistant, merchandise timing, royalty tracker
- Pricing: revenue share, no subscription
- Riskiest assumption: access to listener data

**Model answer.** I'll start with the economics. Assume a stream pays $0.003 to $0.005. A million streams a month grosses $3,000 to $5,000, and after splits the artist might keep around $1,500. Most never reach a million. The problem is structural, so the answer is more revenue lines, built on direct fan relationships.

Three root causes. Streaming platforms sit between artist and fan, so the artist doesn't know who the fans are. Income is concentrated in one source the artist doesn't control. And admin, which I'll assume takes half their working time, crowds out creating.

The product is a revenue operating system for the artist, with five parts.

- Fan membership. Top listeners are invited to an inner circle at $5, $10 or $25 a month, set by the artist: music 72 hours early, behind-the-scenes content, a monthly live Q&A, a direct channel.
- Income forecast. One screen projects streaming, memberships, shows and merchandise on a 90-day trend.
- Booking assistant. It finds venues that hosted similar acts and drafts outreach in the artist's voice; the artist approves each message. Four hours a week becomes a 20-minute review.
- Merchandise on demand, with no inventory, promoted 24 hours after a release, when fan excitement peaks.
- Royalty tracker. It consolidates distributor and performing-rights statements and flags uncollected royalties.

Pricing is 15% of membership revenue with no subscription, so adopting it costs nothing. That is above Patreon's standard 10%, and it pays for the data integration and booking tools.

| Metric | Role |
|---|---|
| Average monthly artist income, target $2,000 or more within 12 months | Primary |
| Top-listener to member conversion; artist churn | Secondary |
| Fan refund rate | Guardrail |

My riskiest assumption is reach. Platforms mostly show artists aggregate data, not identities, so inviting top listeners needs a partnership. The fallback is owned channels: an email sign-up link wherever the music appears.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- The platform between artist and fan named as the root problem
- A membership layer fed by streaming data, with lead generation designed in
- Revenue share instead of subscription; artist income as the primary metric

**Where answers fail.**

- A social media scheduler or merchandise plug-in that ignores the economics
- Metrics about tool usage instead of artist income

**If they push.**

- *80% of income goes to the top 5% of artists.* Some concentration is expected. I'd track median artist income and aim the booking and royalty tools at the middle.

### Q11. Design a product for Apple that helps users manage digital eye strain from prolonged screen use.

*Product design · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you use what only this company has (sensors, the operating system, the health platform) and know what already ships.

**Clarify first.**

- *Which devices?* I'll assume iPhone, iPad and Mac together, because strain is cumulative.
- *Adults or children?* I'll assume adults first, with a stricter mode parents can set.

**Outline.**

- Causes: fewer blinks, sustained close focus, bright light, short viewing distance
- Build on Screen Distance; add enforced 20-20-20 breaks
- One timer across devices
- Evening display changes; Health dashboard; privacy
- Metric: break adherence; guardrail on opt-outs

**Model answer.** Eye strain from screens means dry eyes, blurred vision and headaches, and it is common among heavy users. Apple controls the display, the front sensors, the operating system and the Health platform, so it can do what no app developer can.

Four causes. Blink rate drops sharply during focused screen work, which dries the eye. The focusing muscle stays contracted for hours. Bright, high-contrast light at close range may add fatigue, though the evidence on blue light is mixed. And people hold phones too close.

Apple already ships Screen Distance, which uses the TrueDepth camera to warn when a device is held closer than 12 inches for a while. I'd build on it with a cross-device system in five parts.

First, distance, made both gentler and stricter. For adults, an amber indicator and a haptic instead of an interruption. For children, parents can choose a mode where the screen dims until the device moves back.

Second, enforced breaks on the 20-20-20 rule: every 20 minutes, look 20 feet away for 20 seconds. A full-screen overlay appears and clears itself after 20 seconds. It can't be dismissed early. That is the key choice, because people don't take voluntary micro-breaks.

Third, one timer across devices. Moving from iPhone to Mac continues the clock, because exposure is cumulative.

Fourth, evening comfort beyond Night Shift's color shift: lower contrast, and a reading mode for long reading. I'd test both before claiming a benefit.

Fifth, a visual wellness view in Health: distance, break adherence and total exposure, with weekly trends and sharing with an eye doctor.

Privacy follows the Face ID model: distance is computed on the device and no images leave it.

| Metric | Role |
|---|---|
| Scheduled breaks completed, target 50% | Primary |
| Share of screen time at a healthy distance; self-reported strain in a 7-day survey | Secondary |
| Users switching the feature off | Guardrail |

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Apple's sensors used creatively, building on what already exists
- An enforced break with a clear justification; one timer across devices
- Parental controls treated as a distinct use case

**Where answers fail.**

- A screen-time tracker or blue-light filter that Apple already has
- No use of the hardware advantages, and no privacy position

**If they push.**

- *Users say the forced 20-second break wrecks their focus.* I'd keep the default, allow a few deferrals a day, and time breaks to land between tasks. An enforced break nobody leaves on has failed.
- *Eye-doctor associations want to review the health claims.* Good. I'd involve them before launch and limit the copy to what evidence supports, with no treatment claims.

### Q12. Design a Google Maps feature that helps users find parking in real time in dense urban areas.

*Product design · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you see a data problem before a feature, and can show uncertain predictions honestly.

**Clarify first.**

- *Street parking, garages, or both?* I'll assume both.
- *Which cities?* I'll assume we start where meter or sensor data and garage partners exist.

**Outline.**

- Problem: navigation ends at the address, not the parking spot
- Data: city feeds, garage partners, crowdsourced signals
- Pre-trip forecast with garage booking (P0)
- En-route street probability overlay
- Parking mode: guidance continues to a likely spot
- Meter reminder; monetization; metrics; risks

**Model answer.** Maps knows where I'm going and stops helping at the address. INRIX has estimated that US drivers spend 17 hours a year hunting for parking. Maps has shipped a coarse parking-difficulty icon and partner links to garage booking. The gap is live, block-level guidance.

Availability is a data problem first. I'd combine three sources.

| Source | What it gives | Limit |
|---|---|---|
| City meter and sensor feeds | Occupancy by block | Few cities; refresh lags turnover |
| Garage partners | Live availability and booking | Garages only |
| Crowdsourced signals, opt-in | A phone that parks marks a space filled; repeated circling marks scarcity | Probabilistic |

Together they feed a block-level probability model.

The feature has four parts.

Pre-trip forecast. With the route, a card says: "Parking is hard near this destination. Garage two minutes' walk away, $12. Reserve?" This is P0. A reservation removes the search entirely and earns a commission.

En-route overlay. Within about 400 meters of the destination, blocks are shaded green, yellow or red by likelihood, and meter zones show their time limits.

Parking mode. Within 500 meters, navigation doesn't end. It switches to a block view with guidance: "Turn right on Market Street; parking likely on the left," with the nearest garage as the fallback. This is the differentiator: the trip ends at the spot, not at the address.

Meter reminder. After "I've parked," a reminder fires 15 minutes before the meter expires.

Revenue comes from booking commissions and promoted garage listings.

| Metric | Role |
|---|---|
| Forecast-card click-through and booking conversion | Primary |
| Search time against a control, target 40% lower | Outcome |
| Guidance that ends in a no-parking zone | Guardrail |

Risks: freshness, since spaces turn over in seconds while feeds refresh in minutes, so the wording is honest: "likely available." And background location for crowdsourcing needs explicit opt-in.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- A three-source data strategy, with uncertainty shown honestly
- Parking mode: navigation extended past the destination
- Clear monetization; search-time reduction as the outcome metric

**Where answers fail.**

- "Add parking information to Maps," with no data source or freshness plan
- A separate parking app that ignores what Maps already knows

**If they push.**

- *The pilot model is 60% accurate. Do you ship?* It depends on the error. Saying "likely" where there is nothing costs trust, so I'd ship only high-confidence blocks and label the rest unknown.
- *A city demands our crowdsourced data in exchange for its feed.* I'd share aggregated, anonymized block-level occupancy, which helps their planning. I wouldn't share individual trips.

### Q13. Design a product that helps gig workers in India (delivery riders, auto-rickshaw drivers) access emergency financial support within hours of an income shock.

*Product design · Stretch · Tailored*

**What they are testing.** Whether you can design credit for people the credit system can't see, with speed as the product and repayment that can't make things worse.

**Clarify first.**

- *Inside a gig platform's app or standalone?* I'll assume inside the platform, which supplies distribution and income data.
- *Loan, insurance or peer lending?* I'll assume a small advance against future earnings.

**Outline.**

- Shock: a breakdown costs three to seven days of income
- Underwriting on platform earnings, not a credit score
- One-tap request, money in 15 minutes over UPI
- Repayment as a share of each payout
- Fraud bounds, credit ladder, fee, regulation

**Model answer.** When a delivery rider's motorcycle breaks down, he loses today's earnings and the next three to seven days. Banks take days. Microfinance needs group liability and branch visits. BNPL needs a credit history he lacks. The gap is liquidity in hours.

I'd build an earned-income advance inside the gig platform's app.

Eligibility comes from platform history, with no credit score. Say 150 or more deliveries in the past 30 days and a completion rate above 90% earns a limit equal to five days of average earnings.

In an emergency he picks a type (vehicle, medical, household), enters an amount up to his limit and confirms. The type calibrates risk and never gates eligibility, because I don't want workers to lie. Money reaches his UPI-linked account within 15 minutes.

Repayment is proportional. Twenty percent of each payout goes to the balance: on a ₹600 day, ₹120. On a day with no earnings nothing is taken, and there is no default event. Repayment can never leave him worse off than he already is.

Fraud is bounded. The platform already knows identity, location and activity, and someone who reports a breakdown while still accepting orders is flagged. In my compliance and KYB work (S7) I treated risk triggers as explicit product states, not edge cases; same here.

Three on-time repayments raise the limit. After five, with consent, his record goes to the credit bureaus and starts a credit file.

The price is a flat ₹50 to ₹100 per advance, about 3 to 7% on ₹1,500, with no interest or late fees. As an APR that is high, and I'd disclose it as one, but it is capped and far below informal lenders.

A regulated lender holds the loans and the platform is its lending service provider. Under RBI's digital lending rules, money goes straight to his bank account and repayments straight to the lender.

| Metric | Role |
|---|---|
| Request to funds under 15 minutes for 95% of advances | Primary |
| Default rate under 5% | Secondary |
| Days back on the road after a shock: 2, against 7 without | Outcome |

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Income-linked repayment named as the structural idea
- Underwriting on platform data; 15-minute disbursement as the core KPI
- A credit-building ladder, and a lawful structure through a regulated lender

**Where answers fail.**

- A standard EMI loan that ignores income volatility
- No answer for speed, or for the missing credit score

**If they push.**

- *Some workers take an advance every week.* That is dependency, not emergency use. I'd add a cooling-off period, show total fees paid, and offer frequent users a small savings buffer.

### Q14. Design a Microsoft Teams feature that helps reduce meeting overload for knowledge workers.

*Product design · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you separate root causes and match an intervention to each, without taking control from the organizer.

**Clarify first.**

- *Teams only, or the Outlook calendar too?* I'll assume both; the meeting is created in the calendar.
- *Reduce time or improve quality?* I'll assume reduce hours first, with quality as a guardrail.

**Outline.**

- Meeting time has grown sharply; focus time is the cost
- Four causes: default durations, over-invitation, stale recurring meetings, meetings replacing async work
- One intervention per cause
- Organization dashboard
- Metric: weekly meeting hours; quality guardrail
- Risk: organizers feel overruled

**Model answer.** Microsoft's Work Trend Index reported a 252% increase in weekly meeting time for the average Teams user between February 2020 and early 2022. The cost is focus time. Microsoft controls the calendar, the meeting and Copilot, so it can act on all four causes.

| Cause | Intervention |
|---|---|
| Durations set by default, not need | Copilot suggests a length from the agenda and attendee count; ending early (25 and 50 minutes) becomes the organization default, not an optional setting |
| Over-invitation | Before sending, Copilot proposes a Core and FYI split; FYI people get the summary within an hour |
| Recurring meetings that outlive their purpose | A health score per series, with a "review this meeting" prompt to the organizer |
| Meetings replacing async work | For titles like "approve," "decide" or "align," Copilot offers to draft a one-page decision doc instead |

The duration change is the cheapest and reaches the most people.

Right-sizing protects relationships. Nobody is uninvited; they move to FYI with a promised summary.

The health score for a recurring series uses three signals: more than 30% of invitees regularly declining, action items that aren't followed up, and six or more weeks without a real agenda change.

At the organization level, admins get a weekly dashboard: meeting hours per employee, how many people have no focus block in a day, and the share of meetings that end with documented actions.

| Metric | Role |
|---|---|
| Weekly meeting hours per active user, target 15% lower within 90 days of adoption | Primary |
| Actual against scheduled duration; decision-doc adoption | Secondary |
| Self-reported productivity and collaboration quality | Guardrail |

Risks: organizers may feel the AI undermines them, so everything is a suggestion. And right-sizing will misjudge meetings where everyone is needed for reasons the model can't see. One tap dismisses it for that series for good.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Root causes separated, with a distinct intervention for each
- An async alternative, not only shorter meetings
- An organization dashboard; meeting hours as the primary metric

**Where answers fail.**

- Showing meeting statistics with nothing the user can act on
- Hard limits that ignore how behavior actually changes

**If they push.**

- *Hours fell 12%, but Copilot summaries are often wrong.* That is urgent, because the FYI split depends on trusted summaries. I'd pause FYI suggestions where quality is low and add organizer review before sending.

### Q15. Design a product for a B2B payments company that helps CFOs at mid-market companies ($50-500M revenue) get real-time visibility into their company's payment flows.

*Product design · Stretch · Tailored*

**What they are testing.** Whether you see a data-unification problem with a trust requirement, and can turn "visibility" into specific CFO decisions.

**Clarify first.**

- *Standalone, or part of our payments platform?* I'll assume a product on our platform that also reads other banks and systems.
- *Receivables, payables, or both?* I'll assume both; cash position needs both.

**Outline.**

- Persona: CFO of a $200M manufacturer; data in silos
- Foundation: one normalized payment record
- Workflows: cash position, receivables risk, payables timing
- Alerts, onboarding, pricing
- Guardrail: accuracy

**Model answer.** I've worked on this from the finance side. BINARY turned manual, Excel-based invoicing into an automated system (S1), and my interchange work traced costs across transaction records, network charges and reconciliation logic (S4).

Take the CFO of a $200M manufacturer. She has four bank accounts, two ERP systems, ACH batches, wires and card programs. She knows today's balance in each account. She can't say what cash she'll have in ten days, which customers are drifting late, or which suppliers she pays inefficiently. Bank files arrive at end of day, the ERP uses another schema, and an analyst reconciles in Excel overnight. She sees the picture 24 to 48 hours late.

The foundation is a unification layer: connectors to banks, ERPs, card platforms and payment processors, normalized into one payment record with date, amount, currency, counterparty, category, status and rail. Each source carries a freshness stamp, because "real time" is only as live as the slowest feed.

| Workflow | What the CFO gets |
|---|---|
| Cash position | Cash across accounts, receivables due, payables for 30 days, and projected cash at 7, 14 and 30 days, based on each counterparty's payment history |
| Receivables risk radar | Each customer green, yellow or red by drift from their usual timing; a Monday alert: "Three customers moved to red, $2.4M exposure" |
| Payables timing | "You pay 14 vendors 10 days early, about $3.2M a year. Paying on terms keeps roughly $88K more cash on hand." |

Alerts go to email and Slack: cash below a threshold, any payment above $500K, a customer turning red.

Onboarding takes two weeks, run by our solutions engineer. Mid-market finance teams can't staff a six-month project.

Pricing is $2,500 to $8,000 a month by number of integrations. At 500 customers that is $15M to $48M in ARR.

Metrics: data lag, from 24 to 48 hours down to near real time. Then CFO NPS above 50 and bad-debt reduction against the prior 12 months. The guardrail is transaction accuracy above 99.95%. After one reconciliation error the CFO stops trusting every number, so accuracy outranks features.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Three layers: data unification, insight, workflow triggers
- Specific CFO workflows instead of a generic dashboard
- A trust-critical accuracy guardrail and a realistic integration plan

**Where answers fail.**

- A reporting tool that summarizes history, with nothing live or predictive
- A consumer-style finance dashboard that ignores ERP integration

**If they push.**

- *The 30-day forecast was off by 18% and she drew on a credit line wrongly.* I'd run a root-cause review with her, then show forecasts as ranges with their drivers and track record.

### Q16. Design a product that helps parents track and manage their child's nutrition habits without creating anxiety or disordered eating patterns.

*Product design · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether the constraint in the prompt, tracking without anxiety, drives your design, your metrics and what you refuse to build.

**Clarify first.**

- *What age?* I'll assume school age, 5 to 12, with the parent as the user.
- *What does success mean?* I'll assume variety across a week, not meal-by-meal precision.

**Outline.**

- Tension: tracking informs, and can teach anxiety
- Principle: patterns across a week, not precision per meal
- Core: food-group logging in seconds, additive framing only
- Close the loop: meal ideas, groceries, pediatrician report
- Child view and ethical guardrails
- Metrics with a child-anxiety guardrail

**Model answer.** Parents want to know their child is eating well. The usual tools, food diaries and calorie counters, carry a risk: clinicians warn that calorie tracking around children can pass on anxiety and feed disordered eating. So I need to give parents enough signal to act without turning meals into surveillance.

My principle is pattern over precision. The question is whether broad needs are met across a week, not whether one lunch was good enough.

Logging uses six groups: grains, protein, dairy or calcium, fruits, vegetables and healthy fats. The parent taps icons. There are no specific foods and no portions, and a meal takes 10 to 15 seconds. The dashboard says: "Maya hit all six groups on five of seven days. Iron-rich foods showed up on three days; five would be great." No calories, no macros, no "bad food" labels.

Feedback only adds. "Add one more iron-rich food this week; here are five ideas Maya might like." Never "too much," "bad" or "avoid." The visual is a tree that grows a leaf for each group covered.

To close the loop, a gap produces three meal or snack ideas and a one-tap grocery add. Each quarter the app generates a one-page pattern report for the pediatrician. That makes it medically useful without being a medical product.

From age 8 the child can tap what they ate and watch their own tree grow.

Then what I won't build: no weight and no BMI, anywhere. And if a parent logs very limited meals for several days, the app says, "Maya's meals have been limited recently. If you have concerns about appetite or variety, a pediatrician can help." That is a quiet check for parental restriction.

| Metric | Role |
|---|---|
| Food-group breadth after three months | Primary |
| Parent anxiety against a calorie-tracking app, by validated questionnaire | Secondary |
| No rise in child food-anxiety scores at six months, by validated screener | Guardrail |

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Pattern-based tracking with consistently additive framing
- Ethical guardrails: no weight tracking, a restriction check, a pediatrician link
- Child eating anxiety proposed as a guardrail metric

**Where answers fail.**

- A calorie tracker with a friendlier interface
- Weight or BMI tracking for children, with no ethical consideration

**If they push.**

- *A psychologist says the growing tree could reward restriction.* I'd test that with clinicians. The tree rewards variety, not quantity, but if evidence shows risk I'd drop the child-facing visual.

### Q17. Design a feature for LinkedIn that helps recent graduates land their first job within 6 months of graduating.

*Product design · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you find the structural reason the platform fails this user (no network) and measure the outcome, a job, instead of activity.

**Clarify first.**

- *Inside the LinkedIn app or separate?* I'll assume inside the app, as a time-boxed mode.
- *What does LinkedIn want from it?* I'll assume long-term member value first, Premium conversion second.

**Outline.**

- Paradox: need experience to get experience
- Causes: network cold start, weak applications, mislabeled entry-level roles, low visibility
- Launch Mode: a 90-day sprint
- Alumni introductions, entry-level filter, application coach, visibility boost
- Metrics and risks

**Model answer.** Graduates need experience to get a job and a job to get experience. LinkedIn adds a second problem: it works best for people who already have a network, and a graduate's connections are classmates and professors, not hiring managers.

I see four causes of the first-job gap. Network cold start. Generic applications, because nobody shows graduates what works. Signal confusion: many "entry-level" postings ask for two years of experience. And low visibility: I'd assume a thin profile surfaces less in recruiter search.

I'd build Launch Mode, a 90-day career sprint the graduate switches on with a graduation month and target roles.

| Feature | What it does |
|---|---|
| Warm alumni introductions | Surfaces 15 alumni in the target industry, those at hiring companies first, and drafts a personal note to each; the user edits and sends |
| Truly entry-level filter | A model trained on which postings actually hired people with no experience; on by default |
| Application coach | Beside each application: skill gaps against the posting, a five-point cover-letter structure, and alumni who hold the role |
| Visibility boost | Temporary extra distribution of the graduate's profile and posts to relevant recruiters |

The alumni introduction is the core. It turns a cold message into a warm one, and I'd expect several times the reply rate.

The boost costs LinkedIn some ranking efficiency. I'd accept that, because a member who lands a first job through LinkedIn tends to stay.

At day 90 the product asks, "Have you landed a job?" A yes improves the models for the next class. A no offers a 30-day extension with a changed strategy: adjacent roles, wider geography.

| Metric | Role |
|---|---|
| 90-day employment rate against a control, target 15% lift | Primary |
| Alumni reply rate, target 35% against an assumed 10% for cold messages | Secondary |

Risks: alumni fatigue, so introductions are capped at 15 per user per month and alumni can opt out. And gaming, so the mode is one-time and tied to a verified graduation date.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Network cold start identified as the structural problem
- Drafted alumni introductions; an entry-level filter backed by hiring data
- Employment as the outcome metric, not clicks or applications

**Where answers fail.**

- Better job recommendations, with nothing for the network gap
- A badge or resume template, measured by usage

**If they push.**

- *Launch Mode users find jobs faster but buy Premium less.* They got the value free. I'd judge the cohort on lifetime engagement and place the Premium offer at their next career move.

### Q18. Design a product that allows professional chefs and serious home cooks to collaborate on recipe development.

*Product design · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you see how this domain differs from document editing, and can borrow version control without importing its jargon.

**Clarify first.**

- *Live or asynchronous?* I'll assume asynchronous iteration on a shared recipe.
- *Who collaborates?* I'll assume chef pairs first, then chef and home cook, then communities.

**Outline.**

- Recipe development is iterative: 10 to 20 test rounds
- A recipe is structured data, closer to code than prose
- Structured editor, version history, parallel tests
- Attribution, then community variations
- Monetization, metrics, risks

**Model answer.** A chef adjusts seasoning, timing, texture and plating across 10 to 20 test rounds before a dish is finished. When two chefs work across cities, or a chef mentors a home cook, that happens in voice notes, scattered documents and phone photos. I want to give recipe development the rigor engineers give code.

A recipe isn't a document. It has ingredients with quantities, steps with times and temperatures, photos, and a sensory result. Changing the salt changes the balance of the dish. So versions matter: was it better at 15 grams or 20? And credit matters, because a dish is professional identity.

The product has five parts.

- A structured editor. Each ingredient is an object with quantity, unit and preparation. Steps can carry a photo or a short clip.
- Version history. Every save records what changed: "Version 8: salt 15g to 18g, roast 25 to 22 minutes." Comments attach to a single ingredient or step, and chef's notes record the result: "Still too salty."
- Parallel tests. A collaborator tries a variation without touching the main recipe, say a butter sauce against an olive oil one, compares them side by side and keeps the winner. The model is Git; the language is culinary.
- Attribution. Every contribution is logged, and the finished recipe credits roles: concept, salt ratios, photography.
- Community. Published recipes are searchable, and anyone can start a credited variation.

Monetization: free for up to three collaborators per recipe. A $15 a month professional tier adds unlimited collaborators, a private workspace and restaurant-ready export. And we take 15% of the fee when a publisher licenses a recipe.

| Metric | Role |
|---|---|
| Collaborative iterations per week | Primary |
| Share of collaborative recipes reaching Published; variation rate | Secondary |
| Attribution disputes | Guardrail |

Risks: recipe theft, so nothing is public unless the author publishes it. And a cold start, so I'd launch with 20 invited chefs.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- The recipe modeled as a structured object, with ingredient-level versions
- Branch-and-compare for parallel tests, in culinary language
- Attribution as a first-class feature, and a licensing angle

**Where answers fail.**

- A shared document with recipe formatting
- A recipe-sharing site that ignores the development workflow

**If they push.**

- *A chef says her sous-chef published her unpublished recipe.* The version log shows who created what, and when. I'd take it down pending review and make publishing owner-only in private workspaces.
- *80% of recipes are never published.* Publishing may be the wrong goal; many dishes are for a menu. I'd add a "finished, private" state and measure completed iterations.

### Q19. Design a product for Amazon that helps customers make more sustainable purchase choices.

*Product design · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you segment by motivation, design for the movable middle instead of the committed few, and make the business case.

**Clarify first.**

- *In the shopping flow or a separate storefront?* I'll assume in the flow, at the point of decision.
- *What does sustainable mean?* I'll assume climate impact: materials, manufacturing, packaging, shipping.

**Outline.**

- Gap: no comparable signal at the moment of choice
- Segments: deep green, light green, indifferent
- Core: a graded Climate Score on the product page
- Search filter, tie-break ranking, badge
- Climate Wallet incentive; shipping signal at checkout
- Business case, metrics, greenwashing risk

**Model answer.** Every purchase carries an environmental cost that the shopping experience mostly hides. Amazon already badges certified products as Climate Pledge Friendly, a yes-or-no signal on a subset of items. The gap is a comparable signal on every product at the moment of choice.

I'd assume three segments. Deep green, about 15%, will seek out and pay more. Light green, about 40%, prefer them when price, quality and convenience are close. The rest respond only to savings. I'll design for light green, the largest group that can be moved.

The core is a Climate Score from A to F on every product page, under the star rating. Inputs: material sourcing reported by sellers; manufacturing carbon intensity from life-cycle data or category benchmarks; packaging efficiency; and shipping distance. Amazon sits at the center of the supply chain and can assemble this. Where data is thin, the score is marked "estimated," with a public methodology.

In search, deep-green shoppers get a filter for A and B products. For everyone else, a higher score wins ties between similar products, and the top scorer carries a "Sustainable Pick" badge. It is a nudge. Intent and price still rule.

The incentive is a Climate Wallet: 1% of the price of A-rated purchases comes back as points, redeemable for discounts on sustainable products or donations to verified projects. That speaks to light-green shoppers through value, not guilt.

At checkout, shipping options show their relative footprint: "standard shipping has a lower carbon impact than same-day for this order."

The business case has two legs. Rules on green claims are tightening, so a substantiated score is preparation. And revenue, if sustainable products carry higher prices and margins, which I'd verify.

| Metric | Role |
|---|---|
| Purchases where the score was viewed | Primary |
| Shoppers who switched to a higher-scored alternative | Influence |
| Customers who trust the score, target above 70% | Guardrail |

Risks: greenwashing, so A and B grades need third-party certification and false claims mean delisting. And price-sensitive shoppers feeling that search got worse, so the tie-break never applies when the price gap exceeds 15%.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Segmentation by motivation, with light green chosen as the target
- A transparent score methodology and an incentive that fits the segment
- The business case, including regulation, and a credibility guardrail

**Where answers fail.**

- An eco filter, with no account of how scores are calculated
- Treating it as a corporate-responsibility exercise with no metrics

**If they push.**

- *Deep-green shoppers love it; light-green behavior hasn't changed.* The score informs but doesn't move price or convenience. I'd test larger Wallet rewards and the score inside comparison views.

### Q20. Design a product that helps oncology patients in the US stay engaged and informed throughout their cancer treatment journey.

*Product design · Stretch*

**What they are testing.** Whether you can design in a clinical setting: knowing where software informs and relays, and where clinicians must decide.

**Clarify first.**

- *Patient app, provider tool, or shared?* I'll assume a patient app connected to the care team.
- *Standalone or integrated with the hospital record?* I'll assume integrated, deployed through hospital systems.

**Outline.**

- Problem: overload at diagnosis, under-reporting in treatment, anxiety after
- Phase 1: the treatment plan in plain language
- Phase 2: daily symptom check-in with escalation to nurses
- Phase 3: "between scans" support
- Moderated peer groups
- Privacy; metrics with a staff-burden guardrail

**Model answer.** A patient leaves the oncologist's office with a diagnosis, a plan, a stack of printouts and more questions than before. The portal shows lab results without context, and search results frighten.

I'd design a patient companion around three phases.

Phase one is diagnosis and planning, weeks 1 to 4. The care team onboards the patient within 48 hours. The app imports diagnosis, plan and appointments from the hospital record through SMART on FHIR. It presents the plan in plain language: "You'll have six rounds of chemotherapy. Round one starts in two weeks. Here is what to expect." Oncology nurses write and review these explainers; I wouldn't rely on generated text here.

Phase two is active treatment. The dangerous gap is under-reported side effects, because patients don't know when to call. So the app runs a daily 90-second check-in: five questions adapted from PRO-CTCAE, a validated patient-reported instrument. When answers cross clinical thresholds, such as nausea that prevents eating or a fever of 100.4°F, the nurse navigator receives the exact scores and responds within two hours. This is a structured relay to the team that already makes these decisions. It isn't diagnosis. Medication reminders carry clinician-approved instructions, not just a time.

Phase three is remission. Many patients feel intense anxiety before surveillance scans. A "between scans" module explains what the scan measures and what results mean, and opens messaging with an oncology social worker for the 72 hours before each scan.

Across phases there are peer groups by cancer type and phase, moderated by trained survivor volunteers and kept clear of clinical advice.

On privacy, the app works under HIPAA as the hospital's business associate, with explicit consent to share with the care team.

| Metric | Role |
|---|---|
| Significant side effects escalated within two hours, against a control | Primary |
| Readmissions against a matched control | Clinical outcome |
| Nurse navigator workload | Guardrail |

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Three journey phases, with scan anxiety addressed in remission
- A validated instrument; escalation designed as a structured relay
- Readmission as the long-term outcome; staff burden as the guardrail

**Where answers fail.**

- A cancer information app with no clinical integration or escalation
- An AI chatbot that answers clinical questions

**If they push.**

- *The FDA classifies symptom escalation as software as a medical device.* I'd treat it as one: a quality system, validated thresholds and the regulatory pathway before scaling. Informational features stay outside that boundary.
- *A severe alert fires and no nurse responds.* The alert escalates on a timer to the on-call line, and the patient immediately sees "call this number, or 911 for these symptoms."

### Q21. Design a wearable product for Apple Watch that helps construction workers on active job sites manage heat stress and dehydration risk.

*Product design · Stretch*

**What they are testing.** Whether you design a safety system, not a wellness feature: what the sensors can honestly support, who acts on an alert, and what a false alarm costs.

**Clarify first.**

- *New hardware or an app on existing watches?* I'll assume a watchOS app on existing rugged models, plus a supervisor dashboard.
- *Who buys it?* I'll assume construction firms, through safety-equipment channels.

**Outline.**

- Severity: heat kills, and construction is over-represented
- Early signs, and what the watch can measure today
- Worker side: personal heat-stress score, two alert levels, hydration nudges
- Supervisor dashboard with location
- Lower thresholds for new workers; compliance log
- Metrics, false positives, liability

**Model answer.** One study of US data found construction workers were 6% of the workforce and 36% of occupational heat deaths. Heat illness can move from cramps to heat stroke quickly. The early signs are measurable: a heart rate that is high for the effort, and rising skin temperature.

An existing rugged watch, dust resistant with more than a day of battery, covers a 10-hour shift. One caveat: the watch reports wrist temperature only during sleep, so I'm assuming daytime readings can be made available. If not, the score runs on heart rate, weather and shift length.

For the worker, a personal Heat Stress Score from 0 to 100. A five-minute calibration at the start of the shift sets the baseline. The score combines the rise in heart rate over baseline, the skin-temperature trend over 20 minutes, local weather, and time on shift. Above 70, an amber alert: "Find shade and drink water in the next 10 minutes." Above 85, red: "Stop work. Move to shade now." Alerts are haptic with large text, because machinery drowns out audio.

Hydration nudges follow NIOSH guidance of a cup of water every 15 to 20 minutes in heat. The worker taps Done or Skip. Five skips in a row raises an alert even at amber.

The supervisor sees a live site map with each worker as a colored dot. A red alert pushes the worker's name, location and five-minute trend.

New workers are at the highest risk. For their first 14 days the thresholds drop to 65 and 80.

For compliance, heat thresholds are configurable by jurisdiction, and every alert, response and intervention is logged.

| Metric | Role |
|---|---|
| Recordable heat-illness incidents against control sites, target 30% lower in year one | Primary |
| Time from red alert to supervisor action; hydration compliance | Secondary |
| False-positive red alerts under 5% | Guardrail |

If workers are pulled off the job for nothing, foremen switch the system off.

On liability, this is a safety aid, not a diagnostic device. Alerts recommend ("move to shade") and never diagnose.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Sensor data combined into a personal score, with honesty about hardware limits
- A supervisor dashboard, and a protocol for unacclimatized workers
- Alignment with safety rules; false positives as the critical guardrail

**Where answers fail.**

- A hydration reminder app with no risk scoring or supervisor role
- A general fitness tracker with no safety or regulatory context

**If they push.**

- *A worker dies of heat stroke while wearing it, and the family sues.* What matters was decided earlier: no diagnostic claims, logged alerts and responses, supervisor escalation, and thresholds validated in field trials.

### Q22. Design a feature for Google Search that helps users evaluate the credibility of health information they find online.

*Product design · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you can design a judgment system at scale: not binary, honest about uncertainty, and defensible when someone calls it censorship.

**Clarify first.**

- *Where does it live?* I'll assume the results page and knowledge panels.
- *How bad is a false flag?* I'll assume very bad; wrongly labeling a credible source is the failure to avoid.

**Outline.**

- Stakes: credible and dangerous content look alike on the page
- Three tiers, not a binary; classification signals
- Page design: consensus badge, fact-check box, click-through notice
- Contested science shown as contested
- Independent review and public methodology
- Metrics and the censorship risk

**Model answer.** Health queries return everything from clinical guidance to pseudoscience, often with equal visual weight. Search already holds health pages to a higher quality bar and shows medical knowledge panels. What's missing is a credibility signal on each result.

I'd classify health content in three tiers.

| Tier | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Matches clinical consensus: guidelines, systematic reviews | National health agencies, major clinic sites |
| 2 | Evidence-informed, not yet in guidelines | A medical school article on a single trial |
| 3 | Unverified, contested or refuted | A supplement retailer's blog recommending its own product |

Classification combines source reputation, citations to peer-reviewed research, conflict of interest (does the publisher sell what it recommends?) and a content model trained on known examples.

On the page there are three treatments. Tier 1 results get a small "medical consensus" badge, which doesn't change ranking. Health knowledge panels get a fact-check box sourced only from Tier 1, with the source and the date last reviewed. Tier 3 results on page one show a notice on click: "This page contradicts established medical guidelines," with two choices, continue or see what health authorities say. That adds friction. It isn't a block.

The hardest case is real disagreement, such as screening ages for some cancers. There the panel says, "Guidelines on this topic vary," and shows the positions of the major bodies. Binary labels fail at the edge of medical knowledge.

For legitimacy, a medical advisory board of physicians from academic centers reviews disputed classifications each quarter, and the methodology and the Tier 1 source list are public.

| Metric | Role |
|---|---|
| Click-through to Tier 1 sources on health queries, target 15% lift | Primary |
| Clicks on page-one Tier 3 results, target 20% lower | Secondary |
| Credible sources wrongly flagged, under 0.5% of flags | Guardrail |

The biggest risk is perceived censorship, above all on politically charged health topics. My defense is that tiers follow clinical guidelines, the method is public and the board is independent.

**What a strong answer shows.**
