- Three tiers instead of a binary, with real scientific uncertainty handled
- Friction instead of blocking for Tier 3; an external advisory board
- False positives named as the critical guardrail

**Where answers fail.**

- "Downrank everything that isn't a government site"
- AI-written health advice in place of results, with safety and liability ignored

**If they push.**

- *The board splits three to two on homeopathy.* The criteria decide, not a vote. If the test is consistency with guidelines and evidence, patient satisfaction doesn't move it to Tier 2. I'd publish the reasoning and the dissent.

### Q23. Design a product that helps refugee families in Europe navigate bureaucratic processes (asylum applications, housing, language learning) after resettlement.

*Product design · Stretch*

**What they are testing.** Whether you can design for a vulnerable user under stress, where trust and distribution matter more than features.

**Clarify first.**

- *Government, NGO or commercial?* I'll assume NGO-led, in Germany first, so it isn't seen as a state tool.
- *What devices and languages?* I'll assume a shared Android phone, Arabic first, with audio for low literacy.

**Outline.**

- Context: many offices, documents and deadlines, in a new language
- Portrait: Fatima, mother of three, Munich
- Journey tracker pre-configured by country and family
- Document wallet, appointment manager, language practice
- Caseworker dashboard for scale
- Trust: not a surveillance tool; metrics

**Model answer.** A family arriving in Germany faces the asylum application, residence registration, the job center, childcare, the integration course and school enrollment. Each has its own office, documents and deadlines, and one missed deadline can cost months. Caseworkers are overloaded; I'll assume one for about 200 applicants.

Picture Fatima, 34, a Syrian mother of three, in Munich for three months. She speaks Arabic and some English. Her asylum interview is in six weeks. She needs help that is proactive, in her language and simple, because stress leaves little bandwidth.

I'd build a resettlement companion with five parts.

- A journey tracker: 12 to 15 milestones from arrival to integration. Each one explains what it is, the documents, the deadline, the place and the steps, in her language and as audio. It is pre-configured by country and family profile, so a family with children sees school enrollment and a single adult sees job-seeking.
- A document wallet: encrypted photos of the passport, residence permit and official letters, a prompt showing which document an office wants, and expiry reminders.
- An appointment manager: directions, what to bring and what she may be asked, with reminders at 48 and 24 hours.
- Language practice: ten minutes a day through a free course partner, tied to the next step. "I would like to register my child for school."
- A caseworker dashboard: progress, overdue milestones, messaging and custom milestones. The app handles routine instruction and the caseworker handles exceptions.

Trust is the central design problem. The app must plainly not be a government surveillance tool. Onboarding states, in every language, "We do not share your data with immigration authorities. Your documents are visible only to you and your caseworker." A named NGO oversees data protection, and trusted NGOs are the distribution channel.

| Metric | Role |
|---|---|
| Milestones completed on time, against families without the app | Primary |
| Appointment no-shows, from an assumed 30% to under 10% | Secondary |
| Would recommend to other refugees | Trust |

**What a strong answer shows.**

- A pre-configured journey tracker, not a general information hub
- A document wallet, and a caseworker dashboard that scales scarce help
- Surveillance perception treated as the primary trust risk

**Where answers fail.**

- A multilingual chatbot or translation app with no proactive journey
- Ignoring how vulnerable the user is, especially on data sharing

**If they push.**

- *The government offers funding in exchange for anonymized completion data.* Only aggregate statistics that can't be tied to people, disclosed to users, with an NGO veto. Otherwise I'd decline. Trust is the product.

### Q24. Design a product that helps enterprise sales teams at B2B SaaS companies reduce their sales cycle from 6 months to 3 months.

*Product design · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you start from where the time actually goes, attack structural bottlenecks instead of writing emails faster, and set a credible target.

**Clarify first.**

- *Standalone tool or a CRM feature?* I'll assume a layer on the existing CRM that writes back to it.
- *What deal size?* I'll assume enterprise deals of $100K and up, with buying committees.

**Outline.**

- Where six months goes: admin, internal chasing, waiting
- Causes: late stakeholders, slow follow-up, security and procurement review
- Stakeholder mapping with gap detection
- Meeting follow-up within 90 minutes
- Security questionnaire automation
- Deal health score; metrics with a staged target

**Model answer.** Enterprise cycles run long less because deals are complicated than because information reaches the right people late. Assume a rep spends 30% of the week on admin, 20% chasing internal approvals and half selling. I want to automate the first and compress the second.

Three causes. Stakeholders appear late: legal, finance and security show up near the end and restart the evaluation. Follow-up is slow: each next step takes 7 to 10 days to appear. And procurement, security and legal review add 4 to 8 weeks the rep doesn't control.

| Bottleneck | Feature | Effect |
|---|---|---|
| Late stakeholders | Map contacts from CRM, email metadata and calendar against typical buying committees; flag gaps and draft role-specific introductions | Committee engaged early |
| Slow follow-up | After each call: a three-sentence summary, next steps with owners and dates, a follow-up draft within 90 minutes, and risk signals such as "budget freeze" | Days become hours |
| Security review | A knowledge base owned by the security team fills 80 to 90% of each questionnaire and routes the rest for review | Three weeks become three days |

The gap alert reads: "You've engaged four people. Deals this size usually involve a CISO and procurement, and you haven't reached either."

The questionnaire is the structural fix. The email drafting is convenience.

A deal health score from 0 to 100 combines stakeholder coverage, days since last contact, sentiment in recent threads and questionnaire status. Managers review deals by health, not size: a $500K deal at 40 gets coaching before a $200K deal at 90.

| Metric | Role |
|---|---|
| Cycle length against a control: under 4 months in year one, 3 months by year two | Primary |
| Questionnaire turnaround, from 21 days to 3; win rate | Secondary |
| Rep time saved, at least 4 hours a week | Guardrail |

Risks: some customers forbid email and calendar access, so there is a metadata-only mode. And over-automation, so every outbound email needs the rep's approval.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Root causes of cycle length named before features
- Security questionnaire automation identified as the structural fix
- A deal health score; cycle length as the primary metric

**Where answers fail.**

- An AI email writer that leaves the structural bottlenecks alone
- "A better CRM with more fields"

**If they push.**

- *Deals close faster but the win rate hasn't moved.* We removed waiting, not the reasons deals are lost. That still raises capacity per rep. For win rate I'd look at qualification.

### Q25. Design a product for Meta that helps teens have healthier relationships with social media.

*Product design · Stretch*

**What they are testing.** Whether you can design against your own engagement metrics: name mechanisms of harm, know what already ships, and defend a wellbeing metric.

**Clarify first.**

- *Which app and ages?* I'll assume Instagram, 13 to 17, as the default for teen accounts.
- *What does healthier mean?* I'll assume less comparison, less unintended use and better sleep, not simply less time.

**Outline.**

- Five mechanisms of harm
- Principles: transparency, autonomy where safe, friction at risky moments
- What Teen Accounts already do
- Additions: appearance-content cap, session breaks, stronger sleep mode, hidden like counts
- Parent and teen balance by age
- Wellbeing metric with an engagement floor

**Model answer.** Internal research reported by the Wall Street Journal in 2021 found that 32% of teen girls said that when they felt bad about their bodies, Instagram made them feel worse. Whether social media causes the wider decline in teen mental health is still debated, so I'll design against specific mechanisms.

I see five. Social comparison from idealized appearance content. Passive consumption: autoplay and endless scroll with no stopping point. Quantified approval: public like and follower counts. Fear of missing out, through Stories. And late-night use that costs sleep.

My principles: transparency over restriction, autonomy where it is safe, and small frictions at risky moments. Heavy frictions push teens elsewhere.

Instagram's Teen Accounts already default to private accounts, a 10 PM to 7 AM sleep mode, a 60-minute daily reminder, and a parent's permission for under-16s to loosen settings. I'd build on that with four changes.

| Mechanism | Change |
|---|---|
| Comparison | Cap appearance-focused content at 20% of a teen's feed and Reels, done in ranking; label heavily filtered images as edited |
| Passive scroll | After 30 minutes, a pause: "Take a break" or "Keep scrolling." A soft stop, plus a plain "My time" view in hours, with no grades |
| Sleep | In sleep mode, hold new Stories until morning and make switching it off a multi-step choice |
| Quantified approval | Hide like counts on everything teens see, by default; creators still see their own |

The appearance cap is invisible on purpose. The goal is less exposure, not awareness of restriction.

The age split matters. At 13 to 15, parents set the defaults. At 16 to 17, the teen sets them and parents can see the settings without overriding.

| Metric | Role |
|---|---|
| Self-reported wellbeing at 90 days, on a validated adolescent scale | Primary |
| Daily session length, target 20% lower; appearance-content exposure; sleep-mode overrides | Secondary |
| Teen daily actives falling more than 25% | Floor |

The floor isn't there to protect revenue. If teens leave for places with fewer protections, I've made them less safe.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Specific mechanisms of harm, with ranking changes as well as visible features
- Autonomy tiered by age between parent and teen
- Wellbeing as the primary metric, with the engagement floor explained

**Where answers fail.**

- Screen-time limits and parental controls that already exist
- A new teen-only network that ignores where teens are

**If they push.**

- *Teen daily actives fall 18% and investors are alarmed.* I'd show the wellbeing data, note the drop is inside the floor we set, and make the case on regulatory exposure and parents' trust.

# Part II — Product improvement

## 2. Product improvement questions

Improvement questions hand you a product that already exists and ask you to make it better. They test whether you can find where the experience breaks for a specific user, tie each fix to a cause, rank the fixes, and say how you would know they worked. The trap is a feature list with no diagnosis.

Each question has an outline to rehearse from and a model answer written to be spoken. Cover the answer, talk through the outline, then compare. Figures and product details are stated assumptions unless a source is named, and products change, so check what already ships before an interview. Four questions are marked Tailored and name the stories (S1 to S10) to draw on.

### Q26. How would you improve Google Maps' restaurant discovery experience?

*Product improvement · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you can break "discovery" into distinct user modes, name the real relevance gap, and rank fixes with a reason and a metric instead of listing features.

**Clarify first.**

- Which surface? I'll assume search results and the home screen on mobile.
- What signal says discovery is failing? I'll assume clicks rarely become visits.
- Which users? I'll assume locals deciding where to eat; pre-visit only.

**Outline.**

- Three discovery modes: browsing, specific search, social
- Where today's experience breaks
- Four improvements, ranked P0 to P3
- Success metrics and one guardrail
- Risks: bias against small businesses, wrong AI labels, privacy

**Model answer.**

People discover restaurants in three modes. Browsing: "what's good nearby?" Specific search: "sushi under $30." Social: "where do my friends eat?" My read is that Maps handles specific search well, is weaker at browsing, and does little for social.

Where does it break? These are assumptions I'd check. Results lean on aggregate rating and review volume, which favors established places over great local ones. Personalization is light, so a vegan and a steak lover see similar lists. The lead photo is often a blurry interior, not the food. Reviews sort by helpfulness, not by fit with my taste.

| Priority | Improvement | Why it ranks here |
|---|---|---|
| P0 | Taste-personalized ranking from searches, saves, ratings and opted-in visit history | Touches every search; fixes "these results aren't for me" |
| P1 | Photo quality classifier, plus a Dishes section naming the most-photographed dishes | Photos drive the decision; clear ML path |
| P2 | "Picks for you" on the home screen: 5 to 7 places, each with a reason and a dish photo | High long-term value, heavier model work |
| P3 | Opt-in friends layer: "3 contacts ate here" | Strong trust signal; needs mutual consent, used less often |

Google Maps introduced a "Your match" score and a "For you" tab in 2018, so I'd check what exists today and build on it.

Metrics. Primary: click-through from results to a listing, as a relevance proxy. Secondary: visit conversion, a click that became a visit, inferred from opted-in location history. Long term: a survey on whether Maps helps people find places they love. Guardrail: accuracy of AI dish labels, because wrong labels cost trust.

The risk to watch is fairness: personalization can bury a small, excellent restaurant.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Names personalization as the P0 fix and says why
- Gives the Dishes feature a clear implementation path
- Makes visit conversion, not clicks, the ultimate measure of success

**Where answers fail.**

- Minor UI changes (better filters, bigger photos) that never touch ranking or personalization
- More review prompts and generic metrics, missing the relevance problem

**If they push.**

- *"Personalized ranking demotes a highly rated restaurant owned by a small minority business. Bias?"* I'd audit exposure by business size, keep quality as a ranking floor, and reserve exploration slots for small and new places.
- *"Owners say the most-photographed dish isn't their specialty."* Let verified owners pin a signature dish, and label the section "most photographed."

### Q27. How would you improve the Instagram DM (Direct Message) experience?

*Product improvement · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you separate the jobs a messaging product does and fix structural problems (what surfaces, what can be found) before cosmetic ones.

**Clarify first.**

- What prompted this? I'll assume the goal is more conversations happening in DMs.
- Which users: teens, creators with fans, or adults chatting? I'll assume friends messaging friends, and flag creators.
- One-to-one or groups; is media in scope? I'll assume both, with media.

**Outline.**

- Three use cases that one inbox tries to serve
- Four friction points
- Four improvements, ranked
- Metrics, with a feed guardrail
- Risk: creators whose fan messages get demoted

**Model answer.**

DMs do three jobs: close friends catching up, creators talking with fans, and groups making plans. One inbox design serves all three and is tuned for none.

The friction I'd expect, to confirm with research. The inbox sorts by recency, not importance, so a close friend's message sits under low-value chats. Group chats run as one stream, so the plan gets lost under the memes. Finding a photo or link from three months ago means endless scrolling. And view-once media leaves people anxious about screenshots.

| Priority | Improvement | Why it ranks here |
|---|---|---|
| P0 | Priority inbox: rank by recency, how often we talk, and live signals such as a story view; strangers' requests stay separate | Affects every DM user; fixes the buried-friend problem |
| P1 | "Shared" tab in every chat: photos, videos, links and posts, searchable by date | Very frequent need, simple build |
| P2 | Threads in groups larger than three: swipe a message to branch a topic | Big win for active groups, harder build |
| P3 | Transcript under voice messages: a 3-minute note reads in 20 seconds | Convenience and accessibility; narrower use |

The inbox follows the principle behind Gmail's Priority Inbox. Before building, I'd check what Instagram already ships in each area and close gaps instead of rebuilding.

Metrics. Primary: daily DM sessions per active user. Secondary: reply rate on received DMs, since a low rate means messages aren't being found, plus session length. Guardrail: the DM-to-feed navigation ratio, because DMs shouldn't pull time from feed, the main revenue surface.

The risk is creators: an inbox tuned for friends demotes fan messages.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Three distinct use cases, not one "messaging user"
- A priority inbox with named ranking signals, plus shared media as a high-frequency fix
- Reply rate as the key engagement metric

**Where answers fail.**

- Dark mode or more emoji reactions: cosmetic, no structural fix
- "Merge DMs with WhatsApp": ignores Instagram's own user context

**If they push.**

- *"A creator with 1M followers says the priority inbox broke their DMs."* The ranking assumed two-way friendships. I'd give creator accounts a separate fan inbox with filters and bulk replies.
- *"Threads cut daily group messages by 15%. Failure?"* Not necessarily: fewer "what did we decide?" messages is the point. I'd judge by active groups and their retention, not message count.

### Q28. How would you improve Paytm's UPI payment experience to increase first-time user activation?

*Product improvement · Standard · Tailored*

**What they are testing.** Whether you treat activation as a funnel, find the stage with the biggest drop, and fix its cause. Stories to draw on: S5 (RuPay credit card launch on UPI).

**Clarify first.**

- What counts as activated? I'll assume a first successful real payment.
- Where is the biggest drop, and for whom? I'll assume PIN creation among first-time UPI users, and verify.

**Outline.**

- Three funnel stages: link bank, create PIN, first payment
- Cause and fix per stage; PIN creation is the biggest drop
- A Rs 1 test payment to trigger first use
- Seven-day habit loop
- Stage-by-stage metrics and a guardrail

**Model answer.**

UPI handles more than 10 billion transactions a month, yet the hard part for any UPI app is getting a new user to a first payment. My Paytm work was on the merchant platform, so I'll treat consumer funnel numbers as assumptions and use the method from RuPay credit card activation (S5): map the funnel, find the biggest drops, fix those first.

| Stage | Why users drop | Fix |
|---|---|---|
| Link bank account | Can't find their bank under the name they know | Type-ahead search mapping everyday names to registered ones; test pre-suggesting the likely bank |
| Create UPI PIN | Needs debit card digits, expiry and a bank OTP; card not at hand; ATM PIN and UPI PIN get confused | Animated card showing where the digits are; Aadhaar OTP path where the bank supports it; save progress and nudge |
| First payment | No immediate need; fear of paying the wrong person | "Send Rs 1 to a friend" test, refunded within 24 hours |

PIN creation is my P0: I expect it to be the single biggest drop.

The Rs 1 test matters because the user feels the product work: the sound, the green check, the SMS. Then a seven-day habit loop. Day 1: the test payment. Days 2 to 4: a contextual prompt such as a mobile recharge. Days 5 to 7: cashback on the second real payment. My working assumption, to verify, is that users with three or more payments in week one retain far better at day 30.

Metrics. Primary: completion from install to first real payment, read stage by stage. Then PIN-setup completion on its own, and D7 retention: another payment in days 2 to 7. Guardrail: bank-linking error rate, because a wrong suggestion is worse than none.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Names PIN creation as the biggest drop and fixes its causes: card guide, Aadhaar OTP alternative
- Designs the Rs 1 test payment, with refund, as the activation trigger
- Measures drop-off stage by stage

**Where answers fail.**

- "Simplify the UI" or "add more banks" with no cause named per stage
- Gamification or marketing before the funnel is diagnosed

**If they push.**

- *"The Rs 1 test has a 15% fraud rate: people open several accounts to collect refunds."* One test per device, SIM and bank account, and flag refund rings. If abuse persists, drop the refund and keep the test.
- *"30% choose Aadhaar OTP, but their 30-day retention is 20% lower."* Probably selection: people without a debit card at hand may be lighter bank users. I'd compare similar segments before blaming the path.

### Q29. How would you improve YouTube's recommendation algorithm to reduce user regret?

*Product improvement · Stretch*

**What they are testing.** Whether you can define a fuzzy outcome so it can be measured, change the ranking objective and not only the UI, and face the watch-time trade-off.

**Clarify first.**

- How is regret measured today? I'll assume surveys exist and I can add signals.
- Which surfaces, and what constraint? I'll assume home feed and Up Next, with watch time allowed to fall at most 8%.

**Outline.**

- Define regret operationally: four signals
- Three mechanisms that produce it
- Fix 1: regret in the ranking objective
- Fix 2: session intent and stopping points
- Fix 3: diversity in Up Next
- Trade-off with watch time; A/B measurement

**Model answer.**

I'll define regret first. It isn't "I disliked this video." It is "I wish the platform hadn't led me here." I'd read it from four signals: a post-session survey answer such as "I watched more than I intended," a longer-than-usual gap before the next session, explicit feedback such as "not interested," and spikes in app deletion.

Three mechanisms produce it. Escalation: a system tuned for watch time learns that each next video should be a little more emotionally charged, so a session that starts with cooking drifts toward outrage. Each step is locally optimal; the path is not. Autoplay: no natural stopping point. Narrowing: the system keeps drilling into one category.

| Improvement | What changes | Mechanism |
|---|---|---|
| Regret-penalized ranking | Add sampled one-question session surveys, time beyond a stated session goal, and spiral detection to the objective | Escalation |
| Session intent | Optional prompt: "Just browsing," "Learning," "30 minutes"; at the limit, pause autoplay and ask | No stopping point |
| Diversity injection | After three videos in one narrow category, slot in another category the user has enjoyed | Narrowing |

The first matters most: the target moves from watch time to satisfied watch time. YouTube has said publicly that it already uses satisfaction surveys alongside watch time, so this extends an existing direction.

The trade-off is real: watch time will dip. My case rests on long-term retention, since people who regret sessions limit or delete the app, and on regulation: the EU's Digital Services Act already requires very large platforms to assess risks from their recommender systems.

I'd A/B test with the regret survey score as primary, then 30-day retention and category diversity per session. Total watch time is the guardrail: say no more than an 8% drop.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- An operational definition of regret with specific signals
- Regret as a ranking signal, not only a reminder in the UI
- The business trade-off argued with retention and regulation

**Where answers fail.**

- A "take a break" reminder alone: treats an algorithm problem as a UI problem
- Removing recommendations entirely, or ignoring the watch-time cost

**If they push.**

- *"Political news is the top regret driver, and de-prioritizing it hurts news partners."* Penalize regretted sessions, not the category: news people don't regret ranks above outrage clips.
- *"Diversity injection cuts regret 12% but costs niche creators revenue."* Inject only on detected spirals, not healthy deep dives, and add creator revenue by tier as a guardrail.

### Q30. How would you improve Uber's driver retention in a market where Lyft offers competitive pay?

*Product improvement · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you segment the supply side, find churn causes beyond pay, and price each fix against the value of a retained driver.

**Clarify first.**

- Which drivers are leaving? I'll assume experienced, full-time drivers in markets where Lyft is poaching.
- Is pay a lever? I'll assume a narrow one: cost per ride can't rise much.

**Outline.**

- Two buckets of levers: economic and non-economic
- Churn causes by tenure
- Three non-pay improvements
- A narrow pay lever, with its math
- Metrics and a cost guardrail

**Model answer.**

Replacing a driver is expensive: assume a few hundred dollars to acquire, screen and onboard each one. The levers fall into two buckets: economic (pay, bonuses, insurance) and non-economic (app, support, predictability).

Churn differs by tenure. New drivers, in their first three months, leave when real earnings fall short of expectations. Experienced drivers leave for a specific financial edge elsewhere, or because irritations pile up: slow support, bugs, acceptance pressure. I'll design for experienced drivers, who have the highest lifetime value and cost the most to replace.

| Improvement | What it is | Why it retains |
|---|---|---|
| Earnings forecast | "Tomorrow 4 to 8 PM near you: 12 to 15 rides, about $140 to $165," from demand history, events and weather | Planning power; less reason to check the rival app |
| Priority support | 500+ lifetime trips earns a 4-hour response SLA, and a proactive note when a suspect rating is excluded | Tenure is respected |
| Financial wellness | Free instant cashout at 200+ trips a month, discounted tax filing from earnings data, group-rate insurance | Smooths erratic income, the root of multi-apping |

If pay is available, I'd add a loyalty rate: $0.05 to $0.10 more per mile for drivers with 1,000+ trips and a 4.8+ rating. At 100 trips a week and 5 miles a trip that is 500 miles, so $25 to $50 a week. It's cheap, and it signals that experience is rewarded.

Metrics. Primary: six-month retention by tenure cohort. Secondary: driver NPS and multi-apping rate, the share of online hours when the driver also works another app, as a loyalty proxy. Guardrail: cost per retained driver must stay below the lifetime value saved.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Segments drivers by tenure before proposing anything
- Offers earnings predictability as a non-pay lever, with a support tier and tax help for experienced drivers
- Uses multi-apping rate as a loyalty proxy

**Where answers fail.**

- A loyalty points program that ignores the structural causes of churn
- "Pay everyone more" with no segmentation

**If they push.**

- *"New drivers see the loyalty rate and demand the same."* Publish it as a ladder with visible milestones, so it reads as a path every driver is on.
- *"The forecast is only 25% accurate during surges, and drivers are angry."* Show ranges, suppress forecasts when volatility is high, and make forecast error a launch gate. A wrong promise is worse than none.

### Q31. How would you improve the Microsoft Teams call experience for a hybrid team?

*Product improvement · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you see the structural asymmetry between people in the room and people remote, and measure equal participation, not just call quality.

**Clarify first.**

- Whose experience, and what is the top complaint? I'll assume both sides, weighted to remote people who feel excluded.
- What hardware, and is AI in scope? I'll assume a standard room camera, mic and display for 6 to 12 people; AI is in scope.

**Outline.**

- The asymmetry: a two-tier meeting
- Friction on each side
- Four fixes: camera, whiteboard, turn-taking, echo
- Primary metric: remote share of talk time
- Guardrail: setup time

**Model answer.**

Hybrid meetings have a built-in asymmetry. People in the room get spatial audio, a shared whiteboard and body language. Remote people get one wide camera angle, miss side conversations and can't tell when to speak. The result is a two-tier meeting.

Friction on the remote side: can't see the whiteboard, can't hear quiet voices, can't find a gap to talk. In the room: it's awkward to address a screen, open laptops cause echo, and nobody notices a remote hand.

| Improvement | What it does | Gap it closes |
|---|---|---|
| Speaker-tracking camera | Software framing zooms to whoever is speaking | Remote people can read faces |
| Whiteboard capture | The room camera corrects perspective, sharpens text and lines, and feeds a digital whiteboard that remote people can annotate | The whiteboard |
| Raised-hand parity | A chime and an on-screen "[Name] wants to speak," plus a facilitator panel showing who hasn't spoken | Turn-taking |
| Laptop suppression | Detect several devices in one room by audio fingerprint; mute all but the room mic | Echo |

Teams Rooms already offers intelligent framing and content cameras on supported hardware, so I'd confirm what ships and focus on rooms without that equipment. Tracking and whiteboard capture come first because they close the largest gaps.

Metrics. Primary: remote share of talk time, words spoken by remote participants over total words, compared with their share of attendees. Secondary: satisfaction by role, targeting under a 10% gap between room and remote, and whiteboard capture accuracy. Guardrail: setup time. If these features add two minutes of fiddling, rooms will switch them off.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Frames the asymmetry as the structural problem
- Picks speaker tracking and whiteboard capture as the highest-impact fixes, with laptop suppression as a technical fix
- Measures participation equity, not only audio and video quality

**Where answers fail.**

- Better audio or more camera angles, with no answer to unequal participation
- "Make every meeting fully remote": avoids the problem

**If they push.**

- *"The speaking-queue AI interrupts a senior executive to prompt a remote participant."* It should never interrupt. Show the prompt only to the facilitator, at natural pauses, with per-meeting sensitivity controls and an off switch.
- *"Remote engagement improves but in-room satisfaction drops. Why?"* The room now carries overhead it didn't before: chimes, prompts, slower pacing. I'd find which feature correlates, then make cues quieter and visual.

### Q32. How would you improve the merchant experience on a B2B payments platform to reduce payment reconciliation time from 3 days to same-day?

*Product improvement · Stretch · Tailored*

**What they are testing.** Whether you can map an operational workflow, find where the time goes, and redesign the data flow instead of speeding up a report. Stories to draw on: S4 (interchange cost reconciliation), S1 (BINARY invoicing automation).

**Clarify first.**

- Which merchants and payment methods? I'll assume mid-market, invoice-based businesses paid by card and bank transfer.
- Is the delay in our settlement or the merchant's ERP matching? I'll assume both, across cloud and older desktop ERPs.

**Outline.**

- Today's three-day cycle
- Three bottlenecks: report latency, ID mismatch, exceptions
- Real-time event feed
- Auto-matching with confidence scores
- Exceptions queue
- Settlement speed tiers; ERP integrations
- Metrics and a false-match guardrail

**Model answer.**

I've worked on reconciliation from the other side: at Paytm I led product design for a rule-based system that computed expected interchange cost and reconciled it against bank reports by transaction ID (S4). The lesson carries over: match on a shared key, automate the clear cases, send people only the exceptions.

The three days aren't a technical necessity. Day 1, the payment batch processes. Day 2, the gateway sends a settlement report as a CSV or PDF. Day 3, finance matches payments to invoices by hand.

| Bottleneck | Cause | Fix |
|---|---|---|
| Report latency | Settlements batch; reports arrive end of day, T+1 | A webhook per payment: ID, amount, currency, customer reference, method, status |
| Format mismatch | We use transaction IDs; the ERP uses invoice numbers | Auto-match on amount, customer and partial reference against open invoices synced from the ERP |
| Exceptions | Assume 3% to 8% mismatch, 30 to 60 minutes each | A queue showing candidate matches and a suggested action |

Clear matches reconcile automatically. Fuzzy ones go to a person with a confidence score: "87% confident this is invoice 2847. Approve or reroute."

For ERPs, I'd build certified integrations for the eight our merchants use most, and a small bridge agent that turns the feed into an import file for older desktop systems.

On the money itself, settlement speed becomes a setting: standard T+1, same-day for a fee, say 0.15% more, and instant where the rails support it. That is also a revenue line.

Targets. Primary: time from payment receipt to reconciled in the ERP, under eight business hours. Auto-match rate of 88% or better. Exception resolution under 15 minutes. Guardrail: false matches under 0.1%, because a wrong auto-match is worse than a slow one.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Identifies the three bottlenecks and their technical causes
- Makes real-time webhooks the architectural fix, with confidence-scored auto-matching
- Sees settlement speed tiers as a monetization lever

**Where answers fail.**

- "Send the settlement report faster": same architecture, same manual matching
- A reconciliation status dashboard that leaves the workflow unchanged

**If they push.**

- *"A CFO reports that auto-match wrongly reconciled 12 transactions: a $45,000 discrepancy."* Reverse them with a full audit trail, find the rule that produced them, raise the confidence threshold for that pattern, and report back with the fix.
- *"A bank partner says real-time settlement exposes them to intraday liquidity risk."* Separate information from money. Real-time events need no change to fund flows; faster settlement stays opt-in, priced, and capped per merchant.

### Q33. How would you improve Airbnb's host onboarding experience to increase the number of new listings that go live within 7 days of signup?

*Product improvement · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you find the step that blocks activation, and tell effort problems (too many fields) from confidence problems (photos, price, readiness).

**Clarify first.**

- Where do hosts drop, and which hosts? I'll assume new individual hosts, dropping most at photos and pricing.
- What exists today? I'll assume a long listing form and basic price tips.

**Outline.**

- Five stages from signup to first booking
- Friction per stage; photos and pricing are worst
- Seven-step wizard with progressive disclosure
- Price suggestion with comparables
- Photo coaching
- Readiness checklist and guest preview
- Metrics, with a listing-quality guardrail

**Model answer.**

Supply growth here is an onboarding problem. My assumption is that a host who isn't live in 7 days rarely goes live by day 30, so the first week is the window.

The journey has five stages: account, listing creation, pricing, calendar, first booking. Account creation loses almost nobody. Listing creation is the first big drop: assume 25 to 30 fields, with photos the biggest blocker, because most hosts don't know how to take a listing-quality photo. Pricing creates dread: "what should I charge?" Calendar setup overwhelms frequent travelers. And some hosts finish but stay unlisted because they don't feel ready.

| Improvement | What it does |
|---|---|
| Seven-step wizard | Basics, five must-have amenities, house rules, photos, price, availability, review. Photos can come later, with a nudge within 72 hours |
| Price suggestion | "Similar two-bedrooms near you charge $89 to $142; we suggest $115." The host can override |
| Photo coaching | A camera overlay shows ideal framing and lighting tips, and rates each shot |
| Readiness checklist | Five items before going live, plus "preview as a guest" |

The checklist covers a verified photo, price, check-in instructions, response setting and a saved guest message. The preview removes the fear of the unknown that keeps listings in draft.

The wizard and price suggestion come first: they attack the two largest drops with the least build.

Metrics. Primary: 7-day activation rate. Secondary: completion by wizard step, photo upload within 7 days, and time to first booking. Guardrail: listing quality score. If the wizard produces weaker listings than the old form, I've traded quality for speed.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Names photos and pricing anxiety as the two highest-friction points
- Uses progressive disclosure and photo coaching instead of a shorter form
- Adds the readiness checklist as a confidence anchor

**Where answers fail.**

- "Simplify the form" without saying which fields hurt most
- "Hire professional photographers for every new host": doesn't scale

**If they push.**

- *"Activation rises 20% but first-booking cancellations rise 8%."* We activated hosts who weren't ready. I'd require check-in instructions and an accurate calendar before the first booking, and track cancellations by onboarding path.
- *"Photo coaching has a 40% error rate in dark rooms."* Detect low light and switch from scoring to simple tips: open curtains, turn on lights, shoot in daytime. Never block publishing on the score.

### Q34. How would you improve LinkedIn's job application experience for job seekers?

*Product improvement · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you see the whole journey, including what happens after "apply," and balance the job seeker's gain against the recruiter's cost.

**Clarify first.**

- Main complaint: finding relevant jobs, applying, or silence afterward? I'll assume all three.
- Easy Apply or external applications? I'll assume Easy Apply, mid-career seekers.
- Which business metric? I'll assume application-to-interview rate.

**Outline.**

- Three break points: discovery, quality, limbo
- Explain the match and score the fit
- AI cover-letter draft the candidate edits
- Status tracker and time-to-hear-back
- Salary insight
- Metrics, with a recruiter guardrail

**Model answer.**

The experience breaks at three points: finding relevant roles, submitting an application that represents me well, and the silence afterward.

| Break point | Problem | Improvement |
|---|---|---|
| Discovery | Matches on title and skills throw false positives | "Why this is a match" on each card, plus a fit score: above 80% shows "Strong match," below 50% shows "Reach" |
| Application quality | Blank-form anxiety; assume 15 to 20 minutes per application | AI drafts a three-paragraph cover letter from the job post, my profile and the company page; I edit before sending |
| After applying | Assume most applications, say 70%, never get a reply | Status tracker with recruiter consent: "Received," "Reviewed," "Not selected"; plus typical time to hear back from that company's history |

The fit score sorts the feed by what I can act on, not just what is relevant. The draft should cut time per application to under five minutes, and it must be editable, never auto-sent.

One addition: when salary isn't listed, show the range that people with a similar background expect for similar roles there, so I can state a number with confidence.

I'd start with the status tracker and the match explanation. The draft is the riskiest: it helps me and can hurt recruiters.

Metrics. Primary: application-to-interview conversion. Secondary: Easy Apply completion, draft usage, and satisfaction with status updates. Guardrail: recruiter satisfaction. If drafts flood recruiters with letters that sound alike, the feature backfires.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Three distinct friction points, each with its own fix
- A match that is explained, and a cover-letter draft that is editable, never auto-sent
- A status tracker for the silence, with application-to-interview rate as the primary metric

**Where answers fail.**

- "Better search filters": ignores quality and communication
- Replacing applications with a chat-based interview

**If they push.**

- *"Drafts lift Easy Apply completions 30%, but recruiters say the letters feel generic."* Ask for one or two candidate-written specifics before generating, vary the structure, and show recruiters the fit evidence first.
- *"50 large employers support status tracking; 90% of small employers don't."* Fall back to inferred signals: application viewed, job closed, typical response time. Label what is confirmed and what is estimated.

### Q35. How would you improve Apple Maps to close the gap with Google Maps in urban navigation accuracy?

*Product improvement · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you recognize a data-quality problem, not an algorithm problem, and design data acquisition that respects the company's constraints: here, privacy.

**Clarify first.**

- Which accuracy gaps: turn-by-turn, listings, transit, live traffic? I'll assume listings, transit and traffic.
- US or global; which travel modes? I'll assume US cities, driving and transit.
- Constraints on collecting data? I'll assume Apple's privacy stance is fixed.

**Outline.**

- The gaps are data problems, in three areas
- Listings: owner verification and imagery signals
- Transit: self-serve GTFS onboarding, real-time feeds
- Traffic: opt-in probe data, licensed feed as backup
- Crowdsourced edits weighted by track record
- Audit-based metrics

**Model answer.**

Apple Maps had a rough launch in 2012 and trust took a hit. I'd assume the remaining gaps sit in three areas: how fresh business listings are, transit coverage outside the biggest cities, and real-time traffic. All three are data problems, not algorithm problems.

| Gap | Why Google leads | Apple's play |
|---|---|---|
| Listing freshness | Huge search-query signal on what is open | Owner verification through Apple Business Connect; Look Around imagery to spot closures: changed signs, dark windows, "For Lease" |
| Transit coverage | Broad feed coverage from agencies | Self-serve GTFS import with automated validation, live within 72 hours; partner for GTFS-Realtime feeds, which many agencies publish free |
| Live traffic | Dense location signal from a large Android base | Opt-in CarPlay program sharing anonymized speed data; a licensed third-party feed where density is thin |

On top of this, crowdsourcing. A "Local Expert" program rewards user edits, for example with store credit, and weights them by track record: edits from people with a history of accuracy publish fast, and edits from new users get reviewed.

I'd start with listings, since a closed restaurant is the error users remember. Transit is next. Traffic takes longest because density has to build.

Metrics. Primary: listing accuracy from a quarterly mystery-shopping audit of a sample, compared with Google Maps. Secondary: completion rate for transit directions, and the median gap between predicted ETA and actual travel time. Long term: Maps sessions per iPhone user.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Three data-quality gaps, each with its own data strategy
- A contributor program that fast-tracks edits by accuracy history
- An audit-based accuracy metric

**Where answers fail.**

- "Improve street imagery coverage" without addressing the data root cause
- "Buy Waze" or "partner with Google": ignores the privacy-first constraint

**If they push.**

- *"The CarPlay program gets 15% opt-in. Enough for mid-size US cities?"* Probably on highways and main roads at peak, not on side streets or off-peak. I'd measure coverage by road segment and hour, and blend in the licensed feed where it is thin.
- *"How would you improve accuracy in India, where addresses are informal?"* Navigate to landmarks and pins instead of street numbers, accept local-language place names, and lean on user and delivery-partner corrections.

### Q36. How would you improve the credit underwriting experience for small merchants applying for working capital loans on a fintech platform?

*Product improvement · Stretch · Tailored*

**What they are testing.** Whether you use the platform's own data as the underwriting asset and still protect the default rate. Stories to draw on: S7 (risk profiling and automated limits from merchant data).

**Clarify first.**

- Where do merchants drop? I'll assume document upload.
- What data do we hold? I'll assume six months or more of payment history for most applicants.
- More approvals, fewer defaults, or both? I'll assume more approvals with defaults flat.

**Outline.**

- Two root causes: thin credit data, no formal documents
- Instant decisions on platform data
- Account Aggregator path for thin-history merchants
- Pre-approved offers; tiered amounts
- Repayment as a share of daily settlement
- Metrics, with default rate as the guardrail

**Model answer.**

These applications fail for two reasons. We can't judge creditworthiness quickly because traditional credit data is thin, and the merchant can't produce documents quickly because they don't keep formal books. The platform's own data solves both. I've used the idea before: at Paytm I led product design for risk-based profiling that scored merchants on vintage, transactions per month, unique payers and line of business to set their GMV limits (S7).

| Improvement | How it works |
|---|---|
| Instant decision | Merchants with 6+ months and 100+ monthly transactions need zero documents; a decision in under 60 seconds from volume, repeat-customer rate, seasonality and payment failures |
| Connected statement | Below that bar, the merchant consents to share bank statements through India's RBI-regulated Account Aggregator framework instead of uploading PDFs |
| Pre-approved offers | The dashboard shows "You're pre-approved for Rs 250,000 at 1.5% a month"; accept, and funds arrive within 2 hours |
| Tiered amounts | "You qualify for Rs 150,000 to Rs 300,000"; the merchant picks what fits their cash flow |
| Revenue-share repayment | Say 5% of each day's settlement, instead of a fixed monthly installment |

Tiered amounts raise acceptance, because a merchant who would refuse the full sum takes a smaller one. Revenue-share repayment moves with sales, which removes the cash-flow mismatch behind most small-business delinquency. Merchant cash advance products such as Shopify Capital work this way.

Targets. Approval rate from, say, 40% to 60%. Application to disbursal under 4 hours for pre-approved merchants. Offer acceptance rate. The guardrail is default rate: it must not rise under the alternative-data model, so I'd validate that model on a small cohort before scaling.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Uses platform data and the Account Aggregator framework in place of document uploads
- Designs pre-approved offers, and revenue-share repayment as a structural cut to default risk
- Treats the alternative-data model's accuracy as the thing to validate

**Where answers fail.**

- A nicer document-upload screen in front of the same credit model
- Ignoring the platform's data advantage

**If they push.**

- *"The alternative-data model has a 12% default rate, against 7% for the traditional one."* Compare like-for-like segments, and profit, not default rate alone: the new model approves people the old one rejected. If the economics are negative after pricing for risk, tighten the cutoff before scaling.
- *"A merchant defaults and says revenue-share repayment left them unable to pay suppliers."* Cap the daily share, offer a pause in a bad month, and show the expected daily deduction before acceptance.

### Q37. How would you improve Slack's search experience to make it more useful for enterprise teams?

*Product improvement · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you see that the product stores information one way (channel, time) and people retrieve it another (topic, decision), and whether you handle the risk of AI answers.

**Clarify first.**

- Who is failing: individuals finding a message, or teams finding past decisions? I'll assume teams finding decisions, across messages, files and links.
- Is AI in scope? I'll assume yes, for general knowledge workers.

**Outline.**

- The mismatch: stored by channel and time, retrieved by topic and decision
- Four failure modes
- AI answers with citations
- Thread-aware results
- One search across messages, files and links
- Semantic filters: decision, action item, open question
- Metrics and a hallucination guardrail

**Model answer.**

Slack organizes conversation by channel and time. People look for knowledge by topic and decision. Those axes don't line up, and that is why search frustrates enterprise users.

Four failure modes. Keyword dependence: I need the exact words someone used. Thread collapse: a result is one message with no context. Files and messages feel like separate searches. And no synthesis: "what did we decide about X?" means reading 50 messages.

| Improvement | What the user gets | Fixes |
|---|---|---|
| AI answers with citations | Ask "what was the final decision on the payment integration?" and get a direct answer linked to the source messages | Keyword dependence, no synthesis |
| Thread-aware results | Each hit shows the message before and after; one click opens the thread | Thread collapse |
| Unified search | One query returns messages, file snippets and shared links, ranked by relevance, recency and my channel membership | Split search |
| Semantic filters | "Decision," "Action item," "Open question": a question nobody answered | Retros, onboarding |

Slack's AI features already include search answers and summaries, so the first item extends that scope toward decisions. I'd lead with it and thread-aware results: together they answer the most common question, what did we decide and why.

Metrics. Primary: click-through on results. Secondary: search abandonment, time-to-knowledge, and answer accuracy from a human audit of samples. Guardrail: factual error rate in AI answers under 0.5%. An answer that sounds right and is wrong is worse than no answer, so every answer carries citations.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Names keyword dependence and thread context collapse as the core problems
- Proposes synthesis with citations, not just semantic matching, plus enterprise filters
- Audits AI answer accuracy with human review

**Where answers fail.**

- Better keyword filters alone: misses synthesis
- A new search UI with more dropdowns

**If they push.**

- *"The AI returns a wrong answer about a decision; an engineer acts on it and causes an incident."* Cite sources on every answer, flag old or conflicting sources, and say "no clear decision found" instead of guessing.
- *"A Fortune 500 privacy team objects to messages going to an external LLM."* Offer processing inside the customer's boundary with no training on their data, admin controls by workspace and channel, and audit logs.

### Q38. How would you improve the onboarding experience for new users on a music streaming app to reduce 7-day churn?

*Product improvement · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you understand cold start: value depends on personalization the product hasn't earned yet, so onboarding must shorten the time to first delight.

**Clarify first.**

- What does onboarding look like today? I'll assume a short genre picker.
- What drives churn by day 7? I'll assume weak early recommendations.
- Free tier, trial-to-paid, or both? I'll assume both, measured on D7 retention.

**Outline.**

- Cold start: value needs personalization, which takes time
- Why a genre picker is too coarse
- Artist-based taste setup
- First playlist with an 8/8/4 mix
- Quick-vote swipes in session one
- Timed introductions on day 2 and day 7
- Metrics

**Model answer.**

A music app's value depends on personalization, and personalization takes listening history to build. New users don't wait weeks for it. So the goal is to shorten time to first delight.

I'll assume onboarding today is a genre picker. That is too coarse. "Pop" could mean 1970s ABBA or this year's chart, and someone can love one and dislike the other. Early playlists come out mediocre, and users leave before they improve.

| Step | What changes | Why |
|---|---|---|
| Taste setup | "Name five artists you love" replaces genres | Artist-level input places the user in the right taste cluster on day 1 |
| First playlist | 20 songs: 8 by the named artists, 8 by similar artists, 4 wildcards rated highly in that cluster | Familiar first, discovery gradually |
| Quick vote | After 3 or 4 songs, show five covers; swipe right or left | A few explicit votes can teach faster than days of passive listening |
| Timed introductions | Day 2: "your personalized weekly playlist is ready." Day 7: a first-week recap | Builds a weekly habit and a milestone |

Two details. Frame the swipes as "help us find your sound," not "rate these songs." And the weekly playlist, modeled on Spotify's Discover Weekly, arrives on day 2 because by then there is a day of real listening behind it.

Metrics. Primary: D7 retention. Secondary: median time to three likes, and trial-to-paid conversion. Guardrail: onboarding completion, with more than 80% finishing the artist step.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Replaces genre with artist-level input and says why
- Designs the 8/8/4 first playlist and the quick-vote swipes
- Introduces the weekly playlist at the right behavioral moment

**Where answers fail.**

- A longer genre quiz: misses the artist-versus-genre insight
- A longer tutorial or more features, with no fix for cold start

**If they push.**

- *"Artist-based onboarding lifts D7 retention 8%, but first-week streams concentrate on a few popular artists."* A cost to watch, not a reason to stop. I'd tune the similar-artist and wildcard slots and track the stream share of smaller artists.
- *"A user names five artists, but the whole family shares the account."* Detect it from clashing listening patterns by time and device, then prompt for profiles or a family plan.

### Q39. How would you improve Amazon's product return experience to reduce customer effort while maintaining cost efficiency?

*Product improvement · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you can find changes that cut customer effort and cost together, and guard against abuse.

**Clarify first.**

- Consumer retail or Amazon Business, and which part of the return? I'll assume consumer, end to end, all categories.
- What is the cost constraint? I'll assume cost per return must not rise.

**Outline.**

- A rare case: a better experience can also cost less
- Five customer friction points
- Conversational start, with exchange built in
- Label-free, box-free drop-off as the default
- Instant refunds for trusted customers
- Prevent apparel returns upstream with fit signals
- Metrics and an abuse guardrail

**Model answer.**

Returns are costly; assume $25 to $30 each to process. They also take effort from the customer. That makes this a rare case where a better experience can lower cost.

Five friction points: a reason dropdown that doesn't match the real reason; printing a label without a printer; finding packaging; waiting days for a refund after the item arrives; and exchanges that need a separate return and a new order.

| Improvement | What changes | Effect |
|---|---|---|
| Conversational start | "Tell us what happened." The flow reads the reason and routes: "Want a large instead? We'll ship it and collect the return together" | Exchange inside the return; better reason data |
| Drop-off by default | A QR code, no label, no box, at partner locations; print-at-home becomes secondary | Less effort; consolidated shipments cost less |
| Instant refund | Refund at initiation for Prime members with, say, 3+ years of history and no abuse pattern | Speed for the trusted |
| Fit signals for apparel | "Most people who returned this said it runs small: consider sizing up"; wider virtual try-on | Prevents the return |

Amazon already offers label-free drop-off and some instant refunds. The change is to make them the default path, and to widen instant refunds with an eligibility model.

Apparel deserves its own fix because its return rate is high, assume above 30%, and the cause is that customers can't try before they buy. Prevention beats processing.

Metrics. Primary: abandonment of the return flow. Secondary: processing cost per return, and refund satisfaction. Guardrail: abuse rate in the instant-refund cohort stays under 2%.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Combines the conversational flow with a same-time exchange
- Makes QR drop-off the default and gates instant refunds with an eligibility model
- Goes upstream: fit signals that prevent apparel returns

**Where answers fail.**

- Tidying the reason dropdown without redesigning the flow
- Extending the return window: treats a product problem as policy

**If they push.**

- *"Instant refunds trigger a 15% rise in return rate from a small cohort."* That is the eligibility model's job. Remove those accounts from instant refunds, add return rate as an eligibility signal, and keep the benefit for everyone else.
- *"A third-party seller says QR drop-off bypasses their return policy."* Offer drop-off only where the seller's policy allows the return, show their terms in the flow, and share the reason data with them.

### Q40. How would you improve the GitHub pull request review experience to speed up code review cycles at enterprise teams?

*Product improvement · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you can find the wait states in a developer workflow and tell product fixes from policy.

**Clarify first.**

- What is the cycle time today, and the target? I'll assume a 3 to 5 day median, and target 24 hours for small PRs.
- Which bottleneck, and is AI in scope? I'll assume reviewer availability, PR size and back-and-forth all matter; AI is in scope.

**Outline.**

- Where a PR waits: assignment, comprehension, round trips
- Smart reviewer suggestions
- AI summary with a risk score and routing
- Automate style feedback before human review
- Review queue and a daily review block
- Metrics; post-merge incidents as the guardrail

**Model answer.**

Assume enterprise teams see median review times of 3 to 5 days, with a long tail past two weeks. The delay is structural: reviewers are switching context from their own work, big PRs are overwhelming, and the handoff between author and reviewer is loose.

| Bottleneck | What I'd assume | Improvement |
|---|---|---|
| Assignment | PRs sit 4 to 8 hours with reviewers who are busy, in another time zone or unaware | Suggest 2 or 3 reviewers ranked by recent work on the files, current review load and past reviews in that area |
| PR complexity | 800+ lines across 20 files | Over 200 lines: an AI summary of key changes and concerns, plus a risk score from files changed, complexity, test coverage change and security-relevant files |
| Comment noise | Say 40% of round trips are style or formatting | Run the repo's linters on every PR and auto-fix, or post one automated comment, before a person looks |

High-risk PRs get tagged and routed to a security reviewer automatically. Copilot can already summarize a PR and review code, so the new part is the risk score and routing.

One coordination feature: a "review sprint." Team leads schedule a daily 30-minute block, and each engineer sees a queue of assigned PRs ranked by age, whether it blocks someone, and author context.

I'd ship linting first, since it removes the most round trips for the least effort, then assignment, then the risk score.

Metrics. Primary: median time from opened to merged, targeting 24 hours for PRs under 200 lines. Secondary: time to first review, share of comments about style (under 10%), and risk-flag accuracy. Guardrail: post-merge incident rate.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Names assignment, PR complexity and style noise as the three bottlenecks
- Adds risk scoring to the AI summary, and a team-level review sprint
- Guards speed with post-merge quality

**Where answers fail.**

- Reminder notifications for unreviewed PRs: surface-level
- Mandatory review SLAs: a policy change, not a product change

**If they push.**

- *"The risk score flags 30% of PRs as high risk, and security reviewers have alert fatigue."* Recalibrate against outcomes: which flagged PRs had real issues? Raise the bar so "high" is a small share, and add a medium tier that needs no security review.
- *"Summaries are wrong 20% of the time on complex refactors. Suppress or invest?"* Suppress them on PR types the model handles badly, say so in the UI, and improve the model offline. A wrong summary costs more trust than a missing one.

### Q41. How would you improve the notification experience for mobile apps to reduce user opt-out rates?

*Product improvement · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you separate a platform-level fix from a per-app fix, and design for the user's attention instead of the sender's volume.

**Clarify first.**

- Platform level (the OS) or one app? I'll assume the platform.
- What drives opt-outs? I'll assume promotional volume.
- Send side, presentation, or both? I'll assume both. The goal: fewer opt-outs without losing critical alerts.

**Outline.**

- Why opt-out matters
- Three causes: volume, timing, all-or-nothing permission
- Category-level permission
- Send-time optimization
- A health score that limits low-quality senders
- Digest for high-volume apps
- Metrics; never delay time-sensitive alerts

**Model answer.**

Opt-out is one of the most costly things a user can do to an app; I'd assume opted-out users churn far more often. Yet notifications are designed around the sender, who wants more sends, not the user, who wants value at the right moment.

Three causes. Volume: too many, mostly promotional. Timing: they arrive at the wrong moment. Control: the permission prompt is all-or-nothing, so someone who wants order updates but not promotions turns everything off. Android has per-category channels, but they sit deep in settings.

| Improvement | How it works | Cause |
|---|---|---|
| Category permission | The first prompt lists 3 to 5 categories, such as account alerts, security, offers and a weekly summary, each with its own toggle | Control |
| Send-time optimization | Learn from past opens and unlock patterns; hold non-urgent notifications for a receptive window; respect Do Not Disturb | Timing |
| Notification health score | Per app, from open, dismissal, post-open engagement and opt-out rates, visible in settings; low scorers lose promotional sends | Volume |
| Digest | Apps that send one user more than 5 a day get bundled into a summary at the user's chosen time | Volume |

The health score is the structural one: it gives every app a reason to send better notifications. iOS already has an opt-in scheduled summary; I'd make a digest the default for heavy senders.

Metrics. Primary: opt-out rate, targeting a 15% reduction from baseline. Secondary: open rate, session depth after an open, and the spread of health scores. Guardrail: missed-alert rate. Time-sensitive notifications must never be held by send-time optimization.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Names the all-or-nothing permission as the structural failure and replaces it with categories
- Treats send-time optimization as infrastructure, and adds a platform health score
- Uses a digest for high-volume apps

**Where answers fail.**

- "Add more notification settings inside the app": per-app, doesn't fix the system
- Cutting frequency 50% across the board, with no granular control

**If they push.**

- *"A healthcare app must send medication reminders to elderly users who have Do Not Disturb on."* That is a time-sensitive category the user grants explicitly. It bypasses send-time holds and, with consent, Do Not Disturb.
- *"The health score penalizes a news app whose breaking alerts get few taps, though users want them."* Score by category, with the right signal for each. For breaking news, low opt-out and low dismissal matter more than taps.

### Q42. How would you improve the Lyft driver app to reduce the number of missed ride requests?

*Product improvement · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you build a taxonomy of why something fails and match a fix to each cause, with safety as a constraint.

**Clarify first.**

- What is the missed rate, and the known reasons? I'll assume a 15-second window and a mix of causes.
- Alert, acceptance window, or post-miss flow? I'll assume all three.
- Which drivers and hardware? I'll assume full-time and part-time, with CarPlay and Android Auto in scope.

**Outline.**

- Double cost: driver income, rider wait
- Five reasons a request is missed
- Escalating alert with a longer window
- Accept from the lock screen
- Car display and voice accept
- Trip preview for intentional skips
- Post-miss guidance; metrics; safety guardrail

**Model answer.**

A missed request costs twice: the driver loses income and the rider waits longer. Assume the acceptance window is 15 seconds. I'll sort misses by cause, because each needs a different fix.

| Miss type | Fix |
|---|---|
| Inattention: the window passes while the driver looks away | A three-stage alert over 25 seconds: full-screen takeover (0 to 10), continuous vibration and rising volume (10 to 20), a loud ping and lock-screen countdown (20 to 25) |
| Phone in pocket or on vibrate | A lock-screen widget showing status and earnings that turns into large Accept and Decline buttons; no unlock needed |
| Another app in front | The same takeover breaks through the banner |
| Car display | A full-screen overlay on CarPlay and Android Auto, read aloud: "New request, pickup 3 minutes away. Accept?" Accept by voice |
| Intentional skip | A preview before accepting: estimated duration, earnings and general direction, not the exact destination, to prevent discrimination |

After a miss, don't just say "you missed a request." Say the rider was matched elsewhere and point to a nearby high-demand area.

I'd ship the escalating alert and lock-screen accept first: they cover the unintentional misses, which I expect to be the majority.

Metrics. Primary: missed request rate, the share of dispatched requests that expire. Secondary: acceptance rate by driver tier, widget adoption, and car-display interactions. Guardrail: safety incidents during the acceptance window. If louder alerts distract a moving driver, we've traded a missed ride for something worse.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Builds the taxonomy of miss types before proposing fixes
- Matches fixes to causes: escalating alerts for inattention, lock-screen accept for phone-in-pocket, voice accept in the car
- Defines a safety guardrail

**Where answers fail.**

- "Extend the window from 15 to 30 seconds": one-dimensional
- Penalizing drivers for misses: policy, not product

**If they push.**

- *"After launch, accident reports in the 20 to 25 second stage rise 3%."* Pause that stage for moving vehicles at once and investigate. Safety outranks this metric, so escalation should depend on speed.
- *"Full-time drivers love the widget; part-timers rarely install it."* Setup effort only pays off with volume. For part-timers I'd rely on defaults that need no setup, and prompt the widget right after a miss.

### Q43. How would you improve the peer-to-peer money transfer experience on a payments super-app to increase monthly active transfer users?

*Product improvement · Standard · Tailored*

**What they are testing.** Whether you see P2P as a relationship habit, not a one-off transaction, and remove friction at recipient, amount and trust. Stories to draw on: S5 (UPI flow and funnel design), S6 (reframing a retention problem).

**Clarify first.**

- Where do users drop: finding the recipient, the amount, or confirming? I'll assume recipient discovery and first-time trust.
- Casual splits or regular transfers? I'll assume both, for senders and receivers.

**Outline.**

- P2P as a relationship feature
- Three friction points: recipient, amount, confirmation anxiety
- Contacts-first entry
- Split calculator and split requests
- Recurring transfers suggested from history
- Verified name and a cancel window for new recipients
- Receiver side; metrics; failure-rate guardrail

**Model answer.**

P2P is the highest-frequency feature in a payments super-app. My working assumption, to verify, is that people who send money every month retain far better than people who only pay merchants. So I'd treat transfers as a relationship feature, not a transaction feature.

Today's flow: find the recipient, enter a UPI ID or phone number, set the amount, add a note, confirm, wait. It breaks in three places: finding the person when I only have a phone number, working out the amount on a split, and anxiety the first time I pay someone new.

| Improvement | What the user sees | Friction removed |
|---|---|---|
| Contacts-first entry | A carousel of recent people, then suggested contacts already on UPI | "I know who, not their UPI ID" |
| Split in the flow | Enter the total and the headcount; the amount pre-fills; one tap sends a split request to the group | Amount |
| Recurring transfers | "You sent Rs 8,000 to Mum on the 1st for four months. Make it recurring?" One tap, or a nudge near the usual date | Builds the habit |
| Confidence for new recipients | The bank-registered name before submit; above Rs 10,000, a two-minute window to cancel | Trust |

On the receiving side, a "request money" link shared over WhatsApp or SMS takes the payer straight to confirmation, and a group request creates one QR code many people can pay.

Recurring transfers are my top priority: one setup produces an active transfer user every month.

Metrics. Primary: monthly active transfer users, as a count and as a share of MAU. Secondary: transfers per active user, and recurring-transfer activation rate. Guardrail: transfer failure rate must not rise.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Names recurring transfers as the highest-impact retention mechanic
- Designs contacts-first discovery and builds the split calculator into the flow
- Adds a settlement-confidence screen for new recipients

**Where answers fail.**

- "Add more payment methods": misses the relationship opportunity
- Copying a social feed of payments without asking whether it fits the culture

**If they push.**

- *"Recurring transfers fail 5% of the time for insufficient balance on the debit date."* Check the balance a day ahead and warn the sender privately, retry once later that day, and tell the recipient only that the transfer is delayed.
- *"Design this for a market moving from cash to digital, where trust is the barrier."* Start with small amounts, instant confirmation on both phones, named recipients, an easy dispute path, and agents who convert cash to digital and back.

### Q44. How would you improve the search experience on the App Store to help users discover better apps?

*Product improvement · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you separate finding from evaluating, judge discovery by what happens after install, and treat developers as a second customer.

**Clarify first.**

- iOS App Store only? I'll assume yes.
- Is the failure finding apps, or judging which is best? I'll assume both, with ranking, result cards and personalization in scope.

**Outline.**

- Search drives most downloads
- Discovery failures: keyword mismatch, crowding, no intent
- Evaluation failures: lookalike cards, uneven screenshots, gamed ratings
- Semantic intent search
- Result cards that show the searched feature
- Use-case chips; personalization
- Developer fairness; metrics

**Model answer.**

Search matters here: Apple's own ads site says almost 65% of downloads follow a search. I see two failures. Discovery: the right app doesn't show up. Evaluation: several show up and I can't tell which is best.

Discovery fails for three reasons. Queries are functional ("app to help me sleep") while matching leans on names and keywords. Popular terms are crowded by large, heavily advertised apps. And intent isn't understood: "budgeting," "budget tracker" and "manage my money" return different results. Evaluation fails because every card looks the same, screenshots vary in quality, and ratings can be gamed.

| Improvement | What it does |
|---|---|
| Semantic search | A language model maps the query to app functions (sleep sounds, timer, white noise), trained on sessions where users installed and stayed |
| Contextual result cards | A 3-second preview of the searched feature, a one-line "what this app does," and a "Best for" label |
| Use-case chips | After "budget tracker": Personal, Business, Couples, Students. A tap re-ranks |
| Personalization | For signed-in users: installed apps, purchases, device |

Developers are the other customer. "Best for" labels and chips create niches where a specialized app can outrank a generic one. I'd also weight reviews by quality: verified users who kept the app 30 days or more count above bursts at launch.

Metrics. Primary: search-to-install conversion. The quality check is 30-day retention after install: many installs with poor retention means discovery is failing. I'd also track traffic distribution across developer size tiers. Guardrail: diversity of results.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Proposes semantic, intent-based search
- Puts a contextual preview and an AI "what this app does" summary in result cards, with use-case filtering
- Uses 30-day post-install retention as the quality metric

**Where answers fail.**

- Stricter screenshot rules or more filters that leave the intent mismatch in place
- "Sell more search ads" as the answer to developer discovery

**If they push.**

- *"Suppose an antitrust proceeding shows first-party apps appear three times as often in results."* Apply one ranking standard to first- and third-party apps, have it audited, and publish the principles.
- *"Semantic search shifts 40% of traffic in some categories to newer apps, and established apps threaten to leave."* Check whether users are better off, by retention and ratings. If so, hold the line, phase the rollout, and give developers intent data to adapt.

### Q45. How would you improve the Google Play Store's app update experience to increase update adoption rates?

*Product improvement · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you treat update delay as user psychology (fear, opacity, interruption) as well as mechanics, and protect stability while speeding adoption.

**Clarify first.**

- How is adoption measured? I'll use the share of devices on the latest version within 7 days.
- Why do users delay, and what is the business driver? I'll assume fear, data cost and inattention; security matters most.

**Outline.**

- Five reasons users delay
- Risk-rated labels: security, feature, maintenance
- Smart scheduling
- Before-and-after changelog
- 72-hour rollback
- Metrics and a crash guardrail

**Model answer.**

I'll assume a meaningful share of Android users run apps two or three versions behind, which raises security exposure and lowers app quality.

Why do people delay? Fear of change: "it works, what if the update breaks it?" Worry about data and battery. Inattention: the badge is easy to ignore. A bad memory of an update that broke something. And opaque notes: "bug fixes and performance improvements" tells me nothing.

| Improvement | How it works | Cause addressed |
|---|---|---|
| Risk-rated labels | Security (red shield, a daily lock-screen reminder until installed), Feature (blue star), Maintenance (grey wrench) with plain notes: "Fixed: crash when you rotate the screen" | Opacity, inattention |
| Smart scheduling | Auto-update apps unused for 14+ days; for active apps, install at the quietest time: "tonight at 3 AM while charging" | Interruption, data |
| Before and after | For major versions: "In 7.2 you had four tabs; in 8.0 they are icons at the bottom. Here's why." A badge for developers who write detailed notes | Fear of change |
| Rollback | For 72 hours the user can return to the previous version, kept in a compressed cache for eligible apps | Bad past experience |

Rollback changes the risk calculation: an update goes from permanent to reversible. Labels and rollback come first because they answer the two strongest reasons, opacity and fear.

Metrics. Primary: 7-day adoption, the share of devices on the current version within 7 days of release. Secondary: adoption of security updates specifically, and a survey measure of update anxiety. Guardrail: update-related crash rate, so we never speed adoption of a less stable version.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Names fear of change and changelog opacity as the highest-friction causes
- Designs risk-tier labels, and smart scheduling for inactive apps
- Offers rollback as the anxiety reducer

**Where answers fail.**

- "Make auto-update the default for everything": ignores user choice
- Removing manual updates without engaging with why people hesitate

**If they push.**

- *"A banking app ships an update that crashes on startup, and 40 million users have auto-updated."* User-initiated rollback won't scale to that. I'd rely on staged rollouts that halt on crash spikes, and a server-side rollback or fast hotfix the developer can trigger.
- *"60% of developers label security updates as feature updates."* Don't rely on self-labeling. Check labels against declared fixes, default unknowns to maintenance, and audit samples, with penalties for repeat mislabeling.

# Part III — Root cause analysis

## 3. Root cause analysis questions

Root cause questions give you a metric that moved and ask why. They test whether you investigate in an order that rules things out cheaply: pin down the metric, confirm the data is real, break the number into parts, segment, look at what changed inside the company, then outside it, then test hypotheses and recommend. The usual failure is guessing a cause in the first minute.

Every model answer follows that order and puts its hypotheses in a table: the hypothesis, the test, and what the result would show. Rehearse by saying the order aloud before you name any cause. Numbers in the prompts are hypotheticals, and product details are assumptions unless a source is named. Three questions are marked Tailored and name the stories to draw on.

### Q46. Google Search ad revenue dropped 15% week-over-week. Walk me through your investigation.

*Root cause analysis · Standard*
