**What they are testing.** Whether you validate the data, decompose revenue into its drivers, and isolate the drop by dimension before naming a cause.

**Clarify first.**

- Is the 15% in revenue, impressions or RPM? I'll assume total revenue.
- Global, or one market or vertical? Unknown, so I'll segment.
- Gradual, or a step on one day? I'll assume a step.

**Outline.**

- Confirm the metric and the pipeline
- Revenue = impressions × CTR × CPC, less invalid traffic
- Segment: geography, device, format, vertical, advertiser tenure
- Internal: launches, policy enforcement, serving incidents
- External: advertiser demand, competitors, privacy changes
- Recovery by cause; update cadence

**Model answer.**

A 15% drop in a week is large, so first: is it real? I'd check the reporting pipeline for delayed event ingestion or de-duplication errors.

If it's real, I decompose. Revenue is impressions times click-through rate times cost per click, less the invalid-traffic adjustment. Impressions fall with query volume or ad coverage. CTR falls if ads are less clickable or sit lower on the page. CPC falls if bids or quality scores shift. A stricter invalid-click filter removes billable clicks. A daily view shows which one moved, and when.

In parallel I segment by geography, device, ad format, advertiser vertical, and new versus existing advertisers. One country suggests a regional event. Mobile only suggests an OS or rendering change. Existing advertisers cutting budgets is an ROI problem; fewer new ones, an acquisition problem.

| Hypothesis | Test | What it would show |
|---|---|---|
| Auction or quality-score change cut ad rank | Deployment history, 7 to 14 days | A step on the launch day, across advertisers |
| Policy enforcement removed many ads | Enforcement logs | Loss in one category |
| Ad-serving incident | Uptime and incident logs | Impressions dip for a bounded period |
| Fewer queries classified as commercial | Share of queries eligible for ads | Coverage down; CTR and CPC steady |
| Advertiser demand shifted | Top advertisers' spend, week over week | A few advertisers explain it |
| Privacy change cut attribution confidence | Bid trends by platform | Slow decline, not a step |

The first four rows are internal changes; the last two are external. My top suspect is the first: a silent ranking change is a common cause of a sudden step.

Recovery by cause. Code change: roll back, then re-ship a targeted fix. Enforcement: check calibration, and give clean-history advertisers a temporary exception path. Demand: account teams call the highest-value advertisers who cut spend. I'd alert revenue leadership and ads policy at once and update every two hours.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Validates data integrity first
- Decomposes revenue into impressions × CTR × CPC, and isolates across five or more dimensions
- Ranks hypotheses with reasons and names a data pull for each

**Where answers fail.**

- Hypotheses listed with no decomposition or investigation order
- "The algorithm changed," said before validating the data

**If they push.**

- *"The drop is 100% in the Healthcare vertical in Germany."* First hypothesis: a policy or regulatory enforcement action on health ads in that market. I'd read enforcement logs before anything technical.
- *"It started on a Tuesday, but nothing deployed that day."* Look beyond code: experiment ramps, config pushes, enforcement, a model refresh, or an external event.

### Q47. Instagram's daily active users dropped 8% month-over-month. Walk through your investigation.

*Root cause analysis · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you break a user count into flows and cohorts, and let the pattern across segments separate internal from external causes.

**Clarify first.**

- Is only DAU down, or engagement too? I'll check both.
- Global or local; gradual or a step? Unknown, so I'll segment and date it.

**Outline.**

- Validate the DAU definition and pipeline
- DAU = retained + reactivated + new − churned
- Segment: geography, age, device, account age, surface
- Internal: launches and experiments in the last 45 days
- External: competitors, OS changes, press
- Recovery by cause

**Model answer.**

At Instagram's scale 8% is a very large number of people, so first: is DAU measured the same way as last month? A change in what counts as "active," or in analytics tagging, can create an apparent drop.

If the data is clean, I decompose. DAU is retained users plus reactivated plus new, minus churned. Retained down with new flat points to engagement among existing users. A churn spike means something pushed people away. Weak reactivation means the app is losing its pull on lapsed users. Fewer new users is a marketing-channel or app-store problem.

Then I segment by geography, age cohort, device, account age and surface. Concentrated in 18 to 24: competitive pressure is my first frame. Across all ages: more likely something we changed. New accounts leaving faster: onboarding. By surface: is use shifting between feed, Reels and Stories, or is the whole app down?

For internal changes I'd read deployment and experiment logs for the past 45 days, because behavior can lag a launch.

| Hypothesis | Test | What it would show |
|---|---|---|
| Reels ranking change lowered content quality | Session depth, frequency, content diversity | Shorter, fewer sessions after the change |
| Competitor launch pulled younger users | Third-party app-usage panels | Their usage up in the same cohort |
| OS update changed notification delivery | Push-driven DAU by OS version | Push-driven opens down on one version |
| Policy change cut content supply | Feed content volume; creator activity | Fewer posts from affected creators |
| Bug in the latest release | Crash and uninstall rates by app version | A spike on one version |
| Negative press drove uninstalls | Store reviews; searches about deleting accounts | Uninstalls track the news cycle |

Recovery follows the cause. Ranking: revert or retune. Competitive: speed up the features that differentiate. Notifications: work with the OS vendor. Policy: check that the impact is proportionate.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Decomposes DAU into retained + reactivated + new − churned
- Segments by age cohort to frame competitive versus internal causes
- Checks deployment and experiment logs, and raises notification delivery as an OS-specific hypothesis

**Where answers fail.**

- "Maybe TikTok," with no cohort data behind it
- "Run ads to win users back" before finding the cause

**If they push.**

- *"The drop is entirely in India, where DAU fell 18%."* That suggests something local: a regulatory or access issue, a carrier or data-pricing change, or a regional outage.
- *"The cause was a 30-day A/B test on 20% of users that degraded notifications. Stop it?"* Yes. An 8% overall drop from 20% of users implies about 40% in the treated group. I'd add a DAU guardrail that halts such tests automatically.

### Q48. Amazon Prime Video's streaming quality complaints increased 25% in the past week. Diagnose the problem.

*Root cause analysis · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you separate "more complaints" from "worse quality," and localize a fault along the delivery chain.

**Clarify first.**

- What is a "complaint": in-app feedback, tickets, or a technical metric? I'll assume feedback and tickets.
- Are technical failure rates up too, and is it concentrated anywhere? Unknown; my first checks.

**Outline.**

- Signal or noise: complaints against technical metrics
- Delivery chain: origin, CDN, ISP, client, device
- Segment: content type, bitrate, ISP and region, device, time of day
- Internal: app release, CDN config, encoder
- External: ISP peering, demand spikes
- Mitigate by cause; real-time telemetry

**Model answer.**

First I separate signal from noise. Complaints can rise 25% because quality got worse, or because complaining got easier: say a new in-app report button. So I'd pull technical metrics next to complaint volume: buffering ratio, startup failures, average bitrate. Flat metrics with more complaints means a reporting change. Both up means a real quality problem.

Next, the delivery chain: content request, origin, CDN edge, ISP interconnect, client decode, display.

Then I segment. Live versus on-demand, since live uses different delivery. Bitrate tier: trouble only at 4K suggests capacity. ISP and region: concentration there means network, not app. Device and OS: one combination suggests a client bug. Time of day: a peak from 8 to 10 PM in one time zone is congestion.

| Hypothesis | Test | What it would show |
|---|---|---|
| CDN capacity short during a big event | Edge utilization; bitrate distribution | Lower bitrates served at peak |
| App update broke the decoder or adaptive bitrate logic | Complaint rate by app version | Concentrated in the new version |
| ISP peering problem | Complaints by ISP and region | One ISP, one region |
| Encoding change | Complaint rate by title | Titles sharing one encoder configuration |
| Measurement artifact | Change log for the feedback flow | A report button shipped this week |

I'd pull buffering ratio by day for 21 days to date the start, then cross-reference app, CDN and encoder deployments.

Mitigation by cause. Capacity: add edge capacity, and degrade to a lower bitrate instead of buffering. Peering: escalate to the ISP's network operations center. App bug: roll back the version on affected devices. Encoding: re-encode with the previous configuration.

Long term, every session should report buffering, startup time and bitrate switches in real time, so we see degradation before complaints do.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Checks whether complaints reflect real degradation or a change in the complaint UI
- Maps the streaming architecture, and segments by content type, ISP, device and version
- Proposes proactive telemetry as the systemic fix

**Where answers fail.**

- Hypotheses with no architecture behind them, and vague next steps
- "Improve video quality," without separating complaint rate from technical metrics

**If they push.**

- *"90% of complaints come from one ISP's users in the Northeast US."* A peering or interconnect issue. I'd confirm with our network data, escalate to the ISP, and steer traffic through other paths meanwhile.
- *"Telemetry shows a 3% buffering ratio. Good or bad?"* It depends on the baseline. I'd set the threshold from our own data: the level where abandonment starts to rise.

### Q49. A payment gateway's transaction failure rate spiked from 0.5% to 4.2% on a Wednesday afternoon. Walk through your RCA.

*Root cause analysis · Stretch · Tailored*

**What they are testing.** Whether you can run a live incident: classify failures, localize along the payment chain, mitigate in parallel and keep merchants informed. Stories to draw on: S3 (pricing engine and alerting), S5 (launch monitoring and fallbacks).

**Clarify first.**

- Share of initiated transactions, or of authorization attempts? I'll assume authorization attempts.
- Bank declines, or technical failures such as timeouts? Unknown; that is my first pull.

**Outline.**

- P0: triage in the first 15 minutes
- Classify: technical, issuer decline, fraud block
- Segment: method, issuing bank, value, merchant, minute
- Map the chain: merchant, gateway, acquirer, network, issuer
- Hypotheses and checks
- Mitigation by cause
- Merchant communication; post-mortem

**Model answer.**

From 0.5% to 4.2% means about eight times as many payments failing. Merchants are losing revenue, so this is a P0.

First 15 minutes: page the on-call payments engineer, open the incident room, and watch the trend: rising, stable or recovering? Warn merchant success to expect complaints.

Then I classify by error code. Technical failures, such as timeouts and HTTP 500 or 504, point at our infrastructure. Issuer declines, such as code 05 "do not honor," sit with the bank. Fraud blocks come from our own engine. If 90% are one type, I know where to look.

I segment at the same time by payment method, issuing bank, transaction value, merchant and minute. One issuer or one network dominating points there. Failures only above a certain value suggest a bank rule change. A few merchants suggests their integration. A spike at 2:15 PM asks what happened at 2:15.

The chain is merchant API, our gateway, the acquirer, the card network or NPCI, the issuing bank, and back.

| Hypothesis | Test | What it would show |
|---|---|---|
| Deployment malformed a field one bank requires | Deployment log, 24 hours | One issuer declining from the deploy time |
| Acquirer capacity or connectivity | Their status page; our API metrics | Timeouts on that route |
| New fraud rule too aggressive | Fraud engine change log and decisions | A jump in block rate |
| Card network or NPCI outage | Network status pages | Failures across issuers on one rail |
| Traffic spike, such as a flash sale | Transactions per minute | A surge, then cascading timeouts |

Mitigation by cause: disable the fraud rule, roll back the deployment, route to the backup acquirer, or scale up and shed load with clear errors.

Communication: a status page update for merchants within 30 minutes, refreshed every 30, and an incident report to the top 50 merchants within 24 hours of resolution. Then a blameless post-mortem within 48 hours. Its key question: did monitoring catch this within two minutes, or did merchants tell us first?

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Classifies failures at once: technical, issuer decline, fraud block
- Segments by method, bank and error code against a map of the payment chain
- Runs parallel tracks and includes a merchant communication plan

**Where answers fail.**

- No architecture, and no split between technical failures and issuer declines
- "Restart the servers": no diagnosis, no communication plan

**If they push.**

- *"Two hours in, still 4.2% and no root cause."* Widen the team, roll back everything changed in the window, shift traffic off suspect routes to test them, and keep merchants updated.

### Q50. Spotify's Discover Weekly engagement (plays, saves, skips) dropped 20% this week. What do you investigate?

*Root cause analysis · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you check delivery before quality, and can walk a recommendation pipeline step by step to find what changed.

**Clarify first.**

- Total plays, or engagement per delivered playlist? I'll assume totals, and split them.
- Did it reach as many users as last week; any model or pipeline change? Unknown; my first checks.

**Outline.**

- Confirm delivery to the same base
- Engagement = openers × songs played × per-song actions
- Segment: history depth, genre cluster, market
- Walk the pipeline step by step
- Internal: model, data, surfacing; external: catalog
- First data pulls

**Model answer.**

First: did Discover Weekly reach the same number of users this week? If a notification or surfacing bug hid the playlist, engagement per listener may be unchanged while totals fall.

Next I decompose. Engagement is users who opened the playlist, times songs played, times per-song actions. Fewer openers: delivery or notification. Fewer songs per opener: people hear a few, find them irrelevant and leave. More skips: wrong songs for the user. Fewer saves with steady plays: acceptable, but not save-worthy.

Then I segment by listening-history depth, genre cluster and market. New users have thin histories and weaker playlists, so an influx of them drags the average.

I'd assume a six-step pipeline: collect about four weeks of listening history, find nearest neighbors by collaborative filtering, pick songs the neighbors like that the user hasn't heard, apply diversity filters, score and rank, build the 30-song list. I'd ask what changed at each.

| Hypothesis | Test | What it would show |
|---|---|---|
| Model update changed neighborhood size or the diversity filter | Model deployment log, 14 days | Saves fall most: "fine," not "wow" |
| History window shortened | Pipeline configuration | Broad loss of accuracy |
| Some histories stale or partial | Completeness of listening data per user | Drop concentrated in affected users |
| Influx of new users | Engagement by history depth | Deep-history users unchanged |
| Surfacing or notification change | Delivery and open counts | Fewer openers, steady engagement each |
| Popular catalog became unavailable | Catalog availability log | Plays shift to lesser-known substitutes |

The catalog is the external factor: licensing sits outside the model.

My first pulls: the delivery count for both weeks, then plays, saves, skips and skip rate after 30 seconds, split by history depth. Those tell me whether this is delivery, mix or quality before I touch the model.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Confirms the delivery count before judging quality
- Splits engagement into plays, saves and skips, each with its own meaning
- Maps the pipeline, raises data completeness, and segments by history depth

**Where answers fail.**

- "The algorithm got worse," with no structure and no delivery check
- "Improve the recommendation algorithm," without finding what changed

**If they push.**

- *"Skip rate after 30 seconds rose from 18% to 31%."* People gave songs a fair listen and still rejected them, so it's relevance, not surfacing. I'd look at model and data changes first.
- *"Users with under four weeks of history engage 35% less."* Design for cold start: weight onboarding artist picks and early explicit signals, and mix in more familiar tracks.

### Q51. The average checkout conversion rate on an e-commerce platform dropped from 68% to 51%. Diagnose.

*Root cause analysis · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you localize the leak to a funnel step and a segment before theorizing, and remember causes outside your own code: the payment processor, fraud rules.

**Clarify first.**

- How is the funnel defined? I'll assume checkout entered to order completed.
- A step or gradual; concentrated anywhere? Unknown, so I'll date it and segment.

**Outline.**

- Size it: 17 points, 25% relative
- Confirm the definition; find the leaking step
- Segment: device, user type, payment method, category
- Map the stack: form, API, processor, fraud check, bank
- Internal changes; external dependencies
- Recovery by cause

**Model answer.**

From 68% to 51% is 17 points, a 25% relative decline. The fix depends entirely on where the leak is, so I locate it before theorizing.

I'd confirm the definition hasn't changed, then pull abandonment at each step, this period against the prior one: cart view, shipping details, payment method, order review, confirmation. If most of the extra abandonment is at payment, it's a processing issue. At shipping, it's a form problem or a surprise cost.

I segment in parallel. Device: a mobile-only drop suggests an app release or a wallet failure. User type: new users convert lower, so a mix shift toward them drops the average with nothing broken; a drop among returning users means we hurt the core experience. Payment method: cards steady while buy-now-pay-later collapses points to that integration. Category: a drop in high-value items suggests shipping cost or large-order declines.

The stack is the front-end form, our API, the payment processor, the fraud check, bank authorization, then confirmation.

| Hypothesis | Test | What it would show |
|---|---|---|
| Checkout change revealed a surprise cost or confused users | Deployment log, two weeks | Exits where the total appears |
| Processor declines or timeouts rose | Processor authorization-rate dashboard | Say 95% falling to 80% |
| Fraud rule over-blocking | Rule change log; block rate | Say 15% flagged against a normal 2% |
| JavaScript error on "Place order" | Browser error logs; 20 to 30 session recordings | Clicks with no order created |
| New mandatory field | Field completion rates | Drop-off at that field |
| Price or shipping fee increase | Order value and shipping fee over time | Abandonment after the total |

The surprise cost is my leading suspect: it's the most common cause. The processor's rate is external data, so I'd request it early.

Recovery. UX change: revert, then A/B test it properly. Fraud rule: roll back and recalibrate on a small cohort. Script error: hotfix. Processor: investigate with them and route to a backup.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Finds the funnel stage where abandonment rose, and segments by device, user type and payment method together
- Raises the processor's authorization rate, which needs external data, and reviews session recordings
- Names the shipping-cost surprise as the most common cause

**Where answers fail.**

- "Check the checkout page," with no funnel or segmentation
- "Add more payment options," with no diagnosis

**If they push.**

- *"After the fix, conversion recovers to 64%, not 68%."* A second cause is still live, or the mix changed. I'd rerun the same funnel and segment cut on the remaining 4 points.

### Q52. Google Maps' turn-by-turn navigation completion rate (users who followed the route from start to destination) dropped 12%. Diagnose.

*Root cause analysis · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you question what the metric measures (arrival detection, trip mix) before assuming routes got worse.

**Clarify first.**

- How is completion defined? I'll assume the app must detect arrival.
- Which mode and geography; are re-routes up? Unknown, so I'll segment.

**Outline.**

- Four ways a trip ends "not complete"
- Instrumentation: did arrival detection change?
- Segment: mode, geography, device and OS, user tenure
- Pipeline: route, instructions, GPS, off-route and arrival detection
- Internal changes; external: OS, road closures, competitors
- The termination-reason breakdown decides

**Model answer.**

Non-completion can mean four things. The user abandoned mid-route for another app. They went off-route because directions were wrong. They arrived, but the app didn't register it. Or they got close enough and dismissed navigation. Each has a different cause.

So the first check is instrumentation. Did the arrival-detection logic or the metric definition change? If "arrived" events are steady while the rate fell, it's a measurement issue.

Then I segment. Mode: driving down with walking steady points to driving-specific routing or data. Geography: specific cities suggest map data or construction. Device: iOS down with Android steady suggests an OS or SDK change. Tenure: experienced users going off-route means something different from new users doing so.

The pipeline is route calculation, instruction generation, GPS tracking, off-route detection, re-calculation and arrival detection.

| Hypothesis | Test | What it would show |
|---|---|---|
| Routing update mishandles closures or construction | Off-route events against recently modified road segments | Off-route clusters on those segments |
| GPS accuracy degraded: an OS location change, or new smoothing | Median position error by device and OS version | False off-route triggers on one OS |
| Arrival threshold tightened | Arrival-detection change log | Users arrive; the app doesn't register it |
| Trip mix shifted to short trips | Average trip distance, this period against prior | More "close enough" dismissals |
| Competitor feature pulls users mid-route | Share of navigations that lose the foreground | More backgrounding mid-trip |

The decisive pull is the termination-reason breakdown: arrived, user dismissed, off-route and abandoned, app backgrounded. More dismissals point to direction quality. More off-route abandons point to routing or GPS. Then I'd read the Maps release log and GPS accuracy by OS version.

Recovery follows the finding: roll back the routing or arrival change, patch location handling for the affected OS, or, if it's trip mix, fix the metric and leave the product alone.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Breaks completion into specific termination reasons
- Raises GPS accuracy as a non-obvious hypothesis, and checks the arrival-detection logic
- Considers a shift in trip-length mix as a confound

**Where answers fail.**

- "The routes are wrong," without considering GPS accuracy or the metric definition
- "Add more map data," with no diagnosis

**If they push.**

- *"Off-route events rose 40% in San Francisco only."* A local cause: a map-data update, or closures and construction the map doesn't reflect. I'd overlay off-route locations on recent edits.
- *"Completion depends on the app detecting arrival. What are the limits?"* It misses people who arrive and close the app early, and it depends on GPS quality. I'd add proximity to the destination, re-routes per trip, and a sampled "did you arrive?" survey.

### Q53. A fintech app's UPI success rate dropped from 98.5% to 94.1% over a weekend. Diagnose.

*Root cause analysis · Stretch · Tailored*

**What they are testing.** Whether you can tell "our problem" from "ecosystem problem" fast in UPI, and act when the cause is outside your control. Stories to draw on: S5 (RuPay credit card on UPI: bank coordination and fallback logic).

**Clarify first.**

- All transaction types, or one? I'll assume all, and segment.
- Technical failures, or business declines such as a wrong PIN? Unknown; my first pull.

**Outline.**

- Size it (4.4 points, P0); ecosystem or us?
- Classify: technical versus business declines, by response code
- Segment: issuing bank, transaction type, hour
- Map the chain: app, PSP, NPCI switch, issuing bank
- Internal: Friday deploy, fraud rules
- External: bank maintenance, NPCI rules
- Mitigation and reporting

**Model answer.**

That is a 4.4-point drop. On, say, 10 million transactions a day, it means about 440,000 more failures daily. P0.

First triage: is this us or the ecosystem? I'd compare NPCI's published uptime and bank-wise decline data with our own. If everyone is degraded, the cause is NPCI or a bank. If only we dropped, it's ours.

Then I classify by response code. UPI separates technical declines, where a bank or switch fails or times out, from business declines such as an invalid PIN (ZM), insufficient funds (Z9) or a limit exceeded. Technical declines from one bank point to that bank. Errors in our gateway point to us. Limit declines suggest a rule change.

I segment by issuing bank, transaction type and hour. Banks often schedule maintenance late at night and on weekends, so I'd pull failures by bank for Saturday night.

The chain is the app, our PSP server, the NPCI switch, the issuing bank and its core banking.

| Hypothesis | Test | What it would show |
|---|---|---|
| Large issuing banks ran weekend maintenance | Failures by bank and hour | Timeouts from one or two banks, for hours |
| Our Friday deployment broke request formatting | Deployment log; errors by bank | Rejections since Friday, on some banks |
| NPCI or a bank changed limit or velocity rules | Recent NPCI circulars; limit-related declines | Business declines above a new limit |
| Our fraud rule over-blocks weekend patterns | Fraud engine decision log; block rate | Block rate up; rule tuned on weekday data |

Bank maintenance is the most probable cause of a weekend drop. If so, it's not our fault, but it is our problem. I'd show a proactive message: "This bank's UPI is under maintenance. Try another method, or retry in 30 minutes." And I'd offer a guided retry after about 90 seconds, for technical declines only, guarding against a double debit.

NPCI watches members' technical declines, so I'd confirm our reporting obligations with compliance and prepare an incident report.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Checks ecosystem health first: is it us, or everyone?
- Classifies failures by NPCI response code, and names weekend bank maintenance as the likely cause
- Proposes user messaging and a safe retry, and covers the reporting obligation

**Where answers fail.**

- "Check our servers," with no grasp of the UPI chain or response codes
- Treating it as a generic API failure, unaware of NPCI

**If they push.**

- *"Suppose NPCI asks for an improvement plan within 48 hours."* Root cause by bank and code, what we control (retries, monitoring, alerts), what needs bank action, and owners with dates.

### Q54. A consumer app's push notification click-through rate dropped 40% in one week. Investigate.

*Root cause analysis · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you separate delivery from relevance, and spot a mix shift that lowers the average with nothing getting worse.

**Clarify first.**

- Absolute clicks, or clicks over delivered? I'll assume the rate.
- Did send volume change? Unknown; I'll check.
- All notification types, or specific campaigns? I'll segment by type and date.

**Outline.**

- Sent, delivered, clicked: which moved?
- Delivery health by OS version
- Mix shift across notification types
- Internal: copy, personalization, send-time model, campaigns
- External: OS changes to how notifications display
- Lagging signals: opt-outs, fatigue
- Recovery by cause

**Model answer.**

The first question is whether delivery works. I'd pull three counts: sent, delivered to the device, clicked. If delivered fell while sent held, it's an infrastructure or OS problem, and clicks fell with it. If delivered is steady and clicks fell 40%, it's relevance or visibility.

Second, mix. Types have very different baselines. As an illustration: transactional ("your order shipped") 30% to 40%, social 15% to 20%, promotional 5% to 10%. If the promotional share of sends rose, the blended rate falls though no single notification did worse. That is composition, not quality.

Third, internal changes in the past week: copy templates, the personalization model (a specific "you haven't listened to this artist in 3 days" replaced by a generic "check out new music"), the send-time model, or an A/B test.

Fourth, external: an OS release that changes how notifications appear on the lock screen can cut visibility while technical delivery looks normal.

| Hypothesis | Test | What it would show |
|---|---|---|
| OS update reduced visibility | Delivery rate and CTR by OS version | Lower CTR on the new version, same type |
| Copy or personalization change | Model, template and A/B logs | A drop dated to the change |
| Mix shift from a promotional push | Sends by type, this week against last | Per-type CTR steady, blend down |
| Opt-out spike after a heavy week | Opt-out rate by week | Engaged users left the audience |
| Fatigue from rising frequency | Sends per user over the past month | A gradual decline, no step |

Opt-outs are a lagging indicator of last week's quality, so I check them even if this week looks clean.

Recovery. Mix: rebalance volume, and report CTR by type, not blended. Personalization: revert, and A/B test changes. OS change: adapt to the new rules, for example the time-sensitive level on iOS for alerts that qualify.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Validates delivery rate against click rate
- Finds the composition shift: a cause that needs no drop in quality
- Checks an OS update as an external cause, and opt-out rate as a lagging indicator

**Where answers fail.**

- "Notifications got less relevant," without checking delivery
- "Send more notifications" to make up for low CTR

**If they push.**

- *"It's fully explained by a mix shift from a big promotional push."* Report CTR by type, set a per-user promotional budget, and require a forecast of blended impact before large campaigns.
- *"Users on the newer OS version show 50% lower CTR for the same notification type."* Visibility changed, not relevance. I'd study how that version displays notifications, then adapt content, interruption levels and timing.

### Q55. The number of support tickets for a SaaS product increased 3x in two weeks. Diagnose.

*Root cause analysis · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you normalize before reacting, and sort tickets into education gap, product bug or service failure, since each needs a different fix.

**Clarify first.**

- Total volume, or one category? I'll assume total, then segment.
- Did the user base grow too? Unknown; I normalize first.

**Outline.**

- Normalize: tickets per active user
- Segment by category and account age
- Date the spike against launches and changes
- Classify: education gap, product bug, service failure
- Topic clustering as the fastest test
- Immediate mitigation; long-term alerting

**Model answer.**

A tripling is always a signal, but of what? First I normalize: tickets per active user, by week. If users also tripled, the rate is flat and this is a support-scaling problem. If the rate tripled, it's a product or service failure.

Then I segment by category: billing, how-to questions, bug reports, account access, feature requests. If billing is 80% of the increase, something changed in billing. If how-to questions are, a new feature is confusing people.

Next, timing. I'd plot ticket creation by day against the change log. A spike the day after a launch points at that launch. Otherwise I'd look at infrastructure incidents, slower support responses that cause re-submits, or a campaign that brought in new users.

Three kinds of cause look different. An education gap: "how do I do X?" The product works and users can't find the way; these resolve in under five minutes. A product bug: error descriptions, screenshots, consistent steps to reproduce. A service failure: a sudden spike with one symptom, gone when the dependency recovers.

| Hypothesis | Test | What it would show |
|---|---|---|
| New feature lacks in-product guidance | Share of new tickets by feature | One feature dominates |
| Billing change confused users | Category split | Billing on top |
| UI change moved things | Ticket text | "Where did X go?" |
| Deployment bug in a common workflow | Error message or workflow cited | One symptom across many tickets |
| Campaign brought needier new users | Tickets by account age | Accounts under 30 days over-represented |

The fastest test is clustering subject lines and first messages by topic.

Mitigation. Education gap: an in-product tooltip or banner, a help article today, and a brief for agents on the top three questions. Bug: fix it and notify affected users before they re-submit. Billing: a clear explanation and a callback offer.

Prevention: alert the PM and engineering leads when volume exceeds 150% of the 7-day average for two hours, and instrument the paths that end in a ticket.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Normalizes by user base before calling it a quality problem
- Uses topic clustering of ticket text as the fastest route to the trigger
- Distinguishes education gap, product bug and service failure, and proposes a volume alert

**Where answers fail.**

- Causes listed with no normalization or segmentation
- "Hire more support agents," with no diagnosis

**If they push.**

- *"70% of tickets ask how to export data, a feature that existed before the spike."* Something around it changed: the entry point moved, a permission or plan change hid it, or an event such as audit season raised demand.

### Q56. OpenAI's API latency increased from 800ms to 2.4 seconds (p50) over two days. Diagnose.

*Root cause analysis · Stretch*

**What they are testing.** Whether you can decompose latency in an inference stack, and run an incident for developers who have their own customers.

**Clarify first.**

- All models and request sizes, or some? Unknown, so I'll segment.
- Queue time, inference or post-processing; any change or traffic growth in 48 hours? Unknown; my first pulls.

**Outline.**

- Critical: latency tripled at the median
- Latency = queue + inference + post-processing
- Segment: model, token length, region
- Map: load balancer, queue, GPU cluster, formatting, streaming
- Internal: capacity, model, serving code; external: traffic, network
- Mitigate in parallel; tell developers

**Model answer.**

Latency tripled, and at the median, so this is broad, not a tail problem. Developers have latency commitments to their own customers. It's a critical incident.

I decompose first. Total latency is time in queue waiting for GPU capacity, plus inference time, plus post-processing and streaming. A queue spike means capacity. An inference spike means a model or hardware change. A post-processing spike means a serving-layer bug.

Then I segment. By model: if one slowed and another didn't, look at that model's weights or hardware. By request size: if only long-context requests slowed, memory may be the bottleneck. By region: one region is local infrastructure; all regions is a global change.

The path is load balancer, request queue, GPU inference cluster, response formatting, streaming to the client.

| Hypothesis | Test | What it would show |
|---|---|---|
| Traffic surge exceeded GPU capacity | Requests per minute over two days | Queue time up with volume |
| Capacity changed: hardware added or retired | Cluster capacity log | Less effective capacity |
| Model update slowed inference | Model deployment log, 48 hours | Inference time up for one model |
| Serving-layer regression | Serving deployment log | Slower across models |
| Network routing change | Internal network latency | Delay between the cluster and the API edge |

The most useful single pull is the latency waterfall, these 48 hours against the prior period.

Mitigation runs in parallel. Queue: add capacity, prioritize paying traffic, and scale at 70% utilization instead of 90%. Inference: roll back the model and investigate offline. One customer consuming too much: tighter per-developer rate limits.

Communication: within 30 minutes of confirming, post on the status page that requests are completing with elevated latency, and update every 30 minutes. Developers need to know so they can switch on fallbacks.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Decomposes latency into queue, inference and post-processing
- Checks request volume for a traffic surge, the most likely cause, and segments by model and token length
- Includes status-page communication in the incident response

**Where answers fail.**

- "The servers are probably overloaded": the obvious cause, with no decomposition
- Lowering model quality for speed before diagnosing

**If they push.**

- *"Queue time is unchanged; inference tripled, only above 8,000 tokens."* Something that scales with context length: a memory or caching change, or a long-sequence optimization that regressed. I'd compare serving and model configs on long-context paths.
- *"A model variant deployed 48 hours ago is 3x slower per step. Roll back?"* Yes, by default: the latency commitment is broken now. I'd keep the variant only as an opt-in with latency disclosed.

### Q57. A ride-share app's average ETA accuracy (predicted vs. actual pickup time) degraded from ±2 min to ±8 min. Diagnose.

*Root cause analysis · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you ask about the direction of the error, and decompose a prediction into its inputs, including the less obvious one: driver acceptance time.

**Clarify first.**

- Is the error one-directional, always late, or both ways? Unknown; I'll find out first.
- Pickup ETA or trip ETA; concentrated anywhere? I'll assume pickup, and segment.

**Outline.**

- Error grew four times; trust is at stake
- ETA = travel time to pickup + acceptance time
- Direction of error: bias or variance
- Segment: city, time of day, weather
- Inputs: driver GPS, traffic data, history, acceptance model
- Fix by cause, with fallbacks

**Model answer.**

ETA is a trust signal. A rider told 3 minutes who waits 11 remembers it. Going from ±2 to ±8 minutes is four times the error.

I decompose the prediction: travel time from the nearest available driver to the pickup, plus time for the driver to accept and start.

Then the key question: is the error directional? Consistently later than predicted means the model is too optimistic: a bias. Sometimes early, sometimes late is a variance problem. I'd also confirm how actual pickup time is logged before trusting either.

I segment by city and neighborhood, time of day and weather. Rain degrades ETA accuracy reliably.

The inputs are live driver positions, live traffic, historical travel times and an acceptance-time model.

| Hypothesis | Test | What it would show |
|---|---|---|
| Traffic feed degraded or delayed | Feed quality metrics and change log | A consistent bias across a city |
| Model retrained on unrepresentative data | Model deployment log | Error begins at the release |
| Driver GPS degraded after an app release | GPS sample rate by driver-app version | Error concentrated on one version |
| Driver supply fell, so acceptance slowed | Supply and acceptance time by market | Late pickups where supply dropped |
| Road closures missing from map data | Error by neighborhood | Pickups later than predicted in one area |

The supply case is the non-obvious one: the model assumes near-immediate acceptance, which fails when drivers are scarce.

First pull: predicted minus actual by city, hour and weekday, to find the worst cohort. Then traffic-feed metrics, the model log and GPS by app version.

Fixes. Traffic: restore the feed with the provider, and fall back to historical travel times when live quality drops. Model: roll back parameters. GPS: force-update the broken driver-app version. Supply: feed the current acceptance baseline into the estimate.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Decomposes ETA into travel time plus acceptance time
- Treats the direction of the error as the key diagnostic question
- Names traffic-feed quality as the common external cause, and acceptance time as a miscalibrated input

**Where answers fail.**

- "The traffic data might be wrong," with no decomposition or segmentation
- "Improve the ETA algorithm," with no diagnosis

**If they push.**

- *"ETAs run 6 minutes late in Chicago on weekdays from 5 to 8 PM, and are accurate otherwise."* A rush-hour bias: traffic inputs under-state evening congestion there, or acceptance slows at peak demand. I'd split the 6 minutes into travel and acceptance.
- *"The model is accurate in simulation but wrong in production."* Then the inputs differ, not the model: stale or delayed live features, or data missing in production.

### Q58. An e-learning platform's course completion rate dropped from 22% to 11% after a UX redesign. Diagnose.

*Root cause analysis · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you test causation before accepting the obvious suspect, find the stage and element responsible, and choose a targeted fix over a full rollback.

**Clarify first.**

- What changed: navigation, content format, progress tracking, notifications? I'll assume all four may have.
- A/B test or full rollout; did the learner mix change? I'll assume a full rollout, and check.

**Outline.**

- Completion halved: confirm the redesign caused it
- Check timing, counting and learner mix
- Completion funnel by stage, before and after
- Segment: existing versus new enrollees, device, course type
- Element-level hypotheses
- Hotfix versus rollback; confirm with an A/B test

**Model answer.**

Completion halved, and the timing makes the redesign the obvious suspect. But I'd confirm causation first. Did the drop start at launch, or before? If before, something else is going on, such as a different learner cohort. I'd also check that completion is counted the same way in the new design.

With an A/B test, treatment against control answers it. With a full rollout, I'd compare courses started in the 30 days before with the 30 days after, controlling for course type and learner tenure.

Then the funnel: enrolled, finished lesson 1, then 25%, 50%, 75% and 100%. The stage whose drop-off changed points to the element. If lesson 1 completion fell, starting got harder. If early lessons hold and later ones fall, it's motivation or content access.

I'd segment by device, by existing versus new enrollees, and by course format.

| Hypothesis | Test | What it would show |
|---|---|---|
| Progress indicator removed or less visible | Design diff; mid-course drop-off | Later-stage drop-off up on all devices |
| Mobile experience broke | Completion by device | Mobile down sharply, desktop steady |
| Navigation hides in-progress courses | Completion for pre-redesign enrollees | Existing learners hit hardest |
| Lessons re-chunked longer | Lesson length; completion per session | Fewer lessons finished per session |
| Resume reminders removed | Reminder sends and return visits | Returning sessions down |

I rank the progress indicator first. Visible progress is one of the strongest motivators in learning products: effort rises as the goal gets closer, the goal-gradient effect. Mobile is second, navigation third.

Recovery. If it's one element, hotfix it and keep the redesign. If the damage is spread, roll back for learners who are mid-course, and keep new enrollees on the new design while iterating. Either way, A/B test the fix before rolling it out.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Confirms causation with an A/B test or a pre-post comparison
- Maps the completion funnel and finds the stage whose drop-off rose
- Names progress-indicator removal as the likeliest cause, and prefers a hotfix to a full rollback

**Where answers fail.**

- "The new design is worse," with no element named and no evidence
- Rolling back everything without finding the element responsible

**If they push.**

- *"Lesson 1 completion is unchanged at 85%, but drop-off from lesson 5 to 6 rose from 30% to 60%."* Starting is fine. Something at that point changed: a gate, a content format, or a missing "next lesson" prompt. I'd inspect that transition.
- *"Restoring the progress indicator recovers only half the loss."* There is a second cause. I'd rerun the funnel by device and learner type; mobile breakage and missing reminders are next.

### Q59. A B2B SaaS product's monthly churn rate increased from 1.2% to 3.1% in Q3. Diagnose.

*Root cause analysis · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you classify churn before explaining it, find the moment in the customer lifecycle where it happens, and reach for the fastest evidence.

**Clarify first.**

- Voluntary cancellations, or involuntary ones from failed payments? Unknown; my first split.
- Which segment and cohort; when in the contract; any exit surveys? I'll segment and pull tenure.

**Outline.**

- Size it: 158% relative; about 13% a year becomes 31%
- Validate; split voluntary from involuntary
- Segment: size, cohort, tier, geography
- Tenure at churn: renewal, price change, incident
- Internal changes; competitors
- Evidence: exit surveys, adoption depth, health history
- Interventions by cause

**Model answer.**

From 1.2% to 3.1% a month is a 158% relative increase. Annualized, about 13% of customers a year becomes about 31%.

First I'd confirm the calculation is unchanged, then classify. Voluntary churn, where the customer chose to cancel, and involuntary churn, where a payment failed, have different fixes. If involuntary drives it, say a billing migration broke card updates, the fix is operational, not product.

Then I segment churned accounts by size, acquisition cohort, product tier and geography. One cohort churning suggests something different in how it was sold or onboarded.

Next, when in the lifecycle. B2B churn clusters at predictable moments: renewal, 30 to 60 days after a price increase, after a feature is removed, after an incident. If most churned at exactly month 12, it's a renewal decision.

| Hypothesis | Test | What it would show |
|---|---|---|
| Price or contract change hit Q3 renewals | Pricing changes effective in Q2 | Sudden churn at renewal |
| Feature deprecated | Sunset list; exit-survey mentions | One segment citing the feature |
| Quality problems in Q3 | Incident log; CSAT trend | Health declines before churn |
| Competitor offer | Exit surveys naming a competitor | Sudden churn, competitor cited |
| Customer success coverage changed | Accounts per CSM; outreach cadence | Churn where coverage thinned |
| A cohort never adopted the product | Feature adoption, churned against retained | Low-adoption accounts leave at first renewal |

The fastest evidence is exit surveys, clustered by topic. Then adoption depth and health-score history: a sudden exit suggests an external trigger such as price or a competitor; a gradual decline suggests product fit.

Interventions by cause. Price: a save offer at renewal for at-risk accounts. Low adoption: proactive outreach 60 to 90 days before renewal. Competitive: battle cards and CSM training.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Splits voluntary from involuntary churn
- Segments by cohort and tenure, and finds renewal as the trigger point
- Uses exit-survey clustering as the fastest path, and adoption depth as the predictive health signal

**Where answers fail.**

- "Maybe the product got worse," with no segmentation or evidence
- "Offer more discounts," with no diagnosis

**If they push.**

- *"45% of exit surveys cite pricing. Price sensitivity, or a price-value mismatch?"* Look at usage. Heavy users who left would likely stay for a discount; light users don't see the value, and a discount only delays them.
- *"Accounts with 5+ features churn at 0.3%; under 3 features, 7.2%."* Adoption becomes the Q4 priority for product and CS. I'd test causation with an onboarding experiment, since committed customers may simply adopt more.

### Q60. Facebook's ad click-through rate dropped 18% for video ads specifically. Diagnose.

*Root cause analysis · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you treat CTR as a ratio whose numerator or denominator can move, separate real behavior from measurement, and think about advertisers.

**Clarify first.**

- Absolute clicks, or clicks per impression? I'll assume the rate.
- All video placements, or one; since when? Unknown, so I'll segment by placement and date it.

**Outline.**

- CTR is a ratio: which side moved?
- Check click logging by platform
- Segment by placement, platform, geography
- View rate against CTR: content or call-to-action?
- Chain: auction, render, play, click capture, attribution
- Internal changes; external: creative, privacy changes
- Advertiser communication

**Model answer.**

CTR is clicks over impressions, so first: which side moved? If impressions rose about 22% with flat clicks, CTR falls 18% and nothing is broken; we showed more ads for the same clicks. If clicks fell on steady impressions, engagement really dropped.

Second, is it measurement? I'd confirm click logging didn't change, and compare iOS with Android. A logging bug or a privacy-driven tracking change can remove clicks from the data while behavior stays the same.

Then I segment by placement: feed, Stories, Reels, in-stream, Messenger. One placement down is local; all down is systemic.

A useful split is view rate, the share who watch more than half the video, against CTR. View rate steady with CTR down: people watch but don't click, so look at the call-to-action button. Both down: the videos themselves engage less.

The chain is auction, video render, play start, click capture, attribution.

| Hypothesis | Test | What it would show |
|---|---|---|
| Feed change reduced autoplay prominence | Autoplay rate over the period | View rate and CTR fall together in feed |
| App update moved or shrank the CTA button | Release log for the player | View rate steady, CTR down |
| Clicks not registering on one platform | CTR on iOS against Android | One platform down, the other steady |
| Policy restricted a clickable ad category | Impression mix by category | Mix shifts toward low-CTR ads |
| Creative quality fell after a spec change | CTR by creative age and format | New assets underperform |

The button case can be good news: fewer accidental taps means better clicks, but it reads as a drop.

For advertisers: if an app-side change reduced clickability, tell major video advertisers what happened and when it will be fixed. If it's tracking, say that reported CTR understates real engagement.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Separates a rate drop from an absolute drop in clicks
- Compares view rate with CTR to split engagement from call-to-action design
- Raises tracking as a cause that changes measured CTR, not actual behavior

**Where answers fail.**

- "Video ads got less relevant," with no decomposition and no tracking check
- "Improve the creative," with no diagnosis

**If they push.**

- *"The drop is entirely in Reels video ads; feed is unchanged."* Something specific to Reels: a player or CTA layout change, a change in ad position, or a shift in who watches. I'd read the Reels release log first.
- *"An advertiser says the CTR drop cost them $500K in attributed revenue."* Share the root cause and timeline plainly, help them re-read the period with corrected data, and agree a make-good with sales if our change caused it.

### Q61. A mobile gaming app's Day 7 retention dropped from 35% to 19% after a monetization update. Diagnose.

*Root cause analysis · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you read the shape of the retention curve to find which monetization change did the damage, and weigh revenue against lifetime value.

**Clarify first.**

- What changed: interstitial ads, purchase prompts, content gates? I'll assume several at once.
- A/B test or full rollout; anything else in the release? I'll assume a full rollout, and check the release notes.

**Outline.**

- Size it: about 46% fewer users reach day 7
- Confirm causation: control group or cohort comparison
- Daily curve, D1 to D7: where does it bend?
- Each monetization change has a signature
- Tie churn day to progression level; split free from paying
- Decide on revenue per install and LTV

**Model answer.**

From 35% to 19% means about 46% fewer players reach day 7, the threshold for long-term engagement and lifetime value. The monetization update is the prime suspect, but which change, and how?

First, causation. With an A/B test, compare control and treatment. With a full rollout, compare the cohort installed after the update with the cohort from the two weeks before, matching days of the week. I'd also check what else the release changed.

Then the shape of the curve. If D1 also fell, say from 65% to 40%, players leave on day one: monetization hits before they've had fun. If D1 holds and D3 to D7 fall, they enjoyed day one and hit something later.

| Change | Predicted pattern | Test |
|---|---|---|
| Interstitial ads more frequent or badly timed, such as right after a loss | D1 drops at once | When ads fire within a session |
| Soft gate at a progression point: watch a video or pay to continue | D3 to D4 drop as the average player reaches it | Progression level of churners, by day |
| Hard paywall | A cliff at that point | Free users stop there |
| Purchase prompts every session instead of every third | Gradual decline across D2 to D7 | Prompt frequency per session |

So I'd pull daily retention for both cohorts, check event logs for when triggers fire relative to progress, and compute the average level of churners in each day bucket. If D3 churners are all at level 5, there is a gate at level 5. I'd split free from paying users too.

Then the decision. Aggressive monetization can raise short-term revenue from payers, so I'd model revenue per install under old and new settings, including the retention difference. More revenue per install despite lower retention could be acceptable. But losing nearly half of day-7 players is almost certainly negative for lifetime value, so I'd expect to dial back the change the curve points to.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Reads retention day by day to find the trigger point
- Links each monetization type (interstitial, soft gate, hard paywall) to a predicted pattern
- Compares LTV or revenue per install to judge the trade-off

**Where answers fail.**

- "Monetization is too aggressive," without saying which change, or where the curve breaks
- Removing all monetization: ignores the business trade-off

**If they push.**

- *"D1 is unchanged at 68%, but D4 fell from 40% to 21%."* Not the early ads. Players hit something around day 3 or 4, most likely a content gate at the level the average player reaches then.

### Q62. A digital lending app's loan approval rate dropped from 65% to 38% after a new credit model was deployed. Diagnose.

*Root cause analysis · Stretch · Tailored*

**What they are testing.** Whether you ask "intended or not?" first, split a score shift from a threshold shift, and bring in fairness and regulation. Stories to draw on: S7 (risk scoring with human review), S6 (churn propensity model).

**Clarify first.**

- Full rollout or A/B test? I'll assume full rollout.
- What changed: features, training data, or the threshold? Unknown; I'll separate them.

**Outline.**

- Size it: 27 points, 42% relative
- Intended tightening or error? Compare with validation
- Check inputs: feature completeness and ranges
- Segment: score band, income, geography, product
- Split score shift from threshold shift
- Internal causes; external: applicant mix
- Mitigation; fairness and regulatory check

**Model answer.**

That is a 27-point drop, 42% relative. It could be a correctly tighter model, which is good if defaults were too high, or an over-restrictive one rejecting good borrowers. So first: was this intended?

I'd compare with pre-deployment results. If the holdout set showed about 40% approval, 38% in production is the design. If it showed 63%, production has a bug or the population has shifted.

Next, the inputs. I'd check completeness and value ranges for every feature since deployment, because a null or mis-scaled input lowers scores silently.

Then I segment by bureau score band, income band, geography and loan product. A drop in one band suggests the cutoff there changed. A drop concentrated in smaller cities may mean a feature is a proxy for a protected characteristic: a fairness problem.

I also split score from threshold. Approval falls because scores shifted down, a data or feature issue, or because the cutoff rose, a business decision.

| Hypothesis | Test | What it would show |
|---|---|---|
| Feature data incomplete or stale: say an income API returning nulls | Completeness per feature, by day | Null rate up from deployment |
| Distribution shift: trained on older applicants | Training against recent distributions (KS test or PSI) | Current applicants look different |
| Feature bug, such as months computed as days | Production feature ranges against expected | One feature far out of range |
| Threshold set too conservatively | The risk team's threshold decision | Scores match validation; cutoff higher |
| Riskier applicants from a campaign | Average bureau score, now against prior | Applicant mix changed |

If the model is over-rejecting: send applicants just below the cutoff to manual review now, then recalibrate the threshold on live data and track defaults against predictions.

Finally, regulation. A drop concentrated in one group invites fair-lending scrutiny (ECOA in the US, the RBI's Fair Practices Code in India), so I'd run a disparate impact analysis in parallel.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Separates intended tightening from an unintended error
- Names feature completeness as the likeliest technical failure, and distribution shift as the model-quality hypothesis
- Runs a disparate impact analysis, with a manual review queue as immediate mitigation

**Where answers fail.**

- "The model is too strict," without asking whether it's a bug or a threshold choice
- Lowering the threshold to restore approvals, with no view on defaults

**If they push.**

- *"Female applicants' approval rate is 22% lower under the new model, against 14% under the old."* A compliance issue now. Find which features drive the gap, test whether risk justifies them, remove proxies, and involve legal.

### Q63. Meta's News Feed load time increased by 800ms (from 1.2s to 2.0s median). Diagnose.

*Root cause analysis · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you decompose load time by layer, and segment by device and network, where the same regression lands very differently.

**Clarify first.**

- Median, p95, or both? I'll assume both, and compare.
- Which layer: server, network transfer, or client rendering? Unknown; that is my first pull.

**Outline.**

- Size it (67% slower); confirm measurement is unchanged
- Load time = TTFB + transfer + client render
- Segment: device tier, geography, network type
- Pipeline: API call, ranking, payload, parse, render
- Internal changes; external: CDN, third-party calls
- Optimize the layer that moved

**Model answer.**

From 1.2 to 2.0 seconds is 67% slower at the median, and latency costs engagement, so this matters. After confirming the measurement itself hasn't changed, I decompose.

Load time is time to first byte, which is the server; plus network transfer, which is payload size; plus client rendering: parsing, layout, image decode. A waterfall, this period against the prior one, shows which moved. TTFB up 600 ms with rendering steady is a server regression. Transfer up means the payload grew. Rendering up means a costly client change.

Then I segment. Device tier: a heavier client hits low-memory Android phones hardest. Geography: a bigger payload hurts most on slow networks. Network type: if only cellular is affected, it's payload, not server.

The pipeline: the app calls the feed service, the server fetches ranked content and builds the payload, and the client parses it and renders each post.

| Hypothesis | Test | What it would show |
|---|---|---|
| Ranking service added signals or a model step | Server latency dashboard for ranking | TTFB up on all devices |
| Richer content format grew the payload | Average payload size, now against prior | Transfer up, worst on cellular |
| Heavier client components | Render profiling by device tier | Render up, worst on low-end devices |
| CDN configuration or provider issue | CDN latency, two weeks | Static assets slower in some regions |
| Third-party call (ads, shopping) blocks the feed | Response times of integrated services | The feed waits on one dependency |

Payload growth is my leading suspect on mobile; it's the most common cause.

Optimization follows the layer. Server: cache, compute fewer signals per request, speed up inference. Payload: compress harder and lazy-load below the fold. Client: profile the costliest components and lazy-load images. CDN: fix the configuration or switch provider in affected regions.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Decomposes load time into TTFB, transfer and render
- Segments by device tier and geography, and sees that low-end Android and slow networks suffer most
- Names payload size as the most common cause of mobile load regressions

**Where answers fail.**

- "The feed is slow, optimize the server," with no decomposition or segmentation
- "Show fewer posts," with no diagnosis

**If they push.**

- *"It's 1.5 seconds slower on Android devices under 3GB of RAM, and 200 ms on flagships."* That points to client rendering or memory pressure. Low-end devices get priority: a lighter render path for that tier, and a performance budget tested on those phones.
- *"It correlates with last week's in-feed video autoplay launch. Roll back or optimize?"* Compare engagement gained with engagement lost to latency, by device tier. I'd switch it off on low-end devices and slow networks now, and optimize.

### Q64. An HR SaaS product's data export feature is timing out for 30% of enterprise customers. Diagnose.

*Root cause analysis · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you find the threshold behind a failure, and see the architectural cause (a synchronous design) instead of patching the symptom.

**Clarify first.**

- What does "timing out" mean: an HTTP timeout, or a download that never finishes? I'll assume the request times out.
- Are the 30% the largest customers, and when did it start? Unknown; I'll segment and date it.

**Outline.**

- Why it's severe: payroll, compliance, audits
- Define the failure mode
- Segment by data volume and export type
- Map the pipeline: request, query, transform, file, download
- Internal: code and database changes; external: data growth
- Plot time against volume to find the threshold
- Async now; re-architecture after

**Model answer.**

For enterprise HR teams, exports feed payroll, compliance reports and audits. A failed export at month-end can delay payroll, so this is high severity.

First I define the failure. "Timing out" could be a synchronous request that outlasts the connection, a download that starts and never completes, or a background job that dies. I'd also confirm how the 30% is counted.

Then I segment. Are the affected customers the largest, say 100,000+ employee records? If so, it's volume: something scales with data and fails past a threshold. If they are random in size, it's something else: threading, a memory leak or intermittent infrastructure.

The pipeline: user request, backend, database query, transformation, file generation, temporary storage, download link.

| Hypothesis | Test | What it would show |
|---|---|---|
| Query does a full scan: no index on the filter fields | Query plan; execution time against volume | Time climbs steeply with rows |
| Export is synchronous and outlasts the web timeout, often 30 to 60 seconds | Timeout rate by file size | A cutover size above which it fails |
| A new export field added a join | Deployment log, 30 days | Slower from the release date |
| Database degraded: maintenance jobs, disk I/O | Database metrics, two weeks | All query types slower |
| Customers grew past the limit | Timeouts over time, per customer | A gradual onset that looks sudden |

The investigation is one chart: export jobs for 30 days, completion time against row count. A clear inflection, say everything above 50,000 rows failing, makes it a threshold problem.

Now: move large exports to asynchronous generation with an emailed download link. For customers on a payroll deadline, a customer success engineer runs the export directly.

Then re-architect: every export above 10,000 rows async; indexes on the export filter fields; batched generation for very large sets; and an alert on any job running past 45 seconds.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Identifies synchronous generation as the root of the timeout, and async export as the long-term fix
- Names the missing index as the immediate performance fix
- Segments by customer data volume to find the threshold

**Where answers fail.**

- "The server needs more capacity": misses the synchronous design
- Raising the timeout from 30 to 300 seconds: treats the symptom

**If they push.**

- *"After the switch to async, some exports take 45 minutes to arrive."* Show progress and an estimate in the product, keep small exports from queuing behind huge ones, and add workers. Much of the frustration is not knowing.

### Q65. A productivity SaaS's real-time collaboration feature (multi-user simultaneous editing) has a 12% conflict resolution error rate. Diagnose.

*Root cause analysis · Stretch*

**What they are testing.** Whether you define a fuzzy technical error precisely, and know enough about collaborative editing (OT, CRDT) to segment it and direct the investigation.

**Clarify first.**

- What is a "conflict resolution error": lost data, a wrong merge, or an error message? Unknown; I'll define it first.
- 12% of what: documents, sessions, or operations? I'll assume sessions.

**Outline.**

- Define the error (data loss, wrong merge, explicit error) and the denominator
- Map the flow: operation, server transform, broadcast, convergence
- Segment by concurrency and by operation type
- Hypotheses for OT and CRDT failures
- Replay operation logs to find the divergence; fix by cause

**Model answer.**

"Conflict resolution error" needs a definition first. It could be data loss, where an edit is silently dropped; a wrong merge, such as transposed characters or broken formatting; or an explicit error asking the user to resolve it. Silent loss is the worst; an explicit error is annoying but honest. I'd also confirm the denominator: 12% of sessions is very different from 12% of operations.

The flow: a user edits, and the client sends an operation (insert, delete, format). The server applies it using operational transformation or a CRDT, then broadcasts the transformed operation. Every client applies it, and all should converge on one state. An error is a failure to converge.

I segment two ways. By concurrency: if two-user sessions fail 2% of the time and sessions with five or more fail 15%, the algorithm is weak under concurrency. If the rate is flat, I look at operation type: if 90% of errors are on table edits, the table logic is broken. Structured content is much harder than plain text.

| Hypothesis | Test | What it would show |
|---|---|---|
| Transformation bug for one operation pair: say an insert at position 10 against a delete at position 8 | Which operation pairs precede errors | One pair dominates |
| Operations applied out of order under network jitter | Sequence and timestamps of received operations | Out-of-order arrivals before errors |
| A client diverged and sends against a stale version | Errors by client version and session length | One version, or long sessions |
| Mixed CRDT library versions after an update | Errors in mixed-version sessions | Old and new clients merge differently |

The investigation needs two sources: the operation log (client, timestamp, document version, type) and the server's snapshots. Replaying the log against snapshots shows exactly where state diverged. I'd sample 100 failing sessions and look at the operation pair before each error.

Fixes. Transformation bug: correct that function and add regression tests for every operation pair. Ordering: sequence numbers and a server buffer that re-orders before applying. Divergence: verify client state before sending.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Defines the error type precisely before diagnosing
- Maps the OT or CRDT pipeline, and segments by concurrency and operation type
- Uses operation-log replay as the method, with a transformation bug as the lead hypothesis

**Where answers fail.**

- "The concurrency algorithm has a bug," with no failure type and no method
- Making documents single-editor to avoid conflicts

**If they push.**

- *"An enterprise CTO wants a guarantee that collaboratively edited legal documents never lose data."* I wouldn't promise "never." I'd commit to what we can prove: every operation logged, version history with restore, and divergence surfaced, not hidden.

# Part IV — Strategy

## 4. Strategy questions

Strategy questions ask whether a company should enter, build, buy, partner or respond. They are scored on the quality of your reasoning more than on the verdict. The interviewer wants a clear position, a comparison of real options, an honest read of the company's assets and gaps, the main risks, and how you would know you were right. Twenty questions follow, four of them tailored to payments and Indian fintech. To practice, say your clarifying questions aloud, rehearse from the outline, then check yourself against the model answer. Figures introduced with "assume" or "say" are assumptions, not company data. Dated facts are current as of October 2026; recheck them before an interview.

### Q66. Should Google build a super-app (payments, messaging, maps, shopping) for the Indian market?

*Strategy · Stretch*

**What they are testing.** Whether you reason from Google's assets, moat and regulators to a specific recommendation, not a fashionable label.

**Clarify first.**

- From scratch, or built on Google Pay, Maps and Search? I'll assume we build on what exists.
- What is the goal? I'll assume defending daily use in India, with the Competition Commission of India (CCI) watching bundling.

**Outline.**

- Frame: do Pay, Maps and Search together beat the parts?
- For: one-app preference, UPI, WhatsApp's ambitions
- Against: integration time, antitrust
- Recommend: deep integration on open APIs, no new brand
- Why: the moat is search and AI
- Risks, metrics

**Model answer.** The question is whether combining Google Pay, Maps and Search creates more value than they create apart, or distracts from search and AI.

The case for is real. Many Indian users run entry-level phones and prefer one app to five. UPI lets any surface take a payment. WhatsApp, which I assume has several hundred million Indian users, is adding payments and business messaging, so someone else may own the daily habit. And Google's pieces fit: Search for intent, Maps for the business, Pay for the transaction.

The case against: these are separate apps, codebases and teams, and I'd assume real integration takes three to five years. And the CCI fined Google Rs 1,337.76 crore in October 2022 over Android bundling, so a bundle of Search, Pay and Maps invites another case.

So I recommend deep integration, not a new super-app brand. Pay becomes the payment layer for businesses found in Maps. Search becomes the entry point to commerce, including sellers on ONDC, India's government-backed open commerce network. Gemini is the conversational layer on top. I'd keep it open, with third-party APIs and rival payment providers allowed.

The reason is the moat. Google will not out-execute Reliance or Tata in commerce operations or beat WhatsApp on social lock-in. It can be the smartest guide to the ecosystem without owning it.

Three risks. Antitrust, which open APIs and payment choice reduce. Execution, where I'd build a shared profile and data layer before merging any interface. And inertia, so I'd lead with the use case where Google is strongest, local discovery plus payment.

I'd track users active on two or more Google services, sessions using both Maps and Pay, Google Pay's share of UPI volume, and revenue per Indian user.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Integration over a new brand, tied to the search and AI moat
- Antitrust weighed, with open APIs as mitigation
- A specific path: Pay in Maps, commerce through Search, Gemini on top

**Where answers fail.**

- "Clone WeChat" with no look at Google's assets or gaps
- A bundle with no mention of antitrust

**If they push.**

- *PhonePe forms a closed payments-and-commerce loop with Flipkart and ONDC.* That raises urgency, not direction. I'd speed up the Maps-to-Pay loop and stay open, which wins the sellers a closed rival excludes.

### Q67. Should Amazon enter the B2B SaaS market for small business HR and payroll software?

*Strategy · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Build, buy or partner logic for an adjacent market, and spotting the decisive asset (distribution) and barrier (compliance).

**Clarify first.**

- US or global, and which firms? I'll assume US firms with 10 to 100 employees.
- Are build, buy and partner all open? I'll assume yes, with Amazon Business as the lead channel.

**Outline.**

- Market: large, sticky, compliance-heavy
- Assets: Amazon Business reach, AWS, AI, trust
- Fit: procurement platform to operations platform
- Recommend: acquire, then integrate; partner if blocked
- Risks: antitrust, integration, retaliation on AWS
- Metric: cross-sell penetration

**Model answer.** I'll size it with an assumption: say US small-business HR and payroll software is about $20 billion a year, growing near 10%. Incumbents are hard to dislodge. Payroll data is sticky once employees are enrolled, and filing payroll taxes across 50 states takes specialized compliance infrastructure.

Amazon's edge is distribution. Amazon Business reports millions of business customers, many of them small, and no payroll vendor has that channel. AWS supplies the infrastructure, Amazon's AI could automate onboarding documents and compliance checks, and small businesses trust the brand.

The fit is good. Payroll is a small employer's largest recurring cost. Adding it turns Amazon Business from a procurement platform into an operations platform, which raises switching costs and revenue per customer.

ADP and Paychex hold share on older architecture; Gusto and Rippling are the modern products. It is not winner-take-all: they compete on bundles of payroll, HR, benefits and compliance.

I'd acquire, then integrate. Building would take, I'd assume, three to five years, mostly state tax filing and year-end reporting. Buying a company like Gusto brings product, customers and the compliance engine on day one. Its reported valuation has stayed above $9 billion since 2021, so this is a large deal. Amazon adds what the target lacks: a discounted bundle with Amazon Business and AWS, and faster payroll payout over its payment rails.

If a deal can't clear, I'd partner: an AWS Marketplace alliance where Amazon supplies AI infrastructure and distribution for a revenue share.

Risks. The FTC filed an antitrust suit against Amazon in September 2023, so any acquisition faces long review. Integration is real platform work. And payroll vendors that run on AWS could retaliate by moving to another cloud.

Metrics: share of Amazon Business customers who adopt payroll, share of the target segment in five years, say 5%, and payroll revenue relative to Amazon Business sales.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Distribution through Amazon Business named as the decisive asset
- Acquire over build, because compliance infrastructure is the slow part
- The retaliation risk from payroll vendors that are AWS customers

**Where answers fail.**

- "Build it" with no account of compliance or incumbent stickiness
- Entering on price alone

**If they push.**

- *Is payroll deepening Amazon Business relationships or cannibalizing them?* Compare retention and procurement spend for adopters against matched non-adopters. Deepening shows as both rising.

### Q68. Should a major Indian fintech company (like Paytm) expand into Southeast Asia?

*Strategy · Stretch · Tailored*

**What they are testing.** Market selection and entry mode under regulatory and capital limits, and exporting the capability that travels, not the home product.

**Clarify first.**

- Which markets and which product? I'll assume both are mine to choose.
- Organic, partnership or acquisition? I'll assume all are open and capital is limited.

**Outline.**

- Region: large, mobile-first, underbanked, fragmented
- What transfers: thin-file underwriting and KYC
- Market: Indonesia first, Singapore as a base
- Entry: partner with a local platform
- Product: merchant working-capital credit
- Risks, metrics

**Model answer.** Southeast Asia is the natural next market: more than 600 million people, mobile-first, and I'd assume a large share of adults in Indonesia, the Philippines and Vietnam are unbanked or underbanked. No single player dominates the way UPI apps do in India.

Indian fintechs solved the hard problems first. UPI passed 10 billion transactions a month in August 2023, and India built KYC and lending models for thin-file customers. Those capabilities travel. At Paytm I led product work that put KYB checks and risk-based transaction limits into merchant onboarding (S7), the control layer a partner in a thinner-file market lacks.

I'd pick Indonesia first: about 285 million people, roughly 65 million small businesses by the government's count, and e-commerce platforms generating merchant payment demand. Singapore is second, small but a credible regulatory base.
