That gives a first cohort of five, for example Lucknow, Jaipur, Indore, Nagpur and Coimbatore, launched 30 days apart so each city teaches the next.

Fulfillment is hybrid. Each city gets one or two small dark stores, 3,000 to 5,000 square feet, in dense residential areas for fast-moving goods, staples and fresh produce. Ten to twenty local wholesalers cover the long tail without Amazon carrying that inventory. I'd assume this roughly halves capital per city, and that dark-store items, about 85% of orders, make the two-hour promise while partner items get same-day.

Last mile uses Amazon Flex partners, whom I'd assume already deliver in these cities, with insulated bags and a 15-minute pickup window. No new fleet at launch.

| Step | Demand lever | Offer |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | App users in the city with past grocery orders | Rs 200 off the first order; free delivery on three |
| 2 | Amazon Pay | 10% cashback, funded from the payments marketing budget |
| 3 | Offline QR flyers in malls and stations | Rs 100 off, aimed at people new to Amazon |

Pricing matches local supermarkets. Delivery is Rs 25 above a Rs 299 basket and Rs 49 below it; Prime members get free delivery above Rs 199, which makes Prime matter without a cross-subsidy. The discipline: each city reaches positive contribution margin within 18 months, or its dark-store zones are consolidated.

Metrics: orders per dark store per day, 200 by month 3 and 500 by month 12; 90% of orders inside the promised window; 45% of first-time customers reordering within 14 days. Guardrail: fresh-produce spoilage under 3%, or the inventory model is reforecast.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- A multi-factor city matrix, not a list of names
- Hybrid dark-store-plus-wholesaler fulfillment as the capital-efficient model
- Flex and Prime as distribution advantages, with a contribution-margin deadline

**Where answers fail.**

- A generic grocery launch with arbitrary cities
- The app described, with no supply chain or economics

**If they push.**

- *After 90 days, city 1 has 200 orders a day and 7% spoilage; city 2 has 80 orders and 2% spoilage.* City 1 has a forecasting problem: cut fresh range and order smaller lots more often. City 2 has a demand problem: check the catchment, then push steps 2 and 3 harder.

### Q92. You are the PM for Microsoft's launch of a Copilot-powered email triage feature in Outlook for enterprise customers. Walk me through your rollout plan.

*Go-to-market and launch · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Enterprise rollout: buyer and user are different people, privacy positioning precedes demos, and adoption needs change management.

**Clarify first.**

- Is it part of the paid Microsoft 365 Copilot add-on? I'll assume yes.
- Who turns it on? I'll assume admins enable it and users can opt out, with a separate track for regulated industries.

**Outline.**

- The "AI reading my email" fear
- Four personas
- Privacy commitments before outreach
- Three phases: champions, broad release, regulated track
- Change management; opt-out guardrail

**Model answer.** In enterprise the buyer and the user are different people, adoption isn't viral, and this feature triggers a specific fear, AI reading my email: privacy worry, doubt about accuracy and fear of workflow change. The plan has to answer all three.

Four personas. The IT admin wants configuration, data residency and an audit trail. The CISO wants content kept inside the tenant boundary and nothing used to train shared models. The end user wants accuracy, control, and reassurance that triage sorts and never deletes. The executive sponsor wants hours saved per employee.

Before any external message I'd have three commitments confirmed in writing, in a one-page privacy FAQ for account teams: email is processed within the customer's tenant boundary; no email content trains shared models; and admins get granular controls, such as excluding mailboxes or anything labeled confidential, plus an exportable log of triage decisions.

| Phase | Who | How | Measure |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1, months 1 to 3 | 20 accounts in four verticals, each with a sponsor | Customer success manager, two-day workshop, 25 power users | 40% of seats active weekly by week 8 |
| 2, months 4 to 8 | All Copilot tenants | Admin opt-in, user opt-out, a three-screen first-run tour | 35% weekly active 90 days after enablement |
| 3, months 6 to 10 | Finance, healthcare, legal | E-discovery audit export, a privileged-mail exclusion flag, healthcare agreements | Compliance sign-off per industry |

The biggest risk is admins enabling it and users ignoring it, so change management is the accelerator. A weekly card shows each user the time triage saved, which builds trust in small steps. Power users from phase 1 become named internal champions. And sponsors get a one-page script that frames this as a productivity tool, not monitoring.

Metrics: weekly active rate, the share of licensed seats using triage at least once a week; response time on priority mail down 25% against control; satisfaction of 4.2 out of 5 after 30 days; above 80% of priority flags rated correct. Guardrail: opt-outs under 15% at 30 days.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Admin and end-user personas separated, with privacy positioning as a pre-launch requirement
- Champion accounts first, then a regulated-industry track
- Real change management: a visible value card and internal champions

**Where answers fail.**

- A consumer-style launch with no admin, compliance or change plan
- All personas treated as one, privacy in a sentence

**If they push.**

- *Weekly active is 18%: 60% of tenants enabled it, but under 30% of their users ever activated.* The gap is user activation, not admin enablement. Default it on where the admin allows, show the value card earlier, and run champion sessions in the largest tenants.

### Q93. You are a PM at a neobank launching a joint account product for freelancers and their business partners in Southeast Asia. Design the GTM.

*Go-to-market and launch · Standard · Tailored*

**What they are testing.** Using a product's built-in mechanics for growth, with onboarding and licensing realities handled.

**Clarify first.**

- Licensed bank, or a partnership with one? I'll assume a partnership.
- Who is the customer? I'll assume two-person freelance partnerships first.

**Outline.**

- Pain: joint accounts need an entity, a branch visit and weeks
- Structure: partner bank
- Ideal customer: creative pairs with an international client
- Growth: every account is an invite; channels
- Onboarding, pricing, metrics

**Model answer.** Two-person agencies and developer-designer pairs in Southeast Asia are badly served: a joint account usually means a registered entity, a branch visit and weeks of waiting. They need a clean way to pool project income, pay shared costs and keep records for invoicing.

On structure, I'd partner with a licensed bank and be the product layer. Depending on the market, the account is a dual-signature account or a shared e-money wallet. I'd assume that gets us live in about six months, against 18 or more for our own license. Every holder completes KYC before activation.

The primary customer is a two-person creative partnership earning $2,000 to $15,000 a month combined, with at least one international client. That client matters: they need inbound international transfers, which separates us from local neobanks. Agencies of three to five people come second.

The product has built-in virality. Every account needs two people, so every activation produces an invite. The initiator completes KYC and sends a link; the partner's flow should take under five minutes. Both get a small credit after the first incoming payment.

Channels, by intent. Freelance marketplaces, global and regional, where a placement on team-billing pages reaches people who already share a client. Freelancer communities; in the Philippines I'd assume Facebook groups lead. And invoicing tools, which can recommend the account to users with two team members.

Onboarding has two friction points, and it is where I'd spend design time; I worked on Paytm's migration to a custom merchant onboarding engine and on KYB checks inside onboarding (S2, S7). Joint KYC: the account is pending until the partner finishes, with two push reminders and a WhatsApp message after 24 hours. First use: a first-client-payment wizard creates a payment link to share, cutting time to first payment from days to minutes.

Pricing: free with five shared transactions a month and a card each; premium at about SGD 12, MYR 25 or PHP 300 a month for unlimited transactions, virtual cards, international transfers in and expense tagging by project.

Metrics: both KYCs done within 72 hours on 65% of initiated accounts; median time to first payment under five days; 55% of accounts transacting in month 3; invite acceptance at 70%; premium conversion of 18% by day 60.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- The joint account's structural virality turned into the acquisition loop
- A sequential KYC flow designed around the pending state
- Freelance platforms as the highest-intent channel, with specific retention metrics

**Where answers fail.**

- "Social media and partnerships", with no regulatory structure
- Product design with no acquisition plan or metrics

**If they push.**

- *Partner KYC completion is 40%, not 65%.* Find where partners drop, then let the initiator see status and nudge, shorten the partner flow, and show the partner the pending client payment as the reason to finish.

### Q94. Apple is launching a health data marketplace that sells anonymized, aggregated Apple Watch data to pharmaceutical research institutions. Give your GTM and ethics framework.

*Go-to-market and launch · Stretch*

**What they are testing.** Whether ethics leads: consent, anonymization and governance designed before the business model.

**Clarify first.**

- Is consent opt-in per research use, or on by default? I'll assume opt-in and granular.
- Has legal cleared this under health privacy law? I'll assume not yet, so it gates each region.

**Outline.**

- The tension with Apple's privacy brand
- Ethics first: consent, anonymization, use limits, value sharing
- Supply and demand sides
- Governance: an independent board
- Phases by region; revocation as the trust signal

**Model answer.** Apple holds a very large body of longitudinal wearable data: heart rate, ECG, sleep, activity. Research institutions spend heavily to recruit and monitor patients, so the logic is clear. But selling data, even aggregated, cuts against Apple's privacy brand. So the ethics framework comes before any business design, and if it can't be met, we don't launch.

Four principles. Consent is opt-in and granular: a user can join cardiovascular research and not oncology, and can revoke at any time. Revoking stops future use; datasets already licensed can't be recalled, and we say so up front. Anonymization: cohort-level data only, minimum cohort 5,000, differential privacy with epsilon at or below 1, geography generalized to county and age to five-year bands. Use limits: research and regulatory submissions only, never underwriting, employment screening or advertising, enforced by contract with heavy damages and audited access logs. Value sharing: 25% of licensing revenue goes to a fund for health-access programs.

On supply, users with six months or more of Watch data see a four-screen explanation in the Health app and an explicit tap to enroll, never a pre-checked box. Enrollees get a monthly personal health-trends report, so the value is more than altruism.

On demand, I'd start with 20 pharmaceutical and academic institutions. Each needs ethics-board approval for its protocol, a signed data use agreement with audit rights, a named data protection lead and an annual third-party audit. Access is through a logged query API with no bulk download.

Governance: an independent nine-member ethics board reviews each new use case before release. Its recommendations are published, and Apple commits to follow them unless the law prevents it.

| Phase | Months | Scope |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | 1 to 12 | US; 20 institutions; first five datasets; transparency report at month 6 |
| 2 | 13 to 24 | EU, after special-category data review under GDPR |
| 3 | 25 and on | Asia-Pacific, country by country |

Metrics: 10 million enrolled across the US and EU by the end of phase 2, which is 8% of an assumed 125 million eligible wearers; institution satisfaction; papers published; 85% still enrolled at 12 months; and revocation under 5% a year, my best trust signal.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Ethics framework before business model, with a willingness not to launch
- Granular opt-in consent and specific anonymization parameters
- An independent board and transparency reporting built into the sequence

**Where answers fail.**

- "Users can turn it off in settings" as the whole privacy design
- Ethics as a closing paragraph

**If they push.**

- *A researcher needs data on a rare condition, and your 5,000 minimum makes the cohort too small.* I don't lower the floor. I'd offer a consented study that invites affected users directly, with its own ethics approval.

### Q95. LinkedIn is launching in-feed job suggestions based on a user's posts and engagement patterns, not just their stated job title. What is your launch plan?

*Go-to-market and launch · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Launching an inference-based feature whose main risk is a privacy harm to the user, with trust before monetization.

**Clarify first.**

- Opt-in, or shown by default? I'll assume opt-in at launch.
- Which signals? I'll assume public posts and engagement only, never private messages.

**Outline.**

- Value: revealed interest beats stated title
- Core risk: the employer finds out
- Privacy design: opt-in, visibility guarantee, "why this job?", exclusions
- Three phases, monetization last
- Recruiter bright line; metrics; guardrail

**Model answer.** Job discovery on LinkedIn is mostly reactive: you search, or a recruiter finds you. Suggestions based on revealed interest change that. Someone posting about machine learning may want an AI role even if the profile says senior software engineer. That widens the funnel for recruiters and shows members roles they wouldn't have searched for.

The risk is specific. The feature infers career intent from behavior, and the fear is that an employer learns I'm looking. The design has to answer that directly.

Three privacy decisions. Opt-in at launch, with a prompt that says what is used and that your current employer can't see you've enabled it. That guarantee is enforced technically: activity from suggestions is excluded from signals shown to recruiters at the member's own company, similar to LinkedIn's recruiter-only open-to-work setting. Second, transparency: each card has a "why this job?" line, such as "based on your recent posts on machine learning", which also lets users correct us. Third, control: users can exclude topics and block companies.

Signals come in three layers: topics of posts and articles, engagement with job-related content, and skills and course completions. They feed a relevance model separate from the ads auction. At launch these are organic suggestions, not paid placements.

| Phase | Weeks | Who | Gate |
|---|---|---|---|
| Alpha | 1 to 6 | 50,000 members who viewed a job in the last 30 days and post often | Application rate 20% above organic browsing; over 65% thumbs-up |
| Opt-in rollout | 7 to 16 | All members, prompt prioritized to likely movers | 15% opt-in within 90 days of the prompt; zero employer-visibility incidents |
| Monetization | 17 to 26 | Employers opt listings in, labeled "Featured", priced per application at a premium | Trust metrics hold |

On the recruiter side, no company can ever query which of its employees enabled the feature. That is a bright line, written into recruiter terms and blocked in the data model.

Metrics: opt-in rate; application rate per impression against organic browsing, 25% higher at scale; thumbs-up above 65%; then offer rate and a trust survey. Employer-visibility incidents: zero. Guardrail: opt-outs under 20% at 30 days.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Leads with the employer-visibility risk and technical safeguards
- Opt-in with a "why this job?" explanation
- High-intent alpha first; recruiter-side rule stated

**Where answers fail.**

- A recommendation-model answer where privacy is "users can opt out"
- No phasing and no recruiter-side rule

**If they push.**

- *Opt-in is 8% at 90 days; people see the prompt and decline.* Either the employer line raises a fear it doesn't settle, the value is abstract, or the timing is wrong. Test a preview of three real suggestions before the ask, new wording, and prompting after a job view.

### Q96. A startup is building an AI agent that collects income data, categorizes expenses, computes GST and files returns for Indian freelancers. Design the beta-to-production GTM.

*Go-to-market and launch · Stretch · Tailored*

**What they are testing.** Taking an agent that performs a hard-to-reverse action from beta to production: staged autonomy, error thresholds, liability.

**Clarify first.**

- Does the agent file through a licensed GST Suvidha Provider? I'll assume yes.
- Who is liable for a wrong return? I'll assume the user is the legal filer and we indemnify our own errors.

**Outline.**

- Trust is the go-to-market problem
- Three autonomy stages
- Beta: accountant shadow review, an error bar
- Liability: a capped indemnity
- Channels; production criteria; guardrail

**Model answer.** GST-registered freelancers file GSTR-1 and GSTR-3B returns, monthly or quarterly depending on turnover. I'd assume most pay an accountant or lose hours a month to data entry.

The central problem is trust. A wrong return brings notices, interest and penalties. So autonomy is staged, on the principle I held for the merchant operations assistant at Paytm (S8): the model prepares and explains, and a deterministic step and a named person keep authority over the financial action.

| Stage | What the agent does |
|---|---|
| Assist, in beta | Prepares the return; the user reviews a summary and taps submit |
| Supervised, first production release | WhatsApp summary with 24 hours to approve; files on no response only after three clean supervised filings |
| Autonomous, second release, opt-in | Files without review for users with six clean months and liability under Rs 50,000; confirms within an hour |

I'd assume filing without a fresh confirmation needs an authorized-representative arrangement cleared by counsel; otherwise stage two keeps explicit approval.

Beta is about error rate, with 500 GST-registered freelancers. For three months a chartered accountant partner shadow-reviews every return before the user submits, and any line differing by more than Rs 500 is shown to the user. The bar is under 0.5% of returns with such a variance across three filing cycles.

The user stays the legal filer and signs an authorization. We indemnify interest and penalties caused by our computation errors, up to Rs 25,000 a filing, backed by insurance. Wrong data from the user is not covered.

Channels. Freelance platforms, with the offer timed to the days before a deadline; for monthly filers GSTR-3B is due on the 20th. WhatsApp: a freelancer sends invoice PDFs to a number and preparation starts. And accountants as partners, with a 20% revenue share on referred clients.

Production starts when: error rate under 0.5% for three cycles, satisfaction above 4.4 out of 5, the insurance policy in force, the filing provider's uptime confirmed at 99.5% in the 72 hours before deadlines, and the accountant review contract signed.

Metrics: 90% of started returns filed and median time to file under 15 minutes. Guardrail: zero penalty notices caused by agent error in the first six months.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Staged autonomy with entry conditions
- Accountant shadow review in beta, and an insured indemnity as the trust mechanism
- Provider uptime at deadline peaks as a production criterion

**Where answers fail.**

- "We'll test with some users", and no liability framework
- A capability tour with generic channels

**If they push.**

- *The agent filed a GSTR-1 overstating taxable income by Rs 3.2 lakh because of a duplicate invoice.* Tell the user at once, file the amendment, cover interest or penalty under the indemnity, pause auto-filing for affected users, and ship a duplicate-detection fix.

### Q97. Spotify is launching "Your Daily Briefing", an AI-generated 10-minute audio summary of news tailored to each user's listening history. Walk me through the GTM.

*Go-to-market and launch · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Launching generative content: licensing as a precondition, accuracy controls as product constraints, and staged markets.

**Clarify first.**

- Where does content come from? I'll assume licensed publishers and Spotify's catalog only, never the open web.
- Who gets it first? I'll assume Premium users in English-language markets.

**Outline.**

- Opportunity: the daily briefing, personalized
- Licensing first
- Accuracy: source-bound generation, traceable claims, sensitive-topic gate
- Three phases by language
- One labeled sponsor slot; accuracy guardrail

**Model answer.** Spotify reported 777 million monthly users in mid-2026, and daily news briefings are among the most durable podcast formats. But they are made by hand, to one editorial agenda. A personalized briefing gives each listener ten minutes on the subjects they follow.

Licensing decides whether this exists. Summarizing journalism without a license is a live legal risk: the New York Times sued OpenAI and Microsoft in December 2023, and that case is still in court as far as I know. So I'd run two tracks: per-listen deals with 20 to 30 news organizations, which changes the story from "AI making things up" to "AI adapting licensed journalism", and Spotify's own podcast catalog, where the briefing can summarize an episode and link to it.

Accuracy is a product constraint, not a later fix. The model generates only from licensed source text. Every claim traces to a source, and a Sources card after the briefing lists the five to eight articles used. Listeners can flag a claim, and flags reach review within four hours. Elections, medical news and markets get an extra editorial gate and stay close to wire copy.

The first segment is Premium users aged 25 to 45 who play ten or more podcast episodes a month, because personalization needs their listening history.

| Phase | When | Scope | Gate |
|---|---|---|---|
| Alpha | 8 weeks | 50,000 Premium users in the US and UK, enrolled with opt-out | Over 60% finish at least 80% of the briefing; over 75% thumbs-up |
| English launch | Weeks 9 to 18 | US, UK, Canada, Australia; three narrator voices; Sources card; licensing partners announced | Accuracy guardrail holds |
| Other languages | Months 5 to 12 | Spanish and Portuguese first, then German, French, Japanese | Local licensing in place |

Monetization arrives in phase 2: one labeled 30-second sponsor message at the midpoint, limited to brand-safe categories and sold at a premium.

Metrics: weekly active listeners, 5 million at six months; completion above 60%; thumbs-up above 75%; then 8% of listeners tapping through to a full episode, and a 30% premium on the sponsor rate. Guardrail: accuracy complaints under 0.5% of briefings played, or the generation model is audited at once.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Licensing treated as non-negotiable and put first
- Hallucination controls: source-bound generation, traceable claims, gates on sensitive topics
- Markets sequenced by language, with a sponsor slot matched to the audience

**Where answers fail.**

- A personalization answer where licensing is "we'll need partnerships"
- No mention of accuracy as a constraint

**If they push.**

- *Completion is 42%, and 65% of drop-offs come at minute four.* Check what plays then: the sponsor slot, a topic switch or a weak third story. Test moving the sponsor message, reordering by predicted interest, and a shorter default.

### Q98. You are the PM for an Alexa skill that books restaurant reservations autonomously, handling confirmation, waitlists and cancellation reminders. Design the GTM and launch plan.

*Go-to-market and launch · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Launching an agent that acts for the user: borrowed supply, confirmation design that prevents costly mistakes, a metric for unintended actions.

**Clarify first.**

- Existing reservation platforms, or a proprietary network? I'll assume partner platforms.
- Who pays if Alexa books something the user didn't intend? I'll assume that is mine to design.

**Outline.**

- Opportunity: the hands-busy moment
- Supply: an anchor partner, then two more
- Activation: discovery and trust
- Confirmation design: read-back, summary, reminder, liability
- Three phases by metro; unintended-booking guardrail

**Model answer.** Voice bookings are a small share of reservations today. The opening is the moment when you're cooking or driving, think "we should book dinner", and won't open an app. Alexa already sits in the kitchen.

On supply, I'd borrow it. OpenTable is the anchor: it offers real-time availability, confirmation and cancellation, and it was a named Alexa+ partner at the February 2025 launch. I'd add Resy for premium restaurants and SevenRooms for hotel dining, aiming for near-complete coverage in major US metros. Each deal has its own politics: Resy belongs to American Express, and DoorDash agreed to buy SevenRooms in 2025.

Activation has two gaps: people don't know Alexa can do this, and they don't trust it to book the right thing. For discovery I'd use a card in the Alexa app with an example phrase, a spoken hint for users who asked about restaurants in the last 30 days, and a co-branded email to the partner's users.

Trust comes from confirmation design, because a wrong booking can cost a no-show fee. Four rules. Alexa reads back restaurant, date, time and party size and needs an explicit yes; that step is never skipped where a cancellation fee applies. A push notification follows at once with details and a cancel link. A reminder 24 hours ahead, by voice and push, includes a spoken cancel option. And the terms are clear: after explicit confirmation, the user is responsible for the restaurant's cancellation policy.

| Phase | When | Scope | Gate |
|---|---|---|---|
| Alpha | 6 weeks | 10,000 Echo users in New York, San Francisco and Chicago; OpenTable only | Over 75% of started conversations end in a booking; unintended bookings under 1% |
| Launch | Weeks 7 to 16 | Top 20 US metros; second partner; saved dining preferences; a partner-funded Prime perk | 80% completion; unintended under 0.5% |
| Expansion | Months 5 to 12 | Rest of the US, then UK and Canada | Supply coverage validated per market |

Metrics: booking completion, rebooking within 30 days at 40%, and unintended-booking rate. Then time to confirmation under 45 seconds, and cancellation rate against the partner's baseline, since a higher rate signals regret. Guardrail: complaints about unintended bookings under 0.1% of bookings.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Two-step spoken confirmation treated as non-optional
- A three-platform supply strategy that removes the cold start
- A reminder with voice cancel, and a clear liability position

**Where answers fail.**

- Supply as "we'll integrate with restaurant APIs"
- No error handling and no launch sequence

**If they push.**

- *Alpha unintended bookings are 2.8%, mostly distracted users saying "yes".* A bare yes is too weak. Require a repeat-back phrase such as "book it", add a ten-second undo, and hold any fee-bearing booking for app confirmation.

### Q99. A health ministry is deploying a WhatsApp AI chatbot that triages rural patients to emergency care, clinic visit or home management. Design the launch plan.

*Go-to-market and launch · Stretch*

**What they are testing.** Launching a safety-critical AI service to a vulnerable population: narrow scope, conservative outputs, validation first, trust through people.

**Clarify first.**

- Who approves the clinical content? I'll assume the ministry's clinical board.
- Is a human available? I'll assume nurse helplines and community health workers.

**Outline.**

- Need: is this serious enough to travel?
- Scope: four symptom areas
- Safety rules: three outputs, red flags, a human available
- Regulation: device class, validation study, data protection
- Trust through health workers; escalation; guardrail

**Model answer.** In rural India I'd assume doctors are far scarcer than the WHO benchmark and the nearest clinic can be hours away. So the patient's first decision is whether to travel, made today with no information. WhatsApp reaches shared family phones with nothing to install.

Scope is the key decision, and it is narrow. At launch: fever and respiratory symptoms, diarrhea and dehydration, chest pain, and third-trimester pregnancy symptoms. Excluded: mental health, skin conditions and chronic disease management.

Three safety rules. Only three outputs: go to emergency now, visit a clinic within 24 to 48 hours, or manage at home with instructions and the warning signs that mean go in. It never says "do not seek care." Any red-flag pattern, such as chest pain with breathlessness or high fever in an infant under three months, triggers the emergency output with no further questions. And "talk to a nurse" is always available.

On regulation, I'd assume this is software as a medical device under India's Medical Devices Rules, 2017. Before public use it needs a validation study on 500 physician-triaged cases from rural clinic records, with sensitivity above 95% for emergencies. Under the Digital Personal Data Protection Act, 2023, nothing is kept beyond the session without informed consent.

Users reach it through a published number or QR codes at clinics, in Hindi, Tamil, Telugu, Bengali or Marathi. Voice notes are transcribed for low-literacy users, and replies use plain language.

Adoption fails on distrust more than awareness, so activation runs through people. ASHA workers, about a million community health workers, get four hours of training and a reference card. Clinics post the code with a doctor's endorsement, and village councils are briefed.

On an emergency output, the bot creates a referral message summarizing symptoms, offers one-tap dialing of the 108 ambulance line where it operates, and, with consent, alerts the local health-worker coordinator.

Rollout: three pilot districts for three months, one state in months 4 to 12, then national.

Metrics: emergency sensitivity above 95%; over 80% of chats reaching a recommendation; 60% of pilot-district ASHA workers assisting a patient in month one. False escalations up to 15% are acceptable; over-referral is the safer error. Guardrail: zero serious adverse events within 48 hours attributable to a home-management recommendation.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Narrow clinical scope, with sensitivity above 95% as a hard gate
- Community health workers as the trust channel; voice input for low literacy
- An escalation path that generates a referral message

**Where answers fail.**

- Safety as "it will recommend a doctor for serious things"
- No validation study, regulator or community plan

**If they push.**

- *WhatsApp use is 40% in one district, but 15% and 8% in the others.* Change the channel there: assisted use on the ASHA worker's phone, and a voice line running the same triage logic.

### Q100. A payments company is launching a real-time cross-border remittance product for the India-US corridor, competing with Western Union, Wise and Remitly. Design the GTM strategy.

*Go-to-market and launch · Stretch · Tailored*

**What they are testing.** A regulated cross-border launch: positioning beyond price, segments matched to channels, licensing as the critical path.

**Clarify first.**

- How are we licensed? I'll assume we need US money transmitter coverage and an Indian bank partner.
- Can receivers be paid to a UPI ID? I'll assume yes, through that bank.

**Outline.**

- Corridor size and sender profile
- Positioning: guaranteed real-time UPI credit
- Licensing on both sides
- Three sender segments and their channels
- Receiver experience, pricing, phases, guardrail

**Model answer.** India received about $119 billion in remittances in 2023-24, and RBI's survey puts the US share at 27.7%, roughly $33 billion. Senders are mostly professionals and students in tight community networks; I'd assume $1,500 to $3,000 a transfer.

Wise competes on transparent pricing, Remitly on promotions, Western Union on cash pickup. Our position is guaranteed real-time credit to the receiver's UPI ID. I'd assume rivals already deliver many India transfers in minutes, so speed alone needs verifying. The claim to own is the guarantee.

Licensing is the critical path. In the US: registration as a money services business with FinCEN, and money transmitter licenses in about ten states with the largest Indian-American populations; I'd assume 6 to 12 months. In India: RBI's Rupee Drawing Arrangement, where an authorized bank holds our pre-funded rupee account and credits receivers, which also avoids per-transfer wire fees.

Two parts are familiar from Paytm: I launched RuPay credit card journeys on UPI rails with acquiring banks and the card network (S5), and led product work on AML screening in merchant onboarding (S7).

Three sender segments, each with a channel. H-1B professionals sending monthly family support: large employers offering it as a benefit, plus community creators, say $200,000 across 20 partnerships. Students paying tuition: Indian student associations at 50 universities. Indian-Americans sending for festivals and emergencies: referrals, with a free first transfer up to $500 for both sides.

The receiver gets a WhatsApp message with amount sent, rate and rupees received. Pricing: no fee above $500 with a 0.5% spread over mid-market, and $2 flat below that. I'd check that against live incumbent quotes.

| Phase | Months | Scope | Gate |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 1 to 3 | California and New Jersey; first 1,000 users | Completion above 92%; UPI credit success above 99% |
| 2 | 4 to 9 | All licensed states; referrals; employers | 55% send again within 90 days |
| 3 | 10 to 18 | Scheduled and payday transfers | 100,000 active senders; $1.5 billion annualized |

At that volume a 0.5% spread is about $7.5 million a year, so retention and acquisition cost decide viability. Guardrail: suspicious-activity filings are a signal, never a number to minimize; a spike above 0.1% of transfers triggers a KYC review.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Real-time UPI delivery as the positioning, not price alone
- Three sender segments with channel-specific acquisition
- Licensing as a pre-launch constraint; the pre-funded rupee account as cost enabler

**Where answers fail.**

- "We're cheaper", with no regulatory structure or segmentation
- Features with no acquisition plan

**If they push.**

- *Pilot UPI credit success is 94%, with failures at one partner bank in peak hours.* Add automatic fallback to a bank-account credit, route around that bank at peak, and hold expansion until success is above 99%.

# Part VI — Estimation

## 6. Estimation questions

Estimation questions test whether you can turn an open question into a chain of stated assumptions, do the arithmetic cleanly, and judge your own answer. The structure matters more than the number, but a number that is off by ten times with no instinct for it fails. Each answer here states the approach in one sentence, lays the chain out as a table, and ends with a range and a check from a different direction. Rehearse by covering the table and rebuilding it from the outline. Inputs are assumptions unless a source is named. Named figures were checked against public sources in October 2026 and will age, so refresh them before an interview. Five questions are marked Tailored: they sit in payments, India and AI, where you have an edge.

### Q101. Estimate the number of Google Maps queries made per day in India.

*Estimation · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you can turn a vague unit ("query") into something countable, segment users by behavior instead of using one average, and check the result against an independent anchor.

**Clarify first.**

- What counts as a query: a search, a directions request, or any map load? Assume searches and directions requests inside a session.
- Direct Google Maps use only, or map calls made by other apps too? Assume both, reported separately.
- How precise? Assume the right order of magnitude with a stated range.

**Outline.**

- Smartphone users, then regular app users
- Monthly Maps users, then daily users
- Daily users split by session type, each with its own query count
- Map calls embedded in ride-hail, delivery and commerce apps
- Total with a range
- Sanity check against Google's global Maps user base

**Model answer.** I'll go bottom-up: daily Maps users in India, times queries per session by type of use, plus the map calls other apps make on Google's platform.

| Step | Assumption | Result |
|---|---|---|
| Smartphone users | About half of 1.46 billion people | 750M |
| Regular app users | 80% use apps at least weekly | 600M |
| Monthly Maps users | 80%: preinstalled on Android, no strong rival | 480M |
| Daily Maps users | Used about 3 days in 7 | About 200M |
| Navigation sessions | 35% of daily users, 3 queries each | 210M |
| Local discovery | 40%, 2 queries each | 160M |
| Quick lookups | 25%, 1 query | 50M |
| Embedded map calls | 80M daily users of ride-hail and delivery apps, 4 calls each | 320M |

That gives about 420 million direct queries and 320 million embedded calls: roughly 740 million a day. My range is 500 to 800 million, and I'd quote about 700 million.

Sanity check from the other direction: Google has said Maps has more than 2 billion monthly users. My 480 million would be 24% of that. India is roughly a fifth of the world's internet users outside China, so I am slightly high but the right size. If I adjusted anything, I'd trim the direct number.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Daily users split by session type, each with its own query rate
- Embedded third-party map calls counted as a separate population
- A sanity check against a global benchmark, followed by an adjustment

**Where answers fail.**

- One flat rate for every smartphone owner, such as ten queries a day each
- Treating the installed base as daily users, which more than doubles the answer

**If they push.**

- *How would Diwali change it?* Commute navigation falls, while intercity trips and searches for shops, temples and restaurants rise. I'd expect a similar total with a different mix, and I'd model each session type separately.
- *How would you validate it with Google's data?* Count logged search and directions requests per day by surface, then compare with my three session types to see which assumption was off.

### Q102. Estimate the total annual revenue Paytm generates from its merchant QR code business in India.

*Estimation · Stretch · Tailored*

**What they are testing.** Whether you know how money is made on Indian payment rails: most QR payments carry no merchant fee, so the revenue has to be found elsewhere. Story to draw on: S3, S5.

**Clarify first.**

- Which revenue lines count? Assume card-linked MDR (the merchant discount rate), Soundbox device subscriptions, government UPI incentives and float. Exclude merchant lending.
- Which year? Assume the latest full financial year.
- Gross revenue or net of processing costs? Assume gross.

**Outline.**

- Active QR merchants, split by volume
- Payments a day, ticket size, annual value
- The constraint: zero MDR on UPI
- The fee-bearing slice: card-linked payments
- Device subscriptions
- Incentives and float
- Total, range, check against reported revenue

**Model answer.** I worked on merchant pricing at Paytm (S3), so I'll use round public numbers only, nothing internal. My approach: size the payment volume, then apply a fee only where one is allowed.

| Step | Assumption | Result |
|---|---|---|
| Active QR merchants | About 15M paying for a device, plus 10M QR-only | 25M |
| Payments per merchant | 30% take 10 a day, 50% take 4, 20% take 1 | 5.2 a day |
| Annual payments | 130M a day for 365 days | 47.5B |
| Payment value | ₹350 average ticket | ₹16.6T |
| Card-linked MDR | 5% of value is fee-bearing, at 0.8% | ₹6.6B |
| Device subscriptions | 15M devices at ₹100 a month | ₹18B |
| UPI incentive | 10% of a ₹1,500 crore government pool | ₹1.5B |
| Float | One day of payments held, at 6.5% a year | ₹3B |

The key fact: ordinary UPI merchant payments carry zero MDR by government mandate, so 95% of that ₹16.6 trillion earns no fee. Revenue comes to about ₹29 billion, a little over $300 million, and devices are 62% of it. My range is ₹20 to 30 billion, because float and incentives are the softest lines. The device count is Paytm's reported base at March 2026; the rest is assumption.

> [!NOTE]
> This estimate is for the financial year to March 2026, when ordinary UPI merchant payments carried no fee. From 15 October 2026 a 0.4% MDR, capped at ₹300, applies to merchant UPI payments above ₹2,000, with small merchants exempt. If the interviewer asks about the current year, add a fee line for that large-ticket slice.

Sanity check: Paytm reported about ₹84 billion of revenue for the year to March 2026, mostly from payments and loan distribution. QR at ₹29 billion would be a third of the company. That is plausible; ₹60 billion would not be.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Names zero MDR on UPI as the constraint and pivots to cards, devices and incentives
- Segments merchants by activity instead of using one average
- Checks the total against Paytm's disclosed revenue

**Where answers fail.**

- Applying an MDR to all UPI volume, which inflates revenue many times over
- A generic processor margin with no knowledge of UPI rules

**If they push.**

- *What if the UPI incentive ends?* Revenue falls only about 5%, to roughly ₹27.6 billion. The real damage is that small-ticket merchants cost more to serve than they earn, so I'd lean harder on devices and credit.
- *What if 20% of UPI-only merchants start taking RuPay credit cards?* The fee-bearing share rises above 5%. Each extra point of value adds about ₹1.3 billion at 0.8%.

### Q103. Estimate the number of ride-hailing trips completed per day in the United States across all providers (Uber, Lyft, and others).

*Estimation · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you can anchor on public disclosures, build an independent demand-side estimate, and reconcile the gap between them instead of hiding it.

**Clarify first.**

- Rides only, or food delivery trips too? Assume passenger rides only.
- Which day? Assume an average day across the year.
- Are street-hail taxis in scope? Assume only app-booked rides, including taxis and robotaxis booked through an app.

**Outline.**

- Top-down from the two big companies' disclosed volumes
- Strip out delivery and non-US trips
- Add smaller providers
- Bottom-up check: monthly riders times trips, plus heavy users
- Reconcile the gap and give a range

**Model answer.** I'll go top-down from what the two big companies report, then check it bottom-up from riders.

| Step | Assumption | Result |
|---|---|---|
| Uber trips, all products, worldwide | 3.8B reported for Q4 2025, annualized | About 15B a year |
| Uber rides | 60% of trips are rides, not deliveries | 9B |
| Uber US rides | 30% of rides are in the US | 2.7B a year, 7.4M a day |
| Lyft US rides | 945M rides reported for 2025, 90% in the US | 2.3M a day |
| Other providers | 10% on top: app-booked taxis, robotaxis, regional apps | 1.0M a day |
| Total | | 10.7M a day |

Bottom-up: assume 15% of 265 million adults ride in a given month, about 40 million people, at 3.5 trips each. That is 140 million trips. Heavy users matter: 1.8 million car-free commuters at 20 trips add 36 million. Together that is 5.9 million a day, and 6.9 million once business and airport travel makes up 15% of the total.

So top-down says about 11 million and bottom-up says about 7 million. The gap sits in my monthly-rider assumption and in Uber's US share, and both are soft. I'd answer 8 to 11 million a day, about 10 million, with Uber near 70%.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Uses company disclosures as an anchor and builds an independent bottom-up
- Reconciles the two numbers out loud
- Recognizes that a small group of heavy riders drives a large share of trips

**Where answers fail.**

- One approach and no cross-check
- An answer off by an order of magnitude, 500,000 or 100 million, with no sense that it is wrong

**If they push.**

- *San Francisco versus Dallas?* Density, parking cost, car ownership, transit and airport distance. San Francisco has more trips per resident and shorter rides. Dallas has fewer, longer, airport-heavy rides.
- *How else would you validate it?* City trip records. New York and Chicago publish ride-hail trip counts, so I'd scale from those by population and density.

### Q104. Estimate how much storage Google Photos uses per day globally.

*Estimation · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Unit discipline at very large scale (megabytes to petabytes), separate treatment of photos and video, and whether you correct yourself when a sanity check disagrees.

**Clarify first.**

- New storage added each day, or the total stored? Assume net new storage per day.
- Photos only, or video too? Assume both.
- Before or after compression and de-duplication? Assume after.

**Outline.**

- Monthly users, then people who upload on a given day
- Photos per uploader and average size after compression
- Video clips and size
- De-duplication
- Sanity check against Google's published upload count
- Adjust and give a range

**Model answer.** I'll estimate daily uploaders, multiply by items and file size for photos and video separately, then net off de-duplication.

| Step | Assumption | Result |
|---|---|---|
| Monthly users | Over 1B stated in 2020; assume 1.5B now | 1.5B |
| Daily uploaders | 20% back something up on a given day | 300M |
| Photos | 8 each, screenshots included | 2.4B |
| Photo size | 70% compressed to 3.5 MB, 30% original at 5 MB | About 4 MB |
| Photo storage | 2.4B photos at 4 MB | 9.6 PB |
| Video | 30% upload one clip, 60 MB compressed to 36 MB | 3.2 PB |
| De-duplication | 12% saved | 11.3 PB net |

So the chain says about 11 petabytes a day.

Now the check. Google said in 2020 that people upload 28 billion photos and videos a week, which is 4 billion a day. My chain implies only 2.5 billion, so I am low. Scaling my average item, about 5 MB, to 4 billion items gives roughly 18 petabytes after de-duplication. I'd answer 10 to 20 petabytes a day and say 15.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Photos and video estimated separately
- Compression and de-duplication applied explicitly
- A sanity check against a reference point, and a recalibration when it disagrees

**Where answers fail.**

- Unit slips: an answer in gigabytes or terabytes is off by a thousand times or more
- Photos only, with one file size and no compression

**If they push.**

- *What does this cost?* At $0.02 per gigabyte a month, a year of uploads at 15 petabytes a day is about 5,500 petabytes. Keeping that costs roughly $1.3 billion a year, and each year adds another layer. That is the logic for ending free unlimited storage.
- *What if AI deletes 20% of blurry or duplicate photos?* Photos are about three quarters of my bytes, so storage and cost fall about 15%.

### Q105. Estimate the total value of digital payments (UPI + card + wallet) processed through merchant point-of-sale in India per day.

*Estimation · Stretch · Tailored*

**What they are testing.** Whether you know Indian payment rails well enough to separate person-to-person transfers from merchant payments, and can reconcile a rails-based number with a consumption-based one. Story to draw on: S4, S5.

**Clarify first.**

- In-store only, or online checkout too? Assume all merchant payments first, then isolate in-store.
- Value or count? Assume value.
- Which period? Assume an average day in mid-2026.

**Outline.**

- UPI total value from published data
- Strip out person-to-person transfers
- Add cards, then wallets
- Total on the broad reading
- Top-down check from household consumption and digital share
- Reconcile: what the rails count that a shop counter does not

**Model answer.** I'll anchor on published rail data for UPI and cards, then check it top-down from household spending.

| Step | Assumption | Result |
|---|---|---|
| UPI, all payments | About ₹29T a month (NPCI, mid-2026) | ₹29T |
| Merchant share of UPI value | 29%; the rest is person to person | ₹8.4T a month |
| UPI merchant, per day | 30 days | ₹280B |
| Cards | ₹2.4T a month: about ₹2T credit (RBI data) plus debit | ₹80B |
| Wallets | ₹0.3T a month, a shrinking share | ₹10B |
| Total | | ₹370B a day |

That is about ₹370 billion a day, roughly $4 billion, three quarters of it UPI.

Top-down check: assume households spend ₹200 trillion a year, 55% of it at shops and service counters. That is ₹300 billion a day. Assume digital is 65% of value in big cities, 40% in smaller towns and 15% in rural areas, weighted 40/35/25. That blends to 44%, or ₹133 billion.

The two differ by almost three times, and the reason matters. The rails count every merchant-coded payment: online shopping, bills, recharges, investments, and small businesses paying suppliers. So my answer has two parts: about ₹370 billion a day for all digital merchant payments, and ₹130 to 190 billion for payments made in person.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Anchors UPI on published NPCI data
- Separates person-to-person value from merchant value
- Cross-checks the rails against a consumption model and explains the gap

**Where answers fail.**

- Counting all UPI value, which is mostly transfers between people
- Assuming US-style card dominance, with no sense of UPI's scale

**If they push.**

- *What if digital share goes from 44% to 65%?* In-store digital rises from about ₹133 billion to ₹196 billion a day, an extra ₹63 billion, mostly from groceries, fuel and small-town retail.
- *Average ticket, UPI versus cards?* UPI merchant payments average a few hundred rupees and card payments a few thousand. I'd derive each as value divided by count from the same published data.

### Q106. Estimate the number of customer service calls handled by Amazon in the United States per day.

*Estimation · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you can pick a sensible driver (orders), remember the contacts that orders do not explain, and separate phone calls from chat and automated handling.

**Clarify first.**

- Voice calls only, or chat and email too? Assume voice calls answered by a person, with the wider numbers shown on the way.
- Which businesses? Assume retail, Prime and devices. Exclude AWS and seller support.
- Average day or peak? Assume an average day.

**Outline.**

- Households that order, orders per year, orders per day
- Contact rate per order
- Contacts not tied to a recent order
- Share that comes by phone
- Share resolved by the automated phone system
- Range, then a staffing check

**Model answer.** I'll drive it from orders: orders per day, times a contact rate, then narrow to phone calls a person answers.

| Step | Assumption | Result |
|---|---|---|
| Ordering households | 75% of about 135M US households | 100M |
| Orders | 40 a year each | 4B a year, 11M a day |
| Order contacts | 4% of orders: late, damaged, wrong item, returns | 440,000 |
| All contacts | Add 30% for Prime, devices, digital and account issues | 572,000 |
| Phone share | 30%; chat and email take the rest | 172,000 |
| Handled by a person | 35% end in the automated system | 112,000 |

So about 110,000 calls a day reach a person, out of roughly 570,000 contacts across all channels. Contact rate is the swing factor. I'd expect 3% to 8% for e-commerce, and at 8% the answer doubles to about 220,000. My range is 100,000 to 220,000.

Sanity check from staffing: at 40 calls per agent per shift, 110,000 calls need about 2,750 agent-shifts a day, or around 5,000 voice agents once you allow for days off and breaks. That is believable for a company this size. From the customer side, it is 0.4 calls per ordering household per year, about one call every two and a half years, which also feels right.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Contact triggers beyond orders: subscriptions, devices, returns on old orders
- A sanity check from agent headcount
- The weakest assumption named, with its effect on the answer

**Where answers fail.**

- Orders times contact rate, with every contact treated as a phone call
- No check, so an answer of 10,000 or 5 million goes unchallenged

**If they push.**

- *The week after Prime Day?* Orders spike, and contacts follow with a lag as deliveries and returns arrive. A 20% lift in orders adds about 22,000 calls a day.
- *How would you cut contact rate from 8% to 6%?* Fix the top reasons at the source: accurate delivery promises, proactive delay alerts, and self-service returns and refunds.

### Q107. Estimate the total number of credit card transactions made in the United States in a single day.

*Estimation · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Segmenting by frequency of use instead of one flat average, and validating a bottom-up number against a public source.

**Clarify first.**

- Credit only, or debit and prepaid too? Assume credit only.
- In-store and online? Assume both.
- Count or dollar value? Assume count, on an average day.

**Outline.**

- Adults, then credit card holders
- Three usage segments, each with its own daily rate
- Sum to a daily total
- Check against the Federal Reserve's payments data
- Range and what moves it

**Model answer.** I'll go bottom-up from cardholders, segmented by how often they use credit, then check against the Federal Reserve's published count.

| Step | Assumption | Result |
|---|---|---|
| US adults | About 265M | 265M |
| Hold a credit card | About four in five, per Federal Reserve surveys | 212M |
| Daily users | 25% of holders, 2.5 transactions a day | 132M |
| Weekly users | 40%, 0.5 a day | 42M |
| Occasional users | 35%, 0.1 a day | 7M |
| Total | | 182M a day |

So about 180 million credit card transactions a day, online and in-store together. Daily users are a quarter of cardholders but nearly three quarters of the volume, so the size of that segment is what I'd test first. Weekends and December run higher, so I'd quote this as an average day.

Sanity check: the Federal Reserve Payments Study counted 67.1 billion credit card payments in 2024, which is 184 million a day, up from 51 billion in 2021. My estimate lands on it. I'd give a range of 160 to 200 million.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Cardholders segmented by frequency, not a flat average
- An external anchor from a published source
- Two independently derived numbers that converge

**Where answers fail.**

- Wrong order of magnitude: millions instead of hundreds of millions
- Mixing debit into a credit question, or anchoring on a network's global volume without separating the two

**If they push.**

- *What if you add debit?* By count, debit is larger. The Fed's 2022 data had debit at 58% of card payments and credit at 36%. That implies about 290 million debit transactions a day, roughly 475 million in total. Credit skews to larger tickets and rewards-seeking spenders.
- *What if pay-later plans replace 10% of credit transactions?* The networks lose about 18 million transactions a day, though some pay-later purchases still run on cards underneath.

### Q108. Estimate the total market size of AI-powered fraud detection software for financial services companies globally.

*Estimation · Stretch · Tailored*

**What they are testing.** Market sizing through a value chain: from the problem (fraud losses) to the budget (prevention spend) to the slice that is AI. Story to draw on: S7.

**Clarify first.**

- Vendor revenue only, or in-house builds too? Assume both, shown separately.
- Payments only, or lending and insurance fraud as well? Assume all financial services fraud.
- AI-native only, or older rule engines too? Assume AI and machine-learning products only.

**Outline.**

- Annual fraud losses
- Prevention spend as a share of losses
- Technology share of that spend
- AI-native share
- In-house builds at the largest banks
- Growth, then a per-transaction check

**Model answer.** I'll start from what fraud costs, estimate what institutions spend to fight it, then take the technology share and the AI share.

| Step | Assumption | Result |
|---|---|---|
| Fraud losses | Cards alone are about $33B a year (Nilson Report); assume all financial services | $90B |
| Prevention spend | 12% of losses, people and technology | $10.8B |
| Technology | Half of that | $5.4B |
| AI-native vendors | 45% of technology spend | $2.4B |
| In-house builds | 30 largest banks at $40M a year each | $1.2B |
| Total | | $3.6B |

So about $3.6 billion today: two thirds bought from vendors, one third built in-house. I'd give a range of $2.5 to 5 billion. Fraud is moving to online and real-time channels where rules do badly, so I'd assume 15 to 20% annual growth, which takes the market to about $6 billion in three years.

Sanity check from a different direction: industry reports count roughly 1.6 trillion non-cash payments a year. Spread over those, $3.6 billion is about a fifth of a cent per payment. That is a believable price for scoring a transaction. Ten cents would not be.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Fraud loss as the anchor, then spend, technology share and AI share
- In-house development counted, not only vendor revenue
- A sanity check and a view on growth

**Where answers fail.**

- A generic "software is some percent of bank revenue" with no fraud logic
- Counting only vendors and missing what large banks build themselves

**If they push.**

- *Real-time payments only?* A smaller base today, but the fastest-growing one, because an instant transfer cannot be charged back. I'd size it from instant-payment fraud losses with a higher spend ratio.
- *Developed versus emerging markets?* Spend follows loss liability and regulation, so the US, UK and EU dominate. India and Brazil have huge volumes and low spend per transaction.

### Q109. Estimate how many Uber Eats orders are placed in New York City on a Friday evening (6 PM – 11 PM).

*Estimation · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Choosing the right unit (households, not people), adjusting for a local market, and checking demand against supply.

**Clarify first.**

- All five boroughs or Manhattan only? Assume all five.
- Orders placed or completed? Assume placed.
- A typical Friday? Assume yes: no storm, no big game.

**Outline.**

- Households in the city
- Share with an active delivery app
- Share that orders in the Friday evening window
- Uber Eats share of those orders
- Orders per hour
- Supply-side check from restaurants

**Model answer.** I'll count households, because dinner is ordered per home, then apply app use, Friday-night ordering and Uber Eats share.

| Step | Assumption | Result |
|---|---|---|
| Households | 8.5M people in about 3.3M households (Census) | 3.3M |
| Active on any delivery app | 65%: small kitchens, dense restaurant supply | 2.15M |
| Order between 6 and 11 PM | 18% on a typical Friday | 386,000 |
| Uber Eats share | 25% in a three-way market | 96,500 |
| Per hour | 5 hours | About 19,000 |

So roughly 95,000 orders, about 19,000 an hour. A few percent of placed orders are cancelled, so completed orders run slightly lower. My range is 60,000 to 130,000, because the share and the Friday ordering rate are both guesses. The 65% is deliberately above what I'd use nationally: New York has dense restaurant supply and the lowest car ownership of any large US city.

Sanity check from supply: assume 15,000 restaurants are live on Uber Eats that evening, averaging 6 orders each across the five hours. That gives 90,000, close enough to trust the order of magnitude.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Households as the unit, with a reason
- New York penetration set above the national average, with a reason
- A local share split and a supply-side check

**Where answers fail.**

- People times a small unexplained percentage
- Assuming Uber Eats has half the market in a city where Grubhub has long been strong

**If they push.**

- *Super Bowl Sunday?* A higher ordering rate, larger baskets, and a sharp pre-game peak instead of a five-hour spread. I'd expect orders up by a third or more, with courier supply as the limit.
- *How would you take share from 25% to 35%?* Restaurant selection first, then price through membership, then reliability at peak. At 30% share the same evening is about 116,000 orders.

### Q110. Estimate how many merchant-facing AI agents (bots that act autonomously for a merchant) are actively used by small businesses in the United States today.

*Estimation · Stretch · Tailored*

**What they are testing.** Whether you define a fuzzy term before counting it, and whether you separate businesses that use AI tools from businesses that let software act for them. Story to draw on: S8.

**Clarify first.**

- What is an agent? Assume software that completes multi-step tasks and takes actions without approval each time. A writing assistant does not count.
- Unique businesses or deployments? Assume both.
- Agents bundled into platforms such as a store builder? Assume they count if switched on.

**Outline.**

- Small businesses, then the digitally active base
- Share using any AI tool
- Share of those running a true agent
- Split by task type
- Deployments per business
- Check from the platform side

**Model answer.** The line I use comes from my merchant operations assistant work (S8): an assistant retrieves and summarizes, an agent takes actions. I'll count businesses, narrow to agents, then convert to deployments.

| Step | Assumption | Result |
|---|---|---|
| Small businesses | About 36M (SBA), mostly sole proprietors | 36M |
| Digitally active | Online store, booking page or active social selling | 10M |
| Use any AI tool | 30% | 3M |
| Run an agent | 17% of AI users let it act on its own | 510,000 |
| Deployments | 1.3 to 1.5 agents per business | 660,000 to 765,000 |

By task I'd split the 510,000 as 40% customer service, 35% marketing, 15% scheduling and 10% inventory and fulfillment. Businesses overlap across types, which is why deployments exceed businesses.

So about half a million businesses and roughly 700,000 deployments. This market moves fast and my two adoption rates are the weak link, so my range is 300,000 to 1 million businesses.

Sanity check from the platform side: assume the commerce, help-desk, booking and marketing platforms that bundle an agent have 5 million US small-business accounts between them, and one in ten has switched it on. That is 500,000, in line.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- An explicit line between AI tools and AI agents
- Agent types named by the task they perform
- Deployments distinguished from unique businesses, plus a platform-side check

**Where answers fail.**

- Counting every business with a chatbot subscription as an agent user
- Starting from all 36 million businesses with one flat adoption rate

**If they push.**

- *At 60% annual growth, where is this in three years?* About 2.1 million businesses, a fifth of the digitally active base. Platforms that already hold the merchant's data should win.
- *What stops the other 9.5 million?* Trust in letting software act, setup effort, and unclear payback. I'd start with narrow, reversible actions and show the hours saved.

### Q111. Estimate the number of GitHub pull requests opened per day globally.

*Estimation · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Funnel thinking from registered accounts to an action on a given day, awareness of automated activity, and use of a published number as a cross-check.

**Clarify first.**

- Public and private repositories? Assume both.
- Do bots count? Assume yes, shown separately.
- Opened or merged? Assume opened, and a new commit pushed to an existing pull request does not count. Merged is my cross-check.

**Outline.**

- Registered developers to monthly active to daily active
- Share who open a pull request that day, and how many
- Bot pull requests from dependency tools
- Total
- Check against merged pull requests in GitHub's annual report
- Range

**Model answer.** I'll take developer accounts down to people who open a pull request on a given day, add bots, then check against GitHub's published merge count.

| Step | Assumption | Result |
|---|---|---|
| Accounts | 180M reported (Octoverse 2025) | 180M |
| Monthly active | 20% | 36M |
| Daily active | One in three of those | 12M |
| Open a pull request | 15% of daily actives, 1.2 each | 2.16M |
| Repositories with bots | 15% of 20M active repositories | 3M |
| Bot pull requests | 2 a week each | 0.86M a day |
| Total | | 3.0M a day |

So about 3 million a day, a bit over 70% of them opened by people.

Sanity check: GitHub reported 43.2 million pull requests merged a month in its 2025 report, which is about 1.4 million a day. Not every pull request merges. If half to two thirds do, opened pull requests run at 2.2 to 2.8 million a day. My 3 million is just above that band, so I'd answer 2.5 to 3 million.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Human and bot pull requests separated
- A published merge count converted into an opened count
- Two estimates that converge

**Where answers fail.**

- Registered accounts times a flat activity rate, with dormant and student accounts counted as active
- Wild per-developer rates, such as ten pull requests a day

**If they push.**

- *What if AI assistants lift output 20% for 30% of developers?* About 130,000 more human pull requests a day, a 6% rise. The real constraint moves to review capacity, and pull requests opened by coding agents add to the bot line.
- *How long does a pull request take to merge?* The distribution is heavily skewed: a median in hours, a mean in days. I'd segment by repository size, public versus private, bot versus human, and size of change.

### Q112. Estimate the annual revenue loss to U.S. retailers from shoplifting and retail theft.

*Estimation · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you know the industry's own measure (shrink), can isolate the part that is theft by outsiders, and treat trade-association figures with care.

**Clarify first.**

- External theft only, or employee theft too? Assume external: shoplifting and organized retail crime.
- Stores only, or online return fraud as well? Assume stores.
- Loss at retail value or at cost? Assume retail value, as the industry reports it.

**Outline.**

- Total retail sales
- Shrink rate: inventory lost for any reason
- External theft share of shrink
- Where organized retail crime sits
- Per-store check
- Range and caveats

**Model answer.** I'll take retail sales, apply the industry shrink rate, then take the share caused by outside theft.

| Step | Assumption | Result |
|---|---|---|
| Retail sales | About $7T a year, excluding restaurants | $7T |
| Shrink | 1.6% of sales, the National Retail Federation's 2023 survey figure | $112B |
| External theft | 36% of shrink in the same survey | $40B |
| Organized retail crime | About $700,000 per $1B of sales; already inside external theft | $4.9B |

So about $40 billion a year, with organized crime roughly an eighth of it. Employee theft, another 29% of shrink, is not in my number. With it, theft of all kinds is about $73 billion.

Sanity check per store: assuming about a million retail establishments, $40 billion is $40,000 per store per year, or $110 a day. That is believable for a supermarket and high for a boutique, which is how an average should look. My range is $35 to 45 billion.

One caveat: these are survey figures from a trade association, and shrink mixes theft with error. I would present the number as an upper-middle estimate.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Starts from sales and the published shrink rate
- Separates outside theft from employee theft and process errors
- Validates against survey data and a per-store figure

**Where answers fail.**

- Calling all shrink "shoplifting", which nearly triples the answer
- No anchor, so the answer lands at $500 million or $500 billion

**If they push.**

- *A retailer spends $1 billion to cut external theft 15%. Is it worth it?* Only if its own external theft exceeds about $6.7 billion a year, since 15% of that is $1 billion. On a $1 billion theft base it saves $150 million a year, so payback takes almost seven years.
- *Luxury versus grocery?* Luxury has few incidents with high value and heavy security. Grocery has many small ones, more self-checkout loss, and thin margins that make each point of shrink hurt more.

### Q113. Estimate the number of active WhatsApp Business API users (businesses, not consumers) globally.

*Estimation · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Product knowledge (the free Business app is not the paid API), geographic segmentation by where WhatsApp is the default channel, and use of a disclosed figure as an anchor.

**Clarify first.**

- The free WhatsApp Business app, or only the API? Assume API only.
- Registered or active? Assume active: at least one message sent in 30 days.
- Businesses or phone numbers? Assume businesses.

**Outline.**

- Where WhatsApp is the main customer channel
- Businesses that use it with customers, by region
- Share large enough to need the API
- Second route from Meta's disclosed Business app users
- Reconcile and give a range

**Model answer.** WhatsApp has over 3 billion monthly users, and in India, Brazil and much of Southeast Asia it is the default way to reach a business. I'll count businesses that talk to customers on WhatsApp by region, then take the share big enough to pay for the API.

| Step | Assumption | Result |
|---|---|---|
| India | 80M small businesses, 30% serve customers on WhatsApp | 24M |
| Brazil | 20M, 35% | 7M |
| Other regions | Southeast Asia 3M, Middle East and Africa 2M, Europe 1.2M, rest 2.75M | 9M |
| Businesses on WhatsApp | | 40M |
| On the API | 12%: mid-size and large firms using a solutions provider | 4.8M |

So about 4.8 million active API accounts, concentrated in India, Brazil and Southeast Asia.

Sanity check by a second route: Meta said in 2023 that the Business app had over 200 million monthly users. That count includes solo sellers and side businesses, so it is five times my 40 million. If 2 to 3% of those 200 million are big enough to need the API, that is 4 to 6 million. The two routes agree, so I'd answer 4 to 6 million, about 5.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- The Business app and the API treated as different products
- Geography weighted by WhatsApp's real footprint
- Meta's disclosed figure used as an anchor

**Where answers fail.**

- Treating every Business app user as an API customer, which gives 200 million
- Counting consumers, or weighting the US as heavily as India and Brazil

**If they push.**

- *What does that imply for India revenue?* About 2.9 million Indian API accounts. At 500 paid conversations a month and $0.02 each, that is roughly $350 million a year.
- *What drives churn?* Per-conversation price rises, template rejections and quality-rating blocks, a change of solutions provider, and weak return on marketing messages.

### Q114. Estimate the total electricity consumption of all data centers in the United States per year.

*Estimation · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Physical unit fluency (megawatts of capacity versus terawatt-hours of energy), segmentation by facility type, and the two multipliers people forget: utilization and cooling overhead.

**Clarify first.**

- Which facilities? Assume hyperscale, colocation and enterprise server rooms. Exclude crypto mining.
- IT equipment only, or cooling and overhead too? Assume total facility power.
- Which year? Assume now.

**Outline.**

- Facility counts and IT capacity by type
- Utilization: capacity is not load
- PUE (power usage effectiveness) by type
- Average draw times 8,760 hours
- Share of US electricity
- Published anchor and range

**Model answer.** I'll build capacity by facility type, convert it to average draw using utilization and PUE, which is total facility power divided by IT power, then multiply by the hours in a year.

| Step | Assumption | Result |
|---|---|---|
| Hyperscale capacity | 400 sites at 50 MW | 20 GW |
| Colocation capacity | 2,600 sites at 5 MW | 13 GW |
| Enterprise rooms | 1M rooms at 2 kW | 2 GW |
| Average IT load | 60% utilization of 35 GW | 21 GW |
| Facility draw | PUE of 1.2, 1.5 and 2.0 by type | 14.4 + 11.7 + 2.4 = 28.5 GW |
| Energy | 8,760 hours a year | 250 TWh |

So about 250 terawatt-hours a year. The US uses a little over 4,000, so that is about 6% of the country's electricity. My range is 200 to 300.

Sanity check: a national-laboratory study for the Department of Energy put data centers at 176 terawatt-hours in 2023, 4.4% of US electricity, and projected 325 to 580 by 2028. An estimate of 250 for today sits on that path, and AI workloads are the reason the path is so steep.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- PUE applied, with different efficiency by facility type
- Hyperscale, colocation and enterprise sized separately
- A check against published estimates, with AI-driven growth acknowledged

**Where answers fail.**

- Running nameplate capacity at 100% all year: without utilization this chain gives 416, not 250
- Counting millions of server closets at data-center scale

**If they push.**

- *What if AI load grows 50% a year for three years?* If AI is 50 of today's 250, it becomes about 170 and the total about 370. The limit is grid connection and transformers, not chips.
- *What is a PUE cut from 1.20 to 1.10 worth?* On 12 gigawatts of hyperscale IT load, about 10.5 terawatt-hours a year. Cooling design, warmer set points and site choice get you there.

### Q115. Estimate the total revenue opportunity for a BNPL product targeting premium credit card holders in the United States who currently pay 0% APR promotional balances.

*Estimation · Stretch · Tailored*

**What they are testing.** Whether you can size a lending product on net economics, not gross fees: who the customer is, why they would switch, who pays, and what the money costs. Story to draw on: S3.

**Clarify first.**

- Standalone product or a feature inside an issuer's app? Assume standalone.
- Who pays: the shopper, the merchant, or both? Assume I test both.
- Revenue or net of funding cost? Assume both. Net is the answer that matters.

**Outline.**

- Premium cardholders
- Share with a live 0% promotional balance, and average balance
- Share that would switch, and why
- Origination volume
- Fee revenue against cost of funds
- The fee level at which it works
- Market-share check

**Model answer.** I'll size the balances sitting on 0% promotions, take the share that would move to fixed installments, then test whether the fee covers the funding cost.

| Step | Assumption | Result |
|---|---|---|
| Premium cardholders | 15% of about 650M card accounts, two cards each | 50M people |
| On a live 0% promotion | 27% | 13.5M |
| Promotional balances | $3,000 average | $40.5B |
| Would switch | 30% | 4.05M users |
| Originations | $3,000 each | $12.15B a year |
| Fee at 3% | A typical installment fee | $365M |
| Cost of funds | 6% a year, nine months outstanding: 4.5% | $547M |

At a 3% fee the product loses about $180 million before a single default. These customers already borrow at zero, so they will not pay much themselves. The fee has to come from merchants. At 5.5%, revenue is $668 million and the net is about $120 million a year, before credit losses and servicing.

The reason to switch is the cliff when a promotion ends. The remaining balance moves to a rate above 20%, and store-card promotions can charge deferred interest back to day one. Fixed installments remove that risk.

So the opportunity is about $670 million gross and $100 to 130 million net, on a one-point spread. Sanity check: $12 billion would be about 12% of a US pay-later market I'd put near $100 billion. That is a ceiling, not a forecast.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Names the end-of-promotion risk as the customer pain and the product's edge
- Calculates net revenue after cost of funds, not just fees
- Concludes that merchant-funded fees are required for the product to work

**Where answers fail.**

- All US cardholders times a flat revenue rate
- Stopping at gross fees and never noticing that the 3% version loses money

**If they push.**

- *What if funding cost rises from 6% to 8%?* Cost of funds becomes $729 million, so the 5.5% fee loses about $60 million. I'd need a fee above 6% or shorter terms.
- *Issuers already offer fixed-fee installment plans. Does that change it?* Yes. The issuer owns the customer and the balance. My edge has to be at the merchant checkout, with offers the issuer cannot make.

# Part VII — Execution and metrics

## 7. Execution and metrics questions

Execution questions come in two forms. Diagnosis questions give you a metric that moved and ask what you would do: confirm the signal, segment it, list distinct hypotheses, order the checks, then respond. Definition questions ask you to set a goal, a North Star, supporting metrics, guardrails, and the trade-off you would make when they conflict. Interviewers listen for priorities, thresholds that trigger action, and what you refuse to optimize. Each answer below keeps its metric set in one table so you can rehearse it. In the room, say the North Star and three or four rows aloud, not all of them. Targets are illustrative assumptions unless a source is named. Six questions are marked Tailored and draw on payments, lending and AI.

### Q116. You are the PM for Instagram Reels. Monthly active Reels viewers drop 12% over a 6-week period. Walk me through how you'd investigate and respond.

*Execution and metrics · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Structured diagnosis under ambiguity: confirm the signal, segment it, generate distinct hypotheses, and sequence the checks by speed and likelihood before proposing a response.

**Clarify first.**

- How is a monthly Reels viewer defined, and has that changed? Assume anyone who viewed one Reel in 30 days, definition unchanged.
- Global or concentrated? Assume unknown. Finding out is step one.
- Did anything ship in the window? Assume several ranking and app changes, none flagged.

**Outline.**

- Validate the data and the definition
- Segment: geography, platform, user tenure, content type
- Hypotheses in three buckets: product, supply, external
- Order the checks by speed: 24 hours, 48 hours, one week
- Response matched to cause
- Recovery metric and an early-warning fix

**Model answer.** Before reacting, I'd confirm the drop is real. Has the viewer definition or the logging changed? Is this a gradual slide, or a step on a date that matches a release or an outage?

Then I'd cut it four ways at once. Geography: one region points to a local cause, a global drop points to product. Platform: an iOS-only or Android-only drop points to a build.
