Tenure: new users point to discovery, established users to a broken habit. Content category: one vertical points to creator supply.

| Hypothesis | Fast check | Response if true |
|---|---|---|
| Feed mix shows fewer Reels | Reels impressions per session, experiment log | Adjust or roll back |
| Ranking quality fell | Skips, completion and replays by cohort | Roll back the model |
| Load time regressed | 95th-percentile load time by build | Treat as a top-severity incident |
| Top creators post less | Posting frequency of the top 1,000 | Outreach and a temporary bonus |
| Moderation over-removes | Removal rate trend | Fix the policy threshold |
| Competitor or season | Year-on-year curve, competitor launches | Roadmap response, not a patch |

In the first 24 hours I'd pull the cuts, the experiment log and load times. Within 48 hours, creator supply and moderation. In the first week, a survey of lapsed weekly viewers asking what they watch instead.

Success is monthly viewers back at baseline within four to six weeks of the fix, with Reels per session and seven-day retention of Reels viewers recovering first.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Validates the signal before reacting
- Segments across geography, device, cohort and content together
- Separates supply, demand and product hypotheses, with a time-sequenced plan and owners

**Where answers fail.**

- Jumping to one cause, usually a competitor
- "Look at the data" with no named cuts and no order

**If they push.**

- *It is a 2-second load regression on Android and the fix takes three weeks.* Roll back the build if possible. If not, lighten Android playback: lower the default bitrate, preload the next Reel, and pause new Android experiments.
- *How do you catch this in 48 hours next time?* Alert on daily and weekly Reels viewers by platform and region against a seasonal baseline. A monthly metric hides a slide for weeks.

### Q117. You are the PM for a Paytm Payments Bank savings account. The 30-day average balance per active account drops sharply. What do you do?

*Execution and metrics · Standard · Tailored*

**What they are testing.** Whether you decompose a ratio before diagnosing it, and whether you bring regulatory awareness to a banking product. Story to draw on: S6, S4.

**Clarify first.**

- How large, over what period? Assume 20% over two months.
- Did the number of active accounts change? Assume I must check. It is the first cut.
- Any regulatory action or rule change? Assume I must check before anything else.

**Outline.**

- Decompose: total deposits divided by active accounts
- Mix shift versus behavior change
- Segment: balance tier, account age, region, outflow channel
- External causes: regulator, competitors, economy
- Size the impact
- Response by cause, then recovery metrics

**Model answer.** Average balance is a ratio, so I'd split it first. If active accounts grew 15% while deposits grew 3%, the average falls about 10% and nobody changed behavior. That is dilution from new low-balance accounts. If accounts are flat and deposits fell, existing customers are moving money out.

Then I'd segment by balance tier, account age and region, and above all by where the money goes. Transfers out to other banks are the strongest signal of a competitive or trust problem. Cash withdrawals in farming regions may be seasonal.

For a payments bank I'd check the regulator first. Balances are capped at ₹2 lakh per customer, and in January 2024 the RBI barred this bank from accepting further deposits. An event like that makes this crisis management, not product tuning.

To size it: say 100 million active accounts at ₹1,500 each, which is ₹150 billion. A 20% fall is ₹30 billion of deposits and about ₹1.95 billion a year of income at 6.5%.

| Cause | Evidence | Response |
|---|---|---|
| Mix shift | Accounts up, deposits flat | Balance-linked rewards; report cohorts separately |
| Competitor yield | Transfers out to banks | Partner fixed deposit inside the app |
| Regulatory event | Circular, press, support spikes | Crisis team; freeze product changes |
| Seasonal cash need | Regional and recurring | Small credit line instead of full withdrawal |

Recovery metric: average balance back at its prior level within eight weeks, with net outflow to other banks as the leading indicator.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Decomposes the ratio immediately
- Raises the regulatory hypothesis for a payments bank
- Segments by outflow channel and gives a different response for each cause

**Where answers fail.**

- Treating the drop as one phenomenon and answering "offer better rates"
- No regulatory awareness for a regulated deposit product

**If they push.**

- *Older accounts are withdrawing ₹500 to 1,000 a week: a slow bleed.* That suggests salary or income now lands elsewhere. I'd find where, then win back the inflow with bill payments and automatic sweeps, not rewards.
- *How do you see it 30 days earlier?* A cohort dashboard of balance by account age and tier, with net transfers out as the lead signal.

### Q118. You are the PM for LinkedIn's "People You May Know" (PYMK) feature. Define the North Star metric and a complete metrics framework for this feature.

*Execution and metrics · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you pick a North Star that measures value instead of volume, and can arrange supporting metrics, guardrails and things you refuse to optimize into one hierarchy.

**Clarify first.**

- Is the goal network growth or connection quality? Assume quality, with growth as a by-product.
- One surface or all? Assume every surface where suggestions appear.
- Is monetization in scope? Assume not as a target.

**Outline.**

- Purpose: connections that lead to real interaction
- North Star: meaningful connection rate
- Feature metrics: acceptance, sends, dismissals, profile views
- Downstream: engagement lift, job outcomes
- Guardrails: privacy, spam, suggestion pool health
- Anti-metrics and the hierarchy

**Model answer.** PYMK exists to create professional connections that people use. So the North Star should not be requests sent or connections accepted, because both reward volume. I'd use meaningful connection rate: the share of connections made through PYMK that produce a real interaction within 90 days, such as a message exchange, engagement with each other's content, or a referral. A connection that stays silent for 90 days was probably not relevant when it was made.

| Metric | Definition | Why it matters |
|---|---|---|
| Meaningful connection rate | Connections with an interaction in 90 days; target 35% | North Star: value, not count |
| Acceptance rate | Suggestions that become connections; assume a 5% baseline | Suggestion quality |
| Request send rate | Suggestions that trigger a request | Use of the feature |
| Dismissal rate | Suggestions explicitly dismissed; keep under 25% | Poor relevance |
| Engagement lift | Feed activity in the 30 days after connecting, against a control | A better network makes a better feed |
| Job outcomes | Referrals or applications through these connections in 6 months | Real-world value |
| Privacy complaints | "Why am I seeing this person?" reports; under 0.01% | Trust |
| Spam rate | Connections later flagged as spam; at or below platform baseline | Gaming |

I'd also watch suggestion pool health, so heavy users are not served weak third-degree suggestions.

What I would refuse to optimize: total connections per member, suggestions shown per session, and conversion to Premium. Each rewards blasting low-quality suggestions or bending the graph for commercial reasons.

The hierarchy: executives see meaningful connection rate and acceptance rate. The product team sees dismissals, profile views and engagement lift. Data science sees acceptance by suggestion source, latency and freshness. Guardrails sit across all levels.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- A quality North Star, with "meaningful" defined precisely
- Named anti-metrics that prevent gaming
- A hierarchy with an owner at each level

**Where answers fail.**

- "Connections made" as the North Star
- Ten metrics with no priority and no guardrails

**If they push.**

- *Acceptance rises 8% but the meaningful rate falls 10%. Do you ship?* No. Meaningful connections per suggestion fall about 3%, and members' networks fill with noise. I'd find which suggestion source drove it.
- *A researcher shows PYMK can reveal undisclosed relationships.* Treat it as a privacy incident: remove the sensitive signals, add suppression rules for blocked and one-sided contacts, and publish the change.

### Q119. You are the PM for Google Search. Describe the metrics you'd track to evaluate the quality of AI-generated answer summaries (AI Overviews) above organic results.

*Execution and metrics · Stretch*

**What they are testing.** Whether you can define quality for an AI answer across users, accuracy and the publisher ecosystem, with stricter bars where errors cause harm.

**Clarify first.**

- Quality for whom: users, publishers, or both? Assume both, plus advertising as a guardrail.
- Do all query types share one bar? Assume not. Health, finance and legal queries get their own.
- Short-term engagement or long-term trust? Assume both.

**Outline.**

- Three dimensions: user value, accuracy, ecosystem health
- User value: resolution, helpfulness, reformulation
- Accuracy: errors, citation support, freshness
- Ecosystem: publisher clicks and traffic
- Guardrails: ads, return rate, opt-out
- Alert thresholds by severity

**Model answer.** I'd define quality on three dimensions and measure all of them, because an overview can satisfy users while being wrong, or be right while starving the sites it learned from.

| Metric | Definition | Why it matters |
|---|---|---|
| Query resolution | No follow-up search within 5 minutes, against a holdout without overviews | Did it meet the need |
| Helpfulness | Thumbs-up share on a 1% survey; target 75%, and 85% for factual queries | Direct user judgment |
| Reformulation | Query rewritten right after an overview | Missed intent |
| Factual error rate | Overviews with an error, from raters, automated checks and user reports; under 1%, and 0.1% for high-stakes topics | Core accuracy |
| Citation support | Cited sources exist and back the claim; target 100% | Invented citations destroy trust |
| Freshness | Time-sensitive answers built on stale sources | Outdated is wrong |
| Publisher click-through | Clicks to cited sources; healthy above 3%, a problem under 1% | Ecosystem health |
| Opt-out and return | Users who disable overviews; users back within 24 hours | Long-term trust |

Abandonment needs care. Leaving after reading can mean success or failure, so I'd read it only alongside helpfulness. Citation support became non-negotiable after the widely reported errors of May 2024, such as the advice to put glue on pizza.

Two guardrails. If traffic to frequently cited domains falls 15% or more, the design is taking value without returning it, and I'd shorten what is shown. If organic click-through falls more than 20% on overview queries, ad clicks and bids will follow, so I'd track cost per click below overviews.

Alerts: act immediately if the error rate on health, finance or legal queries passes 0.5%, or unsupported citations pass 0.1%. Investigate within 48 hours if helpfulness falls under 70% for a major category, or publisher click-through under 0.5%.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Quality defined across users, accuracy and publishers
- Citation accuracy named explicitly, with stricter thresholds for high-stakes queries
- The advertising guardrail addressed

**Where answers fail.**

- Generic web metrics: click-through, session length, bounce rate
- Engagement only, with accuracy as an afterthought

**If they push.**

- *Helpfulness is 82% overall but 58% for medical queries, with a 3% error rate.* Limit medical overviews to queries with high-authority sources, add clinical reviewers, and switch overviews off for the worst query classes until errors are under target.
- *A publisher says you are stealing journalism. Citation click-through is 2.5%.* Share the data, test more prominent links and shorter summaries, and make publisher traffic a launch criterion.

### Q120. Define the metrics framework for a grocery delivery app's "driver earnings" experience. How do you ensure driver satisfaction without harming delivery efficiency?

*Execution and metrics · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you can hold a two-sided tension in one framework: what drivers earn against what the platform needs, with explicit thresholds that trigger action.

**Clarify first.**

- Employees or independent contractors? Assume contractors.
- Is there a legal pay floor? Assume some cities have one, as New York does.
- Predictable earnings or maximum earnings? Assume drivers want both, measured separately.

**Outline.**

- The tension: cherry-picking versus coverage
- North Star: net earnings per active hour, bottom quartile
- Earnings: predictability, tips, decay
- Efficiency: acceptance, orders per hour, pickup wait
- Satisfaction: retention, NPS, pay disputes
- The corridor: thresholds that trigger a review
- Guardrails

**Model answer.** A driver who accepts only short, high-tip orders earns more per hour, while low-tip and long-distance orders wait. So I'd track both sides and set a healthy range for each.

My North Star is net earnings per active driver hour: base pay, tips and bonuses over hours logged in. I'd judge it by market at the 25th percentile to protect the most exposed drivers. Target: 1.4 times the local minimum wage at peak and at least 1.0 times off-peak.

| Metric | Definition | Why it matters |
|---|---|---|
| Predictability | Shifts within 15% of expected earnings; target 70% | Variance hurts even at a good average |
| Tip receipt rate | Orders with a tip; alert on a 5-point drop | A design change may be suppressing tips |
| Acceptance rate | Offers accepted; a problem under 75% | Offers feel underpaid |
| Orders per driver hour | Deliveries per active hour | Efficiency; too high burns drivers out |
| Pickup wait | Minutes waiting at the store; under 5 | Unpaid time |
| 90-day retention | New drivers still doing 5 orders a week; target 60% | Supply health |
| Pay dispute rate | Driver-weeks with a pay ticket; under 2% | Errors in pay or its display |

I'd add a quarterly NPS by earnings quartile, and keep earnings decay from month 1 to month 3 under 10%.

The corridor: if hourly earnings fall below minimum wage for over 20% of driver hours in a market, base pay is reviewed. If acceptance falls below 70%, pay rises on the order types being refused. If 90-day retention falls below 50%, it is a supply crisis.

Two guardrails. Never hide tips before acceptance: it is unethical, and New York City requires apps to show tip and pay up front. Never raise average earnings at the cost of predictability.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Hourly net earnings as the North Star, at the median or below, not the mean
- Predictability treated as its own need
- A corridor with explicit triggers, and tip-hiding ruled out

**Where answers fail.**

- Consumer metrics only: orders, revenue, delivery time
- Earnings and efficiency metrics listed with no rule for when they conflict

**If they push.**

- *Retention falls from 62% to 48%, concentrated in the lowest earnings quartile.* By my own rule that is a supply crisis. I'd put a temporary earnings floor under that quartile, then fix the cause: zones, offer quality or store wait.
- *A regulator proposes a $30 hourly guarantee.* Idle time is now paid, so utilization becomes the key metric: tighter scheduling, more batching, fewer drivers online.

### Q121. You are the PM for an agentic AI system that autonomously executes financial trades for retail investors. Define the safety metrics framework and escalation thresholds.

*Execution and metrics · Stretch · Tailored*

**What they are testing.** Safety design for an agent whose errors are immediate and often irreversible: what you measure, where the hard limits sit, and who is told how fast. Story to draw on: S8, S7.

**Clarify first.**

- Does the agent follow user rules or its own signals? Assume its own signals, inside limits the user sets.
- Safety for single accounts or the whole platform? Assume both.

**Outline.**

- Three goals: limit harm, keep the user in control, meet regulatory duties
- Execution quality: errors, duplicates, limits
- User outcomes: drawdown, drift, suitability
- Autonomy signals: overrides, pauses, stale consent
- Three escalation tiers with time targets
- Audit trail and complaints

**Model answer.** I'd set three goals: limit harm, keep the user in control, and meet supervision and suitability duties. One rule carries over from my merchant operations assistant work (S8): the model proposes, deterministic limits decide.

| Metric | Definition | Why it matters |
|---|---|---|
| Erroneous and duplicate orders | Wrong ticker, size or direction, under 0.01%; near-identical trades within 5 minutes, zero | Core failure; runaway loop |
| Risk limit adherence | Trades inside the user's position, loss and asset limits; 100% | Any breach is top severity |
| Daily drawdown | Accounts losing over 5% in a day; investigate above 0.5% of accounts | Platform-level harm |
| Portfolio drift | Movement from the target allocation; under 5 points a week without approval | Mandate ignored |
| Overrides and pauses | Agent trades the user cancels or changes, a concern above 15%; sessions paused, above 5% | Preferences misread, low trust |
| Stale consent | Users with no settings review in 60 days; flag at 90 | Agent running on old preferences |

Tier 1 pauses the agent and tells the user within a minute: a loss-limit breach, a duplicate trade, a restricted ticker, or an erroneous order above $5,000. Tier 2 is human compliance review within two hours: a drawdown above 5% in a day, slippage (fill price against intended price) above 0.5% on three trades in a row, or a trade that does not fit the user's risk profile. Tier 3 is a possible regulatory filing: patterns that look like manipulation, repeated errors in one session, or errors touching over 0.1% of accounts.

Every action is logged with a timestamp, rationale and market snapshot, and complaints about agent trades are tracked separately. In the US the reference points are FINRA's supervision rule, its algorithmic trading guidance and the best-interest standard for retail customers. I'd confirm scope with counsel.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Three metric families: execution quality, user outcomes, autonomy guardrails
- Tiered escalation with dollar thresholds and time-to-notify targets
- Regulatory duties and stale consent treated as safety issues

**Where answers fail.**

- Performance metrics such as returns and Sharpe ratio offered as safety metrics
- Metrics with no thresholds, tiers or owner

**If they push.**

- *A flash crash: 3% of accounts breach loss limits in 15 minutes, and users panic.* Keep agents paused. Within minutes send a plain message: what happened, your positions, your options. Add support capacity, and restart only after each user confirms limits.
- *A regulatory exam finds 0.5% of trades were unsuitable.* Make the suitability check block the trade before execution, remediate affected customers, and re-test.

### Q122. You are the PM for a new Spotify podcast creation tool that helps amateurs produce professional-quality episodes. Define success metrics for the first 6 months.

*Execution and metrics · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Success metrics for a supply-side product: whether you separate adoption from outcomes, and connect creator success back to platform health.

**Clarify first.**

- Creation only, or audience growth tools too? Assume creation: record, edit, publish.
- New creators or people migrating from other tools? Assume new amateurs.
- What is the business goal? Assume more quality shows and better creator retention, not tool revenue.

**Outline.**

- Success definition: quality shows that would not otherwise exist
- Adoption: activation, time to first episode, feature use
- Output quality: audio score, listener completion, cadence
- Creator success: audience growth, six-month retention
- Platform health: new shows, share of top-rated shows
- Guardrails: abandonment, policy violations

**Model answer.** The tool succeeds if it creates good shows that would not otherwise exist or survive. It is a supply investment: better shows mean more listening. So I'd measure adoption, output quality, creator success and platform health, with six-month creator retention as the headline, because most new podcasts die after a few episodes.

| Metric | Definition | Why it matters |
|---|---|---|
| Activation rate | Accounts that finish a full episode draft within 30 days; target 45% | Sign-up is not use |
| Time to first episode | Median days from activation to publishing; under 14 | The tool should remove friction |
| AI feature use | Published episodes using noise removal, leveling or auto-chapters | Are the differentiators used |
| Audio quality | Episodes with clean signal, no clipping, and loudness within 2 LUFS of the -14 target; 75% | Measurable output quality |
| Listener completion | Listeners finishing 70% of an episode, against matched shows; 10% higher | Quality that listeners feel |
| Publishing cadence | Episodes per active creator; 2 a month | Predicts audience growth |
| Audience growth | Monthly listener growth against a matched control; 15% faster | Creator success |
| Six-month retention | Activated creators still publishing in month 6; target 40% against an assumed 20 to 25% | The headline |
| New shows | Shows launched with the tool; 50,000 | Catalog supply |

I'd also track follower conversion, and whether tool-made shows are over-represented among top-rated new shows, which would mean the tool lifts catalog quality.

Two guardrails: draft abandonment under 30%, and a policy-violation rate at or below the platform average, so easier creation does not mean more harmful content.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Activation defined as a finished draft, not a sign-up
- Six-month creator retention as the primary measure, because churn is the core problem
- Audio quality measured against a loudness standard, and creator success tied to catalog quality

**Where answers fail.**

- Downloads and listener counts offered as tool metrics
- Adoption only, with no measure of whether the shows are any good

**If they push.**

- *At month 4, retention tracks at 28% and most creators stop after episode 3.* That is where early listeners plateau and effort feels unrewarded. I'd test episode planning prompts, audience milestones shown after each episode, scheduled publishing, and batch recording.
- *What changes if creators can earn money?* Earnings per creator and the share earning anything join the headline, and I'd watch for quantity displacing quality.

### Q123. You are the PM for an enterprise SaaS B2B product (project management tool). Define your churn prediction and prevention metrics framework.

*Execution and metrics · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you can predict churn from behavior, combine the signals into a score, and match each intervention to what the account is worth.

**Clarify first.**

- Logo churn or revenue churn? Assume both.
- What is the contract size range? Assume $5,000 to $500,000 a year, so interventions must be tiered.

**Outline.**

- Outcomes: logo churn and net revenue retention
- Churn types: voluntary, involuntary, early
- Leading signals, 30 to 90 days ahead
- Lagging signals
- Weighted health score and tiers
- Intervention by contract value
- Did the intervention work

**Model answer.** I'd track two outcomes: logo churn, target under 8% a year, and net revenue retention, which is starting recurring revenue plus expansion, minus contraction and churn, over starting revenue, target above 115%. I'd separate voluntary churn, involuntary churn from failed payments, and early churn in the first 90 days, an onboarding failure.

Customers rarely cancel as their first signal. Behavior changes 30 to 90 days earlier, so I'd score it.

| Metric | Definition | Why it matters |
|---|---|---|
| Engagement (35 points) | Project creation trend and breadth of core features used | Strongest predictor |
| Admin login recency (20) | Days since the account admin logged in; alert at 30 | A disengaged champion cannot defend the budget |
| Seat utilization (20) | Licensed seats active in 30 days; outreach under 50% | The buyer will question the bill |
| Support sentiment (15) | Tone of tickets over 60 days, such as "evaluating alternatives" | Stated frustration |
| Renewal outlook (10) | Full points unless within 120 days with no expansion signal | Timing risk |

Scores of 75 and up are green, 50 to 74 yellow, 25 to 49 red with immediate action, and under 25 critical with executive outreach. Lagging signals, such as a cancellation request, a downgrade or unanswered renewal notices, mean prevention failed and only a save offer is left.

Intervention has to fit the economics. A customer success touch costs $500 to 1,500. Under $5,000 of contract value I'd use automated in-product help. Above $10,000, a person handles yellow and red accounts. Above $50,000, quarterly business reviews happen whatever the score.

Then I'd measure the program: red accounts that recover within 60 days, save rate on cancellation requests with a 30% target, and expansion within 90 days of a save.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Logo churn and net revenue retention defined separately
- A weighted health score, with admin login as its own alert
- Human versus automated intervention justified by contract value, plus save and expansion rates

**Where answers fail.**

- NPS, revenue and user counts offered as churn metrics
- Signals listed with no score and no trigger

**If they push.**

- *The model has 65% precision and 72% recall, and a colleague says it is wrong too often.* One in three flagged accounts would not have churned. A wasted touch costs about $1,000, and a missed $50,000 account costs fifty times that, so I'd keep it, and compare it with the team's own hit rate.
- *A $500,000 account scores 40, 90 days from renewal.* Executive sponsor call this week, find the real objection, agree a written success plan with dates, and hold any discount until the cause is known.

### Q124. You are the PM for a new Amazon feature: voice product questions before purchase ("ask Alexa about this product"). Define how you'd measure success.

*Execution and metrics · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Hypothesis-driven measurement: from adoption to answer quality to purchase to returns, with accuracy guardrails for AI-generated answers.

**Clarify first.**

- Which surfaces? Assume Echo devices and the mobile app.
- Where do answers come from? Assume AI synthesis of the listing, reviews and manufacturer content.
- What is the hypothesis? Assume better answers before purchase raise conversion and cut returns.

**Outline.**

- Hypothesis and primary metric
- Measurement design: randomized holdout
- Adoption: initiation, completion, questions per session
- Answer quality: helpfulness, accuracy, hallucination, coverage
- Purchase funnel and returns
- Guardrails: speed, hallucination, privacy

**Model answer.** My hypothesis is that shoppers who get a spoken answer before buying are more confident, so they convert more and return less. Customers get fewer wrong purchases, and Amazon gets higher conversion and lower returns cost. People who ask questions already have higher intent, so I'd judge the feature with a randomized holdout, feature on against feature off, not by comparing askers with non-askers.

| Metric | Definition | Why it matters |
|---|---|---|
| Conversion lift | Purchase rate against the holdout; target 8% higher at 95% confidence | Primary success metric |
| Initiation rate | Eligible product page sessions with a voice question; 3 to 5% early | Adoption, higher for complex products |
| Completion rate | Questions understood and answered; above 85% | The voice experience works |
| Questions per session | Average per user; 1.5 by month 3 | Depth of use |
| Helpfulness | Thumbs-up share, split by answer source; above 70% | Perceived quality |
| Accuracy | Answers that match listing attributes, plus human review of a sample; above 95% | Wrong answers cause returns |
| Hallucination rate | Answers unsupported by the listing or reviews; model review above 0.5% | Trust |
| Unanswered rate | Questions with no usable data | Where the catalog needs enriching |
| Return rate | 30-day returns against the holdout; at least 5% lower | The downstream proof |

I'd also watch cart-to-purchase rate and order value, and ask buyers whether the answer helped them choose, with a 65% target.

Guardrails: no added page load time at the 95th percentile, a hallucination rate above 0.5% blocks further rollout, and voice queries are not used for ad targeting, audited quarterly.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- The purchase-confidence hypothesis stated, with return rate as the final test
- Hallucination treated as a non-negotiable guardrail
- Answer quality segmented by source: reviews versus manufacturer content

**Where answers fail.**

- Engagement only: questions asked, sessions, clicks
- Comparing self-selected askers with everyone else and calling the gap a lift

**If they push.**

- *Conversion is up 12% in electronics and down 2% in apparel.* Voice answers suit specifications and fail on fit and feel. Keep it for specification-led categories. In apparel, route to size charts and review summaries, or switch it off.
- *Support hears "Alexa told me it would do X" while the hallucination rate reads 0.3%.* At this scale 0.3% is many customers, and my metric may miss answers that are supported but misleading. I'd re-label a sample from the calls and measure by product.

### Q125. You are the PM for a fintech with an AI credit scoring model for thin-file borrowers in India. Define the bias and fairness monitoring framework.

*Execution and metrics · Stretch · Tailored*

**What they are testing.** Whether you can make fairness operational: choose a definition, measure it by group, watch for proxies, and set thresholds and remedies in a regulated market. Story to draw on: S7, S10.

**Clarify first.**

- Which protected attributes? Assume gender, caste, religion, geography and age.
- Will we trade accuracy for fairness? Assume a small, pre-agreed amount.

**Outline.**

- The obligation, and the proxy problem
- Choose the fairness definition, with reasons
- Group metrics, thresholds and a proxy audit
- Monitoring cadence
- Escalation and remediation
- Written accuracy-for-fairness policy

**Model answer.** RBI's Fair Practices Code bars lenders from discriminating on grounds such as sex, caste and religion, and RBI's work on digital lending and responsible AI points toward auditable, explainable models. The model cannot use these attributes, yet surnames, pin codes and device data can stand in for them. So I'd monitor outcomes by group and inputs for proxies.

Three definitions compete. Demographic parity wants equal approval rates, but forcing it can push credit onto people who cannot repay. Predictive parity wants a score to mean the same repayment odds in every group. Equalized odds wants equal error rates. I'd make equalized odds primary, because the core injustice is refusing creditworthy people, with predictive parity as the second check.

| Metric | Definition | Why it matters |
|---|---|---|
| Approval rate gap | Highest group rate minus lowest; audit above 15 points | Tripwire |
| Adverse impact ratio | Lowest group's approval rate over the highest; escalate under 0.8 | The four-fifths rule of thumb |
| Error-rate gaps | Creditworthy applicants declined, and poor risks approved, by group; alert above 5 points | Equalized odds: the most direct harm |
| Calibration | Predicted against actual repayment, by group | Predictive parity |
| Proxy audit | Features correlated above 0.4 with a protected attribute | Indirect discrimination |

The two approval tests differ: 60% against 45% fails both, while 90% against 75% has the same 15-point gap and passes the ratio at 0.83.

Cadence: approval gap and ratio monthly, the full error-rate and proxy review quarterly, an independent audit yearly. Under 0.8, I'd freeze model changes, convene product, data science, legal and risk within 72 hours, and find whether a feature, a segment or data quality drives it. Remedies: retrain with fairness constraints, remove the proxy, or add data for the under-served group.

Last, a written policy: we accept up to a 2% loss in AUC to meet equalized odds, approved by the board's risk committee.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Equalized odds chosen over demographic parity, with reasons
- The four-fifths ratio used as the escalation threshold, and proxies monitored
- An explicit, approved accuracy-for-fairness trade-off

**Where answers fail.**

- Overall model accuracy offered as a bias metric
- Group summaries with no thresholds and no Indian regulatory context

**If they push.**

- *The ratio is 0.76 for rural women in Bihar because they have less documented income.* The model is accurate on data that under-describes these women. I'd add other evidence, such as self-help group repayment and savings flows, and offer small starter limits that build history.
- *A data scientist wants surname clusters as a fraud feature.* No. It is a caste proxy. Target the fraud behavior itself with device, network and velocity signals.

### Q126. You are the PM for Facebook Marketplace. Daily active buyers (people who view 5+ listings) decline 15% over 4 weeks. How do you investigate?

*Execution and metrics · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Diagnosis in a two-sided marketplace with a threshold-based metric: whether you question the metric, test supply as well as demand, and run the investigation to a clock.

**Clarify first.**

- Has the five-listing threshold or the logging changed? Assume not, but I'd verify.
- Global or concentrated? Assume unknown.
- What happened to active sellers and listings? Assume unknown. I'd check early.

**Outline.**

- Validate the metric: threshold, bot filtering
- Supply or demand
- Segment: geography, category, buyer cohort, device
- Product hypotheses
- External hypotheses
- 72-hour plan and response

**Model answer.** This metric has a threshold, five listings, so I'd validate it first. Did the threshold or the logging change? Did better bot filtering remove fake sessions? Is median listings per session falling, or are sessions slipping from five views to four?

Next, supply or demand. Marketplace is two-sided. Buyers may be arriving less often, or arriving and finding too little to browse. So I'd check active listings and active sellers over the same four weeks before anything else.

Then I'd segment by geography, category, new versus returning buyers, and app versus desktop.

| Hypothesis | Fast check | Response if true |
|---|---|---|
| Supply fell | Active listings and sellers by category | Seller re-engagement campaign |
| Feed ranking changed | Experiment log; relevance and distance of top results | Roll back or retune |
| Entry point moved | Sessions starting from the Marketplace tab | Restore, then test |
| Stricter listing rules | New listings rejected or abandoned | Ease the rule and coach sellers |
| Load time regressed | Image and search latency by build | Performance fix |
| Location data limited | Share of sessions with precise location | Fall back to stated location |
| Seasonal or competitive | Year-on-year curve, rival promotions | Plan, do not patch |

The plan runs 72 hours. Day one: pull the cut matrix, the release list and app performance, and find where the decline concentrates. Day two: experiment log, infrastructure changes, competitor signals. Day three: a root-cause call and a recommendation, which will be a seller campaign, a rollback or a navigation test.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Validates the metric definition before investigating
- Tests supply against demand explicitly instead of assuming a demand problem
- Five or more distinct hypotheses and a time-boxed plan

**Where answers fail.**

- A single hypothesis and a recommendation with no investigation
- Treating a marketplace as one-sided and never looking at listings

**If they push.**

- *The cause is a change that hides listings under 24 hours old to cut spam.* Freshness is core buyer value. I'd replace the blanket delay with risk-based review: trusted sellers publish at once, new or flagged accounts wait. Then measure spam reports and buyer views together.
- *How would you detect this in 48 hours?* A daily health view of supply (new listings, active sellers, sell-through) and demand (sessions, listings viewed, messages sent) by category and region, with alerts against a seasonal baseline.

### Q127. You are the PM for Google Pay. Define the primary metrics you'd track to evaluate the success of a new "split bill" feature.

*Execution and metrics · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Metrics for a social, multi-party feature: whether you measure both sides of a split, completion as well as initiation, the viral loop, and the cost of nagging.

**Clarify first.**

- Friends splitting a meal, or recurring household bills? Assume friends and one-off bills.
- Which market? Assume India.
- Do we measure recipients as well as initiators? Assume yes.

**Outline.**

- Hypothesis: a habit in social moments
- North Star: weekly active split sessions
- Adoption on both sides, and new users
- Completion and time to settle
- Repeat use and retention lift
- Quality: disputes, wrong amounts, reminder fatigue
- Anti-metric

**Model answer.** A split feature works when it becomes the default at the table: people open the app during the meal or the trip, not only at payment. That raises how often they come back and how much they pay through the app. My North Star is weekly active split sessions: user-weeks with at least one split started and fully settled.

| Metric | Definition | Why it matters |
|---|---|---|
| Initiation rate | Monthly users who start a split; 8% within 90 days | Top of funnel; splits need the right moment |
| Recipient pay rate | Requests paid within 48 hours; above 75% | A split nobody pays has failed |
| New users via split | Recipients new to the app who transact within 30 days | The viral loop |
| Completion rate | Splits fully settled within 7 days; above 60% | Partial splits create social friction |
| Time to settle | Median hours to full settlement; under 36 | Clarity and convenience |
| Completion by group size | Fix the flow if groups of 8 or more fall under 40% | Large groups are harder |
| Repeat use | Splits per active splitter; 2.5 a month by month 3 | Habit |
| Retention lift | 90-day retention against matched non-users; 10% higher | Does it keep people |
| Dispute rate | Splits with a contested amount or payment status; under 2% | Confusion |

I'd also track new payees added through a split, which grows the payment graph, and wrong-amount errors: zero for equal splits and under 1% for custom ones.

One guardrail is reminder fatigue: if over 20% of recipients opt out of split reminders, the cadence is too aggressive and people will switch off notifications for the whole app. One anti-metric: I would never optimize reminders sent. The goal is settlement, not noise.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- A habit-formation North Star
- Initiator and recipient both measured, and the viral loop through non-users named
- Notification fatigue as an explicit guardrail

**Where answers fail.**

- Generic payment metrics: transaction value and daily users
- Measuring only the person who starts the split

**If they push.**

- *Completion is 45%, and groups of five or more are the problem.* Test visible status for each person, a deadline set by the initiator, one-tap payment from the notification, and a way for the initiator to close the split and absorb the remainder.
- *Initiators retain 12% better, but recipients without the app face three steps.* Let recipients pay from any UPI app through a link, and ask them to install only after they have paid.

### Q128. You are the PM for a hospital's patient portal app. Appointment booking sessions have increased 20% but completed bookings have decreased 8%. Investigate.

*Execution and metrics · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you read two diverging metrics as a funnel problem, quantify it, and find the step that broke.

**Clarify first.**

- Is a session an app open, or entry into the booking flow? Assume entry into the flow.
- Did the booking flow change in this period? Assume unknown. I'd check.
- All appointment types or some? Assume unknown.

**Outline.**

- Quantify the conversion drop
- Funnel: drop-off by step, now against 30 days ago
- Segment: appointment type, insurer, device, age, new versus returning
- Hypotheses: capacity, insurance check, usability, speed
- Response by cause
- Why capacity is the first suspect

**Model answer.** Sessions up and completions down means demand rose and conversion collapsed. To size it: if 1,000 sessions used to produce 600 bookings, that was 60%. Now 1,200 sessions produce 552, which is 46%. That is a 14-point fall, about a quarter of conversion gone. This is a funnel problem, not a demand problem.

So I'd compare drop-off at each step with 30 days ago: login, appointment type, provider, date and time, insurance check, confirmation. The step with the biggest change is the proximate cause.

Then I'd cut by appointment type, insurer, device, age, and new versus returning patients. One insurer points to verification. Older patients point to a usability change. New patients only points to a step that needs saved data.

| Hypothesis | Evidence | Response |
|---|---|---|
| No slots available | Drop at slot selection; first available date far out | Open capacity; add a waitlist |
| Insurance check failing | Drop at verification, by payer | Let patients proceed as pending and verify at check-in |
| New required field or login step | Drop at one new screen, worse on mobile or for older patients | Roll back, or make it optional |
| Slow slot loading | Latency up after a backend change | Performance fix; cached availability meanwhile |

Because sessions rose 20%, slot scarcity is my first suspect. A campaign or seasonal illness sends more patients into a calendar that has not grown, and they leave when they cannot find a time. The waitlist matters because it keeps those patients instead of asking them to try again.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Quantifies the conversion collapse, not just the fall in bookings
- Reads rising sessions with falling completions as a funnel issue
- Names slot scarcity and insurance verification as the likeliest causes, with a waitlist as a response

**Where answers fail.**

- Treating it as a demand problem
- One cause, usually a design change, with no funnel and no segments

**If they push.**

- *70% of drop-off is at slot selection, mostly when the first slot is over 21 days away.* That is a capacity problem the app cannot hide. Show the earliest slot across all providers and sites, offer telehealth, add a waitlist that rebooks from cancellations, and take the wait-time data to operations.
- *The slot service now takes 3.5 seconds, up from 0.8, and the fix needs six weeks.* Do both. In the interim, load providers first, fetch slots lazily, cache availability briefly and show progress. Fund the real fix, because every booking pays that delay.

### Q129. You are a PM at a payments company. Define the metric framework for a real-time payment fraud detection system: North Star and key operational metrics.

*Execution and metrics · Stretch · Tailored*

**What they are testing.** Metrics for a decision system with two costly kinds of error, plus the operational, model-health and fairness measures that keep it safe in production. Story to draw on: S7.

**Clarify first.**

- Scoring before authorization, or after? Assume before, in real time.
- Which instruments? Assume online card payments and UPI.

**Outline.**

- The tension: missed fraud versus false declines
- North Star: fraud-adjusted approval rate
- Detection quality: precision, recall, false positive rate
- Business impact: fraud loss and false-decline cost
- Operations: latency, availability, fallback
- Model health: drift, freshness, novel patterns
- Fairness guardrail

**Model answer.** A fraud model fails in two ways. Miss fraud and you pay the losses. Decline a good customer and you lose the sale and some future spend. The North Star has to punish both, so I'd use fraud-adjusted approval rate: legitimate transactions approved, divided by all transactions attempted. A system that approves 99% of attempts but lets 2 points of fraud through scores 97. One that approves 98.5% with 0.1 points of fraud scores 98.4 and is better.

| Metric | Definition | Why it matters |
|---|---|---|
| Precision | Flagged transactions that are truly fraud; above 90% | Low precision means false declines |
| Recall | Fraud that is caught; above 85% | Missed fraud is direct loss |
| False positive rate | Legitimate transactions declined; under 0.3% | At $500B of volume, 0.5% is $2.5B of good sales refused |
| Fraud loss rate | Chargebacks and write-offs over volume; under 0.05% | The outcome |
| False-decline cost | False positive rate times legitimate volume | Stops "fixing" fraud by declining everyone |
| Latency and availability | 99th percentile under 80 ms for cards and 50 ms for UPI; 99.99% uptime | Must fit the authorization window; downtime forces approve-all or decline-all |
| Drift and freshness | 90th-percentile score shift over 7 days, alert at 5%; lag on velocity, device and location features, alert above 5 minutes | Stale model or stale signals |

As summaries I'd keep F1, about 0.87 at those targets, and AUC above 0.97. A latency breach falls back to rules, and I'd track how often. The share of fraud from unseen patterns tells me when to retrain.

One fairness guardrail: if any group, by region or device type, is falsely declined at more than twice the overall rate, I'd run a bias audit.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- A North Star that captures both catching fraud and approving good customers
- Fraud loss and false-decline cost measured separately
- Latency targets tied to the authorization window, and a fairness guardrail

**Where answers fail.**

- Accuracy and a confusion matrix with no payments context
- Precision and recall with no false-decline cost, latency or availability

**If they push.**

- *False positives jump from 0.3% to 0.8% in 48 hours on international payments, and the fraud pattern is real.* Keep the global threshold. Add a narrow rule for the pattern, send borderline international payments to extra authentication instead of declining, and watch both rates hourly.
- *A large merchant reports 1.2% wrongly declined during a sale.* Sale traffic looks like fraud: new devices, high velocity. Register sale events in advance, set merchant-level thresholds for the window, and give the merchant a fast appeal path.

### Q130. You are the PM for a mental health app offering daily mood tracking and CBT exercises. Define the long-term engagement and clinical effectiveness metrics.

*Execution and metrics · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you can measure a product whose goal is not engagement: clinical outcomes on validated scales, non-negotiable safety, and the ethics of retention.

**Clarify first.**

- Regulated digital therapeutic or wellness app? Assume a wellness app held to clinical standards.
- Which outcome? Assume fewer symptoms of depression and anxiety.
- Is there a crisis pathway? Assume yes, and it is in scope.

**Outline.**

- The tension: engaged is not the same as improving
- Engagement: retention, weekly exercises completed
- Clinical outcomes on validated scales
- Safety: crisis response, adverse events
- Long-term change: independence, relapse
- Ethics guardrails

**Model answer.** Engagement and effectiveness are different things here. Someone can log mood daily and not improve. Someone else improves and uses the app less, which looks like churn and is in fact success. So I'd measure the two separately and never let one stand in for the other.

| Metric | Definition | Why it matters |
|---|---|---|
| 90-day retention | New users still active weekly; above 35% | Outcomes need sustained use |
| Weekly exercise rate | Retained users completing a CBT exercise each week, above 60%; started exercises finished, above 75% | Exercises, not mood logs, are the active ingredient |
| Symptom improvement | Clinically meaningful drop at 8 weeks: 5 points on PHQ-9, 4 on GAD-7; target 40% of engaged users | Primary success measure |
| Wellbeing trend | Monthly WHO-5 for users below clinical thresholds | Outcome for wellness users |
| Mood variability | Standard deviation of daily mood over 30 days | Early clinical signal |
| Self-efficacy | Monthly confidence in managing one's own mental health | CBT builds skill, not dependence |
| Crisis response | Crisis signals that trigger the resource protocol within 60 seconds; 100% | Non-negotiable |
| Adverse events | Exercises followed by reported harm within 48 hours; under 0.5% | Do no harm |
| Independence rate | Users who stop daily use and stay well | The real goal |
| Relapse rate | Improved at 8 weeks, back at baseline at 6 months | Skills are not carrying over |

Streak length I'd monitor but never target. A crisis signal is any positive answer to the self-harm item on PHQ-9, or crisis language in a journal.

Three ethics rules. Never trade clinical benefit for streaks or notifications. No outcome data in marketing without consent. Never position the app as a replacement for professional care.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Names the tension between engagement and clinical effectiveness
- Uses validated scales (PHQ-9, GAD-7, WHO-5) for outcomes
- Crisis safety as non-negotiable at 100%, and independence rate as the ultimate success measure

**Where answers fail.**

- Daily users, session time, streaks and NPS
- No safety metrics and no clinical outcome

**If they push.**

- *Retention is 45% but only 28% of retained users improve.* Engaged, not improving: logging without doing the work, or content that does not fit their severity. I'd check exercise rates among non-improvers, route them to other modules or a clinician, and stop rewarding logging alone.
- *More severe users finish fewer exercises but gain more from each one.* Shorter first exercises, lower-effort starting points, human support for the most severe, and pacing set by the baseline score.

### Q131. You are the PM for a new Amazon feature: "Delivery by Drone" in eligible zip codes. Define the metrics framework for a controlled pilot.

*Execution and metrics · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Pilot design where safety is a precondition, not a target, alongside operational viability, customer value, unit economics and regulatory compliance.

**Clarify first.**

- What is the scope? Assume a few zip codes, light packages and a limited flight radius.
- What is the promise? Assume delivery within 30 minutes of dispatch.
- What is the pilot for? Assume safety and viability first, customer value second.

**Outline.**

- Three questions in order: safe, viable, valued
- Safety preconditions with zero tolerance
- Operational quality: on time, first attempt, weather, battery
- Customer experience: satisfaction over time, repeat choice
- Economics: cost per delivery, utilization
- Compliance: reporting and privacy

**Model answer.** I'd judge the pilot on three questions, in order. Is it safe? Safety is a condition for operating, not a number to optimize. Is it viable in real weather and airspace? Do customers value it enough to choose it again?

| Metric | Definition | Why it matters |
|---|---|---|
| Flight incidents | Path deviations, signal loss over 5 seconds or unplanned landings, per 1,000 flight hours; zero causing damage, injury or a corridor breach | Any such event suspends the pilot |
| Airspace compliance | Flights inside approved corridors; 100% | A breach means suspension and a report |
| Drops and clear-zone errors | Packages released above 10 meters; landings where a person or animal was missed; zero | Ground safety and liability |
| On time, first attempt | Delivered within 30 minutes, above 85% in normal weather; no van fallback, above 80% | The promise, and viability |
| Weather aborts and battery | Dispatches cancelled for weather; field range against the maker's figure | The real operating envelope and radius |
| Satisfaction | Rating above 4.6 of 5, tracked at the 3rd, 5th and 10th delivery | Novelty fades |
| Repeat choice | Customers who pick drone for their next eligible order; above 40% | Real preference |
| Cost per delivery | All-in cost over deliveries, against an assumed van cost of $8 to 12 | Path to parity |

I'd also track landing accuracy within 3 meters, redelivery rate, and fleet utilization, where under 40% means the area is too sparse. For compliance: every reportable incident filed on time, and aerial imagery of homes deleted within 24 hours.

A cost of $25 a delivery is acceptable at pilot scale, if the pilot states the volume at which it reaches van parity.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Safety metrics framed as preconditions for operating, not targets to optimize
- Cost per delivery with a break-even volume
- Regulatory compliance covered, including privacy of aerial imagery

**Where answers fail.**

- Customer delight and delivery speed only
- Safety mentioned with no thresholds and no consequence

**If they push.**

- *At week 4, weather aborts are 35% and batteries run 18% under spec in the cold.* Safety is clean, so I would not suspend. Shrink the radius to the real range, set a seasonal window and make van fallback automatic. A pilot that finds the limits has done its job.
- *What would justify the next 20 zip codes?* Zero serious safety events, on-time above 85%, first-attempt success above 80%, repeat choice above 40%, a cost curve that reaches van parity at a named volume, and the regulator's sign-off.

### Q132. You are a PM at a neobank. Define key metrics for a credit card for young professionals (age 22–30) with no credit history in India.

*Execution and metrics · Standard · Tailored*

**What they are testing.** Metrics for a credit product that must serve inclusion and stay solvent: an outcome North Star, risk measures set for new-to-credit users, and protection against debt traps. Story to draw on: S5.

**Clarify first.**

- Secured or unsecured? Assume unsecured, underwritten on alternative data.
- What is the main goal? Assume credit building with sound unit economics.

**Outline.**

- North Star: credit graduation rate
- Acquisition and activation
- Credit quality: late payments, losses, early stress
- Credit-building outcomes, reporting accuracy
- Spend, revenue and lifetime value
- Guardrails against debt traps

**Model answer.** The product has two jobs: bring people with no credit history into formal credit, and do it profitably. One metric captures both: credit graduation rate, the share of cardholders who reach a bureau score of 700 or more after 12 months, which I'd treat as the door to mainstream credit. Only customers who repay well get there, so a high rate means inclusion and credit quality together.

| Metric | Definition | Why it matters |
|---|---|---|
| Credit graduation rate | Cardholders at a score of 700 or more after 12 months; target 55% | North Star |
| Approval rate | Applications approved; 40 to 55% | Reach, without unaffordable limits |
| Activation and use | Approved users who transact within 30 days, above 70%; accounts active each month, above 70% | No spend means no history and no revenue |
| 30 days past due | Accounts 30 or more days late; under 4% at 6 months and 6% at 12 | Primary risk measure |
| Net credit loss | Write-offs over average receivables; under 2.5% at 12 months | Ultimate risk |
| Early stress | Accounts paying only the minimum, alert above 30%; accounts using over 80% of the limit | Over-extension, or limits set wrong |
| Bureau reporting errors | Accounts misreported; under 0.5% | Our error damages their score |
| Lifetime value | 24-month net revenue per account; above ₹4,000, against ₹800 to 1,200 to acquire | Unit economics |

Supporting numbers: every cardholder has a bureau score within six months. Spend of ₹8,000 to 15,000 a month, about 30% of income at ₹3 to 6 lakh a year. Interchange of ₹150 to 250 per active account at ₹10,000 of spend. Net interest margin above 8%.

Two guardrails. If an account is 30 days late for three months running, the limit freezes automatically and the app starts a financial health check. Interest and fees are shown plainly in Hindi and English.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Credit graduation rate as a North Star covering inclusion and credit quality at once
- Late-payment and loss targets calibrated for new-to-credit borrowers
- A debt-trap guardrail with an automatic trigger

**Where answers fail.**

- Acquisition and spend first, with credit quality as an afterthought
- Generic fintech metrics and no bureau outcome

**If they push.**

- *At 6 months, 7% of accounts are 30 days late, concentrated below ₹25,000 monthly income.* I would not exit the segment. Lower starting limits, make autopay the default, raise limits only after on-time payments, and offer a secured version.
- *A bureau starts scoring UPI and rent history.* That is data we hold. Report it, use it in underwriting, and show customers how their payment behavior moves their score.

### Q133. You are the PM for YouTube's Skip Ad button. A proposal removes "skip after 5 seconds" for all non-premium videos. Walk through the metrics implications.

*Execution and metrics · Stretch*

**What they are testing.** Second-order thinking across stakeholders: whether you see past the first-order revenue gain, quantify both directions, and offer something better than yes or no.

**Clarify first.**

- Who proposes it and why? Assume the ads team, for revenue.
- All ad lengths? Assume every skippable in-stream ad.
- Global launch or a test? Assume the proposal is global. I'd want a test.

**Outline.**

- Direct ad revenue: more billable views, lower bids
- Viewers: abandonment, watch time, return rate
- Creators: views against revenue per view
- Advertisers: completion against creative quality
- Premium: more upgrades, a thinner ad audience
- Recommendation and the test metrics

**Model answer.** Removing skip touches four groups: viewers, advertisers, creators and YouTube. Short-term revenue probably rises. Long-term platform health probably falls. All percentages below are my assumptions.

| Stakeholder | Likely effect | Metric to watch |
|---|---|---|
| YouTube ad revenue | If 35% of skippable ads are watched through today, billable views could almost triple. Bids then fall, because a forced view is worth less. Net lift of 20 to 40%, fading | Revenue per session, cost-per-view bids |
| Viewers | Short ads cost little. With forced 30-second ads, 15 to 25% of sessions end at the ad | Abandonment at ad, watch time, 7-day return |
| Creators | Higher revenue per thousand views, fewer views | Creator earnings, not rate alone |
| Advertisers | Guaranteed completion helps brand campaigns. Weak ads now annoy at full length | Ad recall, complaint rate |
| Premium | Ad-free is worth more: sign-ups up 8 to 15% in a quarter. The best customers leave the ad audience | Premium conversion, value of remaining audience |

Viewer abandonment is the number I'd fear most, because heavy users see the most ads and have the most alternatives.

The creator arithmetic decides the politics. If views fall 10 to 15% and revenue per thousand views rises 30%, creators earn 10 to 17% more. Once advertisers cut bids and the lift is only 5%, a 15% fall in views means 11% less. Break-even for a 30% lift is a 23% fall in views.

My recommendation: do not remove skip everywhere. Test a slightly longer wait before skip on short ads. Keep forced viewing to short formats, which YouTube already sells. Test rewarded viewing, where watching a full ad earns credit toward Premium. Run all three as controlled experiments on the same metric set first.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- All four stakeholder groups analyzed
- Revenue treated as two-directional: more completed views against lower bids
- Watch-time abandonment named as the most dangerous metric, and an alternative to a yes-or-no decision

**Where answers fail.**

- "Remove skip, earn more" with no second-order effects
- Effects described with no numbers, so nothing can be compared

**If they push.**

- *A test shows ad revenue up 28% per session, session length down 19% and 7-day retention down 12%. Launch?* No. With 12% fewer returning viewers the gain is about 13% in week one. The retention loss compounds every week while the revenue lift decays as bids adjust.
- *Top creators threaten to move to a rival.* I'd check how much watch time they represent and whether they have real alternatives. It reinforces the decision either way: creators are the supply.

### Q134. You are the PM for a new B2B SaaS product (AI-powered legal contract review). Define the success metrics for the first year post-launch.

*Execution and metrics · Standard*

**What they are testing.** First-year success for an AI product in a high-stakes domain: whether you know which model error matters more, and can tie model quality to customer value and retention.

**Clarify first.**

- Who is the customer? Assume in-house legal teams at mid-size and large companies.
- What does the AI do? Assume it flags risks and extracts clauses against a standard playbook.

**Outline.**

- Success definition: faster review, no loss of rigor
- AI quality: recall first, then precision, classification, speed
- Adoption: time to first review, active seats, action on reports
- Customer impact: attorney time, turnaround, revision rounds
- Revenue and retention
- Guardrails: missed severe risks, trust

**Model answer.** The product succeeds if it cuts the time and cost of review without lowering the quality of risk-spotting. On model quality, recall matters more than precision. A false alarm costs an attorney a minute. A missed unlimited-liability clause costs the customer real money. And a single accuracy figure misleads, because most clauses are not risks.

| Metric | Definition | Why it matters |
|---|---|---|
| Recall | Real risks the AI flags, against a reference human review; above 92% | The critical number |
| Precision | Flags attorneys confirm as real; above 85% | Too many false alarms and people stop reading |
| Clause classification | Right clause type across 20 core types; above 97% | Prerequisite for flagging |
| Review speed | A 15 to 25 page contract in under 90 seconds, against 2 to 4 hours by hand | Core value proposition |
| Adoption | First contract within 5 business days of activation; over 60% of seats active each month | Leading retention signal; seasonal work, so monthly not weekly |
| Report action rate | Reports where the user acts on at least one flag | Trust in the output |
| Customer impact | Customer-reported cut in attorney hours per contract, above 50%; receipt-to-signature time 30% faster | The return the buyer shows the CFO |
| Net revenue retention | Above 110%, with gross retention above 90% | Expansion beats churn |

I'd also track revision rounds, aiming for 20% fewer, acquisition-cost payback under 18 months, and recurring revenue against plan. Every lost customer gets an exit interview.

Two guardrails. Missed high-severity risks, such as unlimited liability: target zero, each one a product incident with a root-cause review. And a trust score: power users who rely on the AI as their primary review, above 50% by month nine.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Recall named as the critical quality metric, with separate targets for recall and precision
- Missed high-severity risk as a zero-tolerance guardrail
- Customer-reported attorney time saved as the primary business impact

**Where answers fail.**

- One "accuracy" number for the model
- Daily users, revenue and NPS with nothing specific to contract review

**If they push.**

- *Recall is 89% and the AI missed a termination clause in a $10 million contract.* Own it with the customer, search their other contracts for the same miss, add the clause type to the test set and retrain. High-severity recall becomes a release gate.
- *A customer wants reviews against its own playbook.* Then quality is measured per customer: recall and precision against their positions, plus setup time and playbook coverage.

### Q135. You are a PM at a bill-payment and expense platform for Indian SMEs. Define metrics for an "AI CFO Assistant" that gives automated spending recommendations.

*Execution and metrics · Stretch · Tailored*

**What they are testing.** Metrics for an AI recommendation feature in layers: attention, quality, action and outcome, with a hard guardrail on numerical trust. Story to draw on: S8, S6.

**Clarify first.**

- Proactive or reactive? Assume both, proactive first.
- What counts as acted on? Assume an action in the platform within 14 days, or a self-report outside it.

**Outline.**

- Value hypothesis: specific advice that owners act on
- Engagement: activation, weekly use, read rate
- Recommendation quality: relevance, accuracy, freshness, variety
- Action: in-platform and self-reported
- Outcomes: savings, forecast accuracy, receivables, retention
- Guardrails: numerical errors, fatigue

**Model answer.** Most small business owners in India have no finance head and manage cash by instinct. The assistant earns its place if it gives specific advice, such as "₹8 lakh is due in 14 days and only ₹5.2 lakh is expected in", owners act on it, and their finances improve. So I'd measure four layers, with action rate as the primary metric. Advice nobody acts on creates no value.

| Metric | Definition | Why it matters |
|---|---|---|
| Activation | Eligible accounts, with 3 months of history, that open the assistant; 40% in 60 days | Reach |
| Weekly use and reads | Activated users engaging each week, 30%; pushed recommendations read, above 55% | Attention; use clusters at month-end and tax weeks |
| Relevance | Share rated "relevant" in one tap; above 75% | Quality as users see it |
| Accuracy and freshness | Correct math in 100 audited recommendations a month, above 98%; data under 7 days old, above 90% | One wrong or stale number ends trust |
| Action rate | Relevant recommendations acted on in the platform within 14 days; above 25% | Primary success metric |
| Forecast accuracy | 14-day cash forecasts within 15% of actual; above 85% | Trust in alerts |
| SME outcomes | Confirmed savings of ₹8,000 to 15,000 a month per acting account; days sales outstanding down 10 days | Did finances improve |
| Retention lift | 6-month retention against matched non-users; 8% higher | Platform payoff |

I'd also watch variety, with four recommendation types above 10% each by month six, self-reported action outside the platform at 30%, and upgrade to premium at 12%.

Two guardrails. Any recommendation type with an arithmetic error above ₹500 is pulled until it is fixed. And if over 10% of users switch off proactive recommendations within 60 days, frequency or relevance is wrong.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Engagement, quality and action measured as distinct layers
- Numerical accuracy as a zero-tolerance guardrail with a specific trigger
- Receivables improvement as an SME outcome, and retention lift as the platform's payoff

**Where answers fail.**

- Engagement only: opens, reads, daily users
- "Accuracy" with no method for measuring it

**If they push.**

- *Relevance is 78% but action rate is 11%.* Three hypotheses. Acting takes too many steps, so add one-tap actions. The advice is right but small, so rank by rupee impact. Or the owner does not pay the bills, so route it to whoever does.
- *An owner prepays a vendor on our advice and the vendor goes bankrupt.* Support the customer, review how the advice was framed, and add counterparty and lock-in risk to any recommendation that commits cash.

# Part VIII — Technical for PMs

## 8. Technical questions

These ten questions test whether you can explain how a system works at the level a PM needs, then turn that into a product decision. Nobody expects code. They expect the right moving parts in the right order, and the one constraint that drives the trade-off: latency, consistency, cost, permission or statistical power.

Use each question in two passes. First rehearse the explanation from the outline until you can give it in ninety seconds without notes. Then practice the decision: what you would choose, what you give up and what you would measure. Three questions are marked Tailored and name the story to bridge to when asked for real experience. Facts about products and standards are as of October 2026.

### Q136. Explain how a recommendation system works and how you'd improve the recommendations on Netflix's home screen.

*Technical for PMs · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you can explain a multi-stage recommender in plain words, then propose improvements tied to a mechanism and a testable metric.

**Clarify first.**

- Which users? I'll assume established members with viewing history, and touch on cold start.
- What are we optimizing? I'll assume satisfied viewing, meaning titles started and finished, not raw clicks.

**Outline.**

- Three families: collaborative, content-based, hybrid
- Pipeline: candidate generation, ranking, re-ranking with business rules
- Signals: behavior, thumbs, context
- Weaknesses: session intent, shared profiles, stale rows, discovery
- Three improvements, each with an A/B test
- Metrics, and the click-versus-watch trap

**Model answer.** A recommender ranks the items a person is most likely to enjoy. Collaborative filtering says people who watched what you watched also watched this. It needs no understanding of the content, but it fails for brand-new titles. Content-based filtering matches attributes such as genre and cast. It handles new titles but keeps you in a bubble.

Large services combine both in a pipeline. Candidate generation narrows a catalog of thousands to a few hundred, using embeddings learned from viewing behavior. A ranking model scores those candidates for this member in this context: device, time of day, recent viewing. Re-ranking then applies freshness, diversity and business rules such as featuring originals. Netflix publicly describes its home page as personalized rows ordered using viewing history, similar members, title attributes and context; I'll assume this pipeline sits behind it.

| Weakness | Improvement | Test metric |
|---|---|---|
| Same rows for a 20-minute break and a three-hour evening | Predict session length from device, time and last session; match title length | Completion rate |
| Shared profiles blur two people's taste | Detect the switch from device and time patterns; offer a "watching together" mode | Thumbs-up rate |
| Titles I keep skipping stay on top | Decay any title passed over three or more times in 30 days | Home-screen play rate |

Two smaller changes: a labeled "expand your taste" row whose ranker deliberately explores, and removing anything untouched for 30 days from Continue Watching unless I was most of the way through the season.

My primary metrics are hours of satisfied viewing per session, completion rate and play rate from the home screen. If a new ranker lifts clicks but cuts hours watched, it is producing false starts and I would not ship it.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- The three stages in order, including the business-rule layer
- At least three weaknesses, each with a technical cause
- Improvements framed as A/B tests with a named metric

**Where answers fail.**

- "Show better recommendations," with no mechanism
- Only UI changes; nothing on signals, ranking or measurement

**If they push.**

- *A new title has zero viewing history.* Start from content attributes and the audience of similar titles, give it bounded exploration traffic, and let early completion data take over within days.

### Q137. Explain how a payment authorization system works end-to-end, and where in that flow AI fraud detection fits.

*Technical for PMs · Standard · Tailored*

**What they are testing.** Whether you know the parties in a card authorization, the real-time constraint, and that the fraud threshold is a business decision.

**Frame it.** I'll walk through an online card payment at PM depth, then place the two fraud-scoring points and the trade-offs.

**Outline.**

- Five parties: cardholder, merchant, acquirer, network, issuer
- Tokenize, authenticate, authorize, then clear and settle
- Fraud scoring at the network and at the issuer
- The latency budget shapes the model
- Three outcomes: approve, decline, step-up
- Threshold: false declines against fraud losses

**Model answer.** Five parties are involved: cardholder, merchant, the merchant's acquirer, the card network and the cardholder's issuer.

At checkout the gateway replaces the card number with a token, so the merchant never stores it. Where risk or regulation calls for it, the merchant first runs 3-D Secure authentication: the issuer either passes the shopper silently on risk data or challenges with a one-time code or biometric. The acquirer then sends an authorization request through the network, which routes it to the issuer using the first six or eight digits of the card number. The issuer approves or declines within a second or two. Authorization is only a hold; the money moves later, in clearing and settlement.

Fraud models sit at two points. The network scores the transaction from what it sees across all issuers and merchants (velocity, location, merchant category, device) and attaches a risk score. The issuer then decides, because it knows the cardholder's history and balance, using its own model, the network's score and hard rules such as block lists.

Two constraints matter to a PM. Latency: the issuer has a fraction of a second, and scoring gets tens of milliseconds of it, so gradient-boosted trees on precomputed features usually beat a heavier model. Freshness: velocity features must be current to the minute, or the model is blind during a fast attack.

The decision has three outcomes. Low risk is approved, high risk is declined, and the uncertain band gets step-up authentication: a 3-D Secure challenge, or a soft decline that tells the merchant to retry with one. Where I set those thresholds is a business choice: every false decline costs a sale and some trust, every miss is a fraud loss, and I choose the point with the lowest total cost.

My compliance and risk work (S7) followed the same rule: automate, but keep deterministic controls and an audit trail around the decision.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- All five parties, with authorization separated from settlement
- The issuer's real-time budget as the constraint on model choice
- Step-up as a third outcome; the threshold as a business decision

**Where answers fail.**

- "The bank's algorithm checks it," with no parties or latency
- Treating precision and recall as a data-science setting

**If they push.**

- *Card testing with tiny payments is surging.* Add velocity features per merchant, device and card range for low-value attempts, and rate-limit at the gateway.

### Q138. What is an API? Explain how REST APIs work and describe a scenario where a PM must decide based on API design trade-offs.

*Technical for PMs · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Plain-language fluency with APIs, and whether you see versioning, limits and delivery guarantees as product decisions.

**Frame it.** The concept with one analogy, REST mechanics in a minute, then one payments decision in depth.

**Outline.**

- API as a contract; the menu analogy
- REST: resources, URLs, HTTP methods, stateless, status codes
- Four trade-offs: granularity, versioning, rate limits, authentication
- Scenario: polling versus webhooks for payment status
- Decision: at-least-once delivery, idempotent consumers, polling kept
- Metrics

**Model answer.** An API is a contract that lets one system ask another to do something without knowing how it works inside. Think of a restaurant menu: it lists what you can order and what you get back, and the kitchen stays hidden.

REST is a style for APIs over HTTP. Everything is a resource with a URL, and the HTTP method says what to do with it: GET reads, POST creates, PUT replaces, PATCH updates some fields and DELETE removes. So `GET /accounts/123` returns that account. REST is stateless: each request carries everything needed to process it, so any server can handle it, which is why it scales. Responses are usually JSON with a status code, such as 200 OK, 401 not authenticated, 404 not found and 429 too many requests.

Four design choices are mine as much as engineering's.

| Choice | Trade-off |
|---|---|
| Granularity | One large response or many small calls; matters on mobile |
| Versioning | Adding an optional field is safe; removing or renaming one breaks partners and needs a new version |
| Rate limits | Tiers by customer segment, balancing fairness and cost |
| Authentication | Static API keys, short-lived OAuth tokens, or mutual TLS for high-security partners |

My scenario: I run a payments API. A large e-commerce customer polls us every five seconds for transaction status and asks for webhooks, where we push each status change to them. Webhooks are faster and cheaper for both sides, but delivery is hard. Their endpoint will sometimes be down, so we need retries with exponential backoff and a dead-letter queue. Exactly-once delivery is not realistic. At-least-once is, so the customer will sometimes receive duplicates and must handle events idempotently.

I'd ship at-least-once webhooks, document the idempotency requirement with sample code, sign each event, and keep polling for customers who can't build that yet. I'd track delivery success against a 99.9 percent target, retry-queue depth with an alert at 10,000 pending events, and webhook adoption.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Statelessness, methods and status codes explained correctly
- Three or more trade-offs a PM owns
- Idempotency named as the cost of at-least-once delivery

**Where answers fail.**

- Describing an API as a website or a database
- "Stability versus flexibility," with no scenario

**If they push.**

- *A customer's endpoint errors during their deploys and 1.5% of webhooks fail.* I still own the outcome: retry over a longer window, offer a replay endpoint, and alert them when their error rate climbs.

### Q139. What is A/B testing, and how would you design an A/B test for a new checkout flow on an e-commerce site?

*Technical for PMs · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you can design a trustworthy experiment: hypothesis, unit, sample size, guardrails and a decision rule fixed before the data arrives.

**Clarify first.**

- What changes? I'll assume a one-page checkout replacing a three-step flow.
- What lift is worth shipping? I'll assume completion is 72% today and 2 points is the smallest gain worth the build.

**Outline.**

- What an A/B test is, and why it beats before-and-after
- Significance, power, minimum detectable effect, sample size
