- Hypothesis, primary metric, user-level randomization
- Guardrails and a sample-ratio check
- Two full weeks; no peeking
- A decision rule for each outcome

**Model answer.** An A/B test randomly splits users into a control group that keeps today's experience and a treatment group that gets the change. Both run at once, so a difference in the metric comes from the change, not the day of the week.

Four numbers set the design. Significance: I use p below 0.05, meaning that if the change truly did nothing, a gap this large would show up less than 5 percent of the time. It is not the probability that the change works. Power: an 80 percent chance of detecting a real effect. Minimum detectable effect: 2 points, from 72 to 74 percent. Sample size follows from those three: about 7,750 users per arm, 15,500 in total.

My hypothesis: one-page checkout raises completion, the share of users who start checkout and finish, by at least 2 points. I randomize by user, with a persistent cookie for guests, because people abandon and come back; splitting by session would show one person both versions.

| Guardrail | Why |
|---|---|
| Average order value | Conversion must not rise by shrinking baskets |
| Payment error rate | The new flow must not break payments |
| Time to complete | Should fall, not rise |
| Sample ratio | A split far from 50/50 means assignment is broken |

Say the site sees 50,000 checkout starts a week. A 50/50 split fills the sample in about two days. I still run two full weeks, to cover weekly cycles and let novelty fade, and I don't stop early, because repeated peeking inflates false positives.

The decision rule is written beforehand. A significant win with clean guardrails: ship. A significant loss: don't ship, and find the step where users drop. Inconclusive: I have not proved there is no effect, so I extend the test or decide on cost and roadmap fit. A win with a guardrail regression: fix and retest.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Significance, power, effect size and sample size connected
- User-level randomization, with the reason
- Guardrails and the peeking problem named before launch

**Where answers fail.**

- "Show half the users the new design and see which sells more"
- Stopping the first day the result turns significant

**If they push.**

- *Mobile only, at 30% of traffic?* The sample per arm is the same, so it fills in about a week, not two days.

### Q140. Explain the concept of "eventual consistency" in distributed systems and describe a situation where a PM must decide between eventual consistency and strong consistency.

*Technical for PMs · Stretch*

**What they are testing.** Whether you can decide, feature by feature, where stale data is harmless and where it costs money.

**Frame it.** Definitions in plain words, the CAP trade-off in two sentences, then product cases and a three-question rule.

**Outline.**

- Many replicas; the question is when they agree
- Strong: every read sees the latest write, at a latency and availability cost
- Eventual: fast and available, briefly stale
- CAP: during a partition, choose consistency or availability
- Cases: balances, like counts, scarce inventory, profile photos
- Rule: cost of a stale read, write volume, can we compensate

**Model answer.** Systems at scale keep copies of data on many servers. Consistency is about when those copies agree.

With strong consistency, once a write succeeds every later read returns it, from any server, as if there were one machine. To get that, a write is confirmed only after a quorum of replicas has it. That coordination adds latency, and if replicas can't reach each other the system refuses requests. With eventual consistency a write is confirmed at once and spreads to the other copies afterward. It is fast and stays available, but for milliseconds to seconds different servers can give different answers.

The CAP theorem frames the choice: network partitions will happen, and during one you must pick consistency or availability. Even without a partition, stronger consistency costs latency.

| Case | A stale read costs | Choice |
|---|---|---|
| Debiting a balance | Double spend | Strong, always |
| Like counter | Off by one for a moment | Eventual |
| Last concert tickets | Oversell that can't be restocked | Strong at checkout |
| Profile photo | Old photo for seconds | Eventual |

Take the payment. A customer sends ₹50,000 and immediately sends it again. If the second request reads a balance that has not yet seen the first debit, both succeed on money that covers one. Tens of milliseconds of coordination is a cheap price to prevent that. A like count is the opposite: nobody is harmed if two people see 1,247 and 1,248 for a moment, and at that volume coordination would be expensive.

So I ask three questions about each piece of data. What does a stale read cost: money or cosmetics? How frequent are the writes? And if the stale window causes a problem, can the business recover with a refund or a restock? Money and scarce inventory get strong consistency. Counters, feeds, profiles and restockable inventory get eventual consistency, and users should always see their own writes.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Both models explained, with the coordination cost
- CAP used correctly: the choice bites during partitions
- Different features given different models, by a stated rule

**Where answers fail.**

- "Data is synced across servers," with no trade-off
- One consistency model for the whole product

**If they push.**

- *A slightly stale balance display with a strongly consistent debit?* Acceptable, if the debit path checks the true balance. To avoid confusion I'd show "updating" after a payment.

### Q141. Explain how a Large Language Model (LLM) works at a conceptual level, and the key trade-offs a PM faces between fine-tuning and Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG).

*Technical for PMs · Stretch · Tailored*

**What they are testing.** Whether you can explain an LLM without jargon, and choose how to adapt one on freshness, cost, control and risk.

**Frame it.** How the model works, the adaptation options in order of cost, then one decision.

**Outline.**

- Next-token prediction; transformer and attention; two training phases
- Knowledge is frozen; fluent is not the same as correct
- Ladder: prompt and context, retrieval, fine-tuning
- Rule: retrieval for knowledge, fine-tuning for behavior
- Scenario: merchant support assistant chooses retrieval

**Model answer.** An LLM is a neural network trained to predict the next token, a word or word-piece, given everything before it. Its architecture is the transformer, introduced in 2017, whose attention mechanism lets each token weigh every other token in the input, so it tracks long-range context. Pre-training on a huge body of text teaches language and world knowledge; post-training with curated examples and human feedback teaches it to follow instructions and behave safely. Two consequences matter: its knowledge stops at the training cutoff, and it produces plausible text, which is not always true.

To adapt a model I climb a ladder. First, prompting: instructions, examples and relevant material in the context. Second, retrieval-augmented generation: search a knowledge base at question time and pass the best passages to the model. Third, fine-tuning: continue training on my own examples so the weights change.

| Dimension | Fine-tuning | RAG |
|---|---|---|
| Fresh knowledge | Frozen until retrained | Update the index |
| Facts and citations | Unreliable for facts | Grounded and citable |
| Style and format control | Strong | Moderate |
| Cost | Labeled data and training up front; can be cheaper per request | Indexing pipeline; longer prompts per request |
| Upkeep | Redo when the base model changes | Keep content current |

My rule: retrieval when the model lacks knowledge, fine-tuning when it lacks a behavior.

Take a merchant support assistant at a payments company, answering on payouts, disputes and compliance. Policies change monthly, and a wrong answer can cost a merchant a valid dispute. I choose RAG. We update documents instead of retraining, every answer cites the policy and its date, and the assistant says "I can't find that" when retrieval comes back empty. I evaluate retrieval and answers separately, because a wrong passage still produces a wrong answer. I'd fine-tune only if prompting could not hold the tone and format.

That is the boundary from my merchant operations assistant work (S8): the model interprets the question and retrieves evidence from approved systems, and it is never the authority.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Transformer and attention explained without equations
- Freshness as RAG's advantage; behavior as fine-tuning's
- A scenario where the cost of a wrong answer drives the choice

**Where answers fail.**

- "AI trained on lots of text," then a coin-flip between methods
- Confusing fine-tuning with prompt engineering

**If they push.**

- *A competitor says their fine-tuned model "learned all our policies."* I'd ask for accuracy on last month's policy changes, with citations. Fine-tuning does not reliably store facts or show a source.

### Q142. What is database indexing, and how would you use this knowledge as a PM to make product decisions?

*Technical for PMs · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you understand why some queries are fast and others slow, the write cost of indexes, and how that shapes scoping with engineering.

**Frame it.** The book-index analogy, the read-write trade-off, then four product situations and what I'd say to engineering.

**Outline.**

- Index: a sorted lookup structure (B-tree) pointing to rows
- Full scan versus index lookup
- Cost: every write updates every index, plus storage
- Read-heavy dashboard: composite index on the common filter
- Write-heavy ledger: few indexes, a replica for reporting
- Text search: a separate inverted index
- My job: name the critical queries and respect the cost

**Model answer.** An index is a sorted lookup structure, usually a B-tree, that maps column values to where the rows live. Think of the index at the back of a book: you look up "UPI" and jump to those pages without reading all 500.

Say a transactions table holds 500 million rows and a merchant asks for their payments since January. Without an index the database scans every row, which takes many seconds. With a composite index on merchant and date, equality column first, it walks the tree and answers in milliseconds.

Indexes are not free. Every insert must update every index on the table, and each one takes storage. On a ledger taking tens of thousands of writes a second, eight indexes means eight times the index work, and the database becomes the bottleneck. Indexes buy read speed with write speed.

| Situation | What I decide with engineering |
|---|---|
| Merchant analytics dashboard, read-heavy | Indexes on the most-used filters are a launch requirement |
| Live payment ledger, write-heavy | Minimum indexes on the write path; reporting runs on a read replica |
| Free-text ticket search | A search engine with an inverted index, synced from the database, slight lag accepted |
| New dashboard filter on a 500-million-row table | The online index build takes hours and slows writes; schedule it off-peak, or decide the filter is not worth it |

I don't choose indexes; engineers and database administrators do. My part is to say which queries are business-critical and how fast they must be, to take the write-cost warning seriously, and to notice when the trade-off will reach the user as a slow dashboard or a slow payment confirmation.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- The B-tree idea and the read-write trade-off
- Distinct scenarios matched to the right structure, including search
- A clear line between the PM's role and the engineer's

**Where answers fail.**

- "Indexes make searches faster," with no cost
- Confusing indexing with caching or sharding

**If they push.**

- *The dashboard takes 150 ms for small merchants and 8 seconds for those with over a million transactions.* Those are the highest-value merchants, so it ranks high. Fix the query plan and index first; pre-aggregate if that is not enough.

### Q143. What is OAuth 2.0, and why does a PM need to understand it when building a product with third-party integrations?

*Technical for PMs · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you understand delegated access well enough to make scope, consent and token decisions.

**Frame it.** The problem it solves, the authorization-code flow in five steps, then the decisions a PM owns.

**Outline.**

- Problem: handing passwords to third parties
- Four roles: user, client app, authorization server, resource server
- Authorization-code flow with PKCE
- Scopes, consent screen, tokens
- Decisions: flow, least privilege, token lifetime, app review
- OAuth is authorization; OpenID Connect adds login

**Model answer.** Before OAuth, a budgeting app that wanted my bank transactions asked for my bank password. It could then do anything I could, and the only way to cut it off was to change my password. OAuth 2.0 gives the app limited access without my credentials.

There are four roles: me, the resource owner; the client app; the bank's authorization server; and the bank's API, the resource server. The authorization-code flow runs like this:

1. I tap "connect your bank." The app redirects me to the bank with its client ID, a redirect address and the scopes it wants.
2. I sign in at the bank and see a consent screen: "this app wants to read your balance and transactions."
3. The bank sends me back to the app with a short-lived code.
4. The app's server exchanges the code for an access token, and usually a refresh token.
5. The app calls the bank's API with the access token. It expires quickly, and the refresh token gets a new one without asking me again.

What I own as PM:

- **Flow.** Authorization code with PKCE for anything a user delegates; current security guidance requires PKCE for mobile and browser apps and recommends it everywhere. Client credentials for server-to-server calls. The older implicit and password flows should not be used.
- **Scopes.** Ask for the minimum. A budgeting app needs read access, not transfers. Broad scopes alarm users and widen the damage of a breach.
- **Consent screen.** Plain words for each permission; three clear asks are granted more readily than twelve.
- **Tokens.** Short-lived access tokens, silent refresh, graceful re-consent when refresh expires, and no tokens in browser-accessible storage.
- **App review.** If we are the provider, decide which partners may request which scopes.

One distinction to state: OAuth grants access to data. "Sign in with X" is OpenID Connect, an identity layer on top.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- All four roles, and the server-side exchange of code for token
- Scope minimization tied to user trust and breach impact
- Client credentials distinguished from authorization code with PKCE

**Where answers fail.**

- "It's logging in with Google," which confuses OAuth with identity
- No mention of scopes, consent or revocation

**If they push.**

- *A partner wants a scope that initiates transfers.* Only with a clear user benefit, capped or per-payment consent, a security review of the partner and easy revocation.

### Q144. Explain how push notifications work technically, and describe the product trade-offs a PM must navigate when designing a notification strategy.

*Technical for PMs · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you know the delivery mechanics and their limits, and can turn permission, relevance and fatigue into a policy with a health metric.

**Frame it.** The delivery path, what "best effort" means for design, then the strategy.

**Outline.**

- Path: app, device token, our server, platform push service, device
- Best effort: late, lost or duplicated messages are possible
- Permission is scarce: ask in context
- Types: transactional, informational, promotional
- Caps, quiet hours, specific copy
- Health metric: opt-out rate by type

**Model answer.** When the user allows notifications, the operating system registers the app with the platform's push service, APNs on Apple devices and FCM on Android, and gets back a device token: an address for this app on this device. The app sends the token to our backend. To notify someone, the backend calls the push service with the token, a payload and a priority. If the phone is offline the service holds the message for a limited time: FCM's default is up to four weeks, and APNs may keep only the most recent message per app.

The key fact is that delivery is best effort. A push can arrive late, never, or occasionally twice. So a payment confirmation must not exist only as a push. The app fetches the truth from our server when it opens and handles duplicates safely, and we fall back to SMS or email for anything critical.

Permission is the scarce resource. iOS requires an explicit prompt, and so has Android since version 13. On iOS the prompt appears once; after a refusal, only settings can reverse it. So I ask in context, after a moment that makes the value obvious ("want to know when this payment is confirmed?"), not on first launch.

| Type | Examples | Policy |
|---|---|---|
| Transactional | Payment received or failed, one-time code | Always, high priority |
| Informational | Statement ready, tax document | Daytime, at most one a week |
| Promotional | Cashback, new feature | At most two a month, easy opt-out |

I add a daily cap, quiet hours from 10 pm to 7 am local time with security alerts exempt, and specific copy: "Your ₹15,000 transfer to Sharma Hardware is confirmed" beats "you have a payment update."

My test for any notification: would the user thank us for it? The health metric is opt-out rate by type, alongside delivery and open rates. If any type pushes opt-outs past 5 percent, I pause it and review its value and frequency.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Token flow, the push service and time-to-live explained
- Best-effort delivery turned into design rules
- A type taxonomy with caps; opt-out rate as the health metric, with a threshold

**Where answers fail.**

- "The app sends a message to the phone"
- "Send fewer, better notifications," with no mechanism or metric

**If they push.**

- *Opt-outs jump from 2% to 7% after a daily spending summary at 8 pm.* Pause it, make it opt-in, test a weekly cadence and another hour, and watch opt-outs before open rate.

### Q145. What is a vector database, and how would you use this knowledge as a PM building an AI-powered financial advisor that gives personalized investment recommendations?

*Technical for PMs · Stretch · Tailored*

**What they are testing.** Whether you can explain embeddings and similarity search, and see what they must never decide in a regulated advice product.

**Clarify first.**

- Who is the user? I'll assume retail investors, so suitability rules are strict.
- What does similarity search power? Mainly research retrieval; preference matching second.

**Outline.**

- Embeddings: meaning as vectors; similar things sit close
- Vector index: approximate nearest-neighbor search plus filters
- Uses: research retrieval, preference matching, peer signals
- Trade-offs: recall versus speed, freshness, chunking
- Numbers come from systems of record; suitability is a hard rule
- Show sources: explainability and an audit trail

**Model answer.** A regular database finds exact matches in rows and columns. It can't find "documents about rising bad loans at small-business lenders" if they use different words. An embedding model turns text into a long list of numbers that captures meaning, so similar passages land near each other. A vector database, or a vector index inside the database you already run, stores millions of these and returns the nearest neighbors to a query, with ordinary filters such as "published this year."

In an advisor I'd use it three ways. Retrieval: we embed the user's question, fetch the most relevant passages from research, filings and regulator circulars, and the model answers from them with citations. Preference matching: represent each investor's history and stated interests as a vector and find related opportunities. And a weak "investors like you" signal.

| Trade-off | My call |
|---|---|
| Recall versus speed | About 99% recall inside 200 ms; a missed marginal document costs little, slowness loses users |
| Freshness | Per source: filings within a day, regulator circulars within hours, news within minutes |
| Chunking | Small overlapping passages; table rows indexed separately so figures can be found |

Two boundaries matter more than tuning. Prices, holdings and returns never come from embeddings or the model's memory; they are fetched from the system of record at answer time. And similarity must not decide suitability: a small-cap fund can look "similar" to a cautious investor's favorite stocks, so risk profile is a hard filter.

On regulation, advisers owe clients suitable advice and must be able to justify it, so I treat explainability and auditability as product requirements. Every recommendation shows the three to five sources behind it, with dates, and is logged.

It is the pattern from my operations assistant work (S8): retrieve evidence from approved sources, show it, and leave authority with deterministic systems.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Embeddings and approximate search explained correctly
- Three distinct uses, with freshness set as a product requirement
- Citations tied to explainability; suitability enforced by rules

**Where answers fail.**

- "A database for AI data"
- Letting similarity or the model produce numbers or decide suitability

**If they push.**

- *Add live stock prices to the vector store?* No. Prices change every second, and embeddings capture meaning, not values. Fetch them from a market-data service at answer time.

# Part IX — AI product management

## 9. AI product management questions

These twenty-five questions test product judgment when the system is probabilistic. Interviewers look for four habits. You set quality bars before you see what the model can do. You design the failure path as carefully as the success path. You match autonomy to how reversible and costly an action is. And you say who is accountable.

The questions come in three kinds: craft (readiness, quality, trust, governance, lifecycle), strategy for named products, and system designs, many in payments. Eleven are marked Tailored and name the story to bridge to. Thresholds in the answers are starting points to defend, not facts; say so aloud. Company and regulatory details are as of October 2026 and move quickly, so recheck them the week of the interview.

### Q146. You are the PM for Google's AI Overviews feature in Search. How do you think about the product strategy for the next 3 years?

*AI PM · Stretch*

**What they are testing.** Whether you can set a multi-year strategy for an AI feature that reshapes its own ecosystem: quality, publishers, monetization, regulation.

**Clarify first.**

- What are we optimizing? Long-term trust in and use of Search, with ad revenue as a constraint.
- Scope? The global product as of October 2026.

**Outline.**

- Today: scale, early accuracy failures, publisher and regulatory pressure
- Year 1: accuracy on high-stakes queries; a publisher deal worth staying for
- Year 2: personalization with consent; multimodal answers
- Year 3: task completion, its ad model, enterprise
- Risks: regulation, self-preferencing, rivals

**Model answer.** AI Overviews launched in the US in May 2024 and soon produced widely shared errors, including advice to put glue on pizza. The idea is sound: a synthesized answer saves reading ten links. Three tensions remain. Answers must be right where being wrong hurts. Publishers supply the content and lose clicks; as of October 2026 the UK regulator has ordered Google to let them opt out of AI features without losing ordinary ranking, and the EU is investigating. And ads already run inside AI answers, so the question is whether they replace the clicks they displace.

| Year | Bet | Measure |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Check every claim against its cited source on health, finance, legal and news queries | Audited error rate under 0.5% |
| 1 | Publisher program: clear attribution, referral links, revenue share for partners | Low opt-out rate; referral clicks per answer |
| 2 | Personalized answers with consent (a cardiologist and a patient get different depth); multimodal answers with comparisons and step-by-step visuals | Helpfulness up 15% on the universal answer |
| 3 | Task completion: "weekend trip to Goa" returns an itinerary with live availability and a book action; an enterprise version over a company's own documents | Completed tasks; commission or per-booking auction revenue |

The order matters. Trust comes first because personalization and agents multiply the damage of a wrong answer. Since publishers can now leave, the program has to make staying pay: by year three I want a quarter of answer sessions to include a publisher click, with specialist sites surfacing more than under ten blue links. The agentic year moves ads from cost per click to cost per completed action.

Risks: EU transparency duties for AI answers; competition scrutiny of self-preferencing, so AI answers must be distinct from organic results and not bury alternatives; and assistant-style rivals signing publisher deals first.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- A sequenced arc: quality, then personalization, then agentic
- A publisher mechanism that creates new value, not only compensation
- A monetization model for agentic search, and named regulatory risks

**Where answers fail.**

- A list of UI features with no sequence
- Ignoring publishers or regulation

**If they push.**

- *Satisfaction is up 22%, organic clicks down 18%, ad clicks down 9%.* I'd present it as user value now against ecosystem and revenue health: keep the feature, limit it where it adds least, and fund answer-level ads and publisher referrals to close the gap.

### Q147. Design an agentic AI system that autonomously manages a merchant's entire payment operations: reconciliation, dispute filing, fraud escalation, and supplier payment scheduling.

*AI PM · Stretch · Tailored*

**What they are testing.** Whether you can split agent work by risk and reversibility and design autonomy, trust and liability.

**Clarify first.**

- Segment? Small and mid-size merchants on an existing gateway.
- Autonomy? Earned per action type, not granted wholesale.

**Outline.**

- Loop: read, plan, act within authority or ask, log
- Three tiers by risk and reversibility
- Four domains mapped to tiers
- Trust: activity feed, earned autonomy, undo and cancel windows
- Liability, audit trail, metrics

**Model answer.** The agent runs one loop: read data, plan, act if the action is within its authority or ask if not, and log everything. I set that authority by risk and reversibility.

| Tier | Rule | Examples |
|---|---|---|
| 1. Acts alone | Low risk, reversible, rule-clear | Match settlements to orders within ₹1; flag suspicious transactions without blocking; remind suppliers three days before due |
| 2. Recommends, merchant confirms | Money moves or judgment is needed | File a dispute scored above 70% winnable; supplier payments over ₹50,000; block a payment method |
| 3. Analysis only | High consequence or ambiguous | Shutting a payment channel; any regulatory or bank communication |

Reconciliation: it matches gateway settlement files, bank statements and orders, closes matches itself, and raises exceptions with a likely cause and proposed fix. Disputes: on a chargeback notice it gathers the evidence, scores winnability from past outcomes by reason code, drafts the response and shows the deadline; after approval it files and tracks. Fraud: velocity and amount rules plus a light model on the merchant's history; it flags and alerts at once. Supplier payments: it reads amounts and due dates from invoices and presents batches 48 hours ahead.

Payments can't be undone, so recurring payments under ₹50,000 run without a tap only to beneficiaries the merchant has paid before, and only after that 48-hour cancel window.

Trust is built three ways. A live feed: "9:42, reconciled 47 transactions, two need you." Earned autonomy: everything is recommend-and-confirm for 30 days; then the agent proposes action types for Tier 1 and the merchant decides. And one-tap undo for 24 hours on reversible actions.

Liability: we indemnify Tier 1 errors up to ₹25,000 per incident; approved Tier 2 actions are the merchant's. Every action stores its supporting data.

Metrics: hours saved per merchant; dispute win rate against merchant-filed disputes; exception rate under 2 percent. Guardrail: under 0.1 percent of autonomous actions reversed.

My reconciliation work (S4) taught me such errors hide between systems; the assistant work (S8) set the boundary that deterministic systems keep authority over financial state.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Autonomy tiered by action risk, not one switch
- All four domains, with concrete actions
- Trust earned progressively; liability designed in

**Where answers fail.**

- A dashboard with alerts called an agent
- Every action equally autonomous

**If they push.**

- *An approved ₹8 lakh payment goes to the wrong account because the invoice was corrupted.* Help recover it through the bank at once, then fix the cause: check account details against the stored beneficiary and re-confirm when they differ.

### Q148. How do you evaluate whether an AI feature in a consumer product is ready to ship? Define your AI readiness checklist.

*AI PM · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you treat an AI launch differently from a deterministic one: thresholds set in advance, failure designed, monitoring and a kill switch ready.

**Clarify first.**

- Generative or predictive? I'll cover both and flag where they differ.
- Regulated domain? I'll assume general consumer, and note what tightens for health or finance.

**Outline.**

- Why AI readiness differs: probabilistic, with new kinds of failure
- Five gates: quality, safety, operations, UX, legal
- Thresholds fixed before development
- Offline evaluation, then shadow mode, then small live exposure
- Red on safety or legal stops launch; yellow elsewhere means limited beta

**Model answer.** A normal feature is deterministic, so readiness means "does it work." An AI feature is right most of the time, and it fails in new ways: fabricated facts, bias, odd behavior on unusual inputs. So my checklist asks whether it fails rarely, gracefully and visibly enough to catch.

| Gate | What must be true |
|---|---|
| Model quality | Meets preset thresholds on a held-out set that mirrors production; no regression for any subgroup (language, age, device); red-teamed with adversarial inputs, including prompt injection; confirmed in shadow mode, then on about 5% of traffic |
| Safety and ethics | Harmful outputs in a 10,000-output sample: none in health, finance and legal topics, under 0.01% elsewhere; bias audit across groups; test for leaking training or user data; a fallback for every failure mode |
| Operations | Live dashboard for quality, output distribution and latency; drift detected within 24 hours; incident playbook and on-call; kill switch independent of a release; rollback inside two hours |
| User experience | Confidence communicated honestly; an opt-out to a non-AI path; plain-language reasons for consequential decisions |
| Legal | Counsel confirms the applicable rules; risk assessment documented; terms disclose AI and data use |

Two points I'd stress. Thresholds come first. For fraud detection I might require recall above 92 percent and precision above 85. For a summarizer, a human helpfulness rating above 4 out of 5 on a rubric, with an automated grader checked against those human labels. If I set the bar after seeing results, the model is deciding the requirement.

And averages hide harm. A model that is 5 percent better overall but clearly worse for one group is not ready.

The decision rule: all five gates green to launch broadly. Any red in safety or legal is a hard stop. A yellow in quality or UX can go to a limited beta with tighter monitoring and a dated plan to reach green.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Thresholds set before development, not fitted afterward
- Red-team testing, monitoring and rollback as launch criteria
- Uncertainty communication and opt-out as readiness items

**Where answers fail.**

- A standard launch checklist with "model accuracy" added
- No fallback, kill switch or subgroup analysis

**If they push.**

- *Helpfulness is 4.2 in English and 2.9 in Hindi.* Ship English and hold Hindi, with a date and an evaluation target. Launching a known-poor experience spends trust in that market.

### Q149. You are the PM for an AI copilot that briefs private-bank wealth managers before client meetings. Define the product and its success criteria.

*AI PM · Stretch · Tailored*

**What they are testing.** Whether you can design AI for an expert in a regulated setting: where the model synthesizes and where it must not generate.

**Clarify first.**

- Scope? A pre-meeting brief from portfolio data, research, news and CRM notes; live in-meeting help comes later.
- Clients? High-net-worth individuals.

**Outline.**

- Pain: 1.5 to 2.5 hours of preparation per meeting
- A four-section brief, two hours before
- Retrieval for text, APIs for numbers, per-client access
- Every idea sourced; positioned as research synthesis
- Metrics and a zero-discrepancy guardrail

**Model answer.** Assume a wealth manager with 80 to 120 relationships spends 1.5 to 2.5 hours preparing each meeting: performance, news, ideas that fit the client, personal context. At five meetings a week that is 7.5 to 12.5 hours. The copilot cuts each to about 35 minutes with a brief delivered two hours before.

| Section | Content |
|---|---|
| Portfolio snapshot | Value, month- and year-to-date return against benchmark, biggest movers, allocation against the client's investment policy; flags drift above 3% |
| Market context | The five events of the past week that touch this client's holdings, each with why it matters to them |
| Opportunities and risks | Three to five discussion ideas from house research, tax position and life events, each with its source and evidence strength |
| Relationship context | Last contact, topics from the last meeting, milestones, a suggested opener |

Architecture: retrieval over two stores. Client documents and notes sit in a private index only the assigned manager can query; research, filings and news sit in a shared index refreshed daily. Every number comes from the portfolio system through an API. The model writes sentences; it never produces a figure. Each idea cites a named report and date, one tap from the source, and is labeled as a suggestion that needs the manager's judgment.

Regulation shapes positioning. Advice must come from a registered person, so the output is research synthesis for the manager, who stays responsible for what reaches the client. Outputs are logged under the bank's record-keeping rules, and client data stays in compliant hosting.

Metrics: preparation time down about 70 percent, from two hours to 35 minutes; usefulness rated above 4.2 of 5 after each meeting; over 25 percent of ideas discussed and acted on within 30 days; briefs generated in under 90 seconds. Guardrail: every figure matches the portfolio system, and any mismatch is a top-severity incident.

It is the boundary from my operations assistant work (S8): the model gathers and summarizes evidence, and systems of record keep authority.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- A specific four-section brief, not a chatbot
- Numbers from structured systems, never generated
- "Research synthesis, not advice"; acted-on ideas as the quality metric

**Where answers fail.**

- A dashboard or Q&A bot with no brief
- No design for numerical accuracy or the regulatory role

**If they push.**

- *A client asks, "was an AI reading my data?"* Disclose it up front. Indian securities rules already require advisers to tell clients how they use AI tools; I'd add that a human reviews everything.

### Q150. What are the key differences in PM skills required for an AI product vs. a traditional software product?

*AI PM · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you can say precisely what changes when the product is probabilistic, beyond "learn more about ML."

**Frame it.** One root difference, five competencies with a practice each, then what stays the same.

**Outline.**

- Root: deterministic versus probabilistic
- Probabilistic thinking and failure design
- Technical depth at PM level
- Responsible AI: bias, transparency, deployment judgment
- Data and evaluation strategy
- Agent and trust design
- Shared base: users, influence, prioritization

**Model answer.** Traditional software is deterministic: same input, same output, and a bug is a deviation you fix. An AI product is probabilistic. It is right some share of the time, and uncertainty is part of its nature, not a defect. That one difference changes the job in five ways.

| Competency | In practice |
|---|---|
| Probabilistic thinking | Set the accuracy bar before development; design what the user sees when the model is wrong (keyword search, not a blank page); show confidence in the interface |
| Technical depth | Know when prompting, retrieval or fine-tuning fits; what drives cost per request; what precision, recall and graded evaluations do and don't tell you |
| Responsible AI | Choose a fairness definition for the use case; make bias testing a launch gate; design explanations and source attribution; separate "can do" from "should deploy" |
| Data and evaluation | Decide which signals and labels improve the product, who labels them, and the consent, retention and deletion rules; own the evaluation set |
| Agent and trust design | Define what the system may do alone, with confirmation, or never; make actions reversible; leave an explanation trail |

A sentence on each. If I set requirements from what the model happens to achieve, the model is running the product. On technical depth, a traditional PM can often defer; I can't, because choosing retrieval over fine-tuning, or a smaller model over a larger one, is a product decision about freshness, cost and latency. On fairness, a lending model trained on past approvals repeats past discrimination unless I require the audit. On data, I manage a learning system, not only features. And as products move from assistants to agents, I design autonomy models where I used to design flows.

What stays the same is the foundation: understanding why users distrust a black box, getting ML engineers to optimize the metric users feel, explaining limits to executives who overestimate the technology, and prioritizing hard, because AI features are expensive to build and run.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Probabilistic versus binary thinking as the root shift
- Five competencies, each with a concrete practice
- Agent design included, and everything tied back to core PM skills

**Where answers fail.**

- General PM skills plus "understand AI"
- Naming ethics without saying what you would do differently

**If they push.**

- *94% accuracy, but the failing 6% is 180 million people.* Find out who they are. If errors concentrate in one group or in high-harm cases, I hold or restrict; if they are spread thin and low-harm, I ship with a fallback and monitoring.

### Q151. An AI agent at a payments company handles 80% of customer support tickets autonomously. How do you ensure quality and prevent customer harm?

*AI PM · Standard · Tailored*

**What they are testing.** Whether you can draw the line between what an agent may send alone and what needs a human, and keep measuring it.

**Clarify first.**

- Can the agent act or only reply? Replies plus read-only lookups; refunds and blocks need a human.
- The other 20%? Human agents, with context.

**Outline.**

- Two risks: escalating too much or too little
- Three layers: pre-send gate, hard blocks, post-send audit
- Harm rules: identity, retrieved facts, distress
- Handoff package and feedback loop
- Metrics, guardrail, disclosure

**Model answer.** Two risks pull in opposite directions. Escalate too much and the efficiency is gone. Escalate too little and the agent sends a wrong refund status or exposes account data to the wrong person. I define the boundary in three layers.

| Layer | Design |
|---|---|
| 1. Pre-send gate | A composite score from intent confidence, how well the draft answers the question, and whether data lookups succeeded. Below 0.85, a starting threshold tuned on labeled tickets, a human takes it |
| 2. Hard blocks | Never autonomous: reported unauthorized access, legal threats or regulatory complaints, signs of financial distress, amounts above ₹50,000, unverified identity, an active escalation flag |
| 3. Post-send audit | Human QA reviews a random 5% of sent replies within 24 hours for accuracy, helpfulness and tone; a batch scoring under 4.0 of 5 triggers a review |

Three harm rules sit on top. Identity: before any account-specific reply the customer is verified with a one-time code to the registered number. Facts: any date, amount or status comes from the system of record at that moment; the model never writes one from its understanding of policy. Distress: a classifier routes upset customers to a person.

On escalation the agent hands over the request in one line, recent account history, its draft and the reason it stopped; I'd target a 40 percent cut in human handling time. Low satisfaction scores and QA failures are reviewed; the corrected answer joins the test set and we fix the prompt, knowledge base or rule behind it.

Metrics: satisfaction within 5 points of human-handled tickets by day 90; autonomous share held at 80 percent without quality falling; QA score above 4.2. Guardrail: zero replies with wrong account-specific information; one incident suspends autonomous sending for 24 hours and starts a root-cause review. Every reply says it was prepared with AI and offers a human on request.

This follows my operations assistant work (S8): the model interprets and retrieves; identity, permissions and financial state stay with deterministic systems.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- A pre-send gate, hard-block categories and a post-send audit
- Account-specific facts retrieved live, never generated
- Disclosure and a human path as non-negotiable

**Where answers fail.**

- "It escalates when it isn't confident," with confidence undefined
- No audit of what was sent, and no disclosure

**If they push.**

- *The agent quoted a refund date from the system, and a system error delayed it.* The data was right and the customer was still let down. Apologize, have a person own the case, and send updates when a quoted date slips.

### Q152. How do you design for "AI trust" in a consumer product? What UX and product design principles drive user trust in AI features?

*AI PM · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you can turn "trust" into concrete design principles, including preventing over-trust and recovering after failure.

**Clarify first.**

- First-use trust or sustained trust? Both, weighted toward sustaining it through failures.
- Stakes? General principles, plus what changes in health or finance.

**Outline.**

- Why AI trust differs: mostly right, surprisingly wrong
- Transparency, calibration, control, graceful failure, consistency
- A pattern and an anti-pattern for each
- Over-trust: add friction where stakes are high
- Recovery: notify first, own it, fix it

**Model answer.** People trust ordinary software when it works predictably. An AI feature works most of the time and then fails in a surprising way that feels like a choice. So trust is not one setting: users may rely on it for scheduling and doubt it for symptoms.

| Principle | Pattern | Anti-pattern |
|---|---|---|
| Transparency: show your work | Sources, what was searched, why this answer | A bare "AI-generated" label |
| Calibration: don't overclaim or underclaim | Firm wording when confidence is high; hedged wording and alternatives when it is low | The same certainty everywhere, or hedging everything |
| Control | Undo within a time window, preferences ("always ask before sending"), "why did you do this?", an activity log | Autonomy with no way to intervene |
| Graceful failure | An honest "I couldn't find a good answer; try this," a human fallback, two-tap error reporting | A confident answer invented to fill the gap |
| Consistency | Explain changes ("based on your recent listening"), announce model updates, one stable persona | Contradicting yesterday's answer without comment |

Calibration needs engineering work. The wording can only mirror confidence if the system produces a signal that tracks correctness, which models don't do well by default, so I ask for it and test it.

Control resolves a paradox. Agents that act are more useful and feel less safe. People don't need to approve every step; they need to know they can step in.

Over-trust is as dangerous as distrust. In high-stakes areas I add deliberate friction: a symptom checker always says it is informational and to see a clinician; a financial recommendation asks the user to review details before confirming; an agent that sends messages shows a preview with a short pause, so approval does not become a reflex.

Recovery is the last piece. When the AI makes a significant error, we tell the user before they find it, say plainly that the AI made a mistake, make the fix nearly effortless, and if the cause is systemic, tell everyone affected.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Five principles, each with a specific interface pattern
- Over-trust handled with deliberate friction in high-stakes use
- A recovery playbook that starts with proactive notification

**Where answers fail.**

- "Make it accurate and easy to use"
- Confidence labels as the whole answer

**If they push.**

- *Users who see confidence indicators click 15% less.* That may be the indicators working. I'd check whether the lost clicks were on low-confidence items and whether satisfaction and error reports improved.

### Q153. An AI agent at a payments company monitors merchant accounts and autonomously freezes suspicious ones. Define the ethical framework and design safeguards.

*AI PM · Stretch · Tailored*

**What they are testing.** Whether you can bound a high-consequence autonomous action with principles, graduated steps, human review, appeal and bias monitoring.

**Clarify first.**

- What triggers suspicion? Rules plus an anomaly model.
- Regulatory timelines? Compliance confirms them; my timings are design targets.

**Outline.**

- Asymmetry: a wrong freeze hurts the merchant more than a miss hurts us
- Five principles
- Ladder: flag, hold, freeze
- Safeguards: two signals, tenure, 30-minute review, false-positive brake
- Appeal and compensation
- Audit trail and quarterly bias audit

**Model answer.** Freezing a merchant's account stops their revenue, and with it their ability to pay suppliers, staff and rent. A missed fraud costs the platform money; a wrong freeze costs the merchant far more. That asymmetry drives the design.

Five principles. Proportionality: the agent acts only when suspect volume passes a threshold, say 5 percent of the day's volume or ₹5 lakh. Least intervention: the lightest step first. Due process: notify the merchant on every channel at the moment of action with what was detected, what was done and how to respond. Bounded transparency: enough detail to respond, not enough to teach evasion. Non-discrimination: decisions must not track geography or size beyond real differences in fraud.

| Step | Action | Authority |
|---|---|---|
| Flag | Mark transactions for review | Agent alone |
| Hold | Pause new payments; settlement of earlier funds continues | Agent, with analyst review within 30 minutes |
| Freeze | Full account freeze | Analyst decision. The agent executes alone only if no analyst responds in 30 minutes and losses are escalating, with immediate escalation to a senior analyst |

Safeguards: a freeze needs two independent signals, such as velocity plus device mismatch. Tenure counts: three clean years raise the bar. And a brake: if more than 8 percent of frozen accounts in a rolling week prove legitimate, against a 5 percent target, the agent's freeze authority tightens automatically and the model is reviewed.

Appeal: a staffed emergency line, preliminary review inside 4 hours and resolution within 24. A wrongful freeze is lifted at once, with an apology and compensation scaled to lost revenue, minimum ₹5,000.

Every decision logs its signals, confidence and any human action. Each quarter I compare freeze rates by city tier, size and category; a group frozen at twice the average without a matching fraud rate triggers investigation and retraining.

My compliance and risk work (S7) set the rule I'd apply: cut manual load, but keep deterministic controls and auditability around the decision.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Proportionality and least intervention stated as principles
- The flag, hold, freeze ladder with human review before a full freeze
- Bias monitoring with a threshold, and compensation for wrongful freezes

**Where answers fail.**

- A fraud-detection description with no ethics or safeguards
- "Notify and allow appeal," with no timings or limits on the agent

**If they push.**

- *A 2 am freeze on a flash sale cost the merchant ₹4.2 lakh by 7 am.* We bear it: pay the estimated lost margin plus goodwill, and add planned-promotion signals to the model.

### Q154. How do you define and measure "AI quality" for a generative AI product like an AI writing assistant? What makes a good AI response?

*AI PM · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you can break "quality" into measurable dimensions and mix human and automated evaluation.

**Clarify first.**

- General or domain-specific? General, with notes on legal, medical and marketing.
- Whose view? The user's outcome first, model metrics in support.

**Outline.**

- Quality is several things that trade off
- Six dimensions, a measure for each
- Three evidence sources: raters, automated grader, user behavior
- Trade-offs: accuracy versus creativity, completeness versus brevity
- Weights by use case; evaluation cadence

**Model answer.** A good draft is not just accurate. It has to match what the user meant, sound right, be the right length and be safe. An accurate email the user rewrites from scratch has no value.

| Dimension | Question | How I measure |
|---|---|---|
| Factual accuracy | Are claims correct and supported? | Source checks, citation verification, expert sample |
| Intent alignment | Did it do what the user meant? | Rater score; share of drafts used with under 30% edits |
| Fluency and coherence | Well written and consistent? | Rater score; rarely the weak point for current models |
| Tone and style | Right register for the context? | Rater score; direction of user edits |
| Completeness and concision | Everything needed, nothing extra? | Checklist for structured outputs; how much users delete |
| Safety | Free of harmful or biased content? | Classifiers, red-team prompts, user reports |

Safety is a gate, not a score to trade: it must clear its bar before the rest counts.

I blend three sources of evidence. Human raters with a rubric are the reference. An automated grader, a model scoring against the same rubric, gives scale once I've checked it agrees with the human labels. And behavior tells the truth at volume: edit distance between draft and final, deletions and regenerate rate.

Two trade-offs recur. Accuracy against creativity: in fiction an invented detail is the point. Completeness against brevity: each use case gets an expected length range.

The bar moves by use case. Legal drafting weights accuracy, completeness and safety. Medical notes weight accuracy above all, since a wrong dose is a patient-safety failure. Marketing copy weights tone and creativity, with accuracy on product claims. Everyday email weights intent and tone.

Cadence: before launch, raters score a 500-item benchmark across all six dimensions by use case. Weekly, automated monitoring of safety, grader scores and edit rate. Monthly, a 100-item human sample. Quarterly, a benchmark refresh with new real prompts, so we don't tune to a stale test.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Six dimensions, each with a measure
- Named trade-offs, and different bars by use case
- Human and automated evaluation combined on a stated cadence

**Where answers fail.**

- "Accurate and helpful," with no way to measure either
- Relying on one automated score

**If they push.**

- *Model A scores higher on automated metrics; raters prefer B 58 to 42.* Ship B and fix the metric. The automated score is a proxy, and when it disagrees with people the proxy is wrong.

### Q155. You are a PM at OpenAI building a new consumer AI assistant product. How do you think about the roadmap for the next 12 months?

*AI PM · Stretch*

**What they are testing.** Whether you can build a roadmap from segments and themes in a fast-moving market, with trust work inside it.

**Clarify first.**

- Starting point? As of October 2026: hundreds of millions of weekly users; memory, app connectors, voice and an agent mode exist in some form.
- Goal? Weekly retention and paid conversion.

**Outline.**

- Three problems: habit, reliability, differentiation
- Three personas, with assumed shares
- Five themes in order, agents last
- Monetization tiers
- Risks: regulation, distribution

**Model answer.** The product has mass adoption and three problems. Many people try it and don't form a weekly habit. Wrong or stale answers erode trust. And rivals are built into the phones, browsers and office suites people already use.

I plan around three personas; the shares are my assumptions.

| Persona | Users / revenue | Pain |
|---|---|---|
| Daily professional | 30% / 60% | Re-explaining context; tools not connected; needs it right nearly always |
| Curious explorer | 50% / 25% | Doesn't know what it is best for |
| Creator | 20% / 15% | Wants help that keeps their voice |

| When | Theme | Target |
|---|---|---|
| Q1–Q2 | Memory people control: view, edit, delete | 7-day return up 15% for users with memory on |
| Q2–Q3 | Work where the user works: read and write in mail, calendar, documents and code, with scoped permissions | 40% of daily professionals connect a tool within 90 days |
| Q3 | Voice and multimodal as the easy entry for explorers | Explorer weekly retention |
| Q3–Q4 | Reliability: search-grounded answers, citations, stated uncertainty | Accuracy on a professional-query benchmark up 8 points |
| Q4 | Agents that finish multi-step tasks: research, scheduling, running code | Task completion and reversal rates |

All five exist in some form today, so the roadmap is about depth and dependability. Reliability comes before wider autonomy because an agent multiplies the cost of a wrong step. Agents ship with a trust model: every action visible, reversible within a window, and run with minimum permissions.

Monetization follows the personas: a free tier for explorers, a paid tier with memory and connectors for professionals, a premium tier for heavy agent use, and a separate enterprise track with single sign-on, privacy guarantees and audit logs.

Risks. Regulation: EU obligations for general-purpose models are in force and, since August 2026, enforceable, so transparency, safety evaluation and copyright work sits in the plan. Distribution: a rival embedded in the operating system or browser sees the user first, so I'd be the assistant that works across every tool.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Personas with different pains; themes, not features
- Reliability and grounding as a trust investment
- Agents paired with an explicit trust model

**Where answers fail.**

- A feature wish list with no segments or sequence
- No trust or regulatory work on the roadmap

**If they push.**

- *Memory opt-in reaches 35%, and negative privacy press rises 12%.* Keep it opt-in, make the controls more visible, say plainly what is stored, and never store sensitive categories by default.

### Q156. Design an AI feature that helps job seekers on LinkedIn improve their résumé for specific job listings.

*AI PM · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you can design a generative feature around a user outcome, with honesty guardrails and a downstream metric.

**Clarify first.**

- Improve a résumé or write one? Improve an existing one for one listing.
- Business goal? Better application outcomes, not more applications.

**Outline.**

- Problem: a good résumé poorly matched to this listing
- Entry point at "Apply"; gap analysis, then suggestions
- Match report: accept, edit or dismiss
- Balance screening keywords and the human reader
- Collaborator, not ghostwriter: never invent experience
- Privacy, metrics, harms

**Model answer.** Job seekers often have a solid résumé that doesn't speak the language of a particular listing. Recruiters search and filter by skills and titles, so missing vocabulary means not being found. As of October 2026 LinkedIn offers Premium members AI résumé feedback against a job description, so I'd frame this as making that help specific, honest and measurable.

The entry point sits next to Apply: "get résumé tips for this job." The member picks a saved résumé.

| Stage | What the model does |
|---|---|
| 1. Gap analysis | Finds required skills missing from the résumé but implied by the experience; relevant experience described weakly; bullets that state tasks, not outcomes; key requirements mentioned once or never |
| 2. Suggestions | For each gap, a specific prompt: "Add Python to your analyst role, if you used it there." Or: "'Led the analytics team' is stronger with a result. Do you have a number?" |

The output is a match report: an overall score, then tabs for missing keywords, stronger bullets and two or three quick wins. Each suggestion can be accepted, edited or dismissed inline.

Two goals compete. Keyword screens want the listing's exact terms; a human reader wants specific, readable achievement. So keywords are suggested inside real experience bullets, never as a stuffed skills list.

The principle is collaborator, not ghostwriter. Every suggestion is labeled, phrased as "consider," explained with a "why this?" note and applied only on acceptance. It never invents experience; it helps the member state what is true, and says "add this only if accurate."

Privacy: résumé text is kept only if the member saves it, and nothing trains models without opt-in consent.

Metrics: 20 percent of applications open the tips; 35 percent of suggestions are accepted; and recruiter response within 30 days is 15 percent higher for users than non-users. The third matters most, and I'd measure it with a holdout, because motivated applicants self-select into the feature.

Harms: inflated claims, handled by the truthfulness rule, and résumés converging on one style, handled by keeping the member's own wording.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Two stages: gap analysis, then specific suggestions
- Keywords and human readability balanced
- A truthfulness rule; recruiter response as the success metric

**Where answers fail.**

- Keyword stuffing presented as optimization
- Writing experience the member doesn't have; success measured by usage

**If they push.**

- *Optimized résumés get 18% more callbacks and 12% more early dismissals.* Then we are helping people overstate. Drop suggestions for skills with no evidence, and add fit signals so members apply where they match.

### Q157. A fintech is building an AI agent that negotiates loan terms with multiple lenders for borrowers. Design the product and its safety framework.

*AI PM · Stretch · Tailored*

**What they are testing.** Whether you can design an agent that acts for a consumer against better-informed counterparties: autonomy limit, consent, conflicts and regulation.

**Clarify first.**

- Can the agent accept an offer? No; the borrower accepts.
- Market? India, digital lenders reachable by API, data shared through the Account Aggregator framework.

**Outline.**

- Problem: information asymmetry
- Three phases: mandate, outreach, negotiate and compare
- Hard limit: never accepts a loan
- Per-lender consent; identifies itself as automated
- Four safety checks; regulation; metrics

**Model answer.** A first-time borrower doesn't know the going rate, can't compare many lenders and can't weigh rate against fees and prepayment terms. Lenders know all of it. The agent closes that gap.

| Phase | What happens |
|---|---|
| 1. Mandate | The borrower sets amount, tenure, ranked priorities (lowest monthly payment, lowest total cost, no prepayment charge) and hard limits such as fixed rate only; financial data arrives by consent |
| 2. Outreach | The agent applies to 15 to 20 lenders in parallel with the same profile; each returns terms or a decline |
| 3. Negotiate and compare | Where a lender accepts counteroffers, the agent asks it to beat the best competing offer, truthfully, then presents the top three |

The hard limit: the agent never accepts a loan. A multi-year obligation needs the borrower's own reviewed decision.

Consent is per lender, and the borrower can exclude any of them. The agent identifies itself to lenders as automated and never misstates a competing offer.

Four safety checks:

1. It surfaces terms that change total cost: fees outside the headline rate, foreclosure charges where allowed, rate-reset clauses.
2. It justifies its recommendation against the borrower's ranking: "Option A costs ₹230 more a month but has no prepayment charge; you plan to prepay in three years, so it is cheaper overall."
3. It builds in at least 24 hours and a checklist before acceptance.
4. It discloses any referral fee we earn from a lender.

Regulation, as of October 2026: India's digital lending rules treat such a platform as a lending service provider. They require it to show all matching offers with the annual percentage rate and a key fact statement, and give borrowers a cooling-off period. I'd confirm registration with counsel before launch.

Metrics: rate improvement over each lender's first offer, target 0.15 to 0.35 points; share of borrowers who accept an offer; NPS above 55; share of offers where a cost-changing term was surfaced.

The conflict rule comes from a pricing dilemma I faced (S10): when short-term revenue and the smaller customer's interest diverge, protect the trust.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- A hard limit: the agent cannot accept a loan
- Per-lender consent, hidden-term detection, referral-fee disclosure
- Licensing as a pre-launch constraint

**Where answers fail.**

- A comparison website described as an agent
- Autonomy left implicit; no regulatory or conflict analysis

**If they push.**

- *An offer resets to a benchmark rate plus 5 points after three years.* Flag it at the top, show the payment under a few rate paths, and rank on lifetime cost, not the teaser.

### Q158. How do you prevent AI hallucination from harming users, and what product design principles reduce hallucination risk?

*AI PM · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you understand why models fabricate and can reduce the harm through product design.

**Clarify first.**

- Stakes? General use, plus what tightens for health, finance and legal.
- Prevent or contain? Both.

**Outline.**

- Cause: plausible text, no built-in truth check, guessing rewarded
- System side: grounding, verified citations, abstention, scope limits
- Product side: graded display, action gate, expert review
- Standard wording for uncertainty
- Monitoring: sampled audits, user flags, an alert threshold

**Model answer.** A hallucination is a confident statement that is false or unsupported. It happens because the model is trained to produce the most plausible continuation, not to look facts up, and because training and benchmarks have tended to reward a confident guess over "I don't know." It does most harm when the user can't check the answer, acts on it, and hears no doubt in the wording.

I work at two levels. First, what I require of the system:

| Requirement | Detail |
|---|---|
| Ground answers in retrieved sources | I specify the knowledge base and its freshness |
| Cite, and verify the citation | Check that the cited passage actually supports the claim |
| Abstain | "I don't have reliable information on this; here is where to look" |
| Limit scope | A health assistant declines legal questions |
| A usable confidence signal | Built from source agreement and support checks, not the model's self-report alone |

Second, product design that limits harm:

- **Graded display.** Well-supported answers appear normally; weakly supported ones carry a visible "verify this" marker.
- **Action gate.** Before a consequential step, such as a filing, a payment or a medication decision, the user confirms they have checked an authoritative source.
- **Expert review.** For health, legal and financial content that is not real-time, a qualified human reviews before display.
- **Inline sources.** Everyone sees where a claim comes from.
- **Honest correction.** When we find an error, we tell affected users.

The style guide fixes the wording: "multiple sources confirm" for high confidence, "based on available information, it appears" for medium, "I'm not certain" for low, and a clear no-answer state. That wording is tested, because it drifts.

Monitoring: a grader checks a random 1 percent of responses daily against reference sources, and flagged ones go to human review. If more than 1 percent of the sample contains an unsupported claim, the model and retrieval are reviewed. Every response has a one-tap "this looks wrong"; reports are reviewed within 24 hours, and confirmed cases join the regression test set.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- The cause explained: plausible-text generation with no truth check
- Grounding and verified citations in the system; graded display and action gates in the product
- Continuous monitoring with an alert threshold

**Where answers fail.**

- "Add a disclaimer and let users report errors"
- "The model needs to be more accurate," with no design response

**If they push.**

- *Users act faster on "high confidence" answers without reading the citation.* Then the badge is a shortcut. Show the supporting quote itself, and keep the action gate whatever the confidence.

### Q159. Design an AI product that analyzes a merchant's transactions and recommends optimal payment methods (UPI vs. card vs. BNPL) to maximize conversion and reduce cost.

*AI PM · Standard · Tailored*

**What they are testing.** Whether you can design a recommender with a sound objective, proof of lift, and an honest answer to the platform's conflict of interest.

**Clarify first.**

- Advice or automatic reordering? Weekly advice by default; real-time ordering as an opt-in.
- Whose objective? The merchant's: conversion, cost, or a weighted mix.

**Outline.**

- Pain: six methods in a flat list
- Weekly report, then real-time ordering
- Data, model, and why observed rates are not proof
- Actions with one-click apply and a built-in A/B test
- Conflict of interest: merchant-set objective, disclosed

**Model answer.** A mid-size online merchant in India shows six payment methods in a flat list. They differ in success rate, cost and who uses them. Assume, for illustration, that UPI is free to the merchant, cards cost 1.5 to 2.2 percent and pay-later 2.8, and that pay-later converts well on large baskets and badly on small ones. The merchant has no time to work out which to feature.

| Level | What it does |
|---|---|
| 1. Weekly report | Analyzes 90 days of transactions by category, basket value and customer type; says which method to feature, keep secondary or remove ("pay-later converts at 2.3% under ₹500; hide it for small baskets") |
| 2. Real-time ordering (opt-in) | Orders methods per session from basket value, category, device, time and a returning customer's past method |

Data: the merchant's orders with method, outcome, value, category, device and time; anonymized platform benchmarks; session signals such as basket size. Customer data is aggregated or pseudonymized.

Model: gradient-boosted trees predicting conversion for each method in context, retrained weekly. The top method has the highest predicted conversion within the merchant's cost limit, such as fees under 2 percent.

One caution: past conversion by method is confounded, because customers who choose pay-later differ from those who choose UPI. So each recommendation ships with an automated A/B test on half of sessions for 14 days.

The report shows performance by method, flagging anything 10 percent below the category average, and three to five actions with projected impact and a one-click apply.

Conflict of interest: we may earn more on cards than on UPI. So the merchant sets the objective, never platform revenue, and where the two diverge the screen says so.

Metrics: conversion lift against control, target 5 to 10 percent; recommendations applied within 14 days, target 35 percent; then abandonment, cost per successful transaction and merchant retention.

On the RuPay credit card on UPI launch (S5) my concern was conversion risk from going too broad, so every change here is staged; and pricing logic (S3) needs fallbacks and an audit trail.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Two levels: weekly configuration and real-time ordering
- A specified model, validated by A/B test
- The platform's fee conflict named and designed out

**Where answers fail.**

- An analytics dashboard with no recommendations
- Ignoring the platform's own incentive

**If they push.**

- *Featuring pay-later lifted conversion 9% and returns 15%.* The objective was too narrow. Optimize net revenue after returns and fees, and revise the advice where returns erase the gain.

### Q160. How do you build a responsible AI team culture as a PM? What processes and rituals do you put in place?

*AI PM · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you can make responsible AI a set of habits a team follows, even under pressure to ship.

**Frame it.** Why policy alone fails, five rituals I'd run, and how I'd behave as the PM.

**Outline.**

- Culture beats policy: people must feel free to slow down
- Ethics brief before final QA; monthly red team
- No-blame post-mortem within 72 hours
- "Who is not in the room?" review
- Product card before launch
- Partners and my own conduct

**Model answer.** A policy on the wiki changes little. People follow it only when they believe in it and feel safe raising a concern. So I'd build habits that surface problems before launch.

| Ritual | How it runs |
|---|---|
| Ethics brief | 60 minutes before final QA with the ML lead, design, legal and research. Five questions: what the AI decides and who is affected; failure modes and their harm; performance gaps across groups; human override for consequential decisions; what the user understands about the AI's role. Documented, and required to advance |
| Red team | Monthly. Three or four rotating people try to break one feature; findings shared without blame |
| Post-mortem | Within 72 hours of a harmful outcome: what happened, who was affected, which signals we missed, the process change and the product change |
| "Who is not in the room?" | Monthly review of feedback from outside the main persona: other languages, disabled users, low bandwidth, people the feature could endanger |
| Product card | Before launch, a plain-language page: what the AI does, its data, known limits, error rates by group, override options. Internal, with a simpler public version |

The brief stress-tests the feature while changes are cheap; it is not there to block. The red team works only if I treat a finding as a win: we found it before a user did. Post-mortems work only if the question is "what did our process miss?" and never "who did this?", because teams that fear blame stop reporting.

I can't do this alone. I'd work with ethics specialists, legal, research and data science, and protect time for this in sprint planning so feature pressure does not squeeze it out.

My own conduct sets the tone. I say "I'm not comfortable shipping without the bias audit; let's take two weeks." I admit what I don't know. And I publicly credit the engineer who slowed a launch for a good reason.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Five rituals specific enough to run next week
- An explicit no-blame learning culture
- "Who is not in the room?" and personal accountability from the PM

**Where answers fail.**

- "Follow the guidelines and get legal review"
- Ethics as a sign-off at the end

**If they push.**

- *The CEO: a competitor shipped; can we ship now and fix the bias later?* I'd bring the specific harm, who bears it and the fastest safe path: a limited launch that excludes the affected segment, with a date for the fix.

### Q161. Design an agentic AI system that runs a merchant's customer acquisition workflow: identifying leads, drafting personalized outreach, scheduling follow-ups, and closing with a payment link.

*AI PM · Stretch · Tailored*

**What they are testing.** Whether you can design an agent that speaks in someone else's name: human review, lawful data, and earned autonomy.

**Clarify first.**

- Which merchants? Small B2B service providers.
- Channels? Email first; others only where platform rules and consent allow.

**Outline.**

- Four stages: find, draft, follow up, close
- Human gates: the prospect list and every draft batch
- Autonomy earned per template
- Hard limits: data, channels, proof points, no meetings
- Metrics

**Model answer.** An accountant or architect is good at the work and has no time for steady selling. Leads come by word of mouth and follow-up is patchy, so revenue is lumpy. The agent makes acquisition systematic.

| Stage | Agent does | Merchant does |
|---|---|---|
| 1. Find | Builds 50 to 200 prospects a week from the merchant's ideal-client profile, own leads and public business registries | Reviews the list; nobody is contacted without it |
| 2. Draft | Writes a message for each prospect from their context and the merchant's service description | Approves the batch; 20 drafts in under 10 minutes |
| 3. Follow up | One follow-up after 5 days, a final note 7 days later, then stops | Approves in batch |
| 4. Close | Flags a positive reply, drafts the response, and after the meeting drafts the invoice and sends a payment link | Holds the meeting; approves the invoice |

Human review is deliberate: every message carries the merchant's reputation. Autonomy is earned narrowly: after five batches approved with under 10 percent of sentences edited, the merchant may mark specific templates as trusted, and only those send without review.

Hard limits. The agent uses only proof points the merchant supplied. It does not attend meetings. And it follows data and channel rules, as of October 2026: India's data protection law requires a lawful basis for using personal data, with its main duties in force by mid-2027, so no scraped personal emails; professional networks ban bots from sending messages, so the agent drafts and the merchant sends; messaging apps need the prospect's opt-in.

Metrics: prospect-to-meeting rate, where I'd assume 8 to 12 percent for B2B services; meeting-to-paid rate; over 20 percent of new-client revenue from the agent by month six; merchant time on acquisition under 2 hours a week, from 8 or more; and drafts approved without major edits as the quality proxy.

My retention work (S6) taught me that the segment and the problem definition decide the outcome, so the merchant owns the profile and the list.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Human gates at the prospect list and the draft batch
- Channel terms and data-protection rules as design constraints
- A scope limit: the agent does not attend meetings

**Where answers fail.**

- Outreach automation where review is optional
- No answer to where contact data comes from

**If they push.**

- *How do you make "trusted" mode safe?* Per template, with a fixed audience type, claim checks, a daily cap, a sampled audit, and automatic return to review if replies turn negative.

### Q162. How do you think about model versioning and rollback strategy as a PM for an AI product? When should you roll back an AI model?

*AI PM · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you treat model changes as operational risk: versions, rollback triggers, who decides and how fast.

**Clarify first.**

- Our model or a vendor's? Both.
- Consumer scale? Yes, so minutes matter.

**Outline.**

- Why model rollback is harder than code rollback
- The release unit: model, prompt, retrieval index, tools
- Major, minor, patch, each with a rollout rule
- Triggers: automatic, PM decision, leadership
- Rollback as a configuration switch to a warm previous version
- Communication and the 72-hour review

**Model answer.** Rolling back code means returning to the last good commit. A model is harder: "worse" may mean worse for the 1 percent of users who matter most, and if the previous version is not ready to serve, the bad one keeps running.

I version the release unit: model, prompt, retrieval index and tool definitions together.

| Change | Example | Rollout | Previous version kept warm |
|---|---|---|---|
| Major | New base model | Full evaluation, bias audit, red team; 5%, 20%, 50%, 100% | 30 days |
| Minor | Retraining, significant prompt change | Benchmark plus 7 days in shadow | 14 days |
| Patch | Preprocessing fix, small prompt edit | 5% canary for 48 hours | Until the canary passes |

With a vendor model I pin an exact version, not a moving alias, and track its published retirement date.

| Trigger class | Examples | Who acts |
|---|---|---|
| Automatic | Error rate above 2% for 5 minutes; p99 latency over the SLA for 10 minutes; harmful-output rate above 0.1% in an hour | The system rolls back within 5 minutes and pages the on-call PM and ML lead |
| PM decision, within 2 hours | Key benchmark down over 5%; satisfaction down over 8% in 24 hours; a 5-point error gap for a protected group; a severe fabrication in a high-stakes domain | PM |
| Leadership | Regulator objection, legal advice, press | PM with leadership; may also switch the feature off |

Rollback itself is a configuration switch to the previous version, done in minutes, not a deployment. Going two versions back takes the normal pipeline, 30 to 60 minutes.

Communication: on-call is paged at once; engineering gets cause, impact and expected time within 30 minutes; users get a status note within 60 minutes if answers degraded, and direct contact with an apology and correction for harmful outputs.

Within 72 hours: root cause, time to a fixed version, and the evaluation that would have caught it, which joins the standard suite.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Version tiers with warm-standby requirements
- Three trigger classes with numeric thresholds
- Rollback as a configuration switch, with user communication planned in advance

**Where answers fail.**

- "We monitor and roll back if it gets worse"
- Treating model rollback like code rollback

**If they push.**
