- *Rollback will take 45 minutes and 5 million users are on the bad model.* Cut exposure now: switch the feature off or route the affected query types to a safe fallback, and post status.

### Q163. Design an AI-powered "financial health score" that predicts a payments-app user's financial resilience over the next 90 days, with its ethical guardrails.

*AI PM · Stretch · Tailored*

**What they are testing.** Whether you can design a predictive score people see about themselves: definition, consent, framing and fairness.

**Clarify first.**

- Who sees the score? Only the user.
- What should it drive? Saving and paying on time, not selling credit.

**Outline.**

- Forward-looking resilience: cover 1.5 times monthly expenses under stress
- Four weighted sub-scores and three tiers
- Data and consent: app data by default, bank link optional
- UX: trajectory and one next action
- Four guardrails; metrics

**Model answer.** Spending summaries look backward. This score looks forward: over the next 90 days, how well could you absorb a shock such as a medical bill or a lost month of income? I define resilience as covering 1.5 times monthly expenses from liquid savings or income under stress, and weight four sub-scores by how well they predict it.

| Sub-score | Weight | Signal |
|---|---|---|
| Liquidity buffer | 35% | Liquid savings in months of spending: under one month scores 0–40, one to three 41–70, over three 71–100 |
| Income stability | 30% | Regularity of incoming payments over 90 days |
| Spending trajectory | 20% | Discretionary spending trend against income |
| Obligations | 15% | On-time loan, bill and subscription payments |

The composite runs 0 to 100, updated weekly: 75 and up is Strong, 50 to 74 Fair, below 50 At-Risk.

Data: the user's own app activity by default. Linking a bank account through the Account Aggregator framework improves accuracy and is optional. Nothing reaches a third party without separate consent.

The home-screen card shows the score, tier, seven-day change and one next action for the weakest sub-score: "your buffer is 0.8 months; setting aside ₹5,000 this month moves it." The wording is trajectory, never judgment: "up 8 points, your buffer is growing."

| Guardrail | Rule |
|---|---|
| Not a credit score, not advice | Labeled as personal finance insight; sharing with a lender needs its own explicit consent, after legal review |
| No punitive framing | At-Risk always comes with steps and education; no urgency cues |
| Bias audit | Quarterly; a group scoring 10 or more points below average without a real difference in resilience triggers review. Irregular gig income must not read as mismanagement |
| User context | Users can annotate ("freelancer between projects") |

Metrics: At-Risk users reaching Fair or better in 90 days, target 25 percent; users acting on a recommendation within 14 days; bank-link opt-in near 35 percent. Guardrail: group disparity under 10 points.

From my risk work (S7) I'd carry deterministic, auditable controls; from the pricing dilemma (S10), trust over short-term revenue.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Four weighted sub-scores with thresholds
- Separate consent for external sharing; not a credit score
- Non-punitive framing; a quarterly bias audit with a threshold

**Where answers fail.**

- A spending dashboard called a score
- "Add disclaimers" as the ethics plan

**If they push.**

- *A lender wants a pre-approved loan offer inside every At-Risk alert.* No. Offering credit at the moment of stress to the least resilient users contradicts the product's purpose.

### Q164. You are the PM for an AI coding assistant (like GitHub Copilot). How do you improve the feature for senior engineers vs. junior engineers?

*AI PM · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you can segment users of one AI product by what they need from it, and design features and metrics for each without stereotyping.

**Clarify first.**

- How do we know who is senior? Inferred from behavior; the user can override.
- Separate products? No: one product, different defaults.

**Outline.**

- Seniors want acceleration; juniors want understanding
- Infer level from behavior; adjustable; never gate capability
- Seniors: codebase context, less noise, review-grade annotations, multi-file work
- Juniors: explanations, learning notes, review simulation, tests that teach
- Separate metrics
- Risks: mislabeling and false confidence

**Model answer.** Seniors and juniors want opposite things. A senior wants the assistant to take work they already know how to do and stay out of the way; their complaint is obvious, verbose suggestions. A junior wants code they can understand and learn from; their risk is accepting code they don't understand and building debt.

Coding assistants now span inline completion, chat, an in-editor agent and background agents that open pull requests. The segmentation applies across all of them.

I'd infer level from behavior, not self-report: how much of a suggestion is kept or rewritten, review activity, and the complexity of the surrounding code. It only sets defaults. The user can say "senior in Python, new to Rust," and nobody loses capability because of a label.

| Segment | Improvements |
|---|---|
| Senior | Repository-aware suggestions that follow the team's own patterns; trivial completions suppressed, so there is less to dismiss; security, performance and edge-case annotations, which make it a review partner; multi-file refactors delegated in plain language and returned as a reviewable diff |
| Junior | A "why this code?" panel on every suggestion; short learning notes on unfamiliar patterns, in the editor; review simulation after they write code; generated tests with a comment on why each case exists |

Metrics differ. For seniors: acceptance without major edits, up 10 percent from baseline; time to finish non-trivial changes; how often annotations lead to an edit. For juniors: use of the explanation panel; a code-quality score rising over 90 days; test coverage on assisted code.

Two risks. Mislabeling: the inference will sometimes be wrong, which is why it is visible and adjustable. False confidence: reading an explanation is not understanding, so for juniors I'd occasionally ask them to predict what the code does before accepting, and watch whether their unassisted work improves.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- The two different needs: acceleration versus learning
- Level inferred from behavior and adjustable by the user
- Distinct features and distinct metrics per segment

**Where answers fail.**

- One feature list for everyone
- "Juniors need explanations, seniors need advanced code," with no design or metric

**If they push.**

- *Do assistants help or hurt juniors' growth over six months?* I'd run a randomized study across comparable cohorts with periodic unassisted assessments: measure skill without the tool, not output with it.

### Q165. Design an AI-powered "payments health monitor" for a central bank (like RBI) that tracks systemic risk across the national payments network in real time.

*AI PM · Stretch · Tailored*

**What they are testing.** Whether you can design AI for a regulator: risks, models, alert tiers people can act on, and a firm limit on machine authority.

**Clarify first.**

- Observe or intervene? Advisory only.
- Data? Real-time feeds from the large-value, retail and instant-payment systems under the central bank's oversight powers, with under 30 seconds from event to alert.

**Outline.**

- Why: failures can cascade faster than people react
- Four risk dimensions with signals
- Three models: anomaly detection, network graph, contagion simulation
- Three alert tiers with owners
- Advisory only; one-screen dashboard

**Model answer.** A failing settlement bank, a concentrated fraud attack or a liquidity squeeze can spread between participants in a national payments network faster than human oversight reacts. The monitor gives officers early warning.

| Risk dimension | Signals |
|---|---|
| Settlement liquidity | Intraday usage nearing a participant's limit (alert at 80%); unusual use of the central bank's intraday facility; late settlement confirmations |
| Volume anomalies | Spikes or drops by rail, participant or region, such as a 40% fall in one bank's instant payments in 10 minutes |
| Fraud concentration | Dispute spikes by bank and merchant category (5 times normal in 30 minutes); surges in new accounts through one channel |
| Network interconnection | Net exposures between the most connected participants, relative to capital |

Three models work together. Time-series anomaly detection learns each signal's daily, weekly and seasonal pattern and scores deviations, starting from statistical baselines. A graph model of interbank flows finds the participants whose failure would spread furthest and produces a network fragility index from 0 to 100. A contagion simulation runs daily stress scenarios to show who is exposed to whose failure.

| Tier | Trigger | Owner |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | One signal beyond 3 standard deviations | Duty officer reviews, then closes or escalates |
| 2 | Two or more Tier 1 alerts on one participant or region in 30 minutes, or any signal beyond 5 | Director convenes the participant within 15 minutes |
| 3 | Fragility index above 75, or a simulated failure that would break settlement for more than three others | Deputy Governor or Governor: liquidity support, suspension, public statement |

The monitor never acts on the network. Suspending a bank's settlement has economy-wide consequences and needs a named, accountable human, whatever the model's confidence. Officers get one screen: the fragility index, a heat map of alerts, the latest alerts with recommended actions, and a what-if simulator.

Reconciliation (S4) taught me to look between systems for what a normal dashboard hides, and risk work (S7) to keep automation inside governance.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Four risk dimensions with specific signals
- Three complementary models; three alert tiers with named owners
- Advisory-only authority as a principle

**Where answers fail.**

- A reporting dashboard with charts
- One fraud model, or automated intervention

**If they push.**

- *400 Tier 1 alerts a day and only 12% are real.* Officers will stop reading. Model known seasonality, require a second signal before paging, and track precision as a product metric.

### Q166. What is the PM's role in AI model governance? How do you collaborate with ML, legal, and ethics teams to build AI products responsibly?

*AI PM · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you see governance as part of the PM job, know the artifacts, and can say who is accountable.

**Clarify first.**

- Is there an ethics team? I'll assume a small one.
- Our models or a vendor's? Both.

**Outline.**

- Why the PM: the one role across technical, legal, ethical and user views
- Duties before build, during build, pre-launch, post-launch
- Translation work with ML, legal, ethics, policy
- Six artifacts
- Third-party models
- Accountability and escalation

**Model answer.** Governance needs four kinds of knowledge: what the model does, what the law requires, what is right beyond the law, and what the user experiences. The PM is the one role across all four, and makes the product decision, so governance is part of my job, not a compliance add-on.

| Stage | What I do |
|---|---|
| Before build | Write the use-case justification: why AI over a non-AI approach, and the known risks. Set accuracy, fairness and privacy requirements now, not after seeing results |
| During build | Shape the evaluation suite with ML; keep bias testing in the sprint; review interim results ("the test set is 80% urban") |
| Pre-launch | Run the readiness review: ethics brief, privacy review, bias sign-off, regulatory confirmation. Record each sign-off and its conditions |
| Post-launch | Check that monitoring exists and is acted on; quarterly bias audits; a monthly quality, safety and fairness report to leadership |

With each partner my job is translation. For ML, I turn business and ethical needs into constraints: "false positives under 0.5 percent for rural users, even if overall accuracy dips." With legal, involved from design time, rules become features: a right to human review of an automated decision becomes a button. With ethics, a principle becomes a specification, such as a data exclusion. With policy, upcoming regulation stays on the roadmap.

I own six artifacts, which together are the audit trail: the use-case justification, the model requirements, the readiness record, the monthly monitoring report, incident post-mortems and the published product card.

For a third-party model I can't inspect the training. So I check the provider's usage policy against our use, test its outputs in our own evaluation, settle who responds when it misbehaves, confirm how our data is used, and build a fallback.

Accountability: I own the product decision. Sign-offs inform it; they do not transfer it. A concern I can't resolve, such as a skipped bias test, goes to my director and then the head of product. Nothing ships without governance sign-off.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- The PM as integrator of technical, legal, ethical and user views
- Named artifacts and lifecycle duties
- A process for third-party models; the PM accountable

**Where answers fail.**

- "Ethics handles ethics, legal handles legal"
- Governance as one meeting before launch

**If they push.**

- *The bias audit adds three weeks and engineering wants to launch next week.* The audit is a gate. I'd look for ways to shorten it or launch to a lower-risk slice, and take the trade-off to leadership with the specific risk.

### Q167. An AI agent helps SME customers compose and send payment terms negotiation messages to their clients. What are the key design and ethical considerations?

*AI PM · Standard · Tailored*

**What they are testing.** Whether you can scope an AI that writes sensitive messages in a customer's name, and name the outcome that proves it works.

**Clarify first.**

- Draft or negotiate autonomously? Draft only; the owner reviews and sends.
- Recipients? Business clients of the SME, not consumers.

**Outline.**

- Value: owners avoid asking; the draft removes the awkwardness
- Scope: drafts, suggests, tracks; never sends, replies or accepts
- Personalized from invoice and payment history
- Tone, voice, legal, coercion, accuracy, follow-up cap
- Metric: collection, not sends

**Model answer.** Many small business owners avoid chasing payment because they fear sounding aggressive to a larger client, so they put up with late payment. An agent that drafts a well-judged message removes that barrier, and it must never become a tool for harassment.

The scope is narrow on purpose. It drafts messages, suggests an approach from the client's payment history, and reminds the owner when a reply is due. It does not send, answer the client, or accept a counter-proposal: a message from the business can create obligations, an error in these relationships is hard to repair, and owners must recognize the voice as their own.

Drafts draw on the invoice history with that client, the amount outstanding, and the owner's preferences, such as "polite" or "I can offer 2 percent for early payment." For example: "Invoice 042 for ₹1.8 lakh was due on November 1. Could you settle by the 15th? I'm happy to take it in two parts."

| Consideration | Design |
|---|---|
| Tone | Three choices before drafting: direct, warm, formal with a deadline |
| Voice | The owner's first person, sent by the owner, who is the author of record |
| Legal | Templates reviewed by counsel; requests, not ultimatums; any sentence that reads as a new term (penalty rate, extended credit) is highlighted |
| Coercion | An assertiveness check: "this is quite firm; want a softer version?" No threatening language |
| Accuracy | The invoice data used is shown beside the draft |
| Harassment | One message, a follow-up at 7 days, a final one at 14; the cap is per client, across all requests |

Metrics. Primary: overdue invoices collected within 30 days with assisted outreach against without, target a 20 percent improvement. Then drafts sent within 24 hours, target 60 percent, and days to payment.

On the BINARY invoicing work (S1) I separated mechanical steps from judgment-heavy ones and kept maker-checker controls on the exceptions. Here drafting is the mechanical step and sending is the judgment.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- A hard scope limit: drafting only
- Unintended contractual language as a design constraint
- A per-client follow-up cap; collection rate as the primary metric

**Where answers fail.**

- An email template library with AI branding
- Send rate as the success measure

**If they push.**

- *A client replies, "we don't accept AI-generated communications."* The owner wrote and sent it, with drafting help. I'd offer a plain reply saying so, and a setting to write to that client unaided.

### Q168. How do you think about the AI product development lifecycle differently from the traditional software product development lifecycle?

*AI PM · Standard*

**What they are testing.** Whether you can say what changes in how AI products are built and run, stage by stage, and what the PM does differently.

**Frame it.** What stays the same, three structural differences, then a stage-by-stage comparison.

**Outline.**

- Same: user problem, cross-functional work, iterate, communicate
- Behavior comes from data and context, not only code
- Success is probabilistic
- Behavior drifts after launch
- Six stages compared
- A spiral, not a line

**Model answer.** The foundations don't change: start from the user's problem, work across functions, ship and learn.

Three things do change. First, behavior comes from data and context as much as code: training data for a model we build, and prompts, retrieved content and evaluation sets for one we buy. So requirements include data requirements. Second, success is probabilistic. The system is right some share of the time, so I set acceptable error rates beforehand and design what happens in the remaining cases. Third, behavior drifts after launch. The world changes, inputs shift, and with a vendor model the model itself changes under us. Post-launch is a product phase, not a handoff.

| Stage | Traditional | AI |
|---|---|---|
| Requirements | "The button turns green when clicked" | "Recall above 92% with false positives under 0.3%, on a representative sample across regions," plus failure behavior |
| Design | Interaction design | Also how the user knows when to trust it, how low confidence looks, what the fallback is |
| Development | Deterministic coding | Experiments with models, prompts and data, judged against evaluations I helped define |
| Testing | Does it do what was specified? | Also bias, adversarial inputs, rare cases, calibration; "pass" means error rates within bounds |
| Launch | A release | Staged: 1%, 10%, 50%, 100%, with a gate at each step |
| Post-launch | Bugs and requests | Drift monitoring, re-evaluation, periodic bias audits, version decisions |

Two notes. In development my job is to keep experiments aimed at measures that predict user outcomes, and to stop the team tuning to the benchmark. For launch I write the gate in advance: "we go to 50 percent when live accuracy is within 5 percent of offline, the bias check shows no significant gap, and there were no top-severity safety incidents at 10 percent."

I picture it as a spiral: define the problem, gather data, build, evaluate offline, deploy to a few, evaluate online, improve, widen, monitor, and around again. My job is to keep it turning toward the improvement users will feel most.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Three structural differences, each with a consequence for the PM
- A stage-by-stage contrast with AI-specific content
- A spiral model in place of a linear one

**Where answers fail.**

- "AI products need more testing"
- Leaving out drift and post-launch stewardship

**If they push.**

- *How does this change with a third-party foundation model?* I lose control of training and gain speed. The work moves to evaluation sets, prompts, retrieval and guardrails, and to managing the vendor's version changes.

### Q169. Design an AI product that autonomously detects and recovers failed UPI transactions (money deducted but not credited) without user intervention.

*AI PM · Stretch · Tailored*

**What they are testing.** Whether you can design automated recovery in a regulated payment flow: detection, tiered authority, user communication and abuse.

**Clarify first.**

- Whose side? The payer's app.
- Authority? We can raise reversals through the scheme's dispute process.

**Outline.**

- Problem: debit without credit
- Detection: status checks, debit-credit reconciliation, timeout statuses
- Three stages: verify, initiate, escalate
- Never credit a new or unverified payee
- UX: informed, not burdened
- Regulatory anchor and metrics

**Model answer.** The worst moment in a payments app is "money left my account and the shop says it didn't arrive." UPI handled over 24 billion transactions in September 2026, so even a small failure rate affects a great many people. Most cases reverse on their own; the rest need a dispute users find confusing and slow. This product detects failures early and starts recovery itself.

Detection uses three signals: status checks on pending payments, within the limits the scheme sets on such calls; the payer bank's debit confirmation reconciled against the payee's credit confirmation; and timeout or "deemed" statuses with no credit.

| Stage | What happens | Authority |
|---|---|---|
| 1. Verify | Confirm final status with the scheme, the debit with the payer bank and the credit with the payee bank | Agent alone |
| 2. Initiate | Clear failure under ₹10,000: raise the reversal and notify the user | Agent alone |
| 3. Escalate | Ambiguous status, amount over ₹10,000, repeat failures for the same payer and payee in 24 hours, or two failed recovery attempts | Human team with the evidence: 4 hours over ₹25,000, otherwise 24 |

One thing is never automatic: sending money to a new or unverified payee. Recovery flows attract scams, so funds return only to the source.

The user should feel informed, not burdened. Within five minutes: "we spotted a problem with your payment; we're fixing it and you don't need to do anything." Then "your ₹1,850 is back." We ask for input only on escalated cases.

Regulatory anchor: the central bank's turnaround rules require auto-reversal by the next day for a failed transfer and within five days for a failed merchant payment, with ₹100 a day to the customer for delay. I aim well inside that, and log every step for audit.

Metrics: 85 percent recovered without user action; median recovery under 24 hours; 99 percent of affected users notified within 5 minutes; false positives under 0.5 percent; escalations under 15 percent; satisfaction after resolution above 4.5 of 5.

It combines reconciliation between systems (S4) with an assistant that gathers evidence while systems of record keep authority over financial state (S8).

**What a strong answer shows.**

- A staged workflow with specific checks at each stage
- Tiered autonomy with a clear amount threshold
- User action only on escalation; anchored to the regulator's timelines

**Where answers fail.**

- An alert that sends the user to file a ticket
- Full automation that ignores refund fraud

**If they push.**

- *Fake merchants trigger false "failures" to collect refunds.* Refund only against a bank-confirmed debit, back to the source account, with velocity limits per merchant and device.

### Q170. Design an AI-powered "product intelligence" platform for PMs that aggregates signals from user research, analytics, support tickets, and competitive data to autonomously generate prioritized recommendations.

*AI PM · Stretch*

**What they are testing.** Whether you can design an AI synthesis product for experts: architecture, what makes an insight trustworthy, and why it recommends but does not act.

**Clarify first.**

- Internal tool or product? A product for PMs at mid-to-large software companies.
- Recommend or act? Recommend only.

**Outline.**

- Pain: too much signal; synthesis is the bottleneck
- Four layers: ingest, process, synthesize, recommend
- Core idea: independent sources agreeing
- Ranking: evidence times impact, over effort
- Recommendations only, and why
- Trust design; metrics

**Model answer.** PMs are not short of signal: interview transcripts, thousands of support tickets, weekly analytics, survey comments, competitor news. The bottleneck is synthesis; assume it takes 15 to 20 percent of a PM's time. The platform does that synthesis and shows its evidence.

| Layer | What it does |
|---|---|
| 1. Ingest | Connects, with authorization per source, to the research repository, product analytics, support desk, review sites, competitor changelogs and the issue tracker |
| 2. Process | Tags raw input: needs, friction points and requests from transcripts, each with its quote; adoption trends and drop-off points; ticket clusters with volume trends; competitor feature and pricing changes |
| 3. Synthesize | Finds themes that appear across independent sources and scores confidence by how many agree |
| 4. Recommend | For each strong theme: problem statement, users affected and severity, three possible directions, open questions and the research to settle them |

Layer 3 is the product. One source saying "onboarding is confusing" is an anecdote. Interviews saying it, a rising drop-off at that step and a jump in onboarding tickets together are strong evidence. Each theme carries its quotes, data points and ticket cluster.

Ranking: evidence strength times estimated user impact, divided by estimated effort.

The platform recommends; it does not create tickets, change the roadmap or schedule interviews. The data can't see strategy, stakeholder commitments or "we tried that last year and it failed," so the PM keeps full authority to overrule. Drafting an epic for review can come later.

The main risk is over-trust, so I design against it:

- every recommendation links to its raw evidence in one click
- single-source recommendations look different from those with three or more
- dismissing one asks for a reason
- for recommendations acted on, it later checks whether the problem moved

Metrics: over 40 percent of recommendations acted on within 90 days, with under 20 percent signaling a relevance failure; evidence quality rated above 4.0 of 5; 10 or more hours saved per PM per month; and retrospective accuracy, the share where post-launch data confirms the predicted impact.

**What a strong answer shows.**

- Four layers, with cross-signal synthesis as the differentiator
- A deliberate recommend-only model, with the reason
- Evidence citation, confidence and retrospective accuracy

**Where answers fail.**

- A dashboard with AI summaries of each source
- Generic metrics such as satisfaction or NPS

**If they push.**

- *PMs stop doing interviews because "the platform tells us what users want."* It only knows what was already captured. Show coverage gaps, and require recent first-hand research for any high-stakes recommendation.

# Part X — Reference

## Facts to reconcile

Places where two of my own documents state the same fact differently, or where a claim needs a decision before I say it aloud. Nothing here has been resolved for me.

| Fact | The values and where they appear |
|---|---|
| S10: what the MDR dilemma was | story-bank Part 0A (story map): a pricing change that "could have improved short-term economics while disproportionately hurting smaller merchants", with the owner pushing "for a decision that protected long-term merchant trust". story-bank Part 28: the MDR on UPI credit card transactions for small merchants had already been raised from 0.35% to 0.65% in 2023 through normal governance; the issue was that the Payment Charges page kept showing the old rate, found in a routine audit. bank-1 (Q3, Q9) states no S10 fact; this edition says only that the owner "treated small-merchant pricing as a trust problem" |
| S10: how long the dashboard showed the old rate | story-bank Part 28 says "for 3 months post-change" and, in the next sentence, "for approximately 4 months". Not used in this edition Q1-Q25 |
| S2: the owner's role in the onboarding migration | story-bank Part 0A: "I helped move the org toward a custom onboarding engine". story-bank Part 2: "Task: Lead the full migration to a custom onboarding engine". this edition Q3 says only that merchant onboarding and activation "sat in my scope" (story-bank Part 0) |
| S6: size of the device base | story-bank Part 8 headline: "10M+ Devices Automated"; Part 8 Situation: "Approximately 9M active mPOS devices". Not used in this edition Q1-Q25 |
| RuPay credit card monthly activations (story S5) | 0.2M a month in the résumé ("enabling 0.2M monthly activations"); 300K a month in the Story Bank Part 10 ("300,000 new activations every single month") and Part 4 ("300K monthly activations"). Not stated anywhere in Question Bank Parts II and III; reported because S5 is named in Q28, Q43, Q49 and Q53. |
| RuPay credit card activations (S5; cited without a number in Q87 and Q100) | the résumé: "0.2M monthly activations". the Story Bank Part 10: "300K Monthly Activations", "300,000 new activations every single month within 3 months of launch". the Generic PM guide: "crossed the projected 300K activations within the first quarter". Pick one figure and one time base. |
| Role in the Salesforce to custom onboarding migration (S2; Q93 says "I worked on") | the résumé: "Led legacy system migration from Salesforce to custom onboarding engine". the Story Bank Part 0A: "I helped move the org toward a custom onboarding engine". |
